## XVI Again at Hereford / 第十六章 又回赫里福德

===Original===
Some of the people at Carmarthen were taking a great deal of trouble about the matter. One copy of the *Herald* was sent regularly to Mr. Brodrick, another to Isabel, and another to Mr. Owen. It was determined that they should not be kept in ignorance of what was being done. In the first number issued after Mr. Apjohn's last visit to Llanfeare there was a short leading article recapitulating all that was hitherto known of the story. "Mr. Henry Jones," said the article in its last paragraph, "has at length been induced to threaten an action for libel against this newspaper. We doubt much whether he will have the courage to go on with it. But if he does, he will have to put himself into a witness-box, and then probably we may learn something of the truth as to the last will and testament made by Mr. Indefer Jones." All this reached Hereford, and was of course deeply considered there by persons whom it concerned.

Mr. Owen, for some days after the scene which has been described between him and Isabel, saw her frequently, and generally found means to be alone with her for some moments. She made no effort to avoid him, and would fain have been allowed to treat him simply as her dearest friend. But in all these moments he treated her as though she were engaged to be his wife. There was no embracing, no kiss. Isabel would not permit it. But in all terms of affectionate expression he spoke of her and to her as though she were his own; and would only gently laugh at her when she assured him that it could never be so.

"Of course you can torment me a little," he said, smiling, "but the forces arrayed against you are too strong, and you have not a chance on your side. It would be monstrous to suppose that you should go on making me miserable forever—and yourself too."

===Chinese===
卡马森（Carmarthen）方面有好些人正为这件事大费周章。一份《先驱报》（Herald）定期寄给布罗德里克先生（Mr. Brodrick），一份寄给伊莎贝尔（Isabel），还有一份寄给欧文先生（Mr. Owen）。他们决意不让这几个人对事态的进展蒙在鼓里。在阿普约翰先生（Mr. Apjohn）上次造访兰菲尔（Llanfeare）之后出版的第一期上，登了一篇短短的社论，把这件事迄今所知的原委重述了一遍。“亨利·琼斯先生（Mr. Henry Jones），”文章在末段写道，“终于被逼得扬言要以诽谤罪控告本报。他是否有胆量把这场官司打下去，我们深表怀疑。不过他若真要控告，就得站到证人席上去；到那时，关于因德弗·琼斯先生（Mr. Indefer Jones）最后所立遗嘱的若干真相，我们多半便能略知一二了。”这一切传到了赫里福德（Hereford），自然叫那些利害相关的人在那里细细掂量了一番。

自那日他与伊莎贝尔之间那番情形之后，接连几天，欧文先生仍旧常来见她，而且总能设法同她单独相处片刻。她并不躲着他，也巴不得人家容许她只把他当作自己最亲挚的朋友。可是在这些相处的时刻，他的一言一行都像她已许配给自己一般。没有拥抱，没有亲吻。伊莎贝尔不许。但凡传情达意的话，他说起她、对她说话，都把她当成自己的人；而当她说这事断无可能时，他也只是温柔地笑笑罢了。

“你自然可以稍稍折磨我一下，”他微笑着说，“可是列阵与你作对的势力太强大了，你这边毫无机会。要是设想你会永远这样叫我痛苦下去——连你自己也一同痛苦——那简直悖逆情理。”

===Original===
In answer to this she could only say that she cared but little for her own misery, and did not believe in his. "The question is," she said, "whether it be fitting. As I feel that it is not fitting, I certainly shall not do it." In answer to this he would again smile, and tell her that a month or two at furthest would see her absolutely conquered.

Then the newspapers reached them. When it became clear to him that there existed in Carmarthenshire so strong a doubt as to the validity of the will under which the property was at present held, then Mr. Owen's visits to the house became rarer and different in their nature. Then he was willing to be simply the friend of the family, and as such he sought no especial interviews with Isabel. Between him and Isabel no word was spoken as to the contents of the newspaper. But between Mr. Brodrick and the clergyman many words were spoken. Mr. Brodrick declared at once to his intended son-in-law his belief in the accusations which were implied—which were implied at first, but afterwards made in terms so frightfully clear. When such words as those were said and printed there could, he urged, be no doubt as to what was believed in Carmarthen. And why should it be believed without ground that any man had done so hideous a deed as to destroy a will? The lawyer's hair stood almost on end as he spoke of the atrocity; but yet he believed it. Would a respectable newspaper such as the *Carmarthen Herald* commit itself to such a course without the strongest assurance? What was it to the *Carmarthen Herald*? Did not the very continuance of the articles make it clear that the readers of the paper were in accordance with the writer? Would the public of Carmarthen sympathise in such an attack without the strongest ground? He, the attorney, fully believed in Cousin Henry's guilt; but he was not on that account sanguine as to the proof. If, during his sojourn at Llanfeare, either immediately before the old squire's death or after it, but before the funeral, he had been enabled to lay his hand upon the will and destroy it, what hope would there be of evidence of such guilt? As to that idea of forcing the man to tell such a tale against himself by the torment of cross-examination, he did not believe it at all. A man who had been strong enough to destroy a will would be too strong for that. Perhaps he thought that any man would be too strong, not having known Cousin Henry. Among all the possible chances which occurred to his mind—and his mind at this time was greatly filled with such considerations—nothing like the truth suggested itself to him. His heart was tormented by the idea that the property had been stolen from his child, that the glory of being father-in-law to Llanfeare had been filched from himself, and that no hope for redress remained. He sympathised altogether with the newspaper. He felt grateful to the newspaper. He declared the editor to be a man specially noble and brave in his calling. But he did not believe that the newspaper would do any good either to him or to Isabel.

===Chinese===
对这话，她只能回答说，自己的痛苦她并不怎么放在心上，而他的痛苦她也不相信。“问题在于，”她说，“这事情合不合适。既然我觉得不合适，我就断然不会去做。”听了这话，他又会莞尔一笑，告诉她说，至多一两个月，她准会被彻底征服。

后来报纸寄到了他们手里。欧文先生一旦看明白，卡马森郡（Carmarthenshire）对眼下据以持有产业的那份遗嘱是否有效竟存在如此强烈的怀疑，他登门探访的次数便稀疏了下来，性质也不同了。这时他甘愿只做这家人的一位普通朋友，也就不再谋求同伊莎贝尔单独相见。在他和伊莎贝尔之间，报纸上登了些什么，一个字也没有提起。可在布罗德里克先生与那位牧师之间，话就谈得多了。布罗德里克当即向这位准女婿表明，他相信那些影射中的指控——起初还只是影射，后来却说得分明得怕人。他力言，话既然说到了这个地步，又白纸黑字印了出来，卡马森那边信的是什么，已不容置疑。再说，若无凭无据，谁肯相信一个人竟干得出销毁遗嘱这样骇人听闻的勾当？这位律师说起那桩暴行时，头发都几乎竖了起来；然而他信了。像《卡马森先驱报》（Carmarthen Herald）这样一份体面报纸，若非握有十足的把握，岂肯走上这样一条路？这么做到底能让《卡马森先驱报》得着什么？这些文章一期接一期地登下去，岂不明白表示，报纸的读者与撰稿人是同心同德的？若非握有最坚实的根据，卡马森的公众岂会对这样一场攻讦报以同情？他这位律师，对亨利表哥（Cousin Henry）的罪行深信不疑；但他并不因此就对取证之事抱乐观。倘若那人在兰菲尔小住期间——不论恰在老乡绅去世之前还是之后、总之在葬礼之前——得以把遗嘱弄到手并加以销毁，这样的罪行还能指望找到什么罪证？至于靠交叉盘问的煎熬逼那人自己讲出不利于己的情节，这个主意他压根儿不信。一个有能耐毁掉遗嘱的人，必定也有能耐顶住盘问。也许他以为随便什么人都顶得住——那是因为他还不曾见识过亨利表哥。在他心里浮动的种种可能之中——此时他的脑子已被这类思虑塞得满满的——没有一样与真相沾得上边。他心里受着煎熬：一想到产业是从他女儿手里偷走的，做兰菲尔岳丈的体面是从他自己身上窃去的，而补救的希望已荡然无存。他对报纸全然抱着同感。他感激那份报纸。他宣称那位主编是同行中出类拔萃地高贵而勇敢的人物。但他并不相信，报纸能给伊莎贝尔或是他本人带来任何好处。

===Original===
Mr. Owen doubted altogether the righteousness of the proceeding as regarded the newspaper. As far as he could see there was no evidence against Cousin Henry. There seemed to him to be an injustice in accusing a man of a great crime, simply because the crime might have been possible, and would, if committed, have been beneficial to the criminal. That plan of frightening the man into self-accusation by the terrors of cross-examination was distasteful to him. He would not sympathise with the newspaper. But still he found himself compelled to retreat from that affectation of certainty in regard to Isabel which he had assumed when he knew only that the will had been proved, and that Cousin Henry was in possession of the property. He had regarded Isabel and the property as altogether separated from each other. Now he learned that such was not the general opinion in Carmarthenshire. It was not his desire to push forward his suit with the heiress of Llanfeare. He had been rejected on what he had acknowledged to be fitting grounds while that had been her position. When the matter had been altogether settled in Cousin Henry's favour, then he could come forward again.

Isabel was quite sure that the newspaper was right. Did she not remember the dying words with which her uncle had told her that he had again made her his heir? And had she not always clearly in her mind the hangdog look of that wretched man? She was strong-minded—but yet a woman, with a woman's propensity to follow her feelings rather than either facts or reason. Her lover had told her that her uncle had been very feeble when those words had been spoken, with his mind probably vague and his thoughts wandering. It had, perhaps, been but a dream. Such words did not suffice as evidence on which to believe a man guilty of so great a crime. She knew—so she declared to herself—that the old man's words had not been vague. And as to those hangdog looks—her lover had told her that she should not allow a man's countenance to go so far in evidence as that! In so judging she would trust much too far to her own power of discernment. She would not contradict him, but she felt sure of her discernment in that respect. She did not in the least doubt the truth of the evidence conveyed by the man's hangdog face.

===Chinese===
至于报纸的这一做法，欧文先生对它是否正当是全然怀疑的。就他所见，并没有什么证据不利于亨利表哥。仅仅因为一桩罪行可能干得成、干成了又对罪犯本人有利，就指控这人犯下大罪，在他看来有失公道。靠交叉盘问的威吓吓得那人自我控诉，这个办法他也很反感。他不肯对报纸表示同情。然而他发觉自己不得不退让，收回那种言之凿凿的姿态——那是他还只知道遗嘱已验讫、亨利表哥已占有产业的时候摆出来的。他原本一直把伊莎贝尔和那份产业看作毫不相干的两码事。如今他才知道，卡马森郡的一般舆论并不作此想。他并不想把自己对兰菲尔女继承人的求婚再往前推。当初她还是女继承人的时候，他遭到过拒绝，而他也承认那些理由是正当的。等这件事完全了结、亨利表哥稳操胜券，他自可再度登门。

伊莎贝尔却深信报纸所言不虚。伯父临终之际亲口告诉她，他已重新立她为继承人——这些话她岂能不记得？那个可怜虫那副做贼心虚的嘴脸，她又何尝不时时看在眼里？她是个有主见的人——可终究是个女人，带着女人共有的脾性：宁肯跟着感觉走，也不信事实与理智。她的爱人对她说过，伯父讲那些话时已经十分衰弱，神志多半恍惚，心绪飘忽游移，那也许只是一场梦。这样的话，不足以作为证据去断定一个人犯下如此重大的罪行。她知道——她对自己这样申明——老人的话并不含糊。至于那副做贼心虚的神气——她的爱人叮嘱过她，不可以把一个人的相貌当作这样的证据！照这样下判断，她是把自己的眼力信得太过了。她不愿跟他顶撞，可在这一点上，她相信自己的眼力靠得住。那张做贼心虚的脸所提供的证据，她没有一丝一毫的怀疑。

===Original===
She had sworn to herself a thousand times that she would not covet the house and property. When her uncle had first declared to her his purpose of disinheriting her, she had been quite sure of herself that her love for him should not be affected by the change. It had been her pride to think that she could soar above any consideration of money and be sure of her own nobility, even though she should be stricken with absolute poverty. But now she was tempted to long that the newspaper might be found to be right. Was there any man so fitted to be exalted in the world, so sure to fill a high place with honour, as her lover? Though she might not want Llanfeare for herself, was she not bound to want it for his sake? He had told her how certain he was of her heart—how sure he was that sooner or later he would win her hand. She had almost begun to think that it must be so—that her strength would not suffice for her to hold to her purpose. But how sweet would be her triumph if she could turn to him and tell him that now the hour had come in which she would be proud to become his wife! "I love you well enough to rejoice in giving you something, but too well to have been a burden on you when I could give you nothing." That would be sweet to her! Then there should be kisses! As for Cousin Henry, there was not even pity in her heart towards him. It would be time to pity him when he should have been made to give up the fruits of his wickedness and to confess his faults.

Mrs. Brodrick was not made to understand the newspapers, nor did she care much about the work which they had taken in hand. If Isabel could be made to accept that smaller legacy, so that Mr. Owen might marry her out of hand and take her away, that would be enough to satisfy Mrs. Brodrick. If Isabel were settled somewhere with Mr. Owen, their joint means being sufficient to make it certain that no calls would be made on the paternal resources, that would satisfy Mrs. Brodrick's craving in regard to the Welsh property. She was not sure that she was anxious to see the half-sister of her own children altogether removed from their sphere and exalted so high. And then this smaller stroke of good fortune might be so much more easily made certain! A single word from Isabel herself, a word which any girl less endowed with wicked obstinacy would have spoken at once, would make that sure and immediate. Whereas this great inheritance which was to depend upon some almost impossible confession of the man who enjoyed it, seemed to her to be as distant as ever.

===Chinese===
她曾千百次在心里发誓，决不贪图那座宅子和那份产业。伯父初次向她表明要剥夺她的继承权时，她对自己满有把握：她对伯父的爱决不因这一变故而有分毫减损。她一向引以为豪的是，自己能凌驾于金钱的考量之上；纵然落入一贫如洗的境地，也能确信自身的高贵。可如今她却禁不住暗暗巴望，报纸说的或许是真的。世上还有谁比她的爱人更配出人头地、更有把握体面地身居高位？纵然她并不为自己想要兰菲尔，难道不该为他着想而想要它吗？他对她说过，他对她的心多么有把握——多么确信自己迟早会赢得她的应允。她几乎已经开始认定事情必然如此——她的毅力怕是不够，撑不住自己的初衷了。可是，倘若她能转过身去对他说：如今时候到了，她将自豪地做他的妻子——那样的胜利该有多甜！“我爱你，爱得乐于拿东西给你；也正因爱得深，在我拿不出东西的时候，才决不肯成为你的负担。”这在她该有多甜！到那时，还会有亲吻！至于亨利表哥，她心里对他连一点怜悯也没有。等他被迫交出罪恶的果实、认下自己的过错，那时才轮得到可怜他。

布罗德里克太太生来就弄不懂那些报纸，对它们揽下的这桩事也不怎么上心。只要能让伊莎贝尔接受那笔较小的遗赠，好叫欧文先生即刻把她娶走带走，这就够让布罗德里克太太称心如意了。只要伊莎贝尔同欧文先生在什么地方安顿下来，两人合起来的进项足以保准不必再伸手动用父亲的家底，她对那份威尔士产业的渴念也就满足了。至于要不要看自己亲生儿女的那位异母姐姐彻底脱离他们的圈子、攀得那样高，她心中也没有定见。再说，这桩较小的福气要弄到手，稳妥得多、容易得多！只要伊莎贝尔自己一句话——换了哪个姑娘，只要不像她这样刁钻执拗，早一口气说出口了——那件事立时就能说定。至于这份大产业，却要指望那占着它的男人来一通几乎不可能的招供，在她看来还是同先前一样遥不可及。

===Original===
"Bother the newspapers," she said to her eldest daughter; "why doesn't she write and sign the receipt, and take her income like anyone else? She was getting new boots at Jackson's yesterday, and where is the money to come from? If any of you want new boots, papa is sure to tell me of it!"

Her spirit was embittered too by the severity of certain words which her husband had spoken to her. Isabel had appealed to her father when her stepmother had reproached her with being a burden in the house.

===Chinese===
“烦死人的报纸，”她对大女儿说，“她怎么就不肯写封信把收条签了，跟别人一样把年金领了？她昨天还在杰克逊鞋店（Jackson's）挑新靴子呢，钱从哪儿来？你们哪个要买新靴子，爸爸可是准得向我念叨的！”

丈夫对她讲过的几句严厉话，也叫她心里生出怨气。继母责备她在家里是个累赘，伊莎贝尔便向父亲求告了。

===Original===
"Papa," she had said, "let me leave the house and earn something. I can at any rate earn my bread."

Then Mr. Brodrick had been very angry. He too had wished to accelerate the marriage between his daughter and her lover, thinking that she would surely accept the money on her lover's behalf. He too had been annoyed at the persistency of her double refusal. But it had been very far from his purpose to drive his girl from his house, or to subject her to the misery of such reproaches as his wife had cast upon her.

"My dear," he had said, "there is no necessity for anything of the kind. I and your mother are only anxious for your welfare. I think that you should take your uncle's money, if not for your own sake, then for the sake of him to whom we all hope that you will soon be married. But putting that aside you are as well entitled to remain here as your sisters, and, until you are married, here will be your home."

===Chinese===
“爸爸，”她说，“让我离开这个家，出去挣点什么吧。我至少挣得起自己的面包。”

布罗德里克先生听了这话，大为恼火。他也巴望着女儿同她爱人的婚事早日办成，原以为她定会看在爱人的份上收下那笔钱。她两度回绝、执意不从，同样叫他恼火。可是要把女儿赶出自己的家门，或是任她去受他妻子掷来的那种种责难之苦，却远非他的本意。

“我的好孩子，”他说，“无论怎样都没有必要。我和你母亲一心只盼你安好。我看你应当收下你伯父那笔钱，即便不为你自己打算，也该为那位我们都盼着你早日嫁过去的人打算。可撇开这一层不谈，你同你的妹妹们一样有权住在这里；在你出嫁之前，这里就是你的家。”

===Original===
There was comfort in this, some small comfort, but it did not tend to create pleasant intercourse between Isabel and her stepmother. Mrs. Brodrick was a woman who submitted herself habitually to her husband, and intended to obey him, but one who nevertheless would not be deterred from her own little purposes. She felt herself to be ill-used by Isabel's presence in the house. Many years ago Isabel had been taken away, and she had been given to understand that Isabel was removed forever. There was to be no more expense, no more trouble—there should be no more jealousies in regard to Isabel. The old uncle had promised to do everything, and that sore had been removed from her life. Now Isabel had come back again, and insisted on remaining there—so unnecessarily! Now again there were those boots to be bought at Jackson's, and all those other increased expenditures which another back, another head, another mouth, and another pair of feet must create. And then it was so palpable that Hereford thought much of Isabel, but thought little or nothing of her own girls. Such a one as Mrs. Brodrick was sure to make herself unpleasant in circumstances such as these.

"Isabel," she said to her one day, "I didn't say anything about you being turned out of the house."

===Chinese===
这话听着宽心，也算几分安慰，却无助于伊莎贝尔同继母之间融洽相处。布罗德里克太太素来顺从丈夫，也打算听丈夫的话，可尽管如此，她那点小算盘却从不肯撒手。她觉得伊莎贝尔住在这座宅子里，就是她受了亏待。许多年前伊莎贝尔被送走了，她得到的说法是从此永远送走了。再不必破费，再不必操心——关于伊莎贝尔的种种醋意也该从此冰消。老伯父答应包办一切，她生活里的这根刺算是拔掉了。如今伊莎贝尔又回来了，还执意住着不走——全然多余！如今又要到杰克逊鞋店去买靴子了，还有添一副身板、一颗脑袋、一张嘴、一双脚所带来的一切增长的开销。何况赫里福德城里人人看重伊莎贝尔，对她亲生的女儿们却瞧不上眼，这一点再明白不过。布罗德里克太太这路人，处在这种境况里，必定要弄得人人都别扭。

一天她对伊莎贝尔说：“伊莎贝尔，我可没说过要把你撵出家门的话。”

===Original===
"Who has said that you did, mother?"

"You shouldn't have gone to your father and talked about going out as a housemaid."

"I told papa that if he thought it right, I would endeavour to earn my bread."

"You told him that I had complained about you being here."

===Chinese===
“谁说你说过这话了，妈妈？”

“你不该去找你爸爸，说什么要出去当女佣。”

“我告诉爸爸，只要他觉得应该，我就去设法挣自己的面包。”

“你对他说了，说我抱怨你待在这儿。”

===Original===
"So you did. I had to tell him so, or I could not explain my purpose. Of course I am a burden. Every human being who eats and wears clothes and earns nothing is a burden. And I know that this is thought of the more because it had been felt that I had been—been disposed of."

"You could be disposed of now, as you call it, if you pleased."

"But I do not please. That is a matter on which I will listen to no dictation. Therefore it is that I wish that I could go away and earn my own bread. I choose to be independent in that matter, and therefore I ought to suffer for it. It is reasonable enough that I should be felt to be a burden."

Then the other girls came in, and nothing more was said till, after an hour or two, Mrs. Brodrick and Isabel were again alone together.

===Chinese===
“你本来就抱怨过。这话我非说不可，不然我没法说明自己的意思。我当然是个累赘。凡是吃饭穿衣、分文不挣的人，都是累赘。而且我知道，正因为大家觉得我本已经被——已经被打发出去了，如今就更是这样看我。”

“只要你乐意，眼下也‘打发’得掉，就照你自己的说法。”

“可我不愿意。这件事上，我不听任何人的摆布。所以我还巴不得能走开，去挣自己的面包。这件事我偏要自作主张，那么我就该为此受苦。人家觉得我是累赘，这再合理不过。”

随后几个女儿进了屋，一时再无话头；约莫一两个钟头后，屋里又只剩布罗德里克太太和伊莎贝尔两个人。

===Original===
"I do think it very odd that you cannot take that money; I certainly do," said Mrs. Brodrick.

"What is the use of going on about it? I shall not be made to take it."

"And all those people at Carmarthen so sure that you are entitled to ever so much more! I say nothing about burdens, but I cannot conceive how you can reconcile it to your conscience when your poor papa has got so many things to pay, and is so little able to pay them."

===Chinese===
“你竟不能收下那笔钱，我觉得真是奇怪透了；真是奇怪透了，”布罗德里克太太说。

“这事再说下去有什么用？我是决不会被逼着收下的。”

“再说，卡马森那些人都笃定你理应得到多得多的东西！累赘的话我不说了，可你可怜的爸爸要付的账那么多、又那么付不起，我真想不通，你的良心怎么安得下来。”

===Original===
Then she paused, but as Isabel would not be enticed into any further declaration of independence, she continued, "It certainly is a setting up of your own judgment against people who must know better. As for Mr. Owen, of course it will drive him to look for someone else. The young man wants a wife, and of course he will find one. Then that chance will be lost."

In this way Isabel did not pass her time comfortably at Hereford.

===Chinese===
说到这里她停了停，可伊莎贝尔不肯上钩、再发什么独立宣言，她便接着说道：“这分明是拿你自己的判断，去顶撞那些必定比你明白的人。至于欧文先生，这当然会把他逼得另寻别人。年轻人需要一位太太，自然找得到。到那时，这个机会就丢掉了。”

就这样，伊莎贝尔在赫里福德的日子过得并不舒坦。

## IX Alone at Llanfeare / 第九章 独守兰菲尔

===Original===
On the day after the reading of the will, Henry Indefer Jones, Esq., of Llanfeare, as he was now to be called, was left alone in his house, his cousin Isabel having taken her departure from the place in the manner proposed by her. And the lawyer was gone, and the doctor, and the tenants did not come near him, and the butler and the housekeeper kept out of his way, and there was probably no man in all South Wales more lonely and desolate than the new Squire of Llanfeare on that morning.

The cruelty of it, the injustice of it, the unprecedented hardness of it all! Such were the ideas which presented themselves to him as hour after hour he sat in the book-room with his eyes fixed on the volume of Jeremy Taylor’s sermons. He had done nothing wrong﻿—so he told himself﻿—had not even coveted anything that did not belong to him. It was in accordance with his uncle’s expressed desire that he had come to Llanfeare, and been introduced to the tenants as their future landlord, and had taken upon himself the place of the heir. Then the old man had announced to him his change of mind; but had not announced it to others, had not declared his altered purpose to the world at Llanfeare, and had not at once sent him back to his London office. Had he done so, that would have been better. There would have been a gross injustice, but that would have been the end of it, and he would have gone back to his London work unhappy indeed, but with some possibility of life before him. Now it seemed as though any mode of living would be impossible to him. While that fatal paper remained hidden in the fatal volume he could do nothing but sit there and guard it in solitude.

He knew well enough that it behoved him as a man to go out about the estate and the neighbourhood, and to show himself, and to take some part in the life around him, even though he might be miserable and a prey to terror whilst he was doing so. But he could not move from his seat till his mind had been made up as to his future action. He was still in fearful doubt. Through the whole of that first day he declared to himself that his resolution had not yet been made﻿—that he had not yet determined what it would be best that he should do. It was still open to him to say that at any moment he had just found the will. If he could bring himself to do so he might rush off to Carmarthen with the document in his pocket, and still appear before the lawyer as a man triumphant in his own honesty, who at the first moment that it was possible had surrendered all that which was not legally his own, in spite of the foul usage to which he had been subjected. He might still assume the grand air of injured innocence, give back the property to the young woman who had insulted him, and return to his desk in London, leaving behind him in Carmarthenshire a character for magnanimity and honour. Such a line of conduct had charms in his eyes. He was quite alive to the delight of heaping coals of fire on his cousin’s head. She had declared that she would receive nothing at his hands, because she despised him. After that there would be a sweetness, the savour of which was not lost upon his imagination, in forcing her to take all from his hands. And it would become known to all men that it was he who had found the will﻿—he who might have destroyed it without the slightest danger of discovery﻿—he who without peril might thus have made himself owner of Llanfeare. There would be a delight to him in the character which he would thus achieve. But then she had scorned him! No bitterer scorn had ever fallen from the lips or flashed from the eyes of a woman. “We take presents from those we love, not from those we despise!” He had not resented the words at the moment; he had not dared to do so; but not the less had they entered upon his very soul﻿—not the less he hated the woman who had dared so to reply to the generous offer which he had made her.

===Chinese===
宣读遗嘱的第二天，兰菲尔（Llanfeare）的亨利·因德弗·琼斯先生（Henry Indefer Jones, Esq.）——他如今就得这样称呼了——便独自一人留在了宅子里：表妹伊莎贝尔（Isabel）已照她自己提出的办法离开了此地。律师走了，医生也走了，佃户们没有一个走近他，男管家和女管家也都避着他走；那天早晨，只怕整个南威尔士再找不出一个人，比这位兰菲尔的新乡绅更孤独凄凉的了。

这事多么残忍，多么不公，多么史无前例地刻薄！他一个钟头接着一个钟头地坐在书房里，两眼盯着那卷杰里米·泰勒（Jeremy Taylor）的布道文集，心里翻来覆去尽是这类念头。他没有做过一点错事——他这样对自己说——甚至连不属于自己的东西都不曾贪想过。他来到兰菲尔，被引见给佃户们、当作他们日后的东家，接下继承人的名分，桩桩件件都出自伯父明白表示的心愿。后来老人向他一人宣布了自己改变了主意；却没有向旁人宣布，没有把改变了的意向向兰菲尔上下人等言明，也没有立刻把他打发回伦敦的办事处。老人若那样做了，反倒好些。那固然是一桩十足的冤屈，可事情也就到此为止，他也就回伦敦做他的差事去了，纵然苦恼，前面好歹还有几分过日子的指望。如今看来，怎样的活法对他都不可能了。只要那张要命的文书还藏在那本要命的书里，他就什么也做不成，只能坐在那儿，孤零零地守着它。

他心里很清楚，身为一个男人，他理应出门到田庄和四邻走走，露露面，在周围的生活里担一分事，哪怕这么做的时候满心悲苦、饱受恐惧的煎熬。可是在拿定主意、定下往后怎么做之前，他从座位上挪不动身子。他还陷在可怕的犹疑里。头一整天，他都在对自己申辩：决心还没有下——他还没有确定怎样做才最上算。随时都来得及说遗嘱是他刚刚找到的。他若能说服自己这么办，便可以把那份文书揣在口袋里赶到卡马森（Carmarthen）去，站在律师面前依旧是一副为自己的诚实得胜的模样：一有可能，他就交出了那一切并非法定属于他的东西，全不顾自己遭受的恶待。他依旧可以摆出无辜受屈的堂皇气派，把产业退还给那个羞辱过他的年轻女子，回伦敦坐进自己的办事间，在卡马森郡（Carmarthenshire）留下一副宽宏高尚的名声。这样一条路在他眼里自有迷人之处。把炭火堆在表妹头上，那份快意他领会得再真切不过。她曾宣称，凡从他手里来的东西她一概不收，因为她看不起他。此后，逼着她从他手里把一切统统接过去，这里头自有一股甜味，他的想象不曾放过这份滋味。而且世人都会知道，遗嘱是他找到的——他本可以把它毁掉而不致有半点败露之险——他本可以毫无风险地使自己成为兰菲尔的主人。他由此挣得的名声将给他快意。可是她那样奚落过他！女子唇间吐出过、眼中闪过的轻蔑，再没有比这更刻毒的了。“我们收我们所爱的人送的礼，不收我们所看不起的人送的礼！”当时他没有对这句话发作；他不敢；但这话照样钻进了他的灵魂深处——他照样恨透了那个女人，她竟敢这样回敬他慷慨的馈赠。

===Original===
And then there was an idea present to him through it all that abstract justice, if abstract justice could be reached, would declare that the property should be his. The old man had made his will with all the due paraphernalia of will-making. There had been the lawyer and the witnesses brought by the lawyer; and, above all, there had been the declared reason of the will and its understood purpose. He had been sent for, and all Carmarthenshire had been made to understand why it was to be so. Then, in his sickness, the old man had changed his mind through some fantastic feeling, and almost on his deathbed, with failing powers, in a condition probably altogether unfit for such a duty, had executed a document which the law might respect, but which true justice, if true justice could be invoked, would certainly repudiate. Could the will be abolished, no more than justice would be done. But, though the will were in his own power, it could not be abolished by his own hands.

As to that abolishing he was perfectly conscious of his own weakness. He could not take the will from its hiding-place and with his own hand thrust it into the flames. He had never as yet even suggested to himself that he would do so. His hair stood on end as he thought of the horrors attendant on such a deed as that. To be made to stand in the dock and be gazed at by the angry eyes of all the court, to be written of as the noted criminal of the day, to hear the verdict of guilty, and then the sentence, and to be aware that he was to be shut up and secluded from all comforts throughout his life! And then, and then, the dread hereafter! For such a deed as that would there not be assured damnation? Although he told himself that justice demanded the destruction of the will, justice could not be achieved by his own hand after such fashion as that.

No; he could not himself destroy the document, though it should remain there for years to make his life a burden to him. As to that he had made up his mind, if to nothing else. Though there might be no peril as to this world﻿—though he might certainly do the deed without a chance of detection from human eyes﻿—though there would in truth be no prospect of that angry judge and ready jury and crushing sentence, yet he could not do it. There was something of a conscience within him. Were he to commit a felony, from the moment of the doing of the deed the fear of eternal punishment would be heavy on his soul, only to be removed by confession and retribution﻿—and then by the trial with the judge, and the jury, and the sentence! He could not destroy the document. But if the book could get itself destroyed, what a blessing it would be! The book was his own, or would be in a few days, when the will should have been properly proved. But if he were to take away the book and sink it in a well, or throw it into the sea, or bury it deep beneath the earth, then it would surely reappear by one of those ever-recurring accidents which are always bringing deeds of darkness to the light. Were he to cast the book into the sea, tied with strings or cased in paper, and leaded, that it should surely sink, so that the will should not by untoward chance float out of it, the book tied and bound and leaded would certainly come up in evidence against him. Were he to move the book, the vacant space would lead to suspicion. He would be safe only by leaving the book where it was, by giving no trace that he had ever been conscious of the contents of the book.

===Chinese===
而且，在这重重念头之中，始终有一个想法缠着他：抽象的公道——倘若抽象的公道求得到的话——会宣告这份产业理应归他。老人当初立遗嘱，凡立遗嘱应有的一应排场样样齐备。有律师，有律师请来的证人；而顶要紧的是，立这遗嘱的缘由是当众宣告了的，它的用意人人心里明白。他是被专门召了来的，整个卡马森郡也都因此明白了事情为何要这样安排。后来，老人生了病，出于某种异想天开的情绪变了主意，几乎是在临终的病榻上，精力衰竭，多半根本不具备办这种事的心神，却签署下一份法律或许认可、而真正的公道——倘若真正的公道可以申述——必定拒不承认的文书。倘若遗嘱能够废止，那也不过是把公道还了回来。可是遗嘱虽在他掌握之中，却不能由他亲手去废止。

至于亲手废止这一层，他对自己的软弱看得一清二楚。他没法把遗嘱从藏处取出来，亲手塞进火里。直到如今，他甚至不曾对自己提过要这么干。一想到这种行径牵连的种种恐怖，他便毛发倒竖：被押上被告席，站在满法庭愤怒的目光之下，被写成当日臭名远扬的罪人，听到有罪的裁定，接着是判决，然后明白自己从此要被关押起来，终身与一切安乐隔绝！还有，还有，那可怕的来世！干下这样的勾当，岂不是注定要永堕地狱？尽管他对自己说公道要求毁掉遗嘱，公道却断不能这样借他自己的手来实现。

不，他不能亲手毁掉那份文书，哪怕它在那里一放多年，把他的日子变成重负。这一点，纵然别的主意一个都拿不定，他至少是拿定了的。纵然现世并无凶险——纵然他确乎可以干下这件事而不致被人的眼睛察觉——纵然实际上并不会有那愤怒的法官、唯命是从的陪审团和毁灭性的判决候着他，他还是干不成。他心里还有一点良知。他若犯下重罪，从动手的那一刻起，对永恒惩罚的畏惧就会压上他的灵魂，唯有招认和赎罪才卸得掉——随后便是那场庭审：法官、陪审团、判决，一样不缺！他不能毁掉那份文书。可是，倘若那本书能自行毁灭，那该是多好的事！那书本是他自己的产业，再过几天，等遗嘱正式查验认定之后，就更是他的了。可他若把书拿走，沉入井底，抛进海里，或是深埋地下，它必定会借着那种层出不穷的意外重新冒头——这类意外向来专把黑暗勾当曝在光天化日之下。他若把书抛进海里，用绳捆了，拿纸包了，再坠上铅块，叫它一定下沉，遗嘱不致因意外的变故漂出书外，那么这本捆了、绑了、坠了铅的书，到头来必定会在证据席上冒出来指认他。他若挪动那本书，空出来的位置就会惹人起疑。他唯一的安稳之策，就是让书留在原处，不留一丝痕迹，叫人看不出他知道书里藏的东西。

===Original===
And yet, if the document were left there, the book would certainly divulge its dread secret at last. The day would come, might come, ah! so quickly, on which the document would be found, and he would be thrust out, penniless as far as any right to Llanfeare was concerned. Some maidservant might find it; some religious inmate of his house who might come there in search of godly teaching! If he could only bring himself to do something at once﻿—to declare that it was there, so that he might avoid all these future miseries! But why had she told him that she despised him, and why had the old man treated him with such unexampled cruelty? So it went on with him for three or four days, during which he still kept his place among the books.

There would be great delight in possessing Llanfeare, if he could in very truth possess it. He would not live there. No; certainly not that. Every tenant about the place had shown him that he was despised. Their manner to him before the old Squire’s death, their faces as they had sat there during the ceremonies of the will, and the fact that no one had been near him since the reading of the will, had shown him that. He had not dared to go to church during the Sunday; and though no one had spoken to him of his daily life, he felt that tales were being told of him. He was sure that Mrs. Griffith had whispered about the place the fact of his constant residence in one room, and that those who heard it would begin to say among themselves that a practice so strange must be connected with the missing will. No, he would not willingly live at Llanfeare. But if he could let Llanfeare, were it but for a song, and enjoy the rents up in London, how pleasant would that be! But then, had ever any man such a sword of Damocles to hang over his head by a single hair, as would be then hanging over his head were he to let Llanfeare or even to leave the house, while that book with its enclosure was there upon the shelves? It did seem to him, as he thought of it, that life would be impossible to him in any room but that as long as the will remained among the leaves of the volume.

Since the moment in which he had discovered the will he had felt the necessity of dealing with the officials of the office in London at which he had been employed. This was an establishment called the Sick and Healthy Life Assurance Company, in which he held some shares, and at which he was employed as a clerk. It would of course be necessary that he should either resign his place or go back to his duties. That the Squire of Llanfeare should be a clerk at the Sick and Healthy would be an anomaly. Could he really be in possession of his rents, the Sick and Healthy would of course see no more of him; but were he to throw up his position and then to lose Llanfeare, how sad, how terrible, how cruel would be his fate! But yet something must be done. In these circumstances he wrote a letter to the manager, detailing all the circumstances with a near approach to the truth, keeping back only the one little circumstance that he himself was acquainted with the whereabouts of the missing will.

===Chinese===
然而，文书若一直留在那里，那本书终究会泄露它那可怕的秘密。那一天会到来，也可能来得——啊！——那么快：文书被人发现，他被人一脚踢出门去，凡兰菲尔的权利一笔勾销，落得两手空空。哪个使女可能撞见它；或是家中哪个虔敬的人，进那屋里来寻找敬神的教诲！他但凡能说服自己立刻动手做点什么——宣布那东西就在那里，好躲开日后这一切苦难——该多好！可她凭什么对他说她看不起他？老头子又凭什么用这等史无前例的残忍待他？他就这样翻来覆去过了三四天，这期间他始终守在书堆中间，寸步未离。

兰菲尔若能实实在在归他所有，那自然是莫大的乐事。他不打算住在这里。不，断然不。田庄上下每个佃户都让他看出，他们瞧他不起。老乡绅生前他们对他的态度，遗嘱仪式上他们坐在那儿的脸色，以及遗嘱读毕以来没有一个人走近他，都向他表明了这一点。礼拜日他连教堂都不敢去；尽管没人当面数落他的日常起居，他觉出背后有人在传他的闲话。他确信格里菲斯太太（Mrs. Griffith）已经在庄子里悄悄传开了他终日缩在一间屋里的情形，而听到的人彼此一议论，必会说这般古怪的行径定与那份失踪的遗嘱有干系。不，他不愿意住在兰菲尔。可他若能把兰菲尔租出去，哪怕租金少得可怜，自己上伦敦去坐享租银，那该多惬意！然而话又说回来，只要那夹着东西的书还立在书架上，他若出租兰菲尔、甚至只是离家出门，头顶上岂不就要凭一根头发吊着一柄达摩克利斯之剑？他左思右想，只觉得只要那张遗嘱还夹在书页中间，除了那一间屋子，他在这宅子里哪儿也活不成。

自从发现遗嘱的那一刻起，他就觉出不能不跟伦敦那处他一直供职的机构的管事们打交道了。那家商号名叫“疾康”人寿保险公司（the Sick and Healthy Life Assurance Company），他在里面持有若干股份，充任办事员。他自然要么辞去职位，要么回去履职。兰菲尔的乡绅却做着“疾康”的办事员，总归不成体统。他若真能坐享租银，“疾康”自然再见不着他；可他若先辞了职位、随后又失掉兰菲尔，那命运该多么可悲、多么可怕、多么残忍！然而总得有个举动。于是他给经理写了一封信，把种种情形几乎照实细陈，只瞒下了一桩小小的情节：那份失踪的遗嘱藏在哪里，他本人是知道的。

===Original===
“It may turn up at any moment,” he explained to the manager, “so that my position as owner of the property is altogether insecure. I feel this so thoroughly that were I forced at the present to choose between the two I should keep my clerkship in the office; but as the condition of things is so extraordinary, perhaps the directors will allow me six months in which to come to a decision, during which I may hold my place, without, of course, drawing any salary.”

Surely, he thought, he could decide on something before the six months should be over. Either he would have destroyed the will, or have sunk the book beneath the waves, or have resolved to do that magnanimous deed which it was still within his power to achieve. The only one thing not possible would be for him to leave Llanfeare and take himself up to the delights of London while the document was yet hidden within the volume.

“I suppose sir, you don’t know yet as to what your plans are going to be?” This was said by Mrs. Griffith as soon as she made her way into the book-room after a somewhat imperious knocking at the door. Hitherto there had been but little communication between Cousin Henry and his servants since the death of the old Squire. Mrs. Griffith had given him warning that she would leave his service, and he had somewhat angrily told her that she might go as soon as it pleased her. Since that she had come to him once daily for his orders, and those orders had certainly been very simple. He had revelled in no luxuries of the table or the cellar since the keys of the house had been committed to his charge. She had been told to provide him with simple food, and with food she had provided him. The condition of his mind had been such that no appetite for the glories of a rich man’s table had yet come to him. That accursed book on the opposite shelf had destroyed all his taste for both wine and meat.

===Chinese===
“它随时可能冒出来，”他向经理解释道，“所以我作为产业主人的地位全然不稳。这一点我体会得再透彻不过：倘若眼下逼着我在两者之间作一抉择，我就保留我在公司里的办事员职位；不过事态既然这样反常，董事会或许容我六个月时间作出决定，在此期间我保留职位，当然，不支取分文薪水。”

他想，六个月届满之前，他总有把握拿定一个主意。到那时，或者遗嘱已经毁掉，或者那本书已经沉入波涛，或者他已决心去完成那桩宽宏的义举——义举仍在他的能力之内。唯独有一件事断然办不到：文书还藏在书里，他却撇下兰菲尔，自己上伦敦享福去。

“先生，我想您还不知道往后有什么打算吧？”这是格里菲斯太太的话。她相当霸道地敲了敲门，便径自挤进了书房。自老乡绅故去以来，亨利表哥与仆人们之间一直没有多少来往。格里菲斯太太先前已向他预告，说她要辞去伺候他的差事；他带了几分火气回她，她几时乐意走就几时走。打那以后，她每天来一回听候吩咐，而那些吩咐实在简单得很。自从宅子的钥匙交到他手上，他在餐桌和酒窖上没有讲究过一点奢华。他只叫她备些清淡饭菜，她也就照备了。他心境是那般光景，富人餐桌上的种种口福，他半点胃口也没有。对面书架上那本可诅咒的书，早把他对酒肉的兴味毁得一干二净。

===Original===
“What do you want to know for?” he asked.

“Well, sir; it is customary for the housekeeper to know something, and if there is no mistress she can only go to the master. We always were very quiet here, but Miss Isabel used to tell me something of what was expected.”

“I don’t expect anything,” said Cousin Henry.

“Is there anybody to come in my place?” she asked.

===Chinese===
“你打听这个做什么？”他问。

“嗳，先生，女管家照规矩总得知道些底细；没有主母，就只有来问主人了。我们这宅子向来清静，可从前伊莎贝尔小姐总还告诉我一点府上有什么安排。”

“我没有什么安排。”亨利表哥说。

“有谁来接我的位子吗？”她问。

===Original===
“What can that be to you? You can go when you please.”

“The other servants want to go, too. Sally won’t stay, nor yet Mrs. Bridgeman.” Mrs. Bridgeman was the cook. “They say they don’t like to live with a gentleman who never goes out of one room.”

“What is it to them what room I live in? I suppose I may live in what room I please in my own house.” This he said with an affectation of anger, feeling that he was bound to be indignant at such inquiries from his own servant, but with more of fear than wrath in his mind. So they had in truth already begun to inquire why it was that he sat there watching the books!

“Just so, Mr. Jones. Of course you can live anywhere you like﻿—in your own house.”

===Chinese===
“这与你何干？你几时乐意走就几时走。”

“别的仆人也都想走。萨莉（Sally）不肯留下，布里奇曼太太（Mrs. Bridgeman）也不肯。”布里奇曼太太是厨娘。“她们说，不愿意跟一位从来不出一间屋子的先生同住一个宅子。”

“我住哪间屋子，与她们何干？我自己家里，我想住哪间就住哪间。”他说这话时装出一腔怒气，觉得自己家的仆人这般探问，他不能不恼；可他心里怕多于怒。这么说来，他们当真已经动手打听他为什么坐在那里守着这些书了！

“说得是，琼斯先生。您自己的家，自然愿意住哪儿就住哪儿。”

===Original===
There was an emphasis on the last words which was no doubt intended to be impertinent. Everyone around was impertinent to him.

“But so can they, sir﻿—not in their own house. They can look for situations, and I thought it my duty just to tell you, because you wouldn’t like to find yourself all alone here, by yourself like.”

“Why is it that everybody turns against me?” he asked suddenly, almost bursting into tears.

At this her woman’s heart was a little softened, though she did despise him thoroughly. “I don’t know about turning, Mr. Jones, but they have been used to such different ways.”

===Chinese===
末了那几个字咬得重，分明存心放肆。他身边没有一个不对他放肆的人。

“可她们也能这样呀，先生——只不过那不是她们自己的家。她们可以另谋差事。我不过是觉得理当禀告您一声，省得往后您发觉这宅子里就剩您光杆一个人，冷清得慌。”

“为什么人人都跟我作对？”他猛地问道，几乎要落下泪来。

听了这话，她那颗妇人的心倒软了些许，虽说她从心底里瞧他不起。“跟您作对倒说不上，琼斯先生，只是她们过惯的日子跟这差得太远了。”

===Original===
“Don’t they get enough to eat?”

“Yes, sir; there’s enough to eat, no doubt. I don’t know as you have interfered about that; not but what as master you might. It isn’t the victuals.”

“What is it, Mrs. Griffith? Why do they want to go away?”

“Well, it is chiefly because of your sitting here alone﻿—never moving, never having your hat on your head, sir. Of course a gentleman can do as he pleases in his own house. There is nothing to make him go out, not even to see his own tenants, nor his own farm, nor nothing else. He’s his own master, sir, in course;﻿—but it is mysterious. There is nothing goes against them sort of people,”﻿—meaning the servants inferior to herself﻿—“like mysteries.”

===Chinese===
“难道她们吃不饱？”

“有的，先生；吃的管够，这没有说的。您没在吃食上插手克扣过——虽说以主人的名分，您要克扣也使得。毛病不在吃食上。”

“那到底为了什么，格里菲斯太太？她们为什么要走？”

“嗳，多半就坏在您老是一个人坐在这儿——从不挪窝，先生，头上也从不戴帽子。绅士在自己家里，自然爱怎么着就怎么着。没有什么能逼他出门——去瞧瞧自家的佃户不成，瞧瞧自家的田庄不成，什么都不瞧。他是自己的主子，先生，那还用说；——可这事透着蹊跷。没有什么东西比蹊跷事更犯那路人的忌讳，”——她指的是比自己低一等的仆人们——“顶叫他们看不惯的就是蹊跷事儿。”

===Original===
Then they already felt that there was a mystery! Oh! what a fool he had been to shut himself up and eat his food there! Of course they would know that this mystery must have some reference to the will. Thus they would so far have traced the truth as to have learnt that the will had a mystery, and that the mystery was located in that room!

There is a pleasant game, requiring much sagacity, in which, by a few answers, one is led closer and closer to a hidden word, till one is enabled to touch it. And as with such a word, so it was with his secret. He must be careful that no eye should once see that his face was turned towards the shelf. At this very moment he shifted his position so as not to look at the shelf, and then thought that she would have observed the movement, and divined the cause.

“Anyways, they begs to say respectful that they wishes you to take a month’s warning. As for me, I wouldn’t go to inconvenience my old master’s heir. I’ll stay till you suits yourself, Mr. Jones; but the old place isn’t to me now what it was.”

“Very well, Mrs. Griffith,” said Cousin Henry, trying to fix his eyes upon an open book in his hands.

===Chinese===
这么说，他们已经觉出其中有蹊跷了！啊！他把自己关在这里、连饭也在这屋里吃，是天字第一号的傻瓜！他们自然会觉得，这蹊跷必定与那份遗嘱有牵连。这样一来，他们把真相已追到这个地步：查出那份遗嘱有蹊跷，而且蹊跷就出在那间屋里！

有一种挺好玩的游戏，极费心智：猜的人凭几句答话，被一步步引向一个藏着的字眼，直到终于伸手够得着它。他这个秘密，眼下就同那字眼一个样。他务必当心，不能让任何眼睛瞥见他的脸朝书架那边转。就在这一刻，他挪动身子，偏过头不去看书架，转念又想：这个动作只怕已被她看在眼里，连缘故也叫她猜着了。

“不管怎么着，她们恭恭敬敬地请您收下一个月的辞工预告。至于我，我是断不肯给老主人的继承人添麻烦的。琼斯先生，您几时合适我几时走；只是这老宅子在我眼里，再不是从前的老宅子了。”

“好吧，格里菲斯太太。”亨利表哥说着，竭力把两眼盯在自己手里摊开的一本书上。

## XIV An Action for Libel / 第十四章 一桩诽谤诉讼

===Original===
When the man had asked him that question—Is there any secret you can tell?—Cousin Henry did, for half a minute, make up his mind to tell the whole story, and reveal everything as it had occurred. Then he remembered the lie which he had told, the lie to which he had signed his name when he had been called upon to prove the will in Carmarthen. Had he not by the unconsidered act of that moment committed some crime for which he could be prosecuted and sent to gaol? Had it not been perjury? From the very beginning he had determined that he would support his possession of the property by no criminal deed. He had not hidden the will in the book. He had not interfered in the search. He had done nothing incompatible with innocence. So it had been with him till he had been called upon, without a moment having been allowed to him for thinking, to sign his name to that declaration. The remembrance of this came to him as he almost made up his mind to rise from his seat and pull the book down from the shelf. And then another thought occurred to him. Could he not tell Mr. Griffith that he had discovered the document since he had made that declaration—that he had discovered it only on that morning? But he had felt that a story such as that would receive no belief, and he had feared to estrange his only friend by a palpable lie. He had therefore said that there was no secret—had said so after a pause which had assured Mr. Griffith of the existence of a mystery—had said so with a face which of itself had declared the truth.

When the farmer left him he knew well enough that the man doubted him—nay, that the man was assured of his guilt. It had come to be so with all whom he had encountered since he had first reached Llanfeare. His uncle who had sent for him had turned from him; his cousin had scorned him; the tenants had refused to accept him when there certainly had been no cause for their rejection. Mr. Apjohn from the first had looked at him with accusing eyes; his servants were spies upon his actions; this newspaper was rending his very vitals; and now this one last friend had deserted him. He thought that if only he could summon courage for the deed, it would be best for him to throw himself from the rocks.

But there was no such courage in him. The one idea remaining to him was to save himself from the horrors of a criminal prosecution. If he did not himself touch the document, or give any sign of his consciousness of its presence, they could not prove that he had known of its whereabouts. If they would only find it and let him go! But they did not find it, and he could not put them on its trace. As to these wicked libels, Mr. Griffith had asked him why he did not have recourse to a court of law, and refute them by the courage of his presence. He understood the proposition in all its force. Why did he not show himself able to bear any questions which the ingenuity of a lawyer could put to him? Simply because he was unable to bear them. The truth would be extracted from him in the process. Though he should have fortified himself with strongest resolves, he would be unable to hide his guilty knowledge. He knew that of himself. He would be sure to give testimony against himself, on the strength of which he would be dragged from the witness-box to the dock.

He declared to himself that, let the newspaper say what it would, he would not of his own motion throw himself among the lion’s teeth which were prepared for him. But in so resolving he did not know what further external force might be applied to him. When the old tenant had sternly told him that he should go like a man into the witness-box and tell his own story on his oath, that had been hard to bear. But there came worse than that—a power more difficult to resist. On the following morning Mr. Apjohn arrived at Llanfeare, having driven himself over from Carmarthen, and was at once shown into the book-room. The lawyer was a man who, by his friends and by his clients in general, was considered to be a pleasant fellow as well as a cautious man of business. He was good at a dinner-table, serviceable with a gun, and always happy on horseback. He could catch a fish, and was known to be partial to a rubber at whist. He certainly was not regarded as a hard or cruel man. But Cousin Henry, in looking at him, had always seen a sternness in his eye, some curve of a frown upon his brow, which had been uncomfortable to him. From the beginning of their intercourse he had been afraid of the lawyer. He had felt that he was looked into and scrutinised, and found to be wanting. Mr. Apjohn had, of course, been on Isabel’s side. All Carmarthenshire knew that he had done his best to induce the old squire to maintain Isabel as his heiress. Cousin Henry was well aware of that. But still why had this attorney always looked at him with accusing eyes? When he had signed that declaration at Carmarthen, the attorney had shown by his face that he believed the declaration to be false. And now this man was there, and there was nothing for him but to endure his questions.
===Chinese===
当那人向他问出那个问题——“你有什么秘密可以告诉我吗？”——亨利表哥（Cousin Henry）确曾有半分钟当真打定了主意，要把整个事情原原本本说出来，把一切经过和盘托出。随后他想起了自己撒过的那个谎——在卡马森（Carmarthen）被请去认证那份遗嘱时，他曾在一份声明上签下自己的名字。他难道不会因那一时欠考虑的举动而犯下某种罪名，被起诉、被送进监狱吗？那难道不是伪证罪吗？打从一开始他就下定决心，绝不用任何犯罪行为来保住这份财产。遗嘱不是他藏进书里去的。搜查他也没有插手。他所做的任何事情都与清白无违。他一直是这样，直到人家突然要他在那份声明上签字，连一息思索的工夫都不曾给他。就在他几乎打定主意要从座位上站起来、把那本书从架子上取下来的时候，这段记忆涌上了心头。接着另一个念头又冒了出来。他何不告诉格里菲斯先生（Mr. Griffith），说那份文件是他在签下声明之后才发现的——就是那天早上才发现的？但他觉得，这样的说辞绝不会有人相信，而且他害怕用一个一望即知的谎言，把他唯一的朋友也推远了。于是他说没有什么秘密——说这话之前先顿了一顿，而那一顿已经让格里菲斯先生确信其中必有隐情——说这话时那张脸，本身就把实情昭示得清清楚楚。

农夫离开之后，他心里很清楚，这个人疑心他——不，是已经认定他有罪。自从他初到兰菲尔（Llanfeare），他所遇到的每一个人都渐渐成了这样。特意把他唤来的伯父对他掉头不顾；他的表妹鄙夷他；佃户们拒绝接纳他，尽管他们的拒斥实在毫无缘由。阿普约翰先生（Mr. Apjohn）从一开始就用责问的目光看他；他的仆人们是监视他一举一动的眼线；这份报纸在撕咬他的五脏六腑；而如今，这最后一位朋友也弃他而去了。他想，只消鼓得起这点勇气，最好的出路莫过于从悬崖上纵身一跳。

可他身上没有这样的勇气。他心里只剩下一个念头：把自己从刑事起诉的恐怖中救出来。只要他不亲手去碰那份文件，不流露一丝知晓其下落的神色，他们就无法证明他知道它的所在。但愿他们把它找出来，放他脱身！可他们没有找到，而他又不能替他们指点踪迹。至于这些恶毒的诽谤，格里菲斯先生曾问他，为何不诉诸法庭，亲身出面、以勇气把它们驳倒。这个主张的分量他掂得清清楚楚。为什么不显出自己经得起律师的机巧所能抛出的任何盘问？原因很简单：他经不起。在那一道道盘问之下，真相会从他身上被榨出来。哪怕他用最狠的决心把自己武装起来，他也藏不住那桩心知肚明的罪过。这一点他自己知道。他必定会说出于己不利的证词，而凭着那些证词，他将从证人席被拖进被告席。

他对自己说，任凭报纸怎么说，他也绝不肯自己走进那张为他张开的狮口。可就在这样下决心的时候，他并不知道外部还会有什么力量加到他身上。老佃户曾经声色俱厉地告诉他，他应当像个男子汉那样走进证人席，宣誓讲出自己的实情，那已经够他受的了。可还有更难对付的接踵而至——一种更难抗拒的力量。第二天一早，阿普约翰先生亲自驾车从卡马森赶到兰菲尔，立刻被领进了图书室。这位律师，在朋友和主顾们眼里，素来既是个和气可亲的人，又是个办事稳健的人。他席间应酬出色，操枪得力，骑在马上总是兴致盎然。他会钓鱼，人们也知道他好来一局惠斯特牌。他断然算不上一个苛刻或冷酷的人。可在亨利表哥眼里，他的目光中始终有一股严厉，眉间始终拧着一道皱痕，令他坐立不安。自从同这位律师打上交道起，他就怕他。他觉得自己被看穿、被细察，被掂量出了短处。阿普约翰先生自然是站在伊莎贝尔（Isabel）一边的。全卡马森郡（Carmarthenshire）都知道，他曾竭力劝老乡绅立伊莎贝尔为继承人。亨利表哥对此心知肚明。可即便如此，这位律师为什么总用责问的目光看他？当他在卡马森签署那份声明时，这位律师脸上的神色分明表明，他认定那份声明是假的。而如今这个人就坐在面前，除了忍受他的盘问，他别无出路。

===Original===
“Mr. Jones,” said the lawyer, “I have thought it my duty to call upon you in respect to these articles in the *Carmarthen Herald*.”

“I cannot help what the *Carmarthen Herald* may say.”

“But you can, Mr. Jones. That is just it. There are laws which enable a man to stop libels and to punish them if it be worth his while to do so.” He paused a moment, but Cousin Henry was silent, and he continued, “For many years I was your uncle’s lawyer, as was my father before me. I have never been commissioned by you to regard myself as your lawyer, but as circumstances are at present, I am obliged to occupy the place until you put your business into other hands. In such a position I feel it to be my duty to call upon you in reference to these articles. No doubt they are libellous.”

“They are very cruel; I know that,” said Cousin Henry, whining.

“All such accusations are cruel, if they be false.”

“These are false; damnably false.”
===Chinese===
“琼斯先生（Mr. Jones），”律师说，“《卡马森先驱报》（Carmarthen Herald）上登出了这些文章，我认为登门拜访你是我分内的职责。”

“《卡马森先驱报》爱说什么，我管不了。”

“可你管得了，琼斯先生。事情正在这里。法律允许一个人制止诽谤，并且在值得的时候惩治诽谤。”他顿了一顿，亨利表哥却不作声，他便接着说，“多年来我一直是你伯父的律师，先父在他之前也是如此。你从来没有委托过我做你的律师，但照眼下的情形，在你把事务交到别人手里之前，我不得不占据这个位置。处在这样的地位，我觉得就这些文章来见你是我的本分。它们无疑是诽谤。”

“这些文章很恶毒，这我知道，”亨利表哥带着哭腔说。

“这一类的指控，只要是假的，就都恶毒。”

“这些就是假的，假得万恶。”

===Original===
“I take that for granted; and therefore I have come to you to tell you that it is your duty to repudiate with all the strength of your own words the terrible charges which are brought against you.”

“Must I go and be a witness about myself?”

“Yes; it is exactly that. You must go and be a witness about yourself. Who else can tell the truth as to all the matters in question as well as yourself? You should understand, Mr. Jones, that you should not take this step with the view of punishing the newspaper.”

“Why, then?”

“In order that you may show yourself willing to place yourself there to be questioned. ‘Here I am,’ you would say. ‘If there be any point in which you wish me to be examined as to this property and this will, here I am to answer you.’ It is that you may show that you are not afraid of investigation.” But it was exactly this of which Cousin Henry was afraid. “You cannot but be aware of what is going on in Carmarthen.”
===Chinese===
“这个我姑且认定不疑；所以我才来找你，告诉你：你有责任用你自己的话，拿出全部力量，驳斥加在你身上的那些可怕指控。”

“非得我上庭去，给我自己当证人吗？”

“对；正是如此。你必须上庭去，给自己当证人。这桩桩件件的争议事实，除了你自己，还有谁能讲得像你一样真？琼斯先生，你应当明白，你走这一步，并不是为了惩罚那家报纸。”

“那是为了什么？”

“为的是让人看到，你自己愿意站到那里去接受质询。‘我在这里，’你可以这样说，‘凡你们希望我就这份产业和这份遗嘱受查问的任何一点，我都在这里，有问必答。’为的是表明你不怕调查。”然而亨利表哥怕的恰恰就是这个。“卡马森城里眼下闹着什么样的事，你不会不知道。”

===Original===
“I know about the newspaper.”

“It is my duty not to blink the matter. Everyone, not only in the town but throughout the country, is expressing an opinion that right has not been done.”

“What do they want? I cannot help it if my uncle did not make a will according to their liking.”

“They think that he did make a will according to their liking, and that there has been foul play.”

“Do they accuse me?”

“Practically they do. These articles in the paper are only an echo of the public voice. And that voice is becoming stronger and stronger every day because you take no steps to silence it. Have you seen yesterday’s paper?”
===Chinese===
“报纸的事我知道。”

“我的本分是不回避这件事。不止城里，全郡上下人人都在发表意见，说此事办得不公。”

“他们想要什么？我伯父立的遗嘱不合他们的心意，难道这也怪得了我？”

“他们认为，他恰恰立了一份合他们心意的遗嘱，而其中有人做了手脚。”

“他们是在指控我？”

“实际上就是。报上这些文章不过是公众声音的回声。而这声音一天比一天响，正因为你不采取任何行动让它沉默。昨天的报纸你看了没有？”

===Original===
“Yes; I saw it,” said Cousin Henry, gasping for breath.

Then Mr. Apjohn brought a copy of the newspaper out of his pocket, and began to read a list of questions which the editor was supposed to ask the public generally. Each question was an insult, and Cousin Henry, had he dared, would have bade the reader desist, and have turned him out of the room for his insolence in reading them.

“Has Mr. Henry Jones expressed an opinion of his own as to what became of the will which the Messrs. Cantor witnessed?”

“Has Mr. Henry Jones consulted any friend, legal or otherwise, as to his tenure of the Llanfeare estate?”

“Has Mr. Henry Jones any friend to whom he can speak in Carmarthenshire?”
===Chinese===
“看了，我看了，”亨利表哥气喘吁吁地说。

接着阿普约翰先生从口袋里掏出一份报纸，开始朗读一连串问题——照那位编者的意思，这些问题是提给全体公众的。每一个问题都是一记侮辱。亨利表哥但凡胆气够壮，早已喝令读报的人住口，并且为他念出这些问题的放肆无礼，把他轰出屋去。

“对于坎托（Cantor）两位先生见证的那份遗嘱落得了怎样的下落，亨利·琼斯先生可曾表示过他自己的看法？”

“关于他占有兰菲尔庄园一事，亨利·琼斯先生可曾同任何朋友商量过——律师也好，旁人也罢？”

“在卡马森郡，亨利·琼斯先生可还有一个能说得上话的朋友？”

===Original===
“Has Mr. Henry Jones inquired into the cause of his own isolation?”

“Has Mr. Henry Jones any idea why we persecute him in every fresh issue of our newspaper?”

“Has Mr. Henry Jones thought of what may possibly be the end of all this?”

“Has Mr. Henry Jones any thought of prosecuting us for libel?”

“Has Mr. Henry Jones heard of any other case in which an heir has been made so little welcome to his property?”

So the questions went on, an almost endless list, and the lawyer read them one after another, in a low, plain voice, slowly, but with clear accentuation, so that every point intended by the questioner might be understood. Such a martyrdom surely no man was ever doomed to bear before. In every line he was described as a thief. Yet he bore it; and when the lawyer came to an end of the abominable questions, he sat silent, trying to smile. What was he to say?
===Chinese===
“亨利·琼斯先生可曾追问过，他自己为什么遭人孤立？”

“我们的报纸每出一期都要追打他一回，亨利·琼斯先生可想没想过这是为什么？”

“这一切到头来可能是什么收场，亨利·琼斯先生想过没有？”

“以诽谤罪起诉我们——亨利·琼斯先生起过这样的念头没有？”

“一个继承人得到产业，却这样不受欢迎——除了他自己这一桩，亨利·琼斯先生可曾听说过别的例子？”

问题就这样一条接一条念下去，单子几乎开不到头。律师一条一条地读，声音低而平实，读得慢，吐字却清楚有力，务使提问人心里的每一层意思都听得分明。这样的酷刑，想必从前还不曾有人被判去受过。每一行字都在把他写成一个贼。然而他挺住了；等律师把那一串可憎的问题念完，他坐在那里默不作声，竭力想挤出一丝笑容。他能说什么呢？

===Original===
“Do you mean to put up with that?” asked Mr. Apjohn, with the curve of his eyebrow of which Cousin Henry was so much afraid.

“What am I to do?”

“Do! Do anything rather than sit in silence and bear such injurious insult as that. Were there nothing else to do, I would tear the man’s tongue from his mouth—or at least his pen from his grasp.”

“How am I to find him? I never did do anything of that rough kind.”

“It is not necessary. I only say what a man would do if there were nothing else to be done. But the step to be taken is easy. Instruct me to go before the magistrates at Carmarthen, and indict the paper for libel. That is what you must do.”
===Chinese===
“你打算就这么忍下去？”阿普约翰先生问，眉毛一挑——亨利表哥最怕的就是他这副挑眉的样子。

“我能怎么办？”

“办！干什么都行，就是不能一言不发地坐着，忍受这般伤人的侮辱。倘若再没有别的可做，我就要把那人的舌头从他嘴里拔出来——至少也得把他的笔从手里夺下来。”

“可我上哪儿找他去？那样粗野的事，我是从来不做、也做不来的。”

“不必。我只是说说，人在别无他法的时候会做出些什么来。而真正该走的一步很省事。委托我去见卡马森的治安法官（magistrates），告那份报纸一个诽谤。这就是你非做不可的事。”

===Original===
There was an imperiousness in the lawyer’s tone which was almost irresistible. Nevertheless Cousin Henry made a faint effort at resisting. “I should be dragged into a lawsuit.”

“A lawsuit! Of course you would. What lawsuit would not be preferable to that? You must do as I bid you, or you must consent to have it said and have it thought by all the country that you have been guilty of some felony, and have filched your cousin’s property.”

“I have committed nothing,” said the poor wretch, as the tears ran down his face.

“Then go and say so before the world,” said the attorney, dashing his fist down violently upon the table. “Go and say so, and let men hear you, instead of sitting here whining like a woman. Like a woman! What honest woman would ever bear such insult? If you do not, you will convince all the world, you will convince me and every neighbour you have, that you have done something to make away with that will. In that case we will not leave a stone unturned to discover the truth. The editor of that paper is laying himself open purposely to an action in order that he may force you to undergo the cross-questioning of a barrister, and everybody who hears of it says that he is right. You can prove that he is wrong only by accepting the challenge. If you refuse the challenge, as I put it to you now, you will acknowledge that—that you have done this deed of darkness!”
===Chinese===
律师的语气里有一股专断的味道，几乎让人无法抗拒。然而亨利表哥还是做出了微弱的抵抗。“那我就会被拖进一场官司里去。”

“官司！你当然要吃官司。可什么样的官司不比那个强？你要么照我吩咐的办，要么就认下——让全郡的人都说、都认为：你犯下了什么重罪，窃取了你表妹的产业。”

“我什么也没有干过，”这个可怜虫说着，眼泪顺着脸颊往下淌。

“那就出去，当着世人的面这样说，”律师说着一拳狠狠砸在桌上。“去这样说，让人听见你的声音，别坐在这里像婆娘一样哭哭啼啼。像婆娘！哪个正派女人受得了这样的侮辱？你若不去，就是向全世界、向我、向你的每个邻居证明：你干了什么事，把那份遗嘱弄没了。果真如此，我们必得翻个底朝天，把真相查出来。那份报纸的编辑是故意把自己送到诽谤诉讼的枪口上，好逼你受一位出庭律师（barrister）的交叉盘问；听说此事的人个个都说他做得对。你只有应下这场挑战，才能证明他错了。你若拒绝这场挑战——我此刻就把话摆在你面前——你就是承认了：承认你干下了这桩见不得天日的勾当！”

===Original===
Was there any torment ever so cruel, ever so unjustifiable as this! He was asked to put himself, by his own act, into the thumbscrew, on the rack, in order that the executioner might twist his limbs and tear out his vitals! He was to walk into a court of his own accord that he might be torn by the practised skill of a professional tormentor, that he might be forced to give up the very secrets of his soul in his impotence;—or else to live amidst the obloquy of all men. He asked himself whether he had deserved it, and in that moment of time he assured himself that he had not deserved such punishment as this. If not altogether innocent, if not white as snow, he had done nothing worthy of such cruel usage.

“Well,” said Mr. Apjohn, as though demanding a final answer to his proposition.

“I will think of it,” gasped Cousin Henry.

“There must be no more thinking. The time has gone by for thinking. If you will give me your instructions to commence proceedings against the *Carmarthen Herald*, I will act as your lawyer. If not, I shall make it known to the town that I have made this proposition to you; and I shall also make known the way in which it has been accepted. There has been more than delay enough.”

He sobbed, and gasped, and struggled with himself as the lawyer sat and looked at him. The one thing on which he had been intent was the avoiding of a court of law. And to this he was now to bring himself by his own act.
===Chinese===
世上曾有过比这更残忍、更毫无道理的折磨吗！他们竟要他自己动手，把手指伸进拶指的刑具，把身子绑上拉肢的刑架，好让行刑的人扭他的四肢、掏他的五脏！他要自己走进法庭，好让一名以折磨人为业的行家用熟练的手段把他撕开，让他在无力抗拒之中被迫交出灵魂深处的秘密；——否则，就得活在世人的一片骂声之中。他问自己是否罪有应得，而就在那一刻，他向自己确认：这样的惩罚，他不曾活该承受。他纵然算不得全然清白，纵然算不得洁白如雪，也不曾做过什么当受这般虐待的事。

“怎么样，”阿普约翰先生说，仿佛在逼他对这个提议交出最后的答复。

“让我想一想，”亨利表哥气结着说。

“不许再想了。想的时间已经过去了。你若给我委托，叫我对《卡马森先驱报》提起诉讼，我就做你的律师。若不给，我就会让全城的人知道，我曾向你提出过这个建议；我还会让人知道，这个建议是被怎样接受的。该拖的早就拖够了。”

律师坐在那里看着他，他抽泣，喘息，同自己苦苦挣扎。他一心一意要躲避的只有一件事：法庭。而如今，他却要亲手把自己送到那一步。

===Original===
“When would it have to be?” he asked.

“I should go before the magistrates tomorrow. Your presence would not be wanted then. No delay would be made by the other side. They would be ready enough to come to trial. The assizes begin here at Carmarthen on the 29th of next month. You might probably be examined on that day, which will be a Friday, or on the Saturday following. You will be called as a witness on your own side to prove the libel. But the questions asked by your own counsel would amount to nothing.”

“Nothing!” exclaimed Cousin Henry.

“You would be there for another purpose,” continued the lawyer. “When that nothing had been asked, you would be handed over to the other side, in order that the object of the proceedings might be attained.”

“What object?”

“How the barrister employed might put it I cannot say, but he would examine you as to any knowledge you may have as to that missing will.”
===Chinese===
“那得什么时候办？”他问。

“明天我就去见治安法官。那时用不着你到场。对方不会拖延，他们巴不得开庭。巡回法庭（assizes）下月29日就在卡马森开庭。你可能就在那天受盘问——那天是星期五——或者紧接着的星期六。你是作为己方证人被传唤，证明诽谤确有其事。可你自己的律师问出的那些问题，不会有什么分量。”

“不会有什么分量！”亨利表哥失声喊道。

“你在那里是为了另一个目的，”律师接着说。“等那些没有名堂的问题问完，你就会被交到对面手里，好让这场诉讼的真正目的达成。”

“什么目的？”

“受聘的出庭律师会怎么措辞，我说不准，但他会就那份失踪的遗嘱、就你可能知情的种种，对你加以盘问。”

===Original===
Mr. Apjohn, as he said this, paused for a full minute, looking his client full in the face. It was as though he himself were carrying on a cross-examination. “He would ask you whether you have such knowledge.” Then again he paused, but Cousin Henry said nothing. “If you have no such knowledge, if you have no sin in that matter on your conscience, nothing to make you grow pale before the eyes of a judge, nothing to make you fear the verdict of a jury, no fault heavy on your own soul—then you may answer him with frank courage, then you may look him in the face, and tell him with a clear voice that as far as you are aware your property is your own by as fair a title as any in the country.”

In every word of this there had been condemnation. It was as though Mr. Apjohn were devoting him to infernal torture, telling him that his only escape would be by the exercise of some herculean power which was notoriously beyond his reach. It was evident to him that Mr. Apjohn had come there under the guise of his advisor and friend, but was in fact leagued with all the others around him to drive him to his ruin. Of that he felt quite sure. The voice, the eyes, the face, every gesture of his unwelcome visitor had told him that it was so. And yet he could not rise in indignation and expel the visitor from his house. There was a cruelty, an inhumanity, in this which to his thinking was infinitely worse than any guilt of his own. “Well?” said Mr. Apjohn.

“I suppose it must be so.”

“I have your instructions, then?”
===Chinese===
说这话时，阿普约翰先生足足停了一分钟，直直地盯住他这位主顾的脸。那模样就像他本人正在主持一场交叉盘问。“他会问你，你是否知情。”接着他又停住，亨利表哥却一言不发。“你若并不知情，这件事在你良心上并无罪愆，没有什么会让你在法官眼前面无人色，没有什么让你惧怕陪审团的裁断，你自己的灵魂上也没有压着什么重大的过失——那你就可以怀着坦荡的勇气回答他，就可以正面直视着他，用清亮的声音告诉他：就你所知，你的产业归你所有，产权的正当不输于本郡任何一份产业。”

这一番话，字字都是定罪。仿佛阿普约翰先生正把他献给地狱的酷刑，告诉他，唯一的活路在于使出某种赫拉克勒斯式的神力，而那种神力明摆着他根本无从企及。在他看来再明白不过：阿普约翰先生此来，打着顾问和朋友的幌子，实际上却同他身边所有那些人结成一伙，要把他逼上毁灭之路。这一点他确信无疑。这位不受欢迎的访客的声音、眼睛、面孔、一举一动，都向他表明了这一点。然而他却不能满腔义愤地站起来，把这位客人赶出自己的家门。这里面有一种残忍、一种不近人情，照他想来，比他自己的任何罪过都要坏上无穷倍。“怎么样？”阿普约翰先生说。

“我看，也只好这样了。”

“那么，我算是得到你的委托了？”

===Original===
“Don’t you hear me say that I suppose it must be so.”

“Very well. The matter shall be brought in proper course before the magistrates tomorrow, and if, as I do not doubt, an injunction be granted, I will proceed with the matter at once. I will tell you whom we select as our counsel at the assizes, and, as soon as I have learnt, will let you know whom they employ. Let me only implore you not only to tell the truth as to what you know, but to tell all the truth. If you attempt to conceal anything, it will certainly be dragged out of you.”

Having thus comforted his client, Mr. Apjohn took his leave.
===Chinese===
“你没听见我说，我看也只好这样了吗。”

“很好。这件事明天自会依正当程序呈到治安法官面前；倘若如我毫不怀疑的那样颁下禁制令（injunction），我立刻着手进行。届时我们在巡回法庭上选定了律师，我会告诉你；他们聘用了什么人，我一打听到就让你知道。只求你一件事：不但要就你所知讲真话，而且要把真话讲尽。你若想隐瞒任何一点，它必定会从你身上被拖出来。”

就这样把他的主顾“安慰”了一番之后，阿普约翰先生起身告辞了。

## XXIV Conclusion / 第二十四章 结局

===Original===
Isabel spent one pleasant week with her lover at Hereford, and then was summoned into Carmarthenshire. Mr. Apjohn came over at her father’s invitation, and insisted on taking her back to Llanfeare.

“There are a thousand things to be done,” he said, “and the sooner you begin to do them the better. Of course you must live at the old house, and you had better take up your habitation there for a while before this other change is made.” The other change was of course the coming marriage, with the circumstances of which the lawyer had been made acquainted.

Then there arose other questions. Should her father go with her or should her lover? It was, however, at last decided that she should go alone as regarded her family, but under the care of Mr. Apjohn. It was she who had been known in the house, and she who had better now be seen there as her uncle’s representative.

===Chinese===
伊莎贝尔（Isabel）同她的恋人在赫里福德（Hereford）度过了愉快的一周，随后便被召往卡马森郡（Carmarthenshire）。阿普约翰先生（Mr. Apjohn）应她父亲之邀过来，执意要带她回兰菲尔（Llanfeare）去。

“要做的事有千百件，”他说，“你动手越早越好。你自然得住回老宅里去，最好趁另一桩变动还没办，先在那儿安顿下来。”那另一桩变动，自然便是即将举行的婚礼；其中的种种情形，这位律师早已听人告知。

跟着又生出别的问题：该她父亲陪她去，还是该她的恋人去？不过到头来议定，家里人一概不去，她只身前往，由阿普约翰先生一路照应。那宅子里素来认得的是她，如今也最好由她以伯父继承人的身份在那儿露面。

===Original===
“You will have to be called Miss Jones,” said the lawyer, “Miss Indefer Jones. There will be a form, for which we shall have to pay, I am afraid; but we had better take the name at once. You will have to undergo a variety of changes in signing your name. You will become first Miss Isabel Brodrick Indefer Jones, then Mrs. William Owen, then, when he shall have gone through the proper changes, Mrs. William Owen Indefer Jones. As such I hope you may remain till you shall be known as the oldest inhabitant of Carmarthenshire.”

Mr. Apjohn took her to Carmarthen, and hence on to Llanfeare. At the station there were many to meet her, so that her triumph, as she got into the carriage, was almost painful to her. When she heard the bells ring from the towers of the parish churches, she could hardly believe that the peals were intended to welcome her back to her old home. She was taken somewhat out of her way round by the creek and Coed, so that the little tinkling of her own parish church might not be lost upon her. If this return of hers to the estate was so important to others as to justify these signs, what must it be to her and how deep must be the convictions as to her own duties?

At the gate of Coed farmyard the carriage stopped, and the old farmer came out to say a few words to her.

===Chinese===
“往后你得改称琼斯小姐，”律师说，“因德弗·琼斯小姐（Miss Indefer Jones）。恐怕要办一道手续，只怕还得花上一笔费用；不过这姓氏我们最好立刻用起来。你签名时可要经过好一番更迭：先做伊莎贝尔·布罗德里克·因德弗·琼斯小姐（Miss Isabel Brodrick Indefer Jones），再做威廉·欧文太太（Mrs. William Owen），等他把该办的手续一一办妥，再做威廉·欧文·因德弗·琼斯太太。但愿你以此名号长此安处，直到人人认得你是卡马森郡最老的住户。”

阿普约翰先生送她到卡马森（Carmarthen），再从那里一路去兰菲尔。车站上有许多人来迎接她，以致她登上马车时，这番荣归反倒叫她几乎有些受不了。听见教区教堂的钟楼里钟声齐鸣，她简直难以相信，那一阵阵钟响竟是为了迎她回到老家。人家还特意领她绕了些路，打小河湾和科伊德（Coed）那边走，好叫她自己那个教区教堂的小钟叮当不致白响。她这次回到庄园，在旁人眼中竟要紧到这般地步、配受这般礼数，那么在她自己心里又该何等要紧，她对自身职责的信念又该何等深切？

马车在科伊德农家院门口停住，老农夫走出门来，同她说上几句话。

===Original===
“God bless you, Miss Isabel; this is a happy sight to see.”

“This is so kind of you, Mr. Griffith.”

“We’ve had a bad time of it, Miss Isabel;—not that we wished to quarrel with your dear uncle’s judgment, or that we had a right to say much against the poor gentleman who has gone;—but we expected you, and it went against the grain with us to have our expectations disappointed. We shall always look up to you, miss; but, at the same time, I wish you joy with all my heart of the new landlord you’re going to set over us. Of course that was to be expected, but you’ll be here with us all the time.” Isabel, while the tears ran down her cheeks, could only press the old man’s hand at parting.

“Now, my dear,” said Mr. Apjohn, as they went on to the house, “he has only said just what we’ve all been feeling. Of course it has been stronger with the tenants and servants than with others. But all round the country it has been the same. A man, if an estate belong to himself personally, can do what he likes with it, as he can with the half-crowns in his pocket; but where land is concerned, feelings grow up which should not be treated rudely. In one sense Llanfeare belonged to your uncle to do what he liked with it, but in another sense he shared it only with those around him; and when he was induced by a theory which he did not himself quite understand to bring your cousin Henry down among these people, he outraged their best convictions.”

===Chinese===
“上帝保佑您，伊莎贝尔小姐；这光景瞧着真叫人欢喜。”

“您真是太好心了，格里菲斯先生（Mr. Griffith）。”

“我们这阵子过的可不算舒坦，伊莎贝尔小姐——倒不是要同您那位亲爱的伯父的心意争个高低，也不是有资格对那位已经去了的可怜绅士多说三道四——只是我们一直盼着您来，盼了个空，这可真叫我们心里憋得慌。小姐，我们对您永远敬重；可与此同时，我打心底里恭喜您，又要给我们派一位新东家了。这本是意料中的事，可往后一直待在我们中间的还是您呀。”分别之时，伊莎贝尔泪珠儿顺着两颊滚落，只有紧紧握住老人的手。

“嗳，我的孩子，”往宅子去的路上，阿普约翰先生说道，“他方才那些话，不过是把我们大伙儿心头的感受说了出来。佃户和仆人自然比别人感受得更深些，可四乡八里，处处皆然。一个人，倘若那份产业纯粹归他个人所有，他尽可以随心所欲地去处置，如同处置他口袋里的几枚半克朗银币；可一旦涉及土地，便会生出种种情分来，不可粗暴相待。就一层意思说，兰菲尔归您伯父所有，他爱怎么处置便怎么处置；就另一层意思说，他不过是同左右相邻的人共有它罢了。他叫一套自己也不甚了了的理论说动了心，把您的亨利表哥（Cousin Henry）领到这些人中间来，实在是深深触犯了他们心中最珍视的信念。”

===Original===
“He meant to do his duty, Mr. Apjohn.”

“Certainly; but he mistook it. He did not understand the root of that idea of a male heir. The object has been to keep the old family, and the old adherences, and the old acres together. England owes much to the manner in which this has been done, and the custom as to a male heir has availed much in the doing of it. But in this case, in sticking to the custom, he would have lost the spirit, and, as far as he was concerned, would have gone against the practice which he wished to perpetuate. There, my dear, is a sermon for you, of which, I dare say, you do not understand a word.”

“I understand every syllable of it, Mr. Apjohn,” she answered.

===Chinese===
“他是想尽他的本分的，阿普约翰先生。”

“那自然；可他把本分认错了。男嗣这一套观念，他没有弄清它的根子。这一套的用意，原是要把旧家族、旧的情分、旧日的田产聚合在一处，不使离散。英格兰从中获益良多，而男嗣的成规在成全这件事上也出力甚多。可在这件事上，他若死抱成规，就会失去成规的精神，而且就他而论，反倒违背了他想使之绵延不绝的那种做法。得了，我的孩子，这一篇道算是给你讲完了；只怕你一个字也没听懂呢。”

“您讲的每一个音节我都懂，阿普约翰先生。”她答道。

===Original===
They soon arrived at the house, and there they found not only Mrs. Griffith and the old cook, who had never left the premises, but the old butler also, who had taken himself off in disgust at Cousin Henry’s character, but had now returned as though there had been no break in his continuous service. They received her with triumphant clamours of welcome. To them the coming of Cousin Henry, and the death of the old Squire, and then the departure of their young mistress, had been as though the whole world had come to an end for them. To serve was their only ambition—to serve and to be made comfortable while they were serving; but to serve Cousin Henry was to them altogether ignominious. The old Squire had done something which, though they acknowledged it to be no worse on his part than a mistake, had to them been cruelly severe. Suddenly to be told that they were servants to such a one as Cousin Henry—servants to such a man without any contract or agreement on their part;—to be handed over like the chairs and tables to a disreputable clerk from London, whom in their hearts they regarded as very much inferior to themselves! And they, too, like Mr. Griffith and the tenants, had been taught to look for the future reign of Queen Isabel as a thing of course. In that there would have been an implied contract—an understanding on their part that they had been consulted and had agreed to this destination of themselves. But Cousin Henry! Now this gross evil to themselves and to all around them had been remedied, and justice was done. They had all been strongly convinced that the Squire had made and had left behind him another will. The butler had been quite certain that this had been destroyed by Cousin Henry, and had sworn that he would not stand behind the chair of a felon. The gardener had been equally violent, and had declined even to cut a cabbage for Cousin Henry’s use. The women in the house had only suspected. They had felt sure that something was wrong, but had doubted between various theories. But now everything was right; now the proper owner had come; now the great troubles had been vanquished, and Llanfeare would once again be a fitting home for them.

“Oh, Miss Isabel! oh, Miss Isabel!” said Mrs. Griffith, absolutely sobbing at her young mistress’s feet up in her bedroom; “I did say that it could never go on like that. I did use to think that the Lord Almighty would never let it go on like that! It couldn’t be that Mr. Henry Jones was to remain always landlord of Llanfeare.”

When she came downstairs and took her seat, as she did by chance, in the old armchair which her uncle had been used to occupy, Mr. Apjohn preached to her another sermon, or rather sang a loud paean of irrepressible delight.

===Chinese===
不多时他们便到了宅前，在那里迎接她的，不止是从来不曾离开过宅子的格里菲斯太太（Mrs. Griffith）和老厨娘，还有那位上了年纪的男管家——他先前看不惯亨利表哥的为人，一怒而去，如今回来了，仿佛他连续伺候的年头从来没有间断过似的。他们欢声鼎沸地把她迎了进去。在他们看来，亨利表哥的到来、老乡绅的亡故、继而年轻主母的离去，直如天塌地陷，整个世界到了尽头。伺候人，是他们唯一的心愿——伺候人，而且在伺候的日子里过得舒舒服服；可伺候亨利表哥，在他们看来是十足十的耻辱。老乡绅做下的那件事，纵然他们承认在他一方不过是个错失，落到他们身上却严厉得近乎残忍。忽然间人家告诉他们：他们竟是亨利表哥这等人的仆人——伺候这样一个人，而他们自己从没立过什么契约文据；竟像桌椅板凳一般，被打发交割给一个从伦敦来的名声扫地的办事员，而他们心里向来把那人看得远不如自己！况且，他们也同格里菲斯先生和佃户们一样，早被引着把“伊莎贝尔女王”日后的临朝当作理所当然来期盼。这里面原有一层默约——他们自认，安排他们的归宿原是征询过他们、他们也同意了的。可偏偏来了个亨利表哥！如今，这桩加于他们和四邻众人的奇祸已经消弭，公道已经伸张。他们个个深信不疑：乡绅另立过一份遗嘱，而且留在了身后。老男管家原本一口咬定，那份遗嘱是亨利表哥毁掉的，还赌咒发誓，重罪犯人的座椅背后他绝不侍立。园丁同样激烈，连替亨利表哥切一棵白菜都不肯动手。宅子里的女人们只是疑心：她们确信其中必有蹊跷，只是在种种揣测之间拿不定主意。可如今万事归正；如今正当的主人回来了；如今大难已经扫平，兰菲尔将再度成为一个配得上他们安身的家。

“哦，伊莎贝尔小姐！哦，伊莎贝尔小姐！”格里菲斯太太在她卧房里，当真伏倒在年轻主母脚边呜呜咽咽地说，“我早说过，这样的事万万长不了。我素来相信，全能的主绝不容它长久下去！亨利·琼斯先生（Mr. Henry Jones）哪能一直做兰菲尔的主人呢。”

她下楼坐定，无意之间坐的正是伯父素来常坐的那把旧扶手椅，阿普约翰先生便又向她讲了一篇道，毋宁说，是放声唱出了一曲按捺不住的欢歌。

===Original===
“Now, my dear, I must go and leave you—happily in your own house. You can hardly realise how great a joy this has been to me—how great a joy it is.”

“I know well how much we owe you.”

“From the first moment in which he intimated to me his wish to make a change in his will, I became so unhappy about it as almost to lose my rest. I knew that I went beyond what I ought to have done in the things that I said to him, and he bore it kindly.”

“He was always kind.”

“But I couldn’t turn him. I told him what I told you today on the road, but it had no effect on him. Well, I had nothing to do but to obey his orders. This I did most grudgingly. It was a heartbreak to me, not only because of you, my dear, but for the sake of the property, and because I had heard something of your cousin. Then came the rumour of this last will. He must have set about it as soon as you had left the house.”

===Chinese===
“好了，我的孩子，我得走了，留下你自己守着——欢欢喜喜地守在你自己的家里。这件事给了我多大的欢喜，你几乎无从体会——此刻正给了我多大的欢喜。”

“我们欠您多少情，我知道得清清楚楚。”

“打从他头一回向我透露要在遗嘱上改弦更张的那一刻起，我就为这事苦恼得几乎夜不成眠。我知道，我对他说的话有些越出了我该守的本分，而他都和颜悦色地担待了。”

“他一向和颜悦色。”

“可我说不动他。今天我在路上对您讲的那番道理，我也对他讲过，却在他身上不起丝毫作用。罢了，除了照他的吩咐办事，我别无他法；而我遵得万般不情愿。这件事叫我心口发疼，不只是为了您，我的孩子，也是为了那份产业，还因为您那位表哥的情形，我早有耳闻。后来，便起了这最后一纸遗嘱的风声。您前脚离了宅子，他后脚必定就着手去办了。”

===Original===
“He never told me that he was going to do it.”

“He never told anyone; that is quite certain. But it shows how his mind must have been at work. Perhaps what I said may have had some effect at last. Then I heard from the Cantors what they had been asked to do. I need not tell you all that I felt then. It would have been better for him to send for me.”

“Oh, yes.”

“So much better for that poor young man’s sake.” The poor young man was of course Cousin Henry. “But I could not interfere. I could only hear what I did hear—and wait. Then the dear old man died!”

===Chinese===
“他要办这件事，从没同我提过。”

“他谁也没有告诉，这一点确凿无疑。可这也足见他的心思一直在转。也许我说的那些话，到头来终究起了些作用。随后我从坎托家（the Cantors）那里听说，人家请他们办的是什么差事。我那时心里的滋味，不必同您细表。他若是打发人来叫我，倒还好些。”

“可不是。”

“对那个可怜的年轻人来说，更要好上许多倍。”那可怜的年轻人，自然便是亨利表哥。“可我不能插手，只能听见到多少算多少——等着。后来，那位可爱的老人就过世了！”

===Original===
“I knew then that he had made it.”

“You knew that he had thought that he had done it; but how is one to be sure of the vacillating mind of an old dying man? When we searched for the one will and read the other, I was very sure that the Cantors had been called upon to witness his signature. Who could doubt as to that? But he who had so privately drawn out the deed might as privately destroy it. By degrees there grew upon me the conviction that he had not destroyed it; that it still existed—or that your cousin had destroyed it. The latter I never quite believed. He was not the man to do it—neither brave enough nor bad enough.”

“I think not bad enough.”

“Too small in his way altogether. And yet it was clear as the sun at noonday that he was troubled in his conscience. He shut himself up in his misery, not knowing how strong a tale his own unhappiness told against him. Why did he not rejoice in the glory of his position? Then I said to myself that he was conscious of insecurity.”

===Chinese===
“那时我就知道，他立过了。”

“您知道的，只是他自以为立过了；可一个弥留老人的心思游移不定，谁能保得准？我们搜寻前一份遗嘱、宣读后一份遗嘱的那阵子，我就十分断定，坎托家是被请来见证他签字的。这一点谁敢疑心？然而，那文书立得如何隐秘，毁起来也可以一样隐秘。渐渐地，我心里滋长出一种确信：他没有毁掉它；它还在——不然，就是叫您那位表哥毁了。后一层，我从来不大肯信。他不是那种人——既没有那么大的胆，也没有那么坏的心。”

“我想他也没有坏到那一步。”

“他这个人从头到脚都太卑微了。然而他良心不安，却明白得同正午的太阳一样。他把自己关在愁苦里，却不知自己那副郁郁寡欢的样子，正一桩一桩地构成本案对他的控诉。坐上了那样尊荣的位置，他为什么不欢欣鼓舞？于是我对自己说：他自知这位置坐不稳。”

===Original===
“His condition must have been pitiable.”

“Indeed, yes. I pitied him from the bottom of my heart. The contumely with which he was treated by all went to my heart even after I knew that he was misbehaving. I knew that he was misbehaving;—but how? It could only be by hiding the will, or by being conscious that it was hidden. Though he was a knave, he was not cunning. He failed utterly before the slightest cunning on the part of others. When I asked him whether he knew where it was hidden, he told a weak lie, but told the truth openly by the look of his eyes. He was like a little girl who pauses and blushes and confesses all the truth before she half murmurs her naughty fib. Who can be really angry with the child who lies after that unwilling fashion? I had to be severe upon him till all was made clear; but I pitied him from the bottom of my heart.”

“You have been good to all of us.”

===Chinese===
“他那种光景想必可怜得很。”

“岂不可怜。我从心底里怜悯他。人人加诸他的种种羞辱，扎着我的心，哪怕在我已经知道他行为不端之后，也还是如此。我知道他行为不端——可是怎样个不端法？无非是藏起了遗嘱，要么就是心里明白它藏在哪里。他虽是个无赖，却没有奸猾心计；别人略施一点狡狯，他就全然败露。我问他知不知道遗嘱藏在哪里，他撒了个软弱无力的谎，一双眼的眼神却把实话明明白白招了出来。他就像个小姑娘，先顿住脚，红着脸，一五一十全都认了，末了才半声半气地嘟哝出自己那句淘气的小谎。一个孩子这等不情愿地撒谎，谁还当真生得她的气？事情没有水落石出之前，我不得不对他声色俱厉；可我从心底里怜悯他。”

“您待我们人人都好。”

===Original===
“At last it became clear to me that your uncle had put it somewhere himself. Then came a chance remembrance of the sermons he used to read, and by degrees the hiding-place was suggested to me. When at last he welcomed us to go and search in his uncle’s bedroom, but forbade us to touch anything in the book-room—then I was convinced. I had but to look along the shelves till I found the set, and I almost knew that we had got the prize. Your father has told you how he flew at me when I attempted to lift my hand to the books. The agony of the last chance gave him a moment of courage. Then your father shook the document out from among the leaves.”

“That must have been a moment of triumph to you.”

“Yes;—it was. I did feel a little proud of my success. And I am proud as I see you sitting there, and feel that justice has been done.”

“By your means!”

===Chinese===
“到头来我终于明白，是您伯父自己把它搁在了什么地方。接着，偶然想起他素日常读的那些布道文，那藏处便一分一分地在我心里显了形。到最后，他乐意让我们进他伯父的卧房去搜寻，却严禁我们碰书房里的任何东西——这一来我就笃定了。我只消顺着书架看过去，找到那一套，便差不多知道宝物已经到手。您父亲已经给您讲过，我抬手要去够那些书时，他怎样朝我扑过来。最后一线机会的痛楚，给了他一瞬间的胆气。随后，您父亲便把那份文书从书页里抖落了出来。”

“那一刻想必是您扬眉吐气的一刻。”

“是啊——正是。我的成功确实叫我有些得意。如今看您坐在那里，知道公道已经伸张，我依旧得意。”

“这全亏了您！”

===Original===
“That justice has been done, and that everyone has his own again. I own to all the litigious pugnacity of a lawyer. I live by such fighting, and I like it. But a case in which I do not believe crushes me. To have an injustice to get the better of, and then to trample it well under foot—that is the triumph that I desire. It does not often happen to a lawyer to have had such a chance as this, and I fancy that it could not have come in the way of a man who would have enjoyed it more than I do.” Then at last, after lingering about the house, he bade her farewell. “God bless you, and make you happy here—you and your husband. If you will take my advice you will entail the property. You, no doubt, will have children, and will take care that in due course it shall go to the eldest boy. There can be no doubt as to the wisdom of that. But you see what terrible misery may be occasioned by not allowing those who are to come after you to know what it is they are to expect.”

For a few weeks Isabel remained alone at Llanfeare, during which all the tenants came to call upon her, as did many of the neighbouring gentry.

“I know’d it,” said young Cantor, clenching his fist almost in her face. “I was that sure of it I couldn’t hardly hold myself. To think of his leaving it in a book of sermons!”

Then, after the days were past during which it was thought well that she should remain at Llanfeare to give orders, and sign papers, and make herself by very contact with her own property its mistress and owner, her father came for her and took her back to Hereford. Then she had incumbent upon her the other duty of surrendering herself and all that she possessed to another. As any little interest which this tale may possess has come rather from the heroine’s material interests than from her love—as it has not been, so to say, a love story—the reader need not follow the happy pair absolutely to the altar. But it may be said, in anticipation of the future, that in due time an eldest son was born, that Llanfeare was entailed upon him and his son, and that he was so christened as to have his somewhat grandiloquent name inscribed as William Apjohn Owen Indefer Jones.

===Chinese===
“公道已经伸张，人人复得己有。律师这一行好讼逞强的脾性，我一身俱全，并不讳言。我就靠这样的争斗过活，而且喜欢它。可是一桩我自己都不信的官司，会把我压垮。惩治一桩不义，再把它踏踏实实踩在脚下——这才是我求的胜利。这样的机会落到一个律师头上的时候不多，而且我想，它也不会落到一个比我更能受用它的人手里。”终于，他在宅中盘桓够了，向她告别。“上帝保佑您，赐福你们在此安居——您和您的丈夫。您若肯听我的劝，就把这份产业限定继承下去。您无疑会有儿女，务要使它到时候传给长子。这样办的稳妥毋庸置疑。可您也亲眼见了，不让后继的人知道自己将指望什么，会酿成怎样可怕的大祸。”

伊莎贝尔又独自在兰菲尔住了几个星期，其间所有的佃户都来拜望她，四邻的乡绅也有许多登门。

“我早就料定了！”年轻坎托（Cantor）说着，把拳头攥紧，几乎伸到她脸跟前，“我那是笃定到了家，险些按捺不住自己。想想看，他竟把它搁在一本布道文里！”
