“I beg your pardon. I should not have said so. It was very wrong; but, then, why did you ask so foolish a question? Did I not tell you to let there be an end of it? And now will you let me give you one little bit of advice?”

===Chinese===
“天使不天使的，我说不好，”他说，还竭力装出和气的样子。

“不，不。那是我说的傻话。这里头没有天使的事。可是哪怕把整个兰菲尔给了我，哪怕是迁就他，我也决不肯嫁给一个我没有爱上的人——就算我有些爱他也不成。我必须完完全全地爱他，丝毫不掺杂兰菲尔的缘故。而你呢，我离爱上你还远得很。”

“为什么远呢，伊莎贝尔？”他傻头傻脑地问。

“因为——因为——因为你叫我讨厌！”

“伊莎贝尔！”

“请你原谅。我不该这样说。这是很不应该的；可是话说回来，谁叫你问这样傻的问题呢？我不是说过叫你把这话打住么？这会儿，容我给你一句小小的忠告，好不好？”

===Original===
“What is it?” he asked angrily. He was beginning to hate her, though he was anxious to repress his hatred, lest by indulging it he should injure his prospects.

“Do not say a word about me to my uncle. It will be better for you not to tell him that there has been between us any such interview as this. If he did once wish that you and I should become man and wife, I do not think that he wishes it now. Let the thing slide, as they say. He has quite made up his mind in your favour, because it is his duty. Unless you do something to displease him very greatly, he will make no further change. Do not trouble him more than you can help by talking to him on things that are distasteful. Anything in regard to me, coming from you, will be distasteful to him. You had better go about among the farms, and see the tenants, and learn the condition of everything. And then talk to him about that. Whatever you do, never suggest that the money coming from it all is less than it ought to be. That is my advice. And now, if you please, you and I need not talk about it any more.” Then she got up and left the room without waiting for a reply.

When he was alone he resolved upon complying with her advice, at any rate in one respect. He would not renew his offer of marriage; nor would he hold any further special conversation with her. Of course, she was hateful to him, having declared so plainly to him her own opinion regarding himself. He had made the offer, and had thereby done his duty. He had made the offer, and had escaped.

===Chinese===
“什么忠告？”他怒冲冲地问。他开始恨她了，只是极力把恨压住，怕由着性子恨下去，坏了自己的前程。

“关于我的事，对我伯父一个字也不要提。你最好不要告诉他，我们之间有过这样一次谈话。就算他从前一度愿你我做夫妻，我想他如今也不愿意了。人家不是常说么——让这事就这么滑过去。他已拿定主意，偏在你这一边，因为那是他的责任。只要你不做出什么大大惹他不快的事来，他就不会再改主意。凡是避得开的，就不要拿他不乐意听的话去搅扰他。凡是从你嘴里说出来的、关于我的话，都会叫他厌恶。你顶好到各处田庄上走走，看看佃户，把各样事情的底细都摸一摸，然后拿这些去同他谈。无论怎样，千万不要暗示说，这份产业出的进项比应当有的少。这就是我的忠告。现在，对不住，这件事你我之间不必再谈了。”说完她便起身离屋，不等他回答。

撇下他独自一人的时候，他拿定主意照她的忠告办，至少在某一方面如此。他不再重提求婚的话；也不再同她作任何特别的交谈。她当然可恨——她已把自己对他的看法说得那样明明白白。他求过婚了，从此就算尽到了责任。他求过婚了，也就脱了身。

===Original===
But he did not at all believe in the sincerity of her advice as to their uncle. His heart was throbbing with the desire to secure the inheritance to himself﻿—and so he thought, no doubt, was hers as to herself. It might be that the old man’s intention would depend upon his obedience, and if so, it was certainly necessary that the old man should know that he had been obedient. Of course, he would tell the old man what he had done.

But he said not a word till Isabel had gone. He did take her advice about the land and the tenants, but hardly to much effect. If there were a falling roof here or a half-hung door there, he displayed his zeal by telling the Squire of these defaults. But the Squire hated to hear of such defaults. It must be acknowledged that it would have required a man of very great parts to have given satisfaction in the position in which this young man was placed.

But as soon as Isabel was gone he declared his obedience.

===Chinese===
但是，她在伯父这桩事上出的主意，他半点也不信出自诚意。他心口怦怦直跳，一心要把继承权抓到自己手里——他想，她无疑也是这样为她自己打算的。老人的意向或许就要看他听话不听话；果真如此，那就断然少不得叫老人知道，他是听话的。他做过的事，自然要原原本本告诉老人。

可是在伊莎贝尔动身以前，他一个字也没有提。田庄和佃户这层，他倒真听了她的劝，只是收效甚微。这里屋顶眼见要塌，那里一扇门挂得半吊不吊，他便跑去把这些疏失报给乡绅，以示自己的热忱。可乡绅最怕听的便是这些疏失。应当承认，处在这年轻人被摆下的位置上，要想叫人满意，非得是个才干非凡的人物不可。

但伊莎贝尔前脚一走，他后脚就去申明自己如何遵命了。

===Original===
“I have asked her, sir, and she has refused me,” he said in a melancholy, low, and sententious voice.

“What did you expect?”

“At any rate, I did as you would have me.”

“Was she to jump down your throat when you asked her?”

“She was very decided﻿—very. Of course, I spoke of your wishes.”

“I have not any wishes.”

===Chinese===
“我求过她了，先生，她回绝了我。”他说道，声音又低又伤感，一字一顿。

“你原本指望什么？”

“反正，您要我做的，我做了。”

“难道你一开口，她就该张嘴把你吞下去不成？”

“她口气硬得很——硬得很。我自然提了您的心愿。”

“我没有什么心愿。”

===Original===
“I thought that you desired it.”

“So I did, but I have changed my mind. It would not do at all. I almost wonder how you could have had the courage to ask her. I don’t suppose that you have the insight to see that she is different from other girls.”

“Oh, yes; I perceived that.”

“And yet you would go and ask her to be your wife offhand, just as though you were going to buy a horse! I suppose you told her that it would be a good thing because of the estate?”

“I did mention it,” said the young man, altogether astounded and put beyond himself by his uncle’s manner and words.

===Chinese===
“我原以为这是您巴望的。”

“我原是巴望的，可我已经变了主意。这事断断使不得。我倒纳闷，你哪来的胆子向她开口。我看你未必有那份眼力，看得出她跟别的姑娘不一样。”

“哦，是啊；这个我瞧出来了。”

“可你就这样冒冒失失上去，求她做你的妻子，倒像是要去买一匹马！我想，你总对她说过，为着这份产业，这是一桩好事吧？”

“我是提到过一句，”年轻人说。伯父的架势和言词叫他大惊失色，慌得没了方寸。

===Original===
“Yes; just as if it were a bargain! If you will consent to put up with me as a husband, why, then you can go shares with me in the property. That was the kind of thing, wasn’t it? And then you come and tell me that you have done your duty by making the offer!”

The heir expectant was then convinced that it would have been better for him to have followed the advice which Isabel had given him, but yet he could not bring himself to believe that the advice had been disinterested. Why should Isabel have given him disinterested advice in opposition to her own prospects? Must not Isabel’s feeling about the property be the same as his own?

===Chinese===
“是啊；活脱脱一桩买卖！你要是肯委屈些，将就我这么个丈夫，喏，这份产业你就可以来同我合股分红利。就是这一路的话，是不是？然后你就跑来对我说，你把婚一求，责任就尽到了！”

到这时，这位候任继承人才深信，当初若听了伊莎贝尔的劝告，事情本会好些；可他仍旧没法叫自己相信，那份劝告是出于无私。伊莎贝尔凭什么要背着自己的前程，给他出无私的主意？她对这份产业的感受，难道会同他不一个样么？

## XX Doubts / 第二十章 疑虑

===Original===
"I know nothing about it," Cousin Henry had gasped out when asked by Mr. Apjohn, when Ricketts, the clerk, had left the room, whether he knew where the will was hidden. Then, when he had declared he had nothing further to say, he was allowed to go away.

As he was carried back in the fly he felt certain that Mr. Apjohn knew that there had been a will, knew that the will was still in existence, knew that it had been hidden by some accident, and knew also that he, Henry Jones, was aware of the place of concealment. That the man should have been so expert in reading the secret of his bosom was terrible to him. Had the man suspected him of destroying the will—a deed the doing of which might have been so naturally suspected—that would have been less terrible. He had done nothing, had committed no crime, was simply conscious of the existence of a paper which it was a duty, not of him, but of others to find, and this man, by his fearful ingenuity, had discovered it all! Now it was simply necessary that the place should be indicated, and in order that he himself might be forced to indicate it, Mr. Cheekey was to be let loose upon him!

How impossible—how almost impossible had he found it to produce a word in answer to that one little question from Mr. Apjohn! "Nor know where it is hidden?" He had so answered it as to make it manifest that he did know. He was conscious that he had been thus weak, though there had been nothing in Mr. Apjohn's manner to appal him. How would it be with him when, hour after hour, question after question should be demanded of him, when that cruel tormentor should stand there glaring at him in presence of all the court? There would be no need of such hour—no need of that prolonged questioning. All that was wanted of him would be revealed at once. The whole secret would be screwed out of him by the first turn of the tormentor's engine.

===Chinese===
“这事我一概不知。”办事员里基茨（Ricketts）退出房间之后，阿普约翰先生（Mr. Apjohn）问他知不知道遗嘱藏在何处，亨利表哥（Cousin Henry）便是这样喘着气迸出来的一句。随后，他声明再没有别的话要说，人家便放他走了。

乘着出租马车被送回来的路上，他心里断然无疑：阿普约翰先生知道曾经有过一份遗嘱，知道那份遗嘱依然还在，知道它是出于某种意外被藏起来的，也知道他亨利·琼斯本人清楚藏匿的地点。这个人竟能如此精熟地读出他胸中的秘密，在他想来真是可怕。那人若是疑心他毁掉了遗嘱——这桩事本是最自然要叫人起疑的——倒还不至于这样可怕。他什么也没做，没有犯下任何罪行，不过是知道世上有那么一张纸，而把它寻出来是他人的职责，不是他的职责，这个人却凭他可怕的机巧，把这一切全看破了！如今只消把藏处指出来就完事，而为了逼他亲手指出来，便要放出奇基先生（Mr. Cheekey）来对付他！

要他回答阿普约翰先生那一个小小的问题、吐出一个字来，他当时觉得多么不可能——几乎是全然不可能！“也不知道它藏在哪儿？”他那一答，恰恰答得清清楚楚，叫人看出他其实是知道的。他意识到自己就是这样软弱，虽说阿普约翰先生的举止里并没有什么叫他胆寒的东西。往后又待如何？一个钟头接着一个钟头、一个问题接着一个问题地逼问他，那个残忍的折磨人者站在那里，当着满法庭的人怒目瞪着他，那时他又怎么受得住？其实用不着那些钟头——用不着那种拖长的盘问。人家要从他身上掏的东西，一下子就会全露出来。刑吏的机括头一转，整个秘密就会被从他骨头缝里拧出来。

===Original===
There was but one thing quite fixed in his mind. Nothing should induce him to face Mr. Cheekey, unless he should have made himself comparatively safe by destroying the will. In that way he almost thought he might be safe. The suffering would be great. The rack and the thumbscrew, the boots and the wheel, would, to the delight of all those present, be allowed to do their work upon him for hours. It would be a day to him terrible to anticipate, terrible to endure, terrible afterwards in his memory; but he thought that not even Mr. Cheekey himself would be able to extract from him the admission of such a deed as that.

And then by the deed he would undoubtedly acquire Llanfeare. The place itself was not dear to him, but there was rising in his heart so strong a feeling of hatred against those who were oppressing him that it seemed to him almost a duty to punish them by continued possession of the property. In this way he could triumph over them all. If once he could come down from Mr. Cheekey's grasp alive, if he could survive those fearful hours, he would walk forth from the court the undoubted owner of Llanfeare. It would be as though a man should endure some excruciating operation under the hands of a surgeon, with the assured hope that he might enjoy perfect health afterwards for the remainder of his life.

To destroy the will was his only chance of escape. There was nothing else left to him, knowing, as he did, that it was impossible for him to put an end to his own life with his own hands. These little plots of his, which he had planned for the revelation of his secret without the acknowledgment of guilt, had all fallen to pieces as he attempted to execute them. He began to be aware of himself that anything that required skill in the execution was impossible to him. But to burn the will he was capable. He could surely take the paper from its hiding-place and hold it down with the poker when he had thrust it between the bars. Or, as there was no fire provided in these summer months, he could consume it by the light of his candle when the dead hours of the night had come upon him. He had already resolved that, when he had done so, he would swallow the telltale ashes. He believed of himself that all that would be within his power, if only he could determine upon the doing of it.

===Chinese===
他心里只有一件事是笃定的。除非先把遗嘱毁掉、给自己挣得一层相对的安稳，无论什么都休想叫他站到奇基先生面前去。这么一来，他几乎觉得自己就安全了。苦头自然小不了。拉肢架和拇指夹、刑靴和车轮刑，都会被容让着在他身上作工几个钟头，好叫在场的人个个称心快意。那一天对他来说，预想起来可怕，忍受起来可怕，事后追想起来也可怕；可他想，纵然是奇基先生本人，也休想从他嘴里榨出招认这种勾当的一个字来。

而且，这桩事一做，他必定就能得到兰菲尔（Llanfeare）。那地方本身他并不觉得亲，可是一股仇恨正从心底往上升——恨那些欺压他的人，恨得如此强烈——以至于在他看来，继续占有这份产业、借此惩罚他们，几乎成了一项道义。这样他便能把他们统统压倒。只要他能从奇基先生的掌心里囫囵活着下来，只要他能熬过那些可怕的钟头，他从法庭里走出来时，就是确凿无疑的兰菲尔主人了。那情形就像一个人忍着外科医生手下某一场痛楚彻骨的手术，却怀着确凿的指望：此后残年，尽可安享完全的康健。

毁掉遗嘱是他脱身的唯一指望。此外他已无路可走——他深知，要他亲手结束自己的性命是断断做不到的。他那些小诡计，原想既叫秘密泄露、又不认下罪责，可一到着手施行，便全数散了架。他开始明白过来：凡是动手时要有几分技巧的事，他一概办不成。可是烧掉遗嘱，他做得到。他自然可以把那张纸从藏处拿出来，塞进炉栅中间，再用拨火棍压住。或者，这几个月天热不生火，他尽可以等到夜半死寂的时辰，就着烛光把纸烧掉。他已经拿定主意，烧完之后，要把那些会招出形迹的灰烬吞下肚去。他相信自己这一切都做得到，只消他狠得下心去干。

===Original===
And he thought that the deed when done would give him a new courage. The very danger to which he would have exposed himself would make him brave to avoid it. Having destroyed the will, and certain that no eye had seen him, conscious that his safety depended on his own reticence, he was sure that he would keep his secret even before Mr. Cheekey.

"I know nothing of the will," he would say; "I have neither seen it, nor hidden it, nor found it, nor destroyed it."

Knowing what would be the consequences were he to depart from the assertion, he would assuredly cling to it. He would be safer then, much safer than in his present vacillating, half-innocent position.

===Chinese===
他还想，这桩事一旦做成，会给他添一种新的胆气。恰恰是他把自己暴露于其中的那份危险，会叫他勇敢起来去躲开这危险。遗嘱毁掉之后，确知没有一只眼睛看见过他，明白自己的安危全凭自己守口如瓶，他相信，即便站在奇基先生面前，他也能咬住这个秘密不放。

“关于遗嘱我一无所知，”他会这样说，“我没有见过它，没有藏过它，没有找到过它，也没有毁掉过它。”

他知道一旦背离这套说辞会有怎样的后果，因此必定死死抱住它不放。到那时他就安稳了，比眼下这种摇摆不定、半清白半无辜的处境安稳得多。

===Original===
As he was carried home in the fly, his mind was so intent upon this, he was so anxious to resolve to bring himself to do the deed, that he hardly knew where he was when the fly stopped at his hall door. As he entered his house, he stared about him as though doubtful of his whereabouts, and then, without speaking a word, made his way into the book-room, and seated himself on his accustomed chair. The woman came to him and asked him whether money should not be given to the driver.

"What driver?" said he. "Let him go to Mr. Apjohn. It is Mr. Apjohn's business, not mine." Then he got up and shut the door violently as the woman retreated.

Yes; it was Mr. Apjohn's business; and he thought that he could put a spoke into the wheel of Mr. Apjohn's business. Mr. Apjohn was not only anxious to criminate him now, but had been anxious when such anxiety on his part had been intrusive and impertinent. Mr. Apjohn had, from first to last, been his enemy, and by his enmity had created that fatal dislike which his uncle had felt for him. Mr. Apjohn was now determined to ruin him. Mr. Apjohn had come out to him at Llanfeare, pretending to be his lawyer, his friend, his advisor, and had recommended this treacherous indictment merely that he might be able to subject him to the torments of Mr. Cheekey's persecution. Cousin Henry could see it all now! So, at least, Cousin Henry told himself.

===Chinese===
出租马车载着他回家的路上，他满心都盘旋着这件事，一心巴望拿定主意、逼自己动手去做，以致马车停在他门厅前时，他几乎不知道自己身在何处。进得宅来，他直愣愣地四下张望，仿佛拿不准到了什么地方，随后一言不发，径自走进书房，在惯坐的椅子上坐了下来。女仆走过来问他，是不是该给车夫车钱。

“什么车夫？”他说，“让他找阿普约翰先生去算。这是阿普约翰先生的事，不是我的事。”女仆退出去时，他起身砰地一声把门摔上了。

是啊，这确乎是阿普约翰先生的事；而他想，他有法子往阿普约翰先生这桩事的车轮里插上一根辐条，叫它转不动。阿普约翰先生如今一心要给他定罪，早先也一心如此，那时节这份热心本已属横加干涉、有失分寸。阿普约翰先生自始至终是他的仇敌，正是凭着这份仇意，才酿成了伯父对他那种致命的嫌恶。阿普约翰先生如今存心毁掉他。阿普约翰先生跑到兰菲尔来见他，假充他的律师、他的朋友、他的顾问，力荐这桩暗藏奸计的控案，无非是想把他交到奇基先生的迫害之下备受折磨。如今亨利表哥把这一切全看穿了！至少，亨利表哥对自己是这样说的。

===Original===
"He is a clever fellow, and he thinks that I am a fool. Perhaps he is right, but he will find that the fool has been too many for him."

It was thus that he communed with himself.

He had his dinner and sat by himself during the whole evening, as had been his practice every day since his uncle's death. But yet this peculiar night seemed to him to be eventful. He felt himself to be lifted into some unwonted eagerness of life, something approaching to activity. There was a deed to be done, and though he was not as yet doing it, though he did not think that he intended to do it that very night, yet the fact that he had made up his mind made him in some sort aware that the dumb spirit which would not speak had been exorcised, and that the crushing dullness of the latter days had passed away from him. No; he could not do it that night; but he was sure that he would do it. He had looked about for a way of escape, and had been as though a dead man while he could not find it. He had lived in terror of Mrs. Griffith the housekeeper, of Farmer Griffith, of the two Cantors, of Mr. Apjohn, of that tyrant Cheekey, of his own shadow—while he and that will were existing together in the same room. But it should be so no longer. There was one way of escape, and he would take it!

===Chinese===
“他是个精明人，他当我是傻瓜。也许他没看错，可他会发现，傻瓜这回要胜他一着，占了他的上风。”

他就这样在心里自问自答。

他吃过晚饭，整个晚上独自枯坐，自伯父故去以来，天天都是这般光景。可是这一夜在他看来却非同小可。他觉得自己被托举起来，进入一种平生未有的生活热望之中，几乎要振作有为起来了。有一桩事要办，虽然他还没有动手，虽然他并不认为自己打算就在当晚去办，可他拿定主意这件事本身，已经多少让他觉出：那个不肯开口的哑灵已经被驱走了，近来那压得人喘不过气的沉闷已经从他身上褪去。不，今晚他还做不成；但他确信自己终究会做。他四下里寻过逃生之路，寻不着的时候，简直形同行尸走肉。女管家格里菲斯太太（Mrs. Griffith）、格里菲斯先生（Farmer Griffith）、坎托两位先生（the Cantors）、阿普约翰先生、那个暴君奇基，连同他自己的影子——只要他和那份遗嘱还同处一室，他就活在这一重重的恐惧里。但从今往后不该再这样。逃生之路有一条，他就要走这一条！

===Original===
Then he went on thinking of what good things might be in store for him. His spirit had hitherto been so quenched by the vicinity of the will that he had never dared to soar into thoughts of the enjoyment of money. There had been so black a pall over everything that he had not as yet realised what it was that Llanfeare might do for him. Of course he could not live there. Though he should have to leave the house untenanted altogether, it would matter but little. There was no law to make a man live on his own estate. He calculated that he would be able to draw £1,500 a year from the property;—£1,500 a year! That would be clearly his own; on which no one could lay a finger; and what enjoyment could he not buy with £1,500 a year?

With a great resolve to destroy the will he went to bed, and slept through the night as best he could. In the dark of his chamber, when the candle was out, and he was not yet protected by his bed, there came a qualm upon him. But the deed was not yet done, and the qualm was kept under, and he slept. He even repeated the Lord's Prayer to himself when he was under the clothes, struggling, however, as he did so, not to bring home to himself that petition as to the leading into temptation and the deliverance from evil.

The next day, the Friday, and the Saturday were passed in the same way. The resolution was still there, but the qualms came every night. And the salve to the qualm was always the same remembrance that the deed had not been done yet. And the prayer was always said, morning and night, with the same persistent rejection of those words which, in his present condition, were so damning to him—rejection from the intelligence though with the whispering voice the words were spoken. But still there was the resolve the same as ever. There was no other way of escape. A stag, when brought to bay, will trample upon the hounds. He would trample upon them. Llanfeare should all be his own. He would not return to his clerk's desk to be the scorn of all men—to have it known that he had fraudulently kept the will hidden, and then revealed it, not of grace, but because he was afraid of Mr. Cheekey. His mind was quite made up. But the deed need not be yet done. The fewer nights that he would have to pass in that house, after the doing of the deed, the better.

===Chinese===
接着他继续盘算，往后有什么福分在等着他。此前他的精神一直被近在咫尺的那份遗嘱浇得死灭，从来不敢纵身高飞、去想金钱的享受。一片黑纱罩住了万事万物，他至今还不曾体会，兰菲尔究竟能给他带来什么。他当然不住在那里。就算那宅子干脆空着没人住，也无关紧要。没有哪条法律逼着人住在自己的田庄上。他算过，这份产业一年能给他抽出一千五百镑；——一年一千五百镑！那可是明明白白归他自己所有，谁也伸不进一根指头；一年一千五百镑，还有什么享乐买不到手？

他怀着毁掉遗嘱的宏大决心上了床，尽其所能睡过了一夜。卧室的黑暗里，蜡烛熄了，身子还没有得到被褥的掩护，一阵疑惧便涌上了他的心头。但事情还没有做，那阵疑惧被按了下去，他也就睡着了。钻进被窝之后，他甚至在心里把主祷文念了一遍，只是念的时候苦苦挣扎着，不叫“不叫我们遇见试探，救我们脱离凶恶”那一句祈求落到自己心上来。

第二天、那个星期五，还有星期六，都是照老样子过去的。决心还在，疑惧却夜夜都来。给疑惧止痛的膏药，永远是同一样自我宽慰：事情还没有做。祷告也照念不误，早晚各一遍，照旧执拗地拒斥着那几句话——照他如今的处境，那几句话句句都在给他定罪——心智上拒而不纳，话却仍由那低语的声音念出。但决心还是同从前一样坚定。逃路再没有第二条。雄鹿被逼到绝境，也会用蹄子践踏猎犬。他也要践踏他们。兰菲尔应当全然归他所有。他决不回到办事员的写字台前，去受万人的讥笑——叫人知道他曾把遗嘱昧着藏了起来，后来又不得不交出去，并非出于仁义，而是因为怕奇基先生。他的主意已经拿定。但事情还不必马上做。那件事做过之后，他在这宅子里熬的夜，越是少越好。

===Original===
The trial was to be on the Friday. He would not postpone the deed till the last day, as it might be then that emissaries might come to him, watching him to see that he did not escape. And yet it would be well for him to keep his hands clean from the doing of it up to the last moment. He was quite resolved. There was no other escape. And yet—yet—yet, who would say what might not happen? Till the deed should have been done, there would yet be a path open to the sweet easiness of innocence. When it should have been done, there would be a final adieu to innocence. There would be no return to the white way, no possibility of repentance! How could a man repent while he was still holding the guilty prize which he had won? Or how could he give up the prize without delivering himself as a criminal to the law? But, nevertheless, he was resolved, and he determined that the deed should be done on the Tuesday night.

During the whole Tuesday he was thinking of it. Could he bring himself to believe that all that story of a soul tormented for its wickedness in everlasting fire was but an old woman's tale? If he could but bring himself to believe that! If he could do that, then could he master his qualms. And why not? Religious thoughts had hitherto but little troubled his life. The Church and her services had been nothing to him. He had lived neither with the fear nor with the love of God at his heart. He knew that, and was but little disposed to think that a line of conduct which had never been hitherto adopted by him would be embraced in his later life. He could not think of himself as being even desirous to be religious. Why, then, should qualms afflict him?

That prayer which he was accustomed to repeat to himself as he went to rest was but a trick of his youth. It had come down to him from old, innocent days; and though it was seldom omitted, without a shiver, nevertheless it was repeated with contempt. In broad daylight, or when boon companions had been with him round the candles, blasphemy had never frightened him. But now—now in his troubles, he remembered that there was a hell. He could not shake from himself the idea. For unrepented sin there was an eternity of torment which would last forever! Such sin as this which he premeditated must remain unrepented, and there would be torment for him forever. Nevertheless, he must do it. And, after all, did not many of the wise ones of the earth justify him in thinking that that threat was but an old woman's tale?

===Chinese===
庭审定在星期五。他不愿把那件事拖到末一日，因为到了那时候，说不定会派出密探来盯着他，看他逃不逃得脱。可是话说回来，直到最后一刻都把双手保持干净、不动那件事，于他也是好的。他主意已定。逃路再没有别的。可是——可是——可是，谁说得准不会出什么变故？那件事做成之前，通往无辜的甜美安稳的路总还开着。一旦做成，便是同无辜永诀。再也回不到那条洁白的路上，再没有悔改的可能！一个人手里还攥着赢来的罪恶奖赏，叫他怎么悔改？或者，不把自己当作罪犯交到法律手里，他又怎么舍得放下那奖赏？尽管如此，他还是拿定了主意，决意那件事要在星期二夜里做。

整整一个星期二，他都在想这件事。他能不能说服自己相信：灵魂因作恶而在永火里受煎熬的那一整套说法，不过是老太婆讲的故事？他若能让自己信了这一条就好了！若能做到这一步，他就能制住心头的疑惧。这又有什么不能？宗教的念头此前几乎不曾搅扰过他的生活。教会和她的礼拜，于他素来毫无干系。他活着，心里既没有对上帝的畏惧，也没有对上帝的爱。这一点他自己心里有数，也压根不大相信：一条他至今从未奉行过的处世之道，到了晚年倒会被他领受起来。他没法设想自己竟会渴望虔敬起来。那么，疑惧又凭什么来折磨他？

他惯于就寝时对自己念的那段祷文，不过是年轻时落下的一种习惯。那是从往昔清白无邪的日子传下来的；虽说念的时候几乎回回少不了一阵寒噤，终究念时带着轻蔑。光天化日之下，或是酒肉朋友围着烛光聚饮的时候，渎神的话从来不曾吓着过他。可是如今——如今身陷烦恼之中，他记起了世上是有地狱的。这个念头甩它不脱。未及悔改的罪，有永世不尽的煎熬，直到永远！像他蓄谋要犯的这样的罪，必定终生无从悔改，那煎熬也就要伴他到永远。尽管如此，他还是得做。况且说到底，世上许多智者不是也在替他作保，说那威吓不过是老太婆的故事吗？

===Original===
Tuesday night came—the late hours of Tuesday night—the midnight hour at which he was sure that the women were in bed, and the will was taken out from its hiding-place. He had already trimmed the wick and placed the candle on an outspread newspaper, so that no fragment of the ash should fall where it might not be collected. He had walked round the room to make himself sure that no aperture might possibly be open. He put out the candle so as to see that no gleam of light from any source was making its way into the room, and then relighted it. The moment had come for the destruction of the document.

He read it all through yet again;—why he knew not, but in truth craving some excuse for further delay. With what care the dying old man had written every word and completed every letter! He sat there contemplating the old man's work, telling himself that it was for him to destroy it utterly by just a motion of his wrist. He turned round and trimmed the candle again, and still sat there with the paper in his hand. Could it be that so great a result could come from so short an act? The damning of his own soul! Would it in truth be the giving up of his own soul to eternal punishment? God would know that he had not meant to steal the property! God would know that he did not wish to steal it now! God would know that he was doing this as the only means of escape from misery which others were plotting for him! God would know how cruelly he had been used! God would know the injustice with which the old man had treated him! Then came moments in which he almost taught himself to believe that in destroying the will he would be doing no more than an act of rough justice, and that God would certainly condemn no one to eternal punishment for a just act. But still, whenever he would turn round to the candle, his hand would refuse to raise the paper to the flame. When done, it could not be undone! And whether those eternal flames should or should not get possession of him, there would be before him a life agonised by the dread of them. What could Mr. Cheekey do worse for him than that?

The Wednesday would at any rate do as well. Why rob himself of the comfort of one day during which his soul would not be irretrievably condemned? Now he might sleep. For this night, at any rate, he might sleep. He doubted whether he would ever sleep again after the doing of the deed. To be commonly wicked was nothing to him—nothing to break through all those ordinary rules of life which parents teach their children and pastors their flocks, but as to which the world is so careless. To covet other men's goods, to speak evil of his neighbours, to run after his neighbour's wife if she came in his path, to steal a little in the ordinary way—such as selling a lame horse or looking over an adversary's hand at whist, to swear to a lie, or to ridicule the memory of his parents—these peccadillos had never oppressed his soul. That not telling of the will had been burdensome to him only because of the danger of discovery. But to burn a will, and thereby clearly to steal £1,500 a year from his cousin! To commit felony! To do that for which he might be confined at Dartmoor all his life, with his hair cut, and dirty prison clothes, and hard food, and work to do! He thought it would be well to have another day of life in which he had not done the deed. He therefore put the will back into the book and went to his bed.

===Chinese===
星期二夜里到了——星期二的深夜时分——到了午夜那一刻，他确信女人们都已上床，便把遗嘱从藏处取了出来。他早已剪修好烛芯，把蜡烛安在一张摊开的报纸上，好叫烧落的每一星灰都落在收拾得起来的地方。他把屋子四下走了一遍，确信没有任何孔隙开着。他吹熄蜡烛，看是否有一丝光从哪里漏进屋里，然后重新点着。毁掉文书的时刻到了。

他又把遗嘱从头到尾读了一遍；——为什么，他自己也不知道，其实是在给自己找再拖一拖的借口。垂死的老人下笔是多么用心，每一个字都写足，每一笔都收得完完整整！他坐在那里端详老人的手迹，对自己说：只消手腕一动，把它彻底毁掉，全在他这一举。他转过身去又修了修烛芯，仍旧捏着那张纸坐在那儿。这么短促的一个动作，竟能结出这么大的一份后果吗？把自己的灵魂打落地狱！难道果真是把一己的灵魂交出去受永恒的惩罚？上帝会知道，他本来无意窃取那份产业！上帝会知道，他如今也不想窃取它！上帝会知道，他这样做，不过是躲开别人给他设下的苦难的唯一生路！上帝会知道，人家待他是多么残忍！上帝会知道，那老人待他是多么不公！有好些时刻，他几乎教会了自己相信：毁掉遗嘱不过是一桩粗略的公道之举，而上帝绝不会为一桩公道之举把什么人判入永恒的惩罚。可是即便如此，每当他转身朝着蜡烛，那只手总是不肯把纸举向火苗。一经做成，便再不能挽回！且不说那永恒的火焰究竟会不会吞了他，往后的岁月总有一片恐惧在前面煎熬他。奇基先生还能给他更坏的遭遇吗？

星期三做也全然来得及。何必夺去自己一天的安稳呢？这一天里，他的灵魂还不至于被定下万劫不复的罪。现在他可以睡了。至少今夜，他还可以睡。他怀疑那件事做成之后自己还能不能睡着。寻常的作恶，在他算不得什么——破一破那些寻常处世的规矩，父母拿来教训儿女、牧师拿来训导羊群的规矩，世人本就不大当回事，在他更是无所谓。贪图别人的财物，说邻舍的坏话，邻人的妻子若撞到他路上便追在后面献殷勤，寻常路数上占点小便宜——诸如卖一匹瘸腿马，或是打惠斯特牌时偷看对家的牌，对着一桩谎话起誓，或是讥诮亡故的双亲——这些小过从来不曾压弯他的灵魂。不把遗嘱说出来之所以压得他难受，只是因为怕败露。可是烧掉一份遗嘱，从而分明是从表妹手里偷走一年一千五百镑！这是犯重罪！干下那种事，就可能在达特穆尔（Dartmoor）关上一辈子，剃短头发，穿上龌龊的囚衣，吃粗劣的伙食，还得干活！他想，多留一天没做这事的日子总是好的。于是他把遗嘱放回书里，上床去了。

## XXII How Cousin Henry Was Let Off Easily / 第二十二章 亨利表哥如何被从轻发落

===Original===
It was a moment of great triumph and of utter dismay—of triumph to Mr. Apjohn, and of dismay to Cousin Henry. The two men at this moment—as Mr. Brodrick was looking at the papers—were struggling together upon the ground. Cousin Henry, in his last frantic efforts, had striven to escape from the grasp of his enemy so as to seize the will, not remembering that by seizing it now he could retrieve nothing. Mr. Apjohn had been equally determined that ample time should be allowed to Mr. Brodrick to secure any document that might be found, and, with the pugnacity which the state of fighting always produces, had held on to his prey with a firm grip. Now for the one man there remained nothing but dismay; for the other was the full enjoyment of the triumph produced by his own sagacity. "Here is the date," said Mr. Brodrick, who had retreated with the paper to the furthest corner of the room. "It is undoubtedly my brother-in-law's last will and testament, and, as far as I can see at a glance, it is altogether regular."

"You dog!" exclaimed Mr. Apjohn, spurning Cousin Henry away from him. "You wretched, thieving miscreant!" Then he got up on to his legs and began to adjust himself, setting his cravat right, and smoothing his hair with his hands. "The brute has knocked the breath out of me," he said. "But only to think that we should catch him after such a fashion as this!" There was a note of triumph in his voice which he found it impossible to repress. He was thoroughly proud of his achievement. It was a grand thing to him that Isabel Brodrick should at last get the property which he had so long been anxious to secure for her; but at the present moment it was a grander thing to have hit the exact spot in which the document had been hidden by sheer force of intelligence.

What little power of fighting there had ever been in Cousin Henry had now been altogether knocked out of him. He attempted no further struggle, uttered no denial, nor did he make any answer to the words of abuse which Mr. Apjohn had heaped on his head. He too raised himself from the floor, slowly collecting his limbs together, and seated himself in the chair nearest at hand, hiding his face with his hand.

===Chinese===
这是一个大大的得意与彻底的沮丧交并的时刻——得意属于阿普约翰先生，沮丧属于亨利表哥。此刻，正当布罗德里克先生查看那几张纸的时候，这两个人还在地上扭作一团。亨利表哥拼出最后一点疯狂的气力，想从仇敌的钳制中挣脱出来，好抓住那份遗嘱，却忘了此时纵然抓在手里，也挽回不了什么了。阿普约翰先生同样铁了心，务必要让布罗德里克先生有充裕的时间拿到可能找到的任何文书；他带着人一交起手来总会冒出的那股狠劲，把到手的猎物攥得死死的。如今，一个人除了沮丧已一无所有；另一个人却尽享着凭自己的精明挣来的全胜。"日期在这儿，"布罗德里克先生说；他已拿着那张纸退到了屋子最远的角落。"这无疑是我内兄的最后遗嘱，就我一眼看去，完全合乎规范。"

"你这狗东西！"阿普约翰先生喝道，一脚把亨利表哥踢开。"你这卑劣的、偷东西的恶棍！"随后他站起身来整理仪容，把领结扶正，又用手把头发抹平。"这畜生把我打得喘不过气来，"他说，"可想想吧，咱们竟会用这么一种方式把他逮住！"他话音里带着一股得意，怎么也压不下去。他对自己这番作为感到彻头彻尾的自豪。伊莎贝尔·布罗德里克终于能得到那份他多年来一心想替她保住的产业，在他固然是桩了不起的事；可眼下，单凭智力之力一举命中那份文书藏匿的所在，这才是更了不起的事。

亨利表哥骨子里那一点点打架的力气，如今已被彻底打了出去。他不再挣扎，没有说一句否认的话，对阿普约翰先生劈头盖脸泼来的那些骂语，也不作任何回答。他也从地板上撑起身子，慢慢把四肢收拢到一处，在离手边最近的一把椅子上坐下，用手捂住了脸。

===Original===
"That is the most wonderful thing that ever came within my experience," said Mr. Brodrick.

"That the man should have hidden the will?" asked Mr. Apjohn.

"Why do you say I hid it?" moaned Cousin Henry.

"You reptile!" exclaimed Mr. Apjohn.

"Not that he should have hidden it," said the Hereford attorney, "but that you should have found it, and found it without any search;—that you should have traced it down to the very book in which the old man must have left it!"

"Yes," said Cousin Henry. "He left it there. I did not hide it."

===Chinese===
"这是我生平经历过的最不可思议的一件事。"布罗德里克先生说。

"你是说这人竟把遗嘱藏了起来？"阿普约翰先生问。

"你们凭什么说是我藏的？"亨利表哥呻吟道。

"你这下贱的虫豸！"阿普约翰先生喝道。

"不是说他竟把它藏了起来，"这位赫里福德的律师说，"而是说你竟把它找了回来，而且不费一次搜寻就找了回来；——说你竟一路追下去，追到了老头子当年必定搁它的那本书上！"

"是，"亨利表哥说。"是他放在那儿的。不是我藏的。"

===Original===
"Do you mean," said Mr. Apjohn, turning upon him with all the severity of which he was capable, "do you mean to say that during all this time you have not known that the will was there?" The wretched man opened his mouth and essayed to speak, but not a word came. "Do you mean to tell us that when you refused us just now permission to search this room, though you were willing enough that we should search elsewhere, you were not acquainted with the hiding-place? When I asked you in my office the other day whether you knew where the will was hidden, and you wouldn't answer me for very fear, though you were glib enough in swearing that you had not hidden it yourself, then you knew nothing about the book and its enclosure? When you told Mr. Griffith down at Coed that you had something to divulge, were you not then almost driven to tell the truth by your dastardly cowardice as to this threatened trial? And did you not fail again because you were afraid? You mean poltroon! Will you dare to say before us, now, that when we entered the room this morning you did not know what the book contained?" Cousin Henry once more opened his mouth, but no word came. "Answer me, sir, if you wish to escape any part of the punishment which you have deserved."

"You should not ask him to criminate himself," said Mr. Brodrick.

"No!" shrieked Cousin Henry; "no! he shouldn't ask a fellow to tell against himself. It isn't fair; is it, Uncle Brodrick?"

===Chinese===
"你的意思是，"阿普约翰先生转过身来对着他，使出他全部的严厉，"你是想说，这么久以来，你一直不知道遗嘱就在那里？"这个可怜虫张开嘴，想说点什么，却一个字也说不出来。"你是想告诉我们，你方才不许我们搜这间屋子——尽管别的屋子你巴不得我们马上去搜——你对这个藏处竟一无所知？前两天在我事务所里，我问你知不知道遗嘱藏在哪儿，你吓得大气也不敢出，不敢答我的话，赌咒发誓说你本人没有藏它，倒是说得挺溜——那时候你对那本书和书里夹的东西竟毫无所知？你在科伊德对格里菲斯先生说，你有话要吐露——面对这场眼看要来的官司，你那怯懦到可鄙的胆小，当时岂不几乎逼得你说出实情？可你后来是不是又因为害怕而没敢说成？你这卑贱的懦夫！你如今敢当着我们的面说，今儿早晨我们进屋的时候，你不知道那本书里装的是什么？"亨利表哥又张开了一次嘴，还是没有说出话来。"回答我，先生——只要你想要逃脱你应得惩罚的任何一部分。"

"你不该逼他自证其罪。"布罗德里克先生说。

"是啊！"亨利表哥尖声叫道；"是啊！他不该逼着人说于自己不利的话。这不公平；是不是，布罗德里克伯父？"

===Original===
"If I hadn't made you tell against yourself one way or another," said Mr. Apjohn, "the will would have been there still, and we should all have been in the dark. There are occasions in which the truth must be screwed out of a man. We have screwed it out of you, you miserable creature! Brodrick, let us look at the paper. I suppose it is all right." He was so elated by the ecstasy of his success that he hardly knew how to contain himself. There was no prospect to him of any profit in all this. It might, indeed, well be that all the expenses incurred, including the handsome honorarium which would still have to be paid to Mr. Cheekey, must come out of his own pocket. But the glory of the thing was too great to admit of any considerations such as those. For the last month his mind had been exercised with the question of this will, whether there was such a will or not, and, if so, where was its hiding-place? Now he had brought his month's labour, his month's speculation, and his month's anxiety to a supreme success. In his present frame of mind it was nothing to him who might pay the bill. "As far as I can see," said Mr. Brodrick, "it is altogether in order."

"Let us look at it." Then Mr. Apjohn, stretching out his hand, took the document, and, seating himself in Cousin Henry's own chair at the breakfast-table, read it through carefully from beginning to end. It was wonderful—the exactness with which the old Squire had copied, not only every word, but every stop and every want of a stop in the preceding will. "It is my own work, every morsel of it," said Mr. Apjohn, with thorough satisfaction. "Why on earth did he not burn the intermediate one which he made in this rascal's favour,"—then he indicated the rascal by a motion of his head—"and make it all straight in that way?"

"There are men who think that a will once made should never be destroyed," suggested Mr. Brodrick.

===Chinese===
"我若不这样那样地逼你说出于己不利的话，"阿普约翰先生说，"遗嘱这会儿还在原处放着，我们大家全都蒙在鼓里。有些时候，真话非得从一个人身上拧出来不可。我们已经把它从你身上拧出来了，你这可怜的东西！布罗德里克，咱们来看看那张纸。我想它不至于有什么差错。"他成功得如醉如狂，简直不知怎样才能按捺住自己。这一切在他看不到半点利钱。说实在的，招来的全部花费——包括还得付给奇基先生的那笔可观的酬金——多半都得从他自己腰包里掏。可这件事的荣光太大了，容不得他去计较这类事。整整一个月，他脑子里盘桓的都是这份遗嘱的问题：究竟有没有这么一份遗嘱？若有，它藏在何处？如今，他一个月的操劳、一个月的揣度、一个月的悬心，终于大功告成。以他此刻的心境，账单由谁来付，全然不在他心上。"就我看，"布罗德里克先生说，"样样合规，全无问题。"

"给我们瞧瞧。"阿普约翰先生随即伸出手去接过文书，在早餐桌旁亨利表哥自己坐的那把椅子上落座，从头到尾仔细读了一遍。真是奇妙——老乡绅抄写的准确：不但每一个字，连前一份遗嘱里每一处标点、每一处该有标点而没有标点的地方，都照录不误。"这是我的手笔，一字一句都是，"阿普约翰先生心满意足地说。"他当初干吗不把中间立给这个无赖的那一份烧掉，"——说着他朝那个无赖偏了偏头——"那样不就一切都顺顺当当了？"

"有些人认为，遗嘱一经立下，就永远不该销毁。"布罗德里克先生提了一句。

===Original===
"I suppose it was something of that kind. He was a fine old fellow, but as obstinate as a mule. Well, what are we to do now?"

"My nephew will have to consult his lawyer whether he will wish to dispute this document or not."

"I do not want to dispute anything," said Cousin Henry, whining.

"Of course he will be allowed time to think of it," said Mr. Apjohn. "He is in possession now, and will have plenty of time. He will have to answer some rather difficult questions from Mr. Cheekey on Friday."

===Chinese===
"我想就是这类心思吧。他是个顶好的老头儿，可倔得像头骡子。好了，咱们如今该怎么办？"

"我内侄得去请教他的律师，看他究竟愿不愿意对这份文书提出争议。"

"我什么都不想争。"亨利表哥带着哭腔说。

"他当然会有时间去考虑，"阿普约翰先生说。"如今占着产业的是他，时间多的是。到了星期五，他还得回答奇基先生几个相当不好答的问题。"

===Original===
"Oh, no!" shouted the victim.

"I am afraid it must be 'oh, yes,' Mr. Jones! How are you to get out of it; eh? You are bound over to prosecute Mr. Evans, of the *Herald*, for defamation of character. Of course it will come out at the trial that we have found this document. Indeed, I shall be at no trouble to conceal that fact—nor, I suppose, will be Mr. Brodrick. Why should we?"

"I thought you were acting as my lawyer."

"So I was—and so I am—and so I will. While you were supposed to be an honest man—or, rather, while it was possible that it might be so supposed—I told you what, as an honest man, you were bound to do. The *Carmarthen Herald* knew that you were not honest—and said so. If you are prepared to go into the court and swear that you knew nothing of the existence of this document, that you were not aware that it was concealed in that book, that you did nothing to prevent us from looking for it this morning, I will carry on the case for you. If I am called into the witness-box against you, of course I must give my evidence for what it is worth;—and Mr. Brodrick must do the same."

===Chinese===
"哦，不！"这个受难者喊道。

"只怕非得是『哦，是』不可了，琼斯先生！你打算怎么从中脱身，嗯？你已具结在案，必须就毁人名誉一事，控告《卡马森先驱报》的埃文斯先生（Mr. Evans）。到了庭审的时候，我们找到这份文书的经过自然会见分晓。说实话，我根本不费心去隐瞒这个事实——想必布罗德里克先生也不会。我们干吗要瞒？"

"我还当您是替我办事的律师呢。"

"我过去是——现在也是——将来还是。当你还被当成一个诚实人——说得准些，当你还可能被人这样看待的时候——我告诉过你，作为一个诚实人，你责无旁贷该做些什么。《卡马森先驱报》知道你并不诚实——而且说了出来。你若准备走上法庭起誓，说你不知道世上有这份文书，说你不知道它藏在那本书里，说你今儿早晨没有做半点阻拦我们搜寻的事，这案子我就替你继续打下去。我若被传上证人席作对你不利的证，我自然要照实陈述，证词分量几何便是几何；——布罗德里克先生也得照样。"

===Original===
"But it won't go on?" he asked.

"Not if you are prepared to admit that there was no libel in all that the newspaper said. If you agree that it was all true, then you will have to pay the costs on both sides, and the indictment can be quashed. It will be a serious admission to make, but perhaps that won't signify, seeing what your position as to character will be."

"I think you are almost too hard upon him," said Mr. Brodrick.

===Chinese===
"可这案子不会打下去了吧？"他问。

"只要你准备承认，报纸上说的那些话算不得诽谤，就不打了。你若认下那些话句句属实，那么双方的讼费就得由你承担，起诉也就可以撤销。这可是一个分量不轻的认账，不过想想你日后的名声将落到什么境地，这一点或许也就无关紧要了。"

"我觉得你对他的态度差不多太苛刻了。"布罗德里克先生说。

===Original===
"Am I? Can one be too hard on a man who has acted as he has done?"

"He is hard—isn't he, Mr. Brodrick?"

"Hard! Why, yes;—I should think I am. I mean to be hard. I mean to go on trampling you to pieces till I see your cousin, Miss Brodrick, put into full possession of this estate. I don't mean to leave you a loophole of escape by any mercy. At the present moment you are Henry Jones, Esq., of Llanfeare, and will be so till you are put out by the hard hand of the law. You may turn round for anything I know, and say that this document is a forgery."

"No, no!"

===Chinese===
"我太苛刻？对一个行止像他这样的人，还有太苛刻一说么？"

"他是苛刻——是不是，布罗德里克先生？"

"苛刻！嗐，可不是；——我想我够苛刻的。我就是要苛刻。我要接着把你踩成齑粉，直到亲眼看见你的表妹布罗德里克小姐把这份产业的全部产权拿到手才罢休。我不打算凭任何怜悯给你留一条逃脱的活路。此时此刻，你是兰菲尔的亨利·琼斯先生，在法律那只强硬的手把你撵出去之前，你还得是。就我所知，你说不定还会掉过头来说，这份文书是伪造的。"

"不，不！"

===Original===
"That Mr. Brodrick and I brought it here with us and put it in the book."

"I shan't say anything of the kind."

"Who did put it there?" Cousin Henry sobbed and groaned, but said nothing. "Who did put it there? If you want to soften our hearts to you in any degree, if you wish us to contrive some mode of escape for you, tell the truth. Who put the will into that book?"

"How am I to know?"

===Chinese===
"说这份文书是我和布罗德里克先生随身带来、夹进那本书里去的。"

"这种话我断然不会说。"

"那究竟是谁放在那里的？"亨利表哥抽噎呻吟，却一言不发。"究竟是谁放在那里的？你若想叫我们对你心软上一分一毫，你若想让我们替你想出一条脱身之计，就说实话。遗嘱是谁放进那本书里去的？"

"我怎么会知道？"

===Original===
"You do know! Who put it there?"

"I suppose it was Uncle Indefer."

"And you had seen it there?" Again Cousin Henry sobbed and groaned.

"You should hardly ask him that," said Mr. Brodrick.

===Chinese===
"你知道！是谁放在那里的？"

"我想是因德弗伯父。"

"而且你在那里见过它？"亨利表哥又抽噎呻吟起来。

"这个问题你几乎不该问他。"布罗德里克先生说。

===Original===
"Yes! If any good can be done for him, it must be by making him feel that he must help us by making our case easy for us. You had seen it there? Speak the word, and we will do all we can to let you off easily."

"Just by an accident," said he.

"You did see it, then?"

"Yes;—I chanced to see it."

===Chinese===
"不错，要问！若还想给他什么好处，那就只有让他明白：他必须帮我们把案子变得容易，我们才帮得了他。你在那里见过它？把那个字说出来，我们必尽力让你从轻发落。"

"不过是碰巧罢了。"他说。

"这么说你还是见过它？"

"是；——我是偶然看见的。"

===Original===
"Yes; of course you did. And then the Devil went to work with you and prompted you to destroy it?" He paused as though asking a question, but to this question Cousin Henry found it impossible to make any answer. "But the Devil had not quite hold enough over you to make you do that? It was so;—was it not? There was a conscience with you?"

"Oh, yes."

"But the conscience was not strong enough to force you to give it up when you found it?" Cousin Henry now burst out into open tears. "That was about it, I suppose? If you can bring yourself to make a clean breast of it, it will be easier for you."

"May I go back to London at once?" he asked.

===Chinese===
"是；你当然见过。随后魔鬼就来缠上了你，撺掇你把它毁掉？"他停了停，像是在发问，可对这个问题，亨利表哥觉得怎么也答不上来。"可是魔鬼抓你抓得还不够紧，没能叫你真下那个手？实情如此；——是不是？你心里还有个良知在？"

"哦，有的。"

"可是这良知还不够强，没能逼你在发现它的时候把它交出来？"亨利表哥这下放声哭了出来。"大致就是这么回事，我想？你若能说服自己把事情和盘托出，你会好受些。"

"我可以马上回伦敦去么？"他问。

===Original===
"Well; as to that, I think we had better take some little time for consideration. But I think I may say that, if you will make our way easy for us, we will endeavour to make yours easy for you. You acknowledge this to be your uncle's will as far as you know?"

"Oh, yes."

"You acknowledge that Mr. Brodrick found it in this book which I now hold in my hand?"

"I acknowledge that."

===Chinese===
"这个嘛，我想我们最好稍稍花点时间考虑一下。不过我想我可以这样说：你若肯让我们的路好走，我们也会尽力让你的路好走。就你所知，你承认这是你伯父的遗嘱么？"

"哦，承认。"

"你承认是布罗德里克先生从我此刻手里拿着的这本书里找到它的？"

"我承认。"

===Original===
"This is all that I ask you to sign your name to. As for the rest, it is sufficient that you have confessed the truth to your uncle and to me. I will just write a few lines that you shall sign, and then we will go back to Carmarthen and do the best we can to prevent the trial for next Friday." Thereupon Mr. Apjohn rang the bell, and asked Mrs. Griffith to bring him paper and ink. With these he wrote a letter addressed to himself, which he invited Cousin Henry to sign as soon as he had read it aloud to him and to Mr. Brodrick. The letter contained simply the two admissions above stated, and then went on to authorise Mr. Apjohn, as the writer's attorney, to withdraw the indictment against the proprietor of the *Carmarthen Herald*, "in consequence," as the letter said, "of the question as to the possession of Llanfeare having been settled now in an unexpected manner."

When the letter was completed, the two lawyers went away, and Cousin Henry was left to his own meditation. He sat there for a while, so astounded by the transaction of the morning as to be unable to collect his thoughts. All this that had agitated him so profoundly for the last month had been set at rest by the finding of the will. There was no longer any question as to what must be done. Everything had been done. He was again a London clerk, with a small sum of money besides his clerkship, and the security of lowliness into which to fall back! If only they would be silent;—if only it might be thought by his fellow-clerks in London that the will had been found by them without any knowledge on his part—then he would be satisfied. A terrible catastrophe had fallen upon him, but one which would not be without consolation if with the estate might be made to pass away from him all responsibilities and all accusations as to the estate. That terrible man had almost promised him that a way of retreat should be made easy to him. At any rate, he would not be cross-examined by Mr. Cheekey. At any rate, he would not be brought to trial. There was almost a promise, too, that as little should be said as possible. There must, he supposed, be some legal form of abdication on his part, but he was willing to execute that as quickly as possible on the simple condition that he should be allowed to depart without being forced to speak further on the matter to anyone in Wales. Not to have to see the tenants, not to have to say even a word of farewell to the servants, not to be carried into Carmarthen—above all, not to face Mr. Cheekey and the Court—this was all he asked now from a kind Fate.

At about two Mrs. Griffith came into the room, ostensibly to take away the breakfast things. She had seen the triumphant face of Mr. Apjohn, and knew that some victory had been gained. But when she saw that the breakfast had not been touched, her heart became soft. The way to melt the heart of a Mrs. Griffith is to eat nothing. "Laws, Mr. Jones, you have not had a mouthful. Shall I do you a broil?" He assented to the broil, and ate it, when it was cooked, with a better appetite than he had enjoyed since his uncle's death. Gradually he came to feel that a great load had been taken from off his shoulders. The will was no longer hidden in the book. Nothing had been done of which he could not repent. There was no prospect of a life before him made horrid by one great sin. He could not be Squire of Llanfeare; nor would he be a felon—a felon always in his own esteem. Upon the whole, though he hardly admitted as much to himself, the man's condition had been improved by the transactions of the morning.

===Chinese===
"我要你签名的，就只有这些。至于其余的事，你既已向布罗德里克伯父和我招认了实情，那也就够了。我这就写几行字让你签署，然后我们回卡马森去，尽力设法阻止下星期五那场庭审。"说罢，阿普约翰先生摇了摇铃，请格里菲斯太太给他取纸和墨水来。他就用这些写了一封写给他自己的信，先把它念给亨利表哥和布罗德里克先生听，随后便请亨利表哥签名。信里只有上文说过的那两项承认，接着便授权阿普约翰先生以写信人律师的身份，撤回对《卡马森先驱报》业主的起诉；用信里的话说，这是"因为兰菲尔产业的归属问题，如今已经以一种出人意料的方式得到了解决"。

信写完之后，两位律师便走了，剩下亨利表哥独自冥想。他在那儿坐了一阵，被早晨这场变故震骇得心神涣散，理不出一点头绪。这一个月来搅得他心绪翻腾的这一切，都因遗嘱的找到而一了百了。再没有什么事是必须去做的了。一切都做完了。他重新做回伦敦的一个办事员，除了差事还有一小笔钱，身后还有低微的安稳可退！只求他们守口如瓶；只求伦敦的同事们以为那份遗嘱是他们找到的、他本人毫不知情——那样他也就心满意足了。一场可怕的灾祸落到了他头上，可这灾祸未必没有慰藉，倘若随着这份产业，与产业相连的一切责任、一切指控，也能一并从他身上移开。那个可怕的人几乎已经许诺，会给他安排一条好走的退路。无论如何，他不会被奇基先生交叉盘问了。无论如何，他不会被送上法庭了。似乎还有一层近乎允诺的意思：尽量少说。他想，他这边总还须办一个法律上的让位手续，但只要容许他不必再跟威尔士的任何人谈这件事就放他离开，他愿意尽快照办。不必见那些佃户，不必对仆人们说哪怕一句道别的话，不必给弄到卡马森城里去——尤其是，不必面对奇基先生和法庭——这就是他如今向仁慈的命运讨要的全部了。

两点钟左右，格里菲斯太太走进了屋子，名义上是来撤走早餐的杯盘。她方才瞧见了阿普约翰先生那张得胜的脸，知道打了一场什么胜仗。可她一见早餐原封未动，心就软了。要化开一位格里菲斯太太的心，法子就是什么也不吃。"天哪，琼斯先生，您一口都没沾。要不要我给您烤点肉吃？"烤肉他应下了，做好端来，他吃了，胃口比伯父故去以来哪一顿都好。他渐渐觉出，一副重担已从肩头卸下。遗嘱不再藏在那本书里了。他没有做过什么无可追悔的事。摆在前头的人生，不再是被一桩大罪弄得可怖的人生。他做不成兰菲尔的乡绅了；可他也不会沦为重罪犯——一个在他自己眼里永远是重罪犯的人。总的说来，虽然他几乎不肯向自己承认，这个人的处境，已被早晨这番变故改善了几分。

===Original===
"You don't quite agree with all that I have done this morning," said Mr. Apjohn, as soon as the two lawyers were in the fly together.

"I am lost in admiration at the clearness of your insight."

"Ah! that comes of giving one's undivided thoughts to a matter. I have been turning it over in my mind till I have been able to see it all. It was odd, wasn't it, that I should have foretold to you all that happened, almost to the volume?"

"Quite to the volume!"

"Well, yes; to the volume of sermons. Your brother-in-law read nothing but sermons. But you thought I shouldn't have asked those questions."

===Chinese===
"今儿早晨我做的这些事，你不全赞成。"两位律师刚一同坐上轻便马车，阿普约翰先生便这样说。

"我佩服得五体投地——你的眼光真是透彻。"

"嗐！这是把整个心思扑在一件事上的结果。我在心里把它翻来覆去掂量，直到里里外外都看透了。说来也怪，是不是？出了的事我件件都向你预言过，几乎连是哪一本书都料中了。"

"连哪本书都分毫不差！"

"可不是；正应到那卷布道文集上。你内兄别的什么都不读，只读布道文。可你原以为我不该问那些问题。"

===Original===
"I don't like making a man criminate himself," said Mr. Brodrick.

"Nor do I—if I mean to criminate him too. My object is to let him off. But to enable us to do that we must know exactly what he knew and what he had done. Shall I tell you what occurred to me when you shook the will out of the book? How would it be if he declared that we had brought it with us? If he had been sharp enough for that, the very fact of our having gone to the book at once would have been evidence against us."

"He was not up to it."

"No, poor devil! I am inclined to think that he has got as bad as he deserves. He might have been so much worse. We owe him ever so much for not destroying the will. His cousin will have to give him the £4,000 which he was to have given her."

"Certainly, certainly."

"He has been hardly used, you know, by his uncle; and, upon my word, he has had a bad time of it for the last month. I wouldn't have been hated and insulted as he has been by those people up there—not for all Llanfeare twice over. I think we've quenched him now, so that he'll run smooth. If so, we'll let him off easily. If I had treated him less hardly just now, he might have gathered courage and turned upon us. Then it would have been necessary to crush him altogether. I was thinking all through how we might let him off easiest."

===Chinese===
"我不喜欢逼人自证其罪。"布罗德里克先生说。

"我也不喜欢——倘若我也打算给他定罪的话。我的目的是放他一马。可要放得了他，我们就必须确切知道他知道了什么、干了什么。要不要我告诉你，你把遗嘱从书里抖出来的那一刻，我心里想到了什么？倘若他一口咬定是我们随身带来的，那会怎样？他若精明到这一步，那我们径直奔向那本书这件事本身，立刻就会成了对我们不利的证据。"

"他没有这份精明。"

"是啊，可怜的东西！我倾向于认为，他落到这步糟境地已是恰如其分，本来还可能糟得多。他没有毁掉那份遗嘱，单为这一点，我们就欠了他好大一份情。他表妹还得把那四千镑给他——那笔钱原本是该由他来给她的。"

"当然，当然。"

"你知道，他伯父待他确实刻薄；说句实话，这一个月他过的日子也够受的。上头那些人那样恨他、作践他，这份罪换我来受，就是把两倍的兰菲尔给我也不干。我看咱们如今已经把他浇熄了，往后他会走得平顺。果真如此，咱们就让他从轻发落。我刚才若待他稍微松一点，他没准儿会鼓起勇气来跟我们翻脸。到那时候，就非得把他彻底碾碎不可了。我从头到尾心里盘算的，都是怎样让他从轻发落得最容易。"

## XI Isabel at Hereford / 第十一章 伊莎贝尔在赫里福德

===Original===

Isabel had not been many hours at home at Hereford before, as was natural, her father discussed with her the affairs of the property and her own peculiar interest in the will which had at last been accepted. It has to be acknowledged that Isabel was received somewhat as an interloper in the house. She was not wanted there, at any rate by her stepmother—hardly by her brothers and sisters—and was, perhaps, not cordially desired even by her father. She and her stepmother had never been warm friends. Isabel herself was clever and high-minded; but high-spirited also, imperious, and sometimes hard. It may be said of her that she was at all points a gentlewoman. So much could hardly be boasted of the present Mrs. Brodrick; and, as was the mother, so were that mother’s children. The father was a gentleman, born and bred as such; but in his second marriage he had fallen a little below his station, and, having done so, had accommodated himself to his position. Then there had come many children, and the family had increased quicker than the income. So it had come to pass that the attorney was not a wealthy man. This was the home which Isabel had been invited to leave when, now many years since, she had gone to Llanfeare to become her uncle’s darling. There her life had been very different from that of the family at Hereford. She had seen but little of society, but had been made much of, and almost worshipped, by those who were around her. She was to be—was to have been—the Lady of Llanfeare. By every tenant about the place she had been loved and esteemed. With the servants she had been supreme. Even at Carmarthen, when she was seen there, she was regarded as the great lady, the acknowledged heiress, who was to have, at some not very distant time, all Llanfeare in her own hands. It was said of her, and said truly, that she was possessed of many virtues. She was charitable, careful for others, in no way self-indulgent, sedulous in every duty, and, above all things, affectionately attentive to her uncle. But she had become imperious, and inclined to domineer, if not in action, yet in spirit. She had lived much among books, had delighted to sit gazing over the sea with a volume of poetry in her hand, truly enjoying the intellectual gifts which had been given her. But she had, perhaps, learnt too thoroughly her own superiority, and was somewhat apt to look down upon the less refined pleasure of other people. And now her altered position in regard to wealth rather increased than diminished her foibles. Now, in her abject poverty—for she was determined that it should be abject—she would be forced to sustain her superiority solely by her personal gifts. She determined that, should she find herself compelled to live in her father’s house, she would do her duty thoroughly by her stepmother and her sisters. She would serve them as far as it might be within her power; but she could not giggle with the girls, nor could she talk little gossip with Mrs. Brodrick. While there was work to be done, she would do it, though it should be hard, menial, and revolting; but when her work was done, there would be her books.

It will be understood that, such being her mood and such her character, she would hardly make herself happy in her father’s house—or make others happy. And then, added to all this, there was the terrible question of money! When last at Hereford, she had told her father that, though her uncle had revoked his grand intention in her favour, still there would be coming to her enough to prevent her from being a burden on the resources of her family. Now that was all changed. If her father should be unable or unwilling to support her, she would undergo any hardship, any privation; but would certainly not accept bounty from the hands of her cousin. Some deed had been done, she felt assured—some wicked deed, and Cousin Henry had been the doer of it. She and she alone had heard the last words which her uncle had spoken, and she had watched the man’s face narrowly when her uncle’s will had been discussed in the presence of the tenants. She was quite sure. Let her father say what he might, let her stepmother look at her ever so angrily with her greedy, hungry eyes, she would take no shilling from her Cousin Henry. Though she might have to die in the streets, she would take no bread from her Cousin Henry’s hand.

===Chinese===

伊莎贝尔（Isabel）回到赫里福德（Hereford）家里还没有几个钟头，她的父亲便理所当然地同她谈起了那份产业的事情，谈起了那份终于获得认定的遗嘱同她本人的特殊利害。不能不承认，伊莎贝尔在这座宅子里多少是被当作一个闯进来的外人接待的。那里不需要她——无论如何她的继母不需要——兄弟姐妹也几乎不需要——甚至她的父亲，恐怕也说不上由衷地盼她回来。她和继母从来就不是亲密的朋友。伊莎贝尔本人聪明而志行高洁；却也血气刚烈，专断傲慢，有时还很苛刻。可以说，无论从哪一点看，她都是一位淑女。而如今的布罗德里克太太（Mrs. Brodrick）就很难这样自夸了；母亲是什么样，那位母亲生养的儿女也就是什么样。父亲是位绅士，生来如此，也自幼按绅士的身份教养长大；可他第二次结婚，把自己降到了略低于自身身份的地步，既已如此，便安于这个位置了。此后接连添了许多儿女，家里的人口比进项长得快。于是这位律师便成了并不宽裕的人。这就是许多年前伊莎贝尔应邀离开的那个家——那时她去兰菲尔（Llanfeare）做了伯父的宠儿。在那里，她的日子同赫里福德这家人的日子大不相同。她很少见识社交，可身边的人把她看得极重，几乎奉若神明。她本要做——本该做了——兰菲尔的女主人。庄子上的每一个佃户都爱戴她、敬重她。在仆人中间，她说一不二。就连在卡马森（Carmarthen），人们在那里见到她，也把她看作贵妇人，看作众望所归的女继承人——用不了多久，整个兰菲尔便要归她一人执掌。人家说起她，说她具有种种美德；这话说得并不错。她乐善好施，体恤他人，从不放纵自己，样样本分都尽心竭力；而顶要紧的，是对伯父一往情深、体贴入微。可她也变得专断了，惯于颐指气使——纵然不见于行止，也见于心气。她长年与书为伴，喜欢手执一卷诗集、坐着凝望大海，真心领受着上天赋予她的才智。可她也许把自己的过人之处体认得太透了，不免有些看轻别人那些不够高雅的乐趣。而如今她在财富上的地位变故，非但没有消减、反倒添长了她这些小毛病。如今，处在赤贫之中——她决意要赤贫到底——她便只得单凭自身的禀赋来撑持她的优越了。她拿定主意，倘若发现自己不得不住在父亲的宅子里，她一定把对继母和妹妹们的本分尽得完完全全。只要力所能及，她都愿意侍候她们；可她没法陪着姑娘们咯咯傻笑，也没法同布罗德里克太太说些闲言碎语。只要有活要干，她就干，哪怕这活又累又贱、叫人嫌恶；活干完了，还有她的书在。

可以想见，心境如此、性情如此，她在父亲的宅子里难得叫自己快活——也难得叫别人快活。何况在这一切之上，还压着那桩可怕的金钱问题！上一回在赫里福德的时候，她曾告诉父亲：纵然伯父收回了他那番归给她的宏大美意，将来落到她手里的钱也尽够叫她不必成为全家进项的负担。如今这一切全变了。倘若父亲无力、或不肯供养她，无论怎样的困苦、怎样的匮乏她都可以经受；但她决计不肯从表哥手里领受恩赏。一定出过什么事，她深信如此——出过一桩恶事，而干下这桩事的就是亨利表哥（Cousin Henry）。伯父临终说的最后那几句话，只有她一个人听见；而当着佃户们商议伯父遗嘱的时候，她把那男人的脸色细细看在眼里。她有十足的把握。任凭父亲怎么说，任凭继母那双贪婪饥渴的眼睛把她瞪得再凶，她也不从亨利表哥手里拿一个先令。哪怕死在街头，她也不吃亨利表哥手里的面包。

===Original===

She herself began the question of the money on the day after her arrival. “Papa,” she said, “there is to be nothing for me after all.”

Now Mr. Apjohn, the lawyer, like a cautious family solicitor as he was, had written to Mr. Brodrick, giving him a full account of the whole affair, telling him of the legacy of four thousand pounds, explaining that there was no fund from which payment could be legally exacted, but stating also that the circumstances of the case were of such a nature as to make it almost impossible that the new heir should refuse to render himself liable for the amount. Then had come another letter saying that the new heir had assented to do so.

“Oh, yes, there will, Isabel,” said the father.

Then she felt that the fighting of the battle was incumbent upon her, and she was determined to fight it. “No, papa, no; not a shilling.”

===Chinese===

她到家的第二天，就自己提起了钱的问题。“爸爸，”她说，“到头来毕竟是没有我的份了。”

却说律师阿普约翰先生（Mr. Apjohn），像他这样一位谨慎的家庭法律顾问，早已去信布罗德里克先生（Mr. Brodrick），把整件事情原原本本告诉了他，提到了那笔四千镑的遗赠，说明并无一笔资金可以依法从中索取这笔付款，但也言明：此案的种种情形，足以叫那位新继承人几乎无从拒绝承担这笔款项。随后又来了一封信，说新继承人已经应允照此办理。

“哦，有的，伊莎贝尔，会有的，”父亲说。

这时她觉得这一仗非她来打不可，而且打定了主意要打。“不，爸爸，不；一个先令也不要。”

===Original===

“Yes, my dear, yes,” he said, smiling. “I have heard from Mr. Apjohn, and understood all about it. The money, no doubt, is not there; but your cousin is quite prepared to charge the estate with the amount. Indeed, it would be almost impossible for him to refuse to do so. No one would speak to him were he to be so base as that. I do not think much of your Cousin Henry, but even Cousin Henry could not be so mean. He has not the courage for such villainy.”

“I have the courage,” said she.

“What do you mean?”

“Oh, papa, do not be angry with me! Nothing—nothing shall induce me to take my Cousin Henry’s money.”

===Chinese===

“有的，我的好孩子，有的，”他微笑着说。“阿普约翰先生信里都写了，来龙去脉我全明白了。那笔钱毫无疑问是不在那儿的；可你表哥完全预备好把这笔数目设定为产业的负担。说实在的，要他拒绝，几乎是不可能的。他若卑劣到那般地步，就没有人肯同他说话了。你那位亨利表哥，我看得很轻；可就连亨利表哥，也不至于这样下作。这样的恶行，他没有那个胆量。”

“我有这个胆量，”她说。

“你这是什么意思？”

“哦，爸爸，别生我的气！无论什么——无论什么都休想叫我拿亨利表哥的钱。”

===Original===

“It will be your money—your money by your uncle’s will. It is the very sum which he himself has named as intended for you.”

“Yes, papa; but Uncle Indefer had not got the money to give. Neither you nor I should be angry with him; because he intended the best.”

“I am angry with him,” said the attorney in wrath, “because he deceived you and deceived me about the property.”

“Never; he deceived no one. Uncle Indefer and deceit never went together.”

===Chinese===

“那会是你的钱——按你伯父的遗嘱本该归你的钱。正是他亲口定下、打算给你的那笔数目。”

“是的，爸爸；可是因德弗伯父（Uncle Indefer）手里并没有这笔钱可给。你我都不该生他的气；因为他的本意是最好的。”

“我就是生他的气，”律师怒冲冲地说，“因为在产业的事情上他骗了你，也骗了我。”

“从来没有；他谁也没骗过。因德弗伯父同欺骗从来沾不上边。”

===Original===

“There is no question of that now,” said the father. “He made some slight restitution, and there can, of course, be no question as to your taking it.”

“There is a question, and there must be a question, papa. I will not have it. If my being here would be an expense too great for you, I will go away.”

“Where will you go?”

“I care not where I go. I will earn my bread. If I cannot do that, I would rather live in the poorhouse than accept my cousin’s money.”

===Chinese===

“如今不说那个了，”父亲说。“他已经稍稍作了一点补偿；你把这钱收下，自然是毫无问题的。”

“有问题，而且必定有问题，爸爸。我不要这笔钱。倘若我住在这儿，花费大得叫你承受不起，我就走。”

“你上哪儿去？”

“上哪儿我都无所谓。我要自己挣面包。若连这也办不到，我宁可住济贫院，也不接受我表哥的钱。”

===Original===

“What has he done?”

“I do not know.”

“As Mr. Apjohn very well puts it, there is no question whatsoever as to gratitude, or even of acceptance. It is a matter of course. He would be inexpressibly vile were he not to do this.”

“He is inexpressibly vile.”

“Not in this respect. He is quite willing. You will have nothing to do but to sign a receipt once every half-year till the whole sum shall have been placed to your credit.”

“I will sign nothing on that account; nor will I take anything.”

===Chinese===

“他干了什么？”

“我不知道。”

“正如阿普约翰先生说得极好，这里头压根儿谈不上什么感激，甚至也谈不上什么收不收。这是理所当然的事。他若不这么做，那就卑劣得没法形容了。”

“他就是卑劣得没法形容。”

“这件事上他可不卑劣。他挺乐意的。你什么也不用做，只需每半年签一回收条，直到那整笔款子都记到你名下为止。”

“为这个缘故，我一个字也不签；也分文不取。”

===Original===

“But why not? What has he done?”

“I do not know. I do not say that he has done anything. I do not care to speak of him. Pray do not think, papa, that I covet the estate, or that I am unhappy about that. Had he been pleasant to my uncle and good to the tenants, had he seemed even to be like a man, I could have made him heartily welcome to Llanfeare. I think my uncle was right in choosing to have a male heir. I should have done so myself—in his place.”

“He was wrong, wickedly wrong, after his promises.”

“There were no promises made to me: nothing but a suggestion, which he was, of course, at liberty to alter if he pleased. We need not, however, go back to that, papa. There he is, owner of Llanfeare, and from him, as owner of Llanfeare, I will accept nothing. Were I starving in the street I would not take a crust of bread from his fingers.”

===Chinese===

“可这是为什么？他到底干了什么？”

“我不知道。我并没有说他干过什么。我不乐意说他的事。爸爸，千万可别以为我贪图那份产业，也别以为我为那个伤心。倘若他待伯父和气、待佃户厚道，倘若他哪怕看上去还有几分像个男子汉，我本会打心底里欢迎他做兰菲尔的主人。我看伯父挑一个男性继承人，是挑对了的。换了我处在他那个位置，我也会这么挑。”

“他错了，错得恶劣，许下了话又反悔。”

“并没有谁对我许下过诺言：不过是一个口风罢了，他当然高兴改就可以改。不过这也不必再提了，爸爸。如今他就在那儿，做着兰菲尔的主人；作为兰菲尔的主人，从他手里我什么都不接受。哪怕我饿死在街上，也不从他指头上接一块面包皮。”

===Original===

Over and over again the conversation was renewed, but always with the same result. Then there was a correspondence between the two attorneys, and Mr. Apjohn undertook to ask permission from the Squire to pay the money to the father’s receipt without asking any acknowledgement from the daughter. On hearing this, Isabel declared that if this were done she would certainly leave her father’s house. She would go out of it, even though she should not know whither she was going. Circumstances should not be made so to prevail upon her as to force her to eat meat purchased by her cousin’s money.

Thus it came to pass that Isabel’s new home was not made comfortable to her on her first arrival. Her stepmother would hardly speak to her, and the girls knew that she was in disgrace. There was Mr. Owen, willing enough, as the stepmother knew, to take Isabel away and relieve them all from this burden, and with the £4,000 Mr. Owen would, no doubt, be able at once to provide a home for her. But Mr. Owen could hardly do this without some help. And even though Mr. Owen should be so generous—and thus justify the name of “softie” which Mrs. Brodrick would sometimes give him in discussing his character with her own daughters—how preferable would it be to have a relation well-provided! To Mrs. Brodrick the girl’s objection was altogether unintelligible. The more of a Philistine Cousin Henry was, the more satisfaction should there be in fleecing him. To refuse a legacy because it was not formal was, to her thinking, an act of insanity. To have the payment of one refused to her because of informality would have been heartbreaking. But the making of such a difficulty as this she could not stomach. Could she have had her will, she would have been well pleased to whip the girl! Therefore Isabel’s new home was not pleasant to her.

At this time Mr. Owen was away, having gone for his holiday to the Continent. To all the Brodricks it was a matter of course that he would marry Isabel as soon as he came back. There was no doubt that he was “a softie.” But then how great is the difference between having a brother-in-law well off, and a relation tightly constrained by closely limited means! To refuse—even to make a show of refusing—those good things was a crime against the husband who was to have them. Such was the light in which Mrs. Brodrick looked at it. To Mr. Brodrick himself there was an obstinacy in it which was sickening to him. But to Isabel’s thinking the matter was very different. She was as firmly resolved that she would not marry Mr. Owen as that she would not take her cousin’s money;—almost as firmly resolved.

===Chinese===

这番话翻来覆去重新提起过多少遍，结果回回一样。后来两位律师之间通起信来，阿普约翰先生自任去请乡绅（the Squire）允准：把钱付给父亲、凭父亲的收条入账，并不要求女儿出具任何收执。伊莎贝尔一听这话便声明，若果真这么办，她就一定离开父亲的宅子。她要走出这个家，哪怕不知道自己要往哪里去。决不能由着情势这样压她，逼她去吃用表哥的钱买来的肉食。

于是事情就成了这般光景：伊莎贝尔初来乍到，这个新家在她便安适不了。继母几乎不屑同她说话，姑娘们也都知道她失了宠。还有欧文先生（Mr. Owen）——继母心里明白，他倒是顶乐意把伊莎贝尔接走，好叫一家人都卸下这副担子；手握那四千镑（£4,000），欧文先生毫无疑问立刻就能给她安置一个家。可欧文先生没有一点帮衬，这件事只怕办不成。再说，哪怕欧文先生当真慷慨到这个地步——果真坐实了布罗德里克太太同亲生女儿们议论他的为人时有时加在他头上的那个“软柿子”的名声——有一个家资殷实的亲戚，岂不比这强得多！在布罗德里克太太看来，这姑娘的推拒简直全然不可理喻。亨利表哥越是市侩，从他身上刮油水就越该称心如意。只因手续不全就拒绝一笔遗赠，照她想来，简直是发疯。倘若有人以手续不全为由拒付给她一笔款项，那她是非心碎不可的。可这丫头偏偏设下这样一道难题，叫她咽不下去。她若能遂心所欲，乐得抽这丫头一顿鞭子！因此，伊莎贝尔的这个新家，她住得并不痛快。

这时候欧文先生不在家，度假去了欧洲大陆。在布罗德里克全家看来，他一回来就会娶伊莎贝尔，这是顺理成章的事。毫无疑问，他是个“软柿子”。可是，家里有一位手头宽裕的姐夫，同有一位被紧巴的家底箍得紧紧的亲戚，这中间的差别该有多大！拒绝——哪怕只是做做拒绝的姿态——那些美事，便是对那位将享有这些美事的丈夫犯了罪。布罗德里克太太便是这样看这桩事的。在布罗德里克先生本人看来，这里头有一股子倔劲，叫他心里作呕。可在伊莎贝尔想来，这件事全然是另一回事。她不肯嫁欧文先生的心意，同她不肯拿表哥的钱的心意一样坚定——差不多一样坚定。

===Original===

Then there came the angry letter from Cousin Henry, containing two points which had to be considered. There was the offer to her to come to Llanfeare, and live there as though she was herself the owner. That, indeed, did not require much consideration. It was altogether out of the question, and only dwelt in her thoughts as showing how quickly the man had contrived to make himself odious to everyone about the place. His uncle, he said, had made the place a nest of hornets to him. Isabel declared that she knew why the place was a nest of hornets. There was no one about Llanfeare to whom so unmanly, so cringing, so dishonest a creature would not be odious. She could understand all that.

But then there was the other point, and on that her mind rested long.

“I think you ought to be ashamed of what you said to me—so soon after the old man’s death.”

She sat long in silence thinking of it, meditating whether he had been true in that—whether it did behove her to repent her harshness to the man. She remembered well her words;—“We take presents from those we love, not from those we despise.”

===Chinese===

后来亨利表哥那封怒气冲冲的信到了，里头有两点须得斟酌。一点是邀她到兰菲尔去，住在那里，仿佛她自己就是主人。这一层，确实无须多费思量。根本无从谈起；它之所以停留在她的思绪里，只是因为它表明：这人多么快就设法把自己弄得叫庄子上下人人憎恶。他说，他伯父把那地方变成了一窝螫他的马蜂。伊莎贝尔声言，她知道那地方为什么会是马蜂窝。像这样一个没有丈夫气概、卑躬屈节、不老实的家伙，兰菲尔上下没有哪一个人不憎恶他。这一切她都能懂得。

可是另外还有那一点，她的心思在那上头盘桓了很久。

“我看你该为自己对我说的话害臊——老人尸骨未寒，就说出这样的话来。”

她久久默然独坐，翻来覆去地想这句话，掂量他这话是不是在理——她当真该不该为自己待他苛刻而悔愧。她把自己那句话记得清清楚楚——“我们收我们所爱的人送的礼，不收我们所看不起的人送的礼。”

===Original===

They had been hard words—quite unjustifiable unless he had made himself guilty of something worse than conduct that was simply despicable. Not because he had been a poor creature, not because he had tormented the old man’s last days by an absence of all generous feeling, not because he had been altogether unlike what, to her thinking, a Squire of Llanfeare should be, had she answered him with those crushing words. It was because at the moment she had believed him to be something infinitely worse than that.

Grounding her aversion on such evidence as she had—on such evidence as she thought she had—she had brought against him her heavy accusation. She could not tell him to his face that he had stolen the will, she could not accuse him of felony, but she had used such quick mode of expression as had come to her for assuring him that he stood as low in her esteem as a felon might stand. And this she had done when he was endeavouring to perform to her that which had been described to him as a duty! And now he had turned upon her and rebuked her—rebuked her as he was again endeavouring to perform the same duty—rebuked her as it was so natural that a man should do who had been subjected to so gross an affront!

She hated him, despised him, and in her heart condemned him. She still believed him to have been guilty. Had he not been guilty, the beads of perspiration would not have stood upon his brow; he would not have become now red, now pale, by sudden starts; he would not have quivered beneath her gaze when she looked into his face. He could not have been utterly mean as he was, had he not been guilty. But yet—and now she saw it with her clear-seeing intellect, now that her passion was in abeyance—she had not been entitled to accuse him to his face. If he were guilty, it was for others to find it out, and for others to accuse him. It had been for her as a lady, and as her uncle’s niece, to accept him in her uncle’s house as her uncle’s heir. No duty could have compelled her to love him, no duty would have required her to accept even his friendship. But she was aware that she had misbehaved herself in insulting him. She was ashamed of herself in that she had not been able to hide her feelings within her own high heart, but had allowed him to suppose that she had been angered because she had been deprived of her uncle’s wealth. Having so resolved, she wrote to him as follows:

===Chinese===

那些话是够狠的——除非他干下了比单是可鄙的行径更坏的勾当、叫自己背上了罪名，否则那些话全然说不过去。她当年用那几句把人压垮的重话回敬他，并不是因为他是个可怜虫，并不是因为他胸中没有半点宽宏之情、搅扰了老人的残年，也不是因为他全然不像——照她看来——兰菲尔的乡绅该有的样子。而是因为在那一刻，她相信他犯下的勾当比这些坏得不知多少。

她凭着自己手里那样的证据——凭着她自以为握在手中的那样的证据——把那份沉重的指控加到了他头上。她不能当面说他偷了遗嘱，不能指控他犯了重罪；可她却用了当时涌到嘴边的那种激烈说法，让他明白：他在她眼里的地位，同一名重罪犯可以占的地位一般无二。而她说这话的时候，他正竭力向她履行那件被说成是他本分的事！如今他反过头来训斥她——在他再度竭力去尽这同一样本分的时候训斥她——一个受了这般奇耻大辱的男人，出此一言，原是再自然不过的！

她恨他，鄙夷他，心里认定他有罪。她依旧相信他是有罪的。他若无罪，额头上就不会沁出一颗颗汗珠；就不会一阵一阵地忽然涨红、忽然发白；她逼视他的脸的时候，他也不会在她目光底下发颤。他若无罪，就不至于下作到这步田地。然而——如今她的激愤平歇了，凭着她那双看得分明的眼力，她看清了这一点——她原本无权当面指控他。他若有罪，自该由别人去查明，由别人去指控。身为淑女，身为伯父的侄女，她本该在伯父的宅子里，把他当作伯父的继承人来接纳。没有任何本分能强迫她去爱他，也没有任何本分要求她连他的友情都一并领受。可她明白，自己出言羞辱他，是失了检点。她羞愧的是：她没能把心中的情绪收在自己高贵的胸怀里，反倒让他以为，她动怒是因为失掉了伯父的财富。打定了这个主意，她便给他写下这样一封信：

===Original===

> My dear Henry,
> Do not take any further steps about the money, as I am quite determined not to accept it. I hope it will not be sent, as there would only be the trouble of repaying it. I do not think that it would do for me to live at Llanfeare, as I should have no means of supporting myself, let alone the servants. The thing is of course out of the question. You tell me that I ought to be ashamed of myself for certain words that I spoke to you. They should not have been spoken. I am ashamed of myself, and I now send you my apology.
> Yours truly,
> Isabel Brodrick.

===Chinese===

> 我亲爱的亨利：
> 那笔钱的事，请不要再往下进行了，因为我已拿定主意决不接受。但愿不要把钱送来，送来也只是白费一番退还的周折。我看我住到兰菲尔去并不合适，因为我没有法子养活自己，更不用说那些仆人了。这件事自然是谈不上的。你对我说，我该为自己对你讲过的某些话害臊。那些话本不该讲。我为自己害臊，现在给你送去我的道歉。
> 你的忠实的，
> 伊莎贝尔·布罗德里克

===Original===

The reader may perhaps understand that these words were written by her with extreme anguish; but of that her Cousin Henry understood nothing.

===Chinese===

读者或许能够明白，这些字句是她怀着极度的痛苦写下的；这一点，她的亨利表哥却丝毫不懂。

## II Isabel Brodrick / 第二章 伊莎贝尔·布罗德里克
===Original===
When Mr. Indefer Jones spoke of living for two years, he spoke more hopefully of himself than the doctor was wont to speak to Isabel. The doctor from Carmarthen visited Llanfeare twice a week, and having become intimate and confidential with Isabel, had told her that the candle had nearly burnt itself down to the socket. There was no special disease, but he was a worn-out old man. It was well that he should allow himself to be driven out about the place every day. It was well that he should be encouraged to get up after breakfast, and to eat his dinner in the middle of the day after his old fashion. It was well to do everything around him as though he were not a confirmed invalid. But the doctor thought that he would not last long. The candle, as the doctor said, had nearly burnt itself out in the socket.

And yet there was no apparent decay in the old man’s intellect. He had never been much given to literary pursuits, but that which he had always done he did still. A daily copy of whatever might be the most thoroughly Conservative paper of the day he always read carefully from the beginning to the end; and a weekly copy of the *Guardian* nearly filled up the hours which were devoted to study. On Sunday he read two sermons through, having been forbidden by the doctor to take his place in the church because of the draughts, and thinking, apparently, that it would be mean and wrong to make that an excuse for shirking an onerous duty. An hour a day was devoted by him religiously to the Bible. The rest of his time was occupied by the care of his property. Nothing gratified him so much as the coming in of one of his tenants, all of whom were so intimately known to him that, old as he was, he never forgot the names even of their children. The idea of raising a rent was abominable to him. Around the house there were about two hundred acres which he was supposed to farm. On these some half-dozen worn-out old labourers were maintained in such a manner that no return from the land was ever forthcoming. On this subject he would endure remonstrance from no one—not even from Isabel.

Such as he has been here described, he would have been a happy old man during these last half-dozen years, had not his mind been exercised day by day, and hour by hour, by these cares as to the property which were ever present to him. A more loving heart than his could hardly be found in a human bosom, and all its power of love had been bestowed on Isabel. Nor could any man be subject to a stronger feeling of duty than that which pervaded him; and this feeling of duty induced him to declare to himself that in reference to his property he was bound to do that which was demanded of him by the established custom of his order. In this way he had become an unhappy man, troubled by conflicting feelings, and was now, as he was approaching the hour of his final departure, tormented by the thought that he would leave his niece without sufficient provision for her wants.

===Chinese===
因德弗·琼斯先生（Mr. Indefer Jones）说自己还能活上两年，这话说得比医生平日对伊莎贝尔（Isabel）讲的要乐观得多。卡马森（Carmarthen）来的医生每周到兰菲尔（Llanfeare）来两趟，同伊莎贝尔处得亲厚，无话不谈，便对她说了实话：那支蜡烛已经快要烧到烛座了。老人并没有什么特别的病症，只是一个油尽灯枯的暮年人。由着人天天驾车载他在田庄上转一转，是好事；鼓励他吃过早饭就起身，照老习惯晌午用正餐，是好事；凡他身边的事都照旧张罗，只当他不是个缠绵病榻的病人，也是好事。可是医生心里明白，他拖不了多久了。用医生的话说，那支蜡烛眼看就要在烛座里燃尽了。

然而老人的心智并不见丝毫衰颓。他向来算不得潜心文学之人，可一贯做的事如今依旧在做。当日哪一份日报立场最是彻底保守，他便天天读那一份，回回从头到尾读得仔细；每周一期的《卫报》（the Guardian），则差不多填满了他拨给研读的时辰。到了礼拜日，他要把两篇布道文从头到尾读完——医生因堂内有过堂风，不许他到教堂里落座，而他显然认为，拿这个做由头躲开一桩费力的本分，是卑琐而不应当的。每天他雷打不动地拿一个钟头读《圣经》，其余的时光都用在料理产业上。最叫他心满意足的，莫过于哪个佃户进门来；佃户们他个个知根知底，人虽这样老了，连他们孩子的名字也从不忘。涨租的念头，在他是可憎的。宅子周围约有二百英亩地算是他名下耕种的，就在这地上养着五六个衰迈的老雇工，土地年年出不了什么进项。在这个话题上，谁的劝谏他都听不进——连伊莎贝尔的也不听。

倘不是心里日日夜夜、时时刻刻被这份产业上的心事搅扰着——那心事无时无刻不摆在他眼前——单以方才描写的这副样子，这末了六七年他本该是个快活的老人。人间胸膛里再难找出一颗比他更多情的心，而那颗心全部的爱的力量，又尽数倾注在了伊莎贝尔身上。也难有哪个人比他更深地受着责任感的支配；正是这责任感教他对自己认定：产业的事，他必须照他这一门第相沿的成规所要求的那样去办。就这样他成了个不快活的人，为相互冲突的心绪所苦；如今大限之日一天天迫近，他更被一个念头煎熬着：他即将撒手而去，留下侄女，而她日后的用度却没有足够的着落。

===Original===
But the thing was done. The new will was executed and tied in on the top of the bundle which contained the other wills which he had made. Then, naturally enough, there came back upon him the idea, hardly amounting to a hope, that something might even yet occur to set matters right by a marriage between the cousins. Isabel had spoken to him so strongly on the subject that he did not dare to repeat his request. And yet, he thought, there was no good reason why they two should not become man and wife. Henry, as far as he could learn, had given up his bad courses. The man was not evil to the eye, a somewhat cold-looking man rather than otherwise, tall with well-formed features, with light hair and blue-grey eyes, not subject to be spoken of as being unlike a gentleman, if not noticeable as being like one. That inability of his to look one in the face when he was speaking had not struck the Squire forcibly as it had done Isabel. He would not have been agreeable to the Squire had there been no bond between them—would still have been the reverse, as he had been formerly, but for that connection. But, as things were, there was room for an attempt at love; and if for an attempt at love on his part, why not also on Isabel’s? But he did not dare to bid Isabel even to try to love this cousin.

“I think I would like to have him down again soon,” he said to his niece.

“By all means. The more the tenants know him the better it will be. I can go to Hereford at any time.”

“Why should you run away from me?”

“Not from you, Uncle Indefer, but from him.”

===Chinese===
可事情终究做了。新遗嘱已经签立，捆在那一包他历年所立遗嘱的最上面。此后，说来也是顺理成章，一个念头又回到了他心上——那念头勉强算不得指望——就是或许还会出点什么机缘，叫表兄妹两个结了亲，事情便圆满了。伊莎贝尔在这件事上话说得那样决绝，他不敢再把请求提出口。然而在他想来，这两人结为夫妇，并没有什么说不通的地方。据他所知，亨利（Henry）已经改掉了那些不轨的行径。这人瞧着并不面目可憎，毋宁说眉眼间偏于冷淡，个子高，五官端正，头发浅淡，眼睛灰蓝，说不上有哪里惹人注目地像位绅士，却也说不上会落人口实、被人讲他不像绅士。他同人说话时不敢正眼看人的那个毛病，落在乡绅（the Squire）眼里，远不像落在伊莎贝尔眼里那样扎眼。若不是两人之间有这层亲缘，此人原不会叫乡绅看着顺眼——即便如今，若不是有这份干系，乡绅也仍会像先前一样对他心存嫌恶。可眼下情势如此，便有试着相爱的余地；他那边既可试着去爱，伊莎贝尔这边又何尝不可？但他连叫伊莎贝尔试着去爱一爱这位表哥的话，都不敢出口。

“我看，想叫他不久就再下来一趟。”他对侄女说。

“尽管叫他来。佃户们跟他认得越熟越好。我什么时候去赫里福德（Hereford）都行。”

“你干吗要躲开我？”

“不是躲您，因德弗伯父，是躲他。”

===Original===
“And why from him?”

“Because I don’t love him.”

“Must you always run away from the people you do not love?”

“Yes, when the people, or person, is a man, and when the man has been told that he ought specially to love me.”

When she said this she looked into her uncle’s face, smiling indeed, but still asking a serious question. He dared to make no answer, but by his face he told the truth. He had declared his wishes to his nephew.

===Chinese===
“那又为什么要躲他？”

“因为我不爱他。”

“凡是你不爱的人，你就非得躲开不成？”

“是的——要是那许多人、或者说那一个人，是个男人，而这男人又被人交代过，说他理应格外地爱我。”

说这话时她直望着伯父的脸，脸上固然带着笑，问的却是一句正经话。他不敢作答，可那张脸已经把实情说了出来：他已把自己的心愿向侄儿挑明了。

===Original===
“Not that I mean to be in the least afraid of him,” she continued. “Perhaps it will be better that I should see him, and if he speaks to me have it out with him. How long would he stay?”

“A month, I suppose. He can come for a month.”

“Then I’ll stay for the first week. I must go to Hereford before the summer is over. Shall I write to him?” Then it was settled as she had proposed. She wrote all her uncle’s letters, even to her cousin Henry, unless there was, by chance, something very special to be communicated. On the present occasion she sent the invitation as follows:—

===Chinese===
“倒也不是说我就有半分怕他，”她接着说，“或许见见他反倒好些，他若跟我开口，我就同他把话说个明白。他能住多久？”

“我想是一个月。他可以来住一个月。”

“那我头一个礼拜留下来。夏天过完之前我总得去一趟赫里福德。要不要我写信给他？”事情便照她的提议定了下来。伯父的信向来全由她代笔，连写给亨利表哥（Cousin Henry）的也不例外，除非偶然有什么顶要紧的话要说。这一回，邀请信便照下面这样发了去：——

===Original===
> My dear Henry—Your uncle wants you to come here on the 1st July and stay for a month. The 1st of July will be Monday. Do not travel on a Sunday as you did last time, because he does not like it. I shall be here the first part of the time, and then I shall go to Hereford. It is in the middle of the summer only that I can leave him. Your affectionate cousin,

> Isabel Brodrick.

===Chinese===
> 我亲爱的亨利——你伯父要你七月一日到这里来，住上一个月。七月一日是礼拜一。别像上回那样在礼拜日赶路，他不喜欢这样。头一段日子我会在这儿，过后我就去赫里福德。只有盛夏这一段，我才离得开他。你亲爱的表妹，

> 伊莎贝尔·布罗德里克

===Original===
She had often felt herself compelled to sign herself to him in that way, and it had gone much against the grain with her; but to a cousin it was the ordinary thing, as it is to call any different man “My dear sir,” though he be not in the least dear. And so she had reconciled herself to the falsehood.

Another incident in Isabel’s life must be told to the reader. It was her custom to go to Hereford at least once a year, and there to remain at her father’s house for a month. These visits had been made annually since she had lived at Llanfeare, and in this way she had become known to many of the Hereford people. Among others who had thus become her friends there was a young clergyman, William Owen, a minor canon attached to the cathedral, who during her last visit had asked her to be his wife. At that time she had supposed herself to be her uncle’s heiress, and looking at herself as the future owner of Llanfeare had considered herself bound to regard such an offer in reference to her future duties and to the obedience which she owed to her uncle. She never told her lover, not did she ever quite tell herself, that she would certainly accept him if bound by no such considerations; but we may tell the reader that it was so. Had she felt herself to be altogether free, she would have given herself to the man who had offered her his love. As it was she answered him anything but hopefully, saying nothing of any passion of her own, speaking of herself as though she were altogether at the disposal of her uncle. “He has decided now,” she said, “that when he is gone the property is to be mine.” The minor canon, who had heard nothing of this, drew himself up as though about to declare in his pride that he had not intended to ask for the hand of the lady of Llanfeare. “That would make no difference in me,” she continued, reading plainly the expression in the young man’s face. “My regard would be swayed neither one way nor the other by any feeling of that kind. But as he has chosen to make me his daughter, I must obey him as his daughter. It is not probable that he will consent to such a marriage.”

Then there had been nothing further between them till Isabel, on her return to Llanfeare, had written to him to say that her uncle had decided against the marriage, and that his decision was final.

===Chinese===
她常常不得不在落款处对他这样署名，心里老大不情愿；可对一个表哥，这本是寻常事，正如对一个全不相干的男人也称一声“我亲爱的先生”，虽然半点也谈不上亲。于是她也就让自己认下了这份言不由衷。
