伊莎贝尔生活里的另一桩事，也须向读者交代。她惯常每年至少去一趟赫里福德，在父亲家里住上一个月。自她住到兰菲尔以来，这类探访年年不断，赫里福德城里由此也多有认得她的人。在这些因她做客而结识的朋友中间，有一位年轻牧师，威廉·欧文（William Owen），是附属大教堂的小法政牧师（minor canon）；她上回小住的时候，他曾求她嫁给他。那时她自以为是伯父的继承人，把自己看作兰菲尔日后的主人，便认定自己有义务，既要顾到自己日后的职责，又要顾到她对伯父应有的服从，来权衡这样一桩求婚。她从不曾告诉她的情人——也从不曾对自己全然说破——若没有这些牵绊，她一定会答应他；但我们可以告诉读者，事实正是如此。倘若她觉得自己全然自由，她本会把自己交给这个向她献上爱情的人。而实际上她给他的答复却全无指望的意味，对自己的情意只字不提，说起自己来，倒仿佛她整个的人全凭伯父做主。“如今他已经定了，”她说，“他走之后，产业归我。”那位小法政牧师对此一无所闻，登时挺直了身子，像是就要满心骄傲地宣称：他求的本来就不是兰菲尔的女主人。“那对我毫无影响，”她接着说，一眼把这年轻人脸上的神色看得明明白白，“我心里的情意，绝不会被那一种念头拨向这边，也不会被拨向那边。可他既拣中了我做他的女儿，我就得以女儿的身份服从他。他要肯应允这样一门亲事，那是不大可能的。”

此后两人之间再没有进一步的话，直到伊莎贝尔回到兰菲尔，写信告诉他：伯父已经否了这桩婚事，而且一言定局。

===Original===
Now in all this Isabel had certainly been hardly used, though her ill-usage had in part been due to her own reticence as to her own feelings. When she told the Squire that the offer had been made to her, she did so as if she herself had been almost indifferent.

“William Owen!” the Squire had said, repeating the name; “his grandfather kept the inn at Pembroke!”

“I believe he did,” said Isabel calmly.

“And you would wish to make him owner of Llanfeare?”

“I did not say so,” rejoined Isabel. “I have told you what occurred, and have asked you what you thought.”

Then the Squire shook his head, and there was an end of it. The letter was written to the minor canon telling him that the Squire’s decision was final.

===Chinese===
在这整件事里，伊莎贝尔无疑受了亏待，尽管这亏待有一部分要归因于她对自己心迹的缄默。她向乡绅说起有人向她求婚时，那口气便仿佛她自己近乎无动于衷。

“威廉·欧文！”乡绅当时把名字重复了一遍，“他祖父在彭布罗克（Pembroke）开客栈！”

“我想是开过的，”伊莎贝尔回得平平静静。

“那你就想叫他来做兰菲尔的主人？”

“我没有这样说，”伊莎贝尔答道，“我把发生的事告诉了你，问的是你怎么想。”

乡绅于是摇了摇头，这件事便就此了结。信写到小法政牧师那里，告诉他乡绅的决定不容更改。

===Original===
In all this there had been no allusion to love on the part of Isabel. Had there been, her uncle could hardly have pressed upon her the claims of his nephew. But her manner in regard to the young clergyman had been so cold as to leave upon her uncle an impression that the matter was one of but little moment. To Isabel it was matter of infinite moment. And yet when she was asked again and again to arrange all the difficulties of the family by marrying her cousin, she was forced to carry on the conversation as though no such person existed as her lover at Hereford.

And yet the Squire remembered it all—remembered that when he had thus positively objected to the grandson of the innkeeper, he had done so because he had felt it to be his duty to keep the grandson of an innkeeper out of Llanfeare. That the grandson of old Thomas Owen, of the Pembroke Lion, should reign at Llanfeare in the place of an Indefer Jones had been abominable to him. To prevent that had certainly been within his duties. But it was very different now, when he would leave his girl poorly provided for, without a friend and without a roof of her own over her head! And yet, though her name was Brodrick, she, too, was a Jones; and her father, though an attorney, had come of a family nearly as good as his own. In no case could it be right that she should marry the grandson of old Thomas Owen. Therefore, hitherto, he had never again referred to that proposal of marriage. Should she again have spoken of it his answer might perhaps have been less decided; but neither had she again spoken of the clergyman.

All this was hard upon Isabel, who, if she said nothing, still thought of her lover. And it must be acknowledged also that though she did not speak, still she thought of her future prospects. She had laughed at the idea of being solicitous as to her inheritance. She had done so in order that she might thereby lessen the trouble of her uncle’s mind; but she knew as well as did another the difference between the position which had been promised her as owner of Llanfeare, and that to which she would be reduced as the stepdaughter of a stepmother who did not love her. She knew, too, that she had been cold to William Owen, giving him no sort of encouragement, having seemed to declare to him that she had rejected him because she was her uncle’s heiress. And she knew also—or thought that she knew—that she was not possessed of those feminine gifts which probably might make a man constant under difficulties. No more had been heard of William Owen during the last nine months. Every now and then a letter would come to her from one of her younger sisters, who now had their own anxieties and their own loves, but not a word was there in one of them of William Owen. Therefore, it may be said that the last charge in her uncle’s purpose had fallen upon her with peculiar hardness.

===Chinese===
这整件事里，伊莎贝尔对自己的爱情只字未提。倘或提了，伯父也很难再把侄儿的指望压到她头上。可她对待那位年轻牧师的态度那样冷淡，以致在伯父心里留下的印象是：这件事无关紧要。对伊莎贝尔，这却是一件干系无穷的大事。然而，当人家一次又一次要她嫁给表哥、好把一家子的难处都安排妥帖的时候，她只得把话谈下去，仿佛赫里福德那个做她情人的人压根儿不存在。

然而乡绅把这一切都记在心里——记得他当日那样断然回绝那个客栈老板的孙子，是因为他觉得，把一个客栈老板的孙子挡在兰菲尔之外，是他的本分。让老托马斯·欧文（old Thomas Owen）——“彭布罗克狮”（the Pembroke Lion）客栈的老板——的孙子坐上兰菲尔，顶替一个因德弗·琼斯，这在他是可憎的。阻止这件事，确在他的本分之内。可如今情势全然不同了：他将撇下他的姑娘，用度微薄，没有朋友，头顶上没有一片自家的屋檐！然而，她虽说姓布罗德里克（Brodrick），却也是琼斯家的人；她父亲虽是个律师，门第却几乎同他自家一样好。无论如何，叫她去嫁老托马斯·欧文的孙子，总归是不对的。因此，直到如今，他再没提过那桩求婚。倘若她再提起，他的答复或许不至于那样决绝；可她那边，也再没提起过那位牧师。

这一切对伊莎贝尔是沉重的；她嘴上不说，心里却仍惦着她的情人。而且也须承认，她虽然不说，心里也同样惦着自己的前程。把计较遗产的事当作可笑，她是笑过的；她那样笑，为的是好宽一宽伯父心上的愁烦；可她自己同旁人一样清楚，做兰菲尔的主人——那是许给她的位置——同做一个不爱她的继母的继女、被贬到那等位置上去，中间隔着多远。她心里也明白，自己待威廉·欧文是冷淡的，没给过他半点鼓励，倒像是向他言明了：她回绝他，正因为她是伯父的继承人。她还知道——或者自以为知道——自己身上并没有那样一种女人的资质，多半能在困厄之中教一个男人守定不移。这九个月里，威廉·欧文再没有一点音信。她那些妹妹们倒不时来信——她们如今也各有各的忧愁、各人的情爱——可信里没有一个字提到威廉·欧文。因此可以说，伯父心意里这最后一项安排，落在她身上是格外地沉重。

===Original===
But she never uttered a complaint, or even looked one. As for utterance there was no one to whom she could have spoken it. There had never been many words between her and her own family as to the inheritance. As she had been reticent to her father so had he to her. The idea in the attorney’s house at Hereford was that she was stubborn, conceited, and disdainful. It may be that in regard to her stepmother there was something of this, but, let that be as it might, there had been but little confidence between them as to matters at Llanfeare. It was, no doubt, supposed by her father that she was to be her uncle’s heir.

Conceited, perhaps, she was as to certain gifts of character. She did believe herself to be strong of purpose and capable of endurance. But in some respects she was humble enough. She gave herself no credit for feminine charms such as the world loves. In appearance she was one calculated to attract attention—somewhat tall, well set on her limbs, active, and of good figure; her brow was broad and fine, her grey eyes were bright and full of intelligence, her nose and mouth were well formed, and there was not a mean feature in her face. But there was withal a certain roughness about her, an absence of feminine softness in her complexion, which, to tell the truth of her, was more conspicuous to her own eyes than to any others. The farmers and their wives about the place would declare that Miss Isabel was the finest young woman in South Wales. With the farmers and their wives she was on excellent terms, knowing all their ways, and anxious as to all their wants. With the gentry around she concerned herself but little. Her uncle’s habits were not adapted to the keeping of much company, and to her uncle’s habits she had fitted herself altogether. It was on this account that neither did she know the young men around, nor did they know her. And then, because no such intimacies had grown up she told herself that she was unlike other girls—that she was rough, unattractive, and unpopular.

Then the day came for the arrival of Henry Jones, during the approach to which Uncle Indefer had, from day to day, become more and more uneasy. Isabel had ceased to say a word against him. When he had been proposed to her as a lover she had declared that she had loathed him. Now that suggestion had been abandoned, or left in abeyance. Therefore she dealt with his name and with his coming as she might with that of any other guest. She looked to his room, and asked questions as to his comfort. Would it not be well to provide a separate dinner for him, seeing that three o’clock would be regarded as an awkward hour by a man from London? “If he doesn’t like it, he had better go back to London,” said the old Squire in anger. But the anger was not intended against his girl, but against the man who by the mere force of his birth was creating such a sea of troubles.

===Chinese===
可她从不曾口出一句怨言，连神色上也从不曾露出一丝。说到出口，本也没有一个可以向之倾诉的人。遗产的事，她与自家家里人之间向来说不上几句话。她对父亲守口如瓶，父亲对她也一样。赫里福德那律师家里的想法是：这姑娘又倔又自负，还眼高于顶。对继母，或许确有几分如此；但无论怎样，兰菲尔的事情上，她们之间向无多少体己话。她父亲呢，无疑认定她将来是伯父的继承人。

自负，论到性格上的某几种资质，她或许确是自负的。她当真相信自己意志坚定，也经得起磨。可在另一些方面，她却谦卑得很。世人所爱的那种女人的妩媚，她从不认为自己有分毫。论相貌，她本是惹人注目的一路——身材略高，四肢匀称，矫健，体态也好；前额宽阔而秀美，灰色的眼睛明亮，满含灵气，鼻子和嘴都生得端正，脸上没有一处小家气的轮廓。然而她周身总带着几分粗直，脸色间缺一段女性的柔润；说句实话，这一点在她自己眼里，倒比在旁人眼里更显眼。田庄左近的农户和他们的主妇都会说，伊莎贝尔小姐是南威尔士（South Wales）顶标致的姑娘。同这些农户人家，她相处得极好，他们的营生她样样在行，他们的难处她样样挂心。左近的士绅人家，她却不大来往。伯父的习惯本不宜多留宾客，而她对伯父的习惯是全盘迁就的。就为这个，左近的年轻人她不认得，人家也不认得她。于是，只因这些亲近的交情不曾生长起来，她便对自己说：她同别的姑娘不一样——她粗，不入眼，也不讨人喜欢。

随后，亨利·琼斯（Henry Jones）到达的日子到了；这日子一天天逼近，因德弗伯父也一天比一天坐立不安。伊莎贝尔已不再说他一个字的不是。当初有人把他向她荐作夫婿时，她曾声言自己厌恶他。如今那提议已经作罢，或是搁置起来了。于是她提他的名字、张罗他的到来，就像对待任何别的客人一样。她照看他的房间，问起他的起居可有什么要安排。要不要单独给他开一餐饭？伦敦来的人，看着三点钟开饭总觉得别扭。“他要是看不惯，顶好回伦敦去。”老乡绅没好气地说。可这气不是冲着他的姑娘，是冲着那个单凭出身的分量就搅起这一片风波的人。

===Original===
“I have told you what my intentions are,” the Squire said to his nephew on the evening of his arrival.

“I am sure that I am very much obliged to you, my dear uncle.”

“You need not be in the least obliged to me. I have done what I conceive to be a duty. I can still change it if I find that you do not deserve it. As for Isabel, she deserves everything that can be done for her. Isabel has never given me the slightest cause for displeasure. I doubt whether there is a better creature in the world living than Isabel. She deserves everything. But as you are the male heir, I think it right that you should follow me in the property—unless you show yourself to be unworthy.”

===Chinese===
“我的打算，我已经对你交代过了。”侄儿到达的那天晚上，乡绅对他说。

“我真是感激不尽，我亲爱的伯父。”

“你一点也不必感激我。我做的是我认定的本分。倘若我发现你不配，我仍旧可以更改。至于伊莎贝尔，凡能为她做的，她都当得起。伊莎贝尔从不曾给过我半点不快的缘由。我看这世上活着的人里头，未必找得出比伊莎贝尔更好的了。她当得起一切。可你既是男嗣继承人，我想你继我承下这份产业才是正理——除非你显出不配来。”

===Original===
This was certainly a greeting hard to be endured—a speech very difficult to answer. Nevertheless it was satisfactory, if only the old Squire would not again change his mind. The young man had thought much about it, and had come to the resolution that the best way to insure the good things promised him would be to induce Isabel to be his wife.

“I’m sure she is all that you say, Uncle Indefer,” he replied.

Uncle Indefer grunted, and told him that if he wanted any supper, he had better go and get it.

===Chinese===
这样的迎接，当然叫人难堪——这样的话，也叫人难以作答。然而只要老乡绅不再变卦，这番话还是叫人称心的。年轻人对此已经想了许久，拿定了主意：要保牢许给他的那些好处，最稳妥的一着，莫过于劝得伊莎贝尔做他的妻子。

“我相信她全然如您所说，因德弗伯父。”他答道。

因德弗伯父哼了一声，告诉他要是想吃夜宵，就趁早去取。

## XXIII Isabel’s Petition / 第二十三章 伊莎贝尔的请愿

===Original===

The news was soon all about Carmarthen. A new will had been found, in accordance with which Miss Brodrick was to become owner of Llanfeare, and—which was of more importance to Carmarthen at the present moment—there was to be no trial! The story, as told publicly, was as follows;—Mr. Apjohn, by his sagacity, had found the will. It had been concealed in a volume of sermons, and Mr. Apjohn, remembering suddenly that the old man had been reading these sermons shortly before his death, had gone at once to the book. There the will had been discovered, which had at once been admitted to be a true and formal document by the unhappy pseudo-proprietor. Henry Jones had acknowledged his cousin to be the heiress, and under these circumstances had conceived it to be useless to go on with the trial. Such was the story told, and Mr. Apjohn, fully aware that the story went very lame on one leg, did his best to remedy the default by explaining that it would be unreasonable to expect that a man should come into court and undergo an examination by Mr. Cheekey just when he had lost a fine property.

“Of course I know all that,” said Mr. Apjohn when the editor of the paper remarked to him that the libel, if a libel, would be just as much a libel whether Mr. Henry Jones were or were not the owner of Llanfeare. “Of course I know all that; but you are hardly to expect that a man is to come and assert himself amidst a cloud of difficulties when he has just undergone such a misfortune as that! You have had your fling, and are not to be punished for it. That ought to satisfy you.”

“And who’ll pay all the expenses?” asked Mr. Evans.

“Well,” said Mr. Apjohn, scratching his head; “you, of course, will have to pay nothing. Geary will settle all that with me. That poor devil at Llanfeare ought to pay.”

===Chinese===

这消息不多时便传遍了卡马森。一份新的遗嘱找到了，按这份遗嘱，布罗德里克小姐将成为兰菲尔的主人；而——这在眼下的卡马森更关紧要——官司竟不打了！外间公开流传的说法是这样：阿普约翰先生凭他的精明找到了遗嘱。遗嘱藏在一卷布道文里，阿普约翰先生猛然想起，老人故去前不久还在读这几篇布道文，便径直去翻那本书。遗嘱就在那里寻见了，那位不幸的伪业主当即承认它是真确而合乎程式的文书。亨利·琼斯承认了表妹是女继承人，事已如此，便觉得官司再打下去毫无用处。外间讲的就是这样一套，而阿普约翰先生心里全然有数，这故事有一条腿是瘸的，便竭力想补上这个缺陷，解释说：一个人刚刚丢了一份好产业，便指望他立刻到法庭上受奇基先生的盘问，这未免不通情理。

报馆的主编向他指出：这桩诽谤案，倘若算得上诽谤，那么亨利·琼斯先生是不是兰菲尔的主人，诽谤都一样是诽谤。阿普约翰先生答道：“这我自然明白；这我自然明白；可你总不能指望一个人刚遭了那样的祸事，就叫他顶着满天乌云去自证身份！你们已经痛快骂了一场，又不会为此受罚。这总该满意了吧。”

“那么这一大笔开销由谁来付？”埃文斯先生问。

“呃，”阿普约翰先生搔着头皮说，“你当然是一个子儿也不必出。吉尔里（Geary）会同我结清的。兰菲尔那位可怜虫理应付账。”

===Original===

“He won’t have the money.”

“I, at any rate, will make it all right with Geary; so that needn’t trouble you.”

This question as to the expense was much discussed by others in Carmarthen. Who in truth would pay the complicated lawyers’ bill which must have been occasioned, including all these flys out to Llanfeare? In spite of Mr. Apjohn’s good-natured explanations, the public of Carmarthen was quite convinced that Henry Jones had in truth hidden the will. If so, he ought not only to be made to pay for everything, but be sent to prison also and tried for felony. The opinion concerning Cousin Henry in Carmarthen on the Thursday and Friday was very severe indeed. Had he shown himself in the town, he would almost have been pulled in pieces. To kill him and to sell his carcase for what it might fetch towards lessening the expenses which he had incurred would not be too bad for him. Mr. Apjohn was, of course, the hero of the hour, and, as far as Carmarthen could see, Mr. Apjohn would have to pay the bill. All this, spoken as it was by many mouths, reached Mr. Brodrick’s ears, and induced him to say a word or two to Mr. Apjohn.

===Chinese===

“他拿不出这个钱。”

“反正吉尔里那一头，我会料理妥当；这个就不劳你操心了。”

这桩开销问题，卡马森另有旁人议论纷纷。这一准招出来的那笔头绪繁多的律师账单，连同一趟趟开往兰菲尔的轻便马车钱，究竟谁来真付？任凭阿普约翰先生好性儿地解说，卡马森的公众还是笃信不疑：那份遗嘱确实是亨利·琼斯藏起来的。果真如此，那就不单该叫他包赔一切，还该送进大牢，以重罪论处。星期四、星期五两天，卡马森对亨利表哥的舆论真是严厉到了极点。他若敢在城里露面，险些要被众人撕成碎片。就是把他宰了，把尸首卖掉，看能换回几个钱，好冲抵他招下的开销，这也不算对他太过分。阿普约翰先生自然成了时下的英雄；照卡马森人看，账单到头来得由阿普约翰先生来付。这些话众口相传，一齐灌进了布罗德里克先生的耳朵，促使他找阿普约翰先生说了三两句。

===Original===

“This affair,” said he, “will of course become a charge upon the property?”

“What affair?”

“This trial which is not to take place, and the rest of it.”

“The trial will have nothing to do with the estate,” said Mr. Apjohn.

“It has everything to do with it. I only mention it now to let you know that, as Isabel’s father, I shall make it my business to look after that.”

“The truth is, Brodrick,” said the Carmarthen attorney, with that gleam of triumph in his eye which had been so often seen there since the will had tumbled out of the volume of sermons in the book-room, “the whole of this matter has been such a pleasure to me that I don’t care a straw about the costs. If I paid for it all from beginning to end out of my own pocket, I should have had my whack for my money. Perhaps Miss Isabel will recompense me by letting me make her will some day.”

===Chinese===

“这桩事情，”他说，“自然要成为设定在产业上的一笔负担喽？”

“哪桩事情？”

“这场不打了的官司，还有连带着的那些。”

“官司同产业毫不相干，”阿普约翰先生说。

“干系大得很。我如今提这一句，不过叫你知道：作为伊莎贝尔的父亲，照管这件事，我责无旁贷。”

“实话对你说吧，布罗德里克，”这位卡马森律师说，眼里闪着那股得意的光——自打遗嘱从书房那卷布道文里掉出来，这光在他眼里已经闪过多少回了——“这一整桩事于我是天大的乐事，费用我一根草也不放在心上。倘若从头到尾都由我自己掏腰包，那这笔钱也算花得痛快十足了。说不定哪一天，伊莎贝尔小姐肯让我替她立一份遗嘱，就算是对我的报答了。”

===Original===

Such were the feelings and such were the words spoken at Carmarthen; and it need only be said further, in regard to Carmarthen, that the operations necessary for proving the later will and annulling the former one, for dispossessing Cousin Henry and for putting Isabel into the full fruition of all her honours, went on as quickly as it could be effected by the concentrated energy of Mr. Apjohn and all his clerks.

Cousin Henry, to whom we may be now allowed to bid farewell, was permitted to remain within the seclusion of the house at Llanfeare till his signature had been obtained to the last necessary document. No one spoke a word to him; no one came to see him. If there were intruders about the place anxious to catch a glimpse of the pseudo-Squire, they were disappointed.

Mrs. Griffith, under the attorney’s instructions, was more courteous to him than she had been when he was her master. She endeavoured to get him things nice to eat, trying to console him by titbits. None of the tenants appeared before him, nor was there a rough word spoken to him, even by young Cantor.

In all this Cousin Henry did feel some consolation, and was greatly comforted when he heard from the office in London that his stool at the desk was still kept open for him.

===Chinese===

卡马森流行的便是这样的心绪、这样的言语；至于卡马森，此外只须再说一句：验定后立的遗嘱、撤销先立的遗嘱，褫夺亨利表哥，把伊莎贝尔扶上她的全部尊荣、让她圆满受享——这些必要的手续，在阿普约翰先生和他全班助手合力一心地推动下，能办多快，便办多快。

亨利表哥——如今我们可以容许自己向他道别了——得蒙容许，继续幽居在兰菲尔的宅子里，直到最后一份必要文书上的签字弄到手为止。没有一个人同他讲一句话；没有一个人来看他。倘若左近有些闯扰的人，急着一睹那位伪乡绅的尊容，他们也只落得扫兴而归。

格里菲斯太太奉了律师的吩咐，待他比他做她主人的时候还要客气几分。她设法给他弄些好吃的，想拿几样可口的点心来宽他的心。没有一个佃户到他跟前露过面，也没有一句粗话落到他头上，连年轻坎托都没有。

这一切里头，亨利表哥倒是觉出了几分慰藉；等接到伦敦写字楼的通知，说他那张写字台前的凳子还给他留着，他更是大觉宽心。

===Original===

The Carmarthen Herald, in its final allusion to the state of things at Llanfeare, simply declared that the proper will had been found at last, and that Miss Isabel Brodrick was to be restored to her rights. Guided by this statement, the directors in London were contented to regard their clerk as having been unfortunate rather than guilty.

For the man himself, the reader, it is hoped, will feel some compassion. He had been dragged away from London by false hopes. After so great an injury as that inflicted on him by the last change in the Squire’s purpose it was hardly unnatural that the idea of retaliation should present itself to him when the opportunity came in his way. Not to do that which justice demands is so much easier to the conscience than to commit a deed which is palpably fraudulent! At the last his conscience saved him, and Mr. Apjohn will perhaps be thought to have been right in declaring that much was due to him in that he had not destroyed the will. His forbearance was recompensed fully.

As soon as the money could be raised on the property, the full sum of £4,000 was paid to him, that having been the amount with which the Squire had intended to burden the property on behalf of his niece when he was minded to put her out of the inheritance.

It may be added that, notorious as the whole affair was at Carmarthen, but little of Cousin Henry’s wicked doings were known up in London.

===Chinese===

《卡马森先驱报》最后一次提及兰菲尔的局面，只是宣称：那真正合式的遗嘱终于找到了，伊莎贝尔·布罗德里克小姐将恢复她的权利。凭着这一说法，伦敦的董事们便心满意足地把他们的雇员看作时运不济，而非行止有亏。

至于这个人本身，还望读者存几分恻隐。他是被虚妄的指望拽离伦敦的。乡绅最后一回改变心意，给他造成了那样深重的亏损；待到机会凑手而来，报复的念头涌上他的心头，这也很难说多么违背人情。良心要宽宥“没有去做公义所要求的事”，远比宽宥一桩明摆着的欺诈来得容易！到头来，他的良心救了他；阿普约翰先生宣称，他没有毁掉那遗嘱，单凭这一节就该给他记上一大功——人们或许会认为，这话并没有说错。他的隐忍得到了十足的报偿。

产业上能够筹起银钱了，便把四千镑的整数如数付给了他；当初乡绅有意把侄女摒出继承之外时，原就打算在产业上为她设定这样一笔负担。

还可以添上一句：这桩事尽管在卡马森闹得沸沸扬扬，亨利表哥的种种恶行传到伦敦去的，却寥寥无几。

===Original===

We must now go back to Hereford. By agreement between the two lawyers, no tidings of her good fortune were at once sent to Isabel. “There is so many a slip ’twixt the cup and the lip,” said Mr. Apjohn to her father. But early in the following week Mr. Brodrick himself took the news home with him.

“My dear,” he said to her as soon as he found himself alone with her—having given her intimation that an announcement of great importance was to be made to her—“it turns out that after all your Uncle Indefer did make another will.”

“I was always quite sure of that, papa.”

“How were you sure?”

“He told me so, papa.”

“He told you so! I never heard that before.”

===Chinese===

现在我们得回到赫里福德去。两位律师商量妥了，她的好运，消息先不急着传给伊莎贝尔。“杯到嘴边，还难免失手，”阿普约翰先生对她父亲说。可是到了第二个礼拜的头上，布罗德里克先生亲自把消息带回了家。

“我的好孩子，”他一寻着同她单独相处的空当便对她说——此前他已向她递过话，说有一件极要紧的事要向她宣布——“原来闹了半天，你因德弗伯父到底还是另立过一份遗嘱。”

“这个我一向十拿九准，爸爸。”

“你怎么就十拿九准了？”

“他跟我说过，爸爸。”

“他跟你说过！这话我可从来没有听见过。”

===Original===

“He did—when he was dying. What was the use of talking of it? But has it been found?”

“It was concealed within a book in the library. As soon as the necessary deeds can be executed Llanfeare will be your own. It is precisely word for word the same as that which he had made before he sent for your cousin Henry.”

“Then Henry has not destroyed it?”

“No, he did not destroy it.”

“Nor hid it where we could not find it?”

“Nor did he hide it.”

===Chinese===

“他说过的——在他临终的时候。这有什么可说的呢？可遗嘱找着了吗？”

“藏在图书室的一本书里。等必要的契据一办妥，兰菲尔就是你的了。它同他派人来请你亨利表哥之前立的那一份，一字一句，分毫不差。”

“这么说，亨利没有把它毁掉？”

“没有，他没有毁掉。”

“也没有把它藏到我们找不着的地方？”

“他也没有藏。”

===Original===

“Oh, how I have wronged him;—how I have injured him!”

“About that we need say nothing, Isabel. You have not injured him. But we may let all that pass away. The fact remains that you are the heiress of Llanfeare.”

Of course he did by degrees explain to her all the circumstances—how the will had been found and not revealed, and how far Cousin Henry had sinned in the matter; but it was agreed between them that no further evil should be said in the family as to their unfortunate relative. The great injury which he might have done to them he had abstained from doing.

“Papa,” she said to her father when they were again together alone that same evening, “you must tell all this to Mr. Owen. You must tell him everything, just as you have told me.”

===Chinese===

“哦，我多冤枉了他啊；——我多亏待了他啊！”

“这个就不必说了，伊莎贝尔。你没有亏待他。那些且都由它过去。事实摆着：你就是兰菲尔的女继承人。”

当然，那些前前后后的情形，他逐步都向她讲明了——遗嘱怎样找到又怎样隐而不宣，亨利表哥在这桩事里头作孽作到了哪一步；但父女俩也说定了：家里头，对那位不幸的亲戚，不再多说一句坏话。他本可能加在他们身上的大祸，他终究收手没有加。

“爸爸，”当天晚上父女俩又单独相处时，她对父亲说，“这一切你得讲给欧文先生听。要样样都讲，就像讲给我听的这样。”

===Original===

“Certainly, my dear, if you wish it.”

“I do wish it.”

“Why should you not have the pleasure of telling him yourself?”

“It would not be a pleasure, and therefore I will get you to do it. My pleasure, if there be any pleasure in it, must come afterwards. I want him to know it before I see him myself.”

“He will be sure to have some stupid notion,” said her father, smiling.

“I want him to have his notion, whether it be stupid or otherwise, before I see him. If you do not mind, papa, going to him as soon as possible, I shall be obliged to you.”

===Chinese===

“自然照办，我的好孩子，只要你愿意。”

“我愿意。”

“这等乐子，你干吗不自己去享，亲自告诉他？”

“那算不得乐子，所以我才要劳你去讲。我的乐子——倘若这里头真有什么乐子——得往后才有。我要在自己见他之前，先让他知道。”

“他保准又要生什么死心眼的傻念头，”她父亲笑着说。

“我要在我见他之前，让他先抱着他的念头——傻也好，不傻也好。爸爸，倘若你不嫌麻烦，请尽早到他那儿去一趟，我就领你的情了。”

===Original===

Isabel, when she found herself alone, had her triumph also. She was far from being dead to the delights of her inheritance. There had been a period in her life in which she had regarded it as her certain destiny to be the possessor of Llanfeare, and she had been proud of the promised position. The tenants had known her as the future owner of the acres which they cultivated, and had entertained for her and shown to her much genuine love. She had made herself acquainted with every homestead, landmark, and field about the place. She had learnt the wants of the poor, and the requirements of the little school. Everything at Llanfeare had had an interest for her. Then had come that sudden change in her uncle’s feelings—that new idea of duty—and she had borne it like a heroine. Not only had she never said a word of reproach to him, but she had sworn to herself that even in her own heart she would throw no blame upon him. A great blow had come upon her, but she had taken it as though it had come from the hand of the Almighty—as it might have been had she lost her eyesight, or been struck with palsy. She promised herself that it should be so, and she had had strength to be as good as her word. She had roused herself instantly from the effect of the blow, and, after a day of consideration, had been as capable as ever to do the work of her life. Then had come her uncle’s last sickness, those spoken but doubtful words, her uncle’s death, and that conviction that her cousin was a felon. Then she had been unhappy, and had found it difficult to stand up bravely against misfortune. Added to this had been her stepmother’s taunts and her father’s distress at the resolution she had taken. The home to which she had returned had been thoroughly unhappy to her. And there had been her stern purpose not to give her hand to the man who loved her and whom she so dearly loved! She was sure of her purpose, and yet she was altogether discontented with herself. She was sure that she would hold by her purpose, and yet she feared that her purpose was wrong. She had refused the man when she was rich, and her pride would not let her go to him now that she was poor. She was sure of her purpose—but yet she almost knew that her pride was wrong.

But now there would be a triumph. Her eyes gleamed brightly as she thought of the way in which she would achieve her triumph. Her eyes gleamed very brightly as she felt sure within her own bosom that she would succeed. Yes: he would, no doubt, have some stupid notion, as her father said. But she would overcome his stupidity. She, as a woman, could be stronger than he as a man. He had almost ridiculed her obstinacy, swearing that he would certainly overcome it. There should be no ridicule on her part, but she would certainly overcome his obstinacy.

For a day or two Mr. Owen was not seen. She heard from her father that the tidings had been told to her lover, but she heard no more. Mr. Owen did not show himself at the house; and she, indeed, hardly expected that he should do so. Her stepmother suddenly became gracious—having no difficulty in explaining that she did so because of the altered position of things.

“My dearest Isabel, it does make such a difference!” she said; “you will be a rich lady, and will never have to think about the price of shoes.” The sisters were equally plainspoken, and were almost awestruck in their admiration.

===Chinese===

伊莎贝尔独自一人的时候，也有她的凯旋。她对继承产业的那份欢欣，远非无动于衷。生命中曾有一段时期，她把做兰菲尔的主人看作自己注定的归宿，并以那许给她的地位为荣。佃户们早就认下了她是他们所耕田亩未来的主人，对她怀有、也向她表露过许多真心的敬爱。庄子上的每一处宅院、每一道界标、每一块田地，她都一一熟识。穷人的需求、那所小学校的种种要务，她都记在心里。兰菲尔的一切，于她无不津津有味。后来，伯父的心意陡然一变——那套新的责任观念冒了出来——她却像个女中豪杰一般承受住了。她不但从未对他说过一句怨怼的话，还对自己立下誓言：即便在自己心里，也决不归咎于他。一记重击落到她身上，她却把它当作出自全能之主的手来领受——仿佛她瞎了双眼，或是中了风瘫，也未尝不可这样领受。她许诺自己要这样做，而且有力量说到做到。她当即从这一击的震荡中振作起来，只消一日思量，便同往常一样，担得起自己一生的职分。再往后，是伯父最后那场病，那些说出口却存疑不定的话，伯父的死，还有她心中认定的那件事：她表哥是个重罪犯。到那时她才真正不快活起来，觉得要昂然直面厄运，实在艰难。叠加上来的，还有继母的冷嘲热讽，还有父亲对她所下决心的痛心。她回到的那个家，于她是一团彻头彻尾的不快。还有她那铁一般的决心：不肯把自己许给那个爱她、也叫她爱得至深的人！她深信自己的决心，却又对自己浑身别扭。她深信自己守得住这决心，却又害怕这决心本身是个错。她富的时候回绝了那个人，如今穷了，她的骄傲又不许她奔到他跟前去。她深信自己的决心——可她心里几乎明白，她的骄傲是个错。

可如今要有一场凯旋了。想到自己将怎样赢下这场凯旋，她的双眼亮闪闪的。当她暗自确信必能成功时，她的双眼亮得更是灼人。是的：毫无疑问，正如她父亲所说，他会有某种死心眼的傻念头。但她要把他的傻气压服。她身为女人，能强过他身为男人。他曾几乎讥笑过她的执拗，赌咒发誓说定要把它压服。她这边决口不讥笑，可她也定要把他的执拗压服。

有一两天不见欧文先生的影儿。她从父亲那里听说，消息已经报给了她的情人，此后便再没有下文。欧文先生没有登门；说实话，她也几乎没指望他来。继母忽然和颜悦色起来——要解释原委毫不费力：境况变了，她的脸色也就变了。

“我最亲爱的伊莎贝尔，这一下可大不一样了！”她说；“你要做阔小姐了，往后再也不必去想一双鞋要多少钱。”姐妹们说话同样直白，叹服之中几乎带上了敬畏。

===Original===

Three or four days after the return of Mr. Brodrick, Isabel took her bonnet and shawl, and walked away all alone to Mr. Owen’s lodgings. She knew his habits, and was aware that he was generally to be found at home for an hour before his dinner. It was no time, she said to herself, to stand upon little punctilios. There had been too much between them to let there be any question of a girl going after her lover. She was going after her lover, and she didn’t care who knew it. Nevertheless, there was a blush beneath her veil as she asked at the door whether Mr. Owen was at home. Mr. Owen was at home, and she was shown at once into his parlour.

“William,” she said;—throughout their intimacy she had never called him William before;—“you have heard my news?”

“Yes,” he said, “I have heard it;”—very seriously, with none of that provoking smile with which he had hitherto responded to all her assertions.

“And you have not come to congratulate me?”

“I should have done so. I do own that I have been wrong.”

===Chinese===

布罗德里克先生回来后过了三四天，伊莎贝尔戴上帽子、披上披肩，独自一人步行到欧文先生的寓所去。她熟知他的习惯，知道开晚饭前的一个钟头，他多半在家。她对自己说，如今可不是拘泥小节、讲究客套的时候了。两人之间经过的事太多了，姑娘去追自己的情人，根本不成其为问题。她就是去追自己的情人，谁爱知道谁知道。然而，她在门口问欧文先生是否在家时，面纱底下还是飞起了一片红晕。欧文先生在家，她随即被引进了他的客厅。

“威廉，”她说——两人相识到如今，她还从来没有叫过他威廉——“我的消息你听说了？”

“听说了，”他说——神情十分严肃，全无他往日听她宣言时那种撩人的笑意。

“那你还不来恭贺我？”

“我本该来的。我承认，是我不对。”

===Original===

“Wrong;—very wrong! How was I to have any of the enjoyment of my restored rights unless you came to enjoy them with me?”

“They can be nothing to me, Isabel.”

“They shall be everything to you, sir.”

“No, my dear.”

“They are to be everything to me, and they can be nothing to me without you. You know that, I suppose?” Then she waited for his reply. “You know that, do you not? You know what I feel about that, I say. Why do you not tell me? Have you any doubt?”

===Chinese===

“不对；——很不对！你不来同我一道享用，我这份恢复过来的权利，还叫我怎么享它的乐处？”

“它们于我毫无干系，伊莎贝尔。”

“它们于你就是一切，先生。”

“不，亲爱的。”

“它们于我就是一切，而没有你，它们于我便什么都不是。这个你知道，我想？”接着她等他回答。“你知道的，是不是？我说，你知道我对这事怀着怎样的心思。你怎么不说？你心里可有疑虑？”

===Original===

“Things have been unkind to us, Isabel, and have separated us.”

“Nothing shall separate us.” Then she paused for a moment. She had thought of it all, and now had to pause before she could execute her purpose. She had got her plan ready, but it required some courage, some steadying of herself to the work before she could do it. Then she came close to him—close up to him, looking into his face as he stood over her, not moving his feet, but almost retreating with his body from her close presence. “William,” she said, “take me in your arms and kiss me. How often have you asked me during the last month! Now I have come for it.”

He paused a moment as though it were possible to refuse, as though his collected thoughts and settled courage might enable him so to outrage her in her petition. Then he broke down, and took her in his arms, and pressed her to his bosom, and kissed her lips, and her forehead, and her cheeks—while she, having once achieved her purpose, attempted in vain to escape from his long embrace.

===Chinese===

“世情待我们刻薄，伊莎贝尔，把我们拆散了。”

“什么都休想拆散我们。”说到这里她停了片刻。这一切她早已想好，可临到要实行自己的打算，还是得先顿一顿。主意她早就备下了，只是要拿出几分勇气，要先把心神稳住，才做得出来。随后她凑近他——紧贴到他跟前，仰脸望进他的脸。他站在她面前，双脚一动不动，身子却几乎朝后避着她贴过来的这一团热气。“威廉，”她说，“搂住我，吻我。上个月里你求过我多少回！如今我亲自来讨了。”

他顿了一顿，仿佛拒绝尚有可能，仿佛凭他敛住的思虑、定住的胆气，真还能这样辜负她这一番恳求。随后他垮了下来，把她搂进怀里，紧紧贴在胸前，吻她的唇，她的额头，她的双颊——而她，既已得偿所愿，便想从他绵长的拥抱里挣脱出来，却是枉然。

===Original===

“Now I shall be your wife,” she said at last, when her breath had returned to her.

“It should not be so.”

“Not after that? Will you dare to say so to me—after that? You could never hold up your head again. Say that you are happy? Tell me that you are happy. Do you think that I can be happy unless you are happy with me?” Of course he gave her all the assurances that were needed, and made it quite unnecessary that she should renew her prayer.

“And I beg, Mr. Owen, that for the future you will come to me, and not make me come to you.” This she said as she was taking her leave. “It was very disagreeable, and very wrong, and will be talked about ever so much. Nothing but my determination to have my own way could have made me do it.” Of course he promised her that there should be no occasion for her again to put herself to the same inconvenience.

===Chinese===

“如今我要做你的妻子了，”她终于缓过气来，说道。

“事情本不该如此。”

“事到如今，还说不？事到如今，你还敢对我讲这话？你从此再也抬不起头来了。说你快活。告诉我你快活。你若不同我一道快活，你想我一个人快活得起来？”他自然把该给的许诺一样样都给了，叫她全然不必再开口重求。

“还有，欧文先生，往后请你来见我，别再叫我上你这儿来。”这是她临别时说的。“方才这事情很不体面，也大不应该，往后少不得被人说上许久。若不是打定主意非要遂自己的心意不可，我才做不出来呢。”他自然应承她：往后断不会再叫她受这同样的委屈。

## VII Looking for the Will / 第七章 寻找遗嘱

===Original===
The search was carried on up to nine o’clock that evening, and then Mr. Apjohn returned to Carmarthen, explaining that he would send out two men to continue the work on the Tuesday, and that he would come out again on the Wednesday to read whatever might then be regarded as the old Squire’s will—the last prepared document if it could be found, and the former one should the search have been unsuccessful. “Of course,” said he, in the presence of the two cousins, “my reading the document will give it no force. Of those found, the last in date will be good—until one later be found. It will be well, however, that some steps should be taken, and nothing can be done till the will has been read.” Then he took his leave and went back to Carmarthen.

Isabel had not shown herself during the whole of the afternoon. When Mr. Apjohn’s explanation had been given, and the search commenced, she retired and went to her own room. It was impossible for her to take a part in the work that was being done, and almost equally impossible for her to remain without seeming to take too lively an interest in the proceeding. Every point of the affair was clear to her imagination. It could not now be doubted by her that her uncle, doubly actuated by the presence of the man he disliked and the absence of her whom he so dearly loved, had found himself driven to revoke the decision to which he had been brought. As she put it to herself, his love had got the better of his conscience during the weakness of his latter days. It was a pity—a pity that it should have been so! It was to be regretted that there should have been no one near him to comfort him in the misery which had produced such a lamentable result. A will, she thought, should be the outcome of a man’s strength, and not of his weakness. Having obeyed his conscience, he should have clung to his conscience. But all that could not affect what had been done. It seemed to be certain to her that this other will had been made and executed. Even though it should have been irregularly executed so as to be null and void, still it must for a time at least have had an existence. Where was it now? Having these thoughts in her mind, it was impossible for her to go about the house among those who were searching. It was impossible for her to encounter the tremulous misery of her cousin. That he should shiver and shake and be covered with beads of perspiration during a period of such intense perturbation did not seem to her to be unnatural. It was not his fault that he had not been endowed with especial manliness. She disliked him in his cowardice almost more than before; but she would not on that account allow herself to suspect him of a crime.

Mr. Apjohn, just before he went, had an interview with her in her own room.

===Chinese===
搜寻一直进行到那天晚上九点钟，随后阿普约翰先生（Mr. Apjohn）便回卡马森（Carmarthen）去了，临行说明：星期二他要派两个人来接着干这活儿，星期三他自己再跑一趟，宣读届时算作老乡绅遗嘱的那份文书——找得着，便是最后立下的那一份；搜寻若是落空，便是先前的那一份。“自然，”他当着两位表兄妹的面说，“我宣读这份文书，并不能使它生效。凡找出来的几份，以日期最晚的那份为有效——除非日后又找出更晚的来。不过，总得采取几个步骤才好，而在遗嘱宣读之前，什么也办不成。”说罢他便告辞，回卡马森去了。

整整一个下午，伊莎贝尔（Isabel）都没有露面。阿普约翰先生把情形交代明白、搜寻开始之后，她便退了出去，回自己房里去了。正在进行着的这桩活儿，她无法插手；可要她待在一旁袖手，又不免显得对这件事过于热心，这几乎同样办不到。事情的每一点，她的想象里都看得清清楚楚。如今在她心里已不容置疑：伯父嫌恶的那个人近在眼前，他疼爱至极的她又不在身边，两下里一齐催逼，他终于觉得自己不得不推翻原先拿定的那个主意。照她自己的说法，便是到了晚年衰弱之日，他的爱心压倒了他的良知。可惜——事情竟落到这般地步，真可惜！他身边竟没有一个人，在这场酿成如此可悲结果的心境愁苦之中给他安慰，想来令人痛惜。她想，遗嘱应当出自一个人的力量，而不该出自他的软弱。他既已听从了良知，就该把良知坚守到底。可这一切都改变不了既成的事。她觉得几乎可以断定：那份后立的遗嘱确已订立并签署过了。纵然它签署得不合规矩、全然无效，它至少总归存在过一时。如今它到哪里去了呢？心里装着这些念头，她没法在宅子里同那些搜寻的人一道转来转去，也没法去面对她表哥那抖个不住的悲苦。在他这样心神震荡到极点的时刻，他浑身战栗、满头汗珠，在她看来倒也不算反常。他天生不曾被赋予格外的丈夫气，这不是他的错。他这副怯懦之相，叫她比先前更加嫌恶；但她绝不因此便容许自己去疑心他犯了什么罪。

阿普约翰先生临走之前，还到她自己房里同她谈了一回。

===Original===
“I cannot go without a word,” he said, “but its only purport will be to tell you that I cannot as yet express any decided opinion in this matter.”

“Do not suppose, Mr. Apjohn, that I am anxious for another will,” she said.

“I am;—but that has nothing to do with it. That he did make a will, and have it witnessed by these two Cantors, is, I think, certain. That he should afterwards have destroyed the will without telling the witnesses, who would be sure hereafter to think and talk of what they had done, seems to be most unlike the thoughtful consideration of your uncle. But his weakness increased upon him very quickly just at that time. Dr. Powell thinks that he was certainly competent on that day to make a will, but he thinks also he may have destroyed it a day or two afterwards when his mind was hardly strong enough to enable him to judge of what he was doing. If, at last, this new will shall not be forthcoming, I think we must be bound to interpret the matter in that way. I tell you this before I go in order that it may assist you perhaps a little in forming your own opinion.” Then he went.

It was impossible but that she should bethink herself at that moment that she knew more than either Dr. Powell or Mr. Apjohn. The last expression of the old man’s thoughts upon that or upon any matter had been made to herself. The last words that he had uttered had been whispered into her ears; “It is all right. It is done.” Let the light of his failing intellect have been ever so dim, let his strength have faded from him ever so completely, he would not have whispered these words had he himself destroyed that last document. Mr. Apjohn had spoken of the opinion which she was to form, and she felt how impossible to her it would be not to have an opinion in the matter. She could not keep her mind vacant even if she would. Mr. Apjohn had said that, if the will were not found, he should think that the Squire had in his weakness again changed his mind and destroyed it. She was sure that this was not so. She, and she alone, had heard those last words. Was it or was it not her duty to tell Mr. Apjohn that such words had been uttered? Had they referred to the interest of anyone but herself, of course it would have been her duty. But now—now she doubted. She did not choose to seem even to put forth a claim on her own account. And of what use would be any revelation as to the uttering of these words? They would be accepted in no court of law as evidence in one direction or another. Upon the whole, she thought she would keep her peace regarding them, even to Mr. Apjohn. If it was to be that her cousin should live there as squire and owner of Llanfeare, why should she seek to damage his character by calling in question the will under which he would inherit the property? Thus she determined that she would speak of her uncle’s last words to no one.

===Chinese===
“我不留一句话就走不开，”他说，“不过这话只有一个用意：这件事，我眼下还拿不出什么确定的意见来。”

“阿普约翰先生，请别以为我一心巴望着另有一份遗嘱。”她说。

“我是巴望的；——不过这与眼下的道理不相干。他确实立了一份遗嘱，并请坎托（Cantor）两位先生做了见证，这一层我认为是确凿的。至于说他随后把遗嘱毁掉了，却不对见证人交代一声——这两人日后想起自己干过的事，是必定要去思量议论的——这与你伯父那深思熟虑的为人实在太不相像。可偏偏就在那前后，他的衰弱来得极快。鲍威尔医生（Dr. Powell）认为，那一天他立遗嘱的心神是确凿够用的；但鲍威尔医生也认为，过后一两天他也许就把它毁了，那时他的神智已不大够用，判断不出自己在做什么了。倘若到头来这份新遗嘱终究拿不出来，我想我们就只得照这个路子来解释这件事。我走之前把这番话告诉你，为的是也许能对你自己形成看法多少有点帮助。”说罢他便走了。

此刻她不由得想到，她知道的比鲍威尔医生和阿普约翰先生都多。老人关于这件事——也关于任何事——最后吐露的心思，是对她一个人说的。他最后说的话，是贴着她耳朵低声讲出来的：“全都妥了。事情办成了。”纵然他那日渐衰竭的智力之光再黯淡，纵然他的气力消磨得再干净，倘若那份最后的文书是他亲手毁掉的，这些话他决不会低声说出来。阿普约翰先生谈起她应当形成怎样的看法，而她觉得，这件事要叫她心里没有一个看法，是万万办不到的。纵然她愿意，她的心思也腾不空。阿普约翰先生说过，倘若遗嘱找不着，他便认为乡绅是在衰弱之中又一次变了卦，把它毁了。她确信实情并非如此。那几句遗言，她，唯有她一个人听过。把这番话曾经出口的事告诉阿普约翰先生，究竟是不是她的本分？倘若那些话关涉的是她自身以外的任何人的利益，那自然义不容辞。可如今——如今她迟疑了。她连显得像是在替自己提出什么主张都不愿意。再说，把这番话吐露出去又有什么用处？无论朝着哪一头，任何法庭都不会把它当作证据采信。总而言之，她决定对此守口如瓶，连对阿普约翰先生也是一样。倘若她表哥注定要住在这里，做兰菲尔（Llanfeare）的乡绅和主人，她何必去质疑他将据以继承产业的那份遗嘱，败坏他的名声呢？于是她拿定主意：伯父的遗言，她对谁也不提。

===Original===
But what must be her opinion as to the whole transaction? At the present moment she felt herself bound to think that this missing document would be found. That to her seemed to be the only solution which would not be terrible to contemplate. That other solution—of the destruction of the will by her uncle’s own hands—she altogether repudiated. If it were not found, then—! What then? Would it not then be evident that some fraud was being perpetrated? And if so, by whom? As these thoughts forced themselves upon her mind, she could not but think of that pallid face, those shaking hands, and the great drops of sweat which from time to time had forced themselves on to the man’s brow. It was natural that he should suffer. It was natural that he should be perturbed under the consciousness of the hostile feeling of all those around him. But yet there had hardly been occasion for all those signs of fear which she had found it impossible not to notice as she had sat there in the parlour while Mr. Apjohn was explaining the circumstances of the two wills. Would an innocent man have trembled like that because the circumstances around him were difficult? Could anything but guilt have betrayed itself by such emotions? And then, had the will in truth been made away with by human hands, what other hands could have done it? Who else was interested? Who else was there at Llanfeare not interested in the preservation of a will which would have left the property to her? She did not begrudge him the estate. She had acknowledged the strength of the reasons which had induced the Squire to name him as heir; but she declared to herself that, if that latter document were not found, a deed of hideous darkness would have been perpetrated by him. With these thoughts disturbing her breast she lay awake during the long hours of the night.

When Mr. Apjohn had taken his departure, and the servants had gone to their beds, the butler having barred and double-barred the door after his usual manner, Cousin Henry still sat alone in the book-room. After answering those questions from Mr. Apjohn, he had spoken to no one, but still sat alone with a single candle burning on the table by his elbow. The butler had gone to him twice, asking him whether he wanted anything, and suggesting to him that he had better go to his bed. But the heir, if he was the heir, had only resented the intrusion, desiring that he might be left alone. Then he was left alone, and there he sat.

His mind at this moment was tormented grievously within him. There was a something which he might do, and a something which he might not do, if he could only make up his mind. “Honesty is the best policy!” “Honesty is the best policy!” He repeated the well-known words to himself a thousand times, without, however, moving his lips or forming a sound. There he sat, thinking it all out, trying to think it out. There he sat, still trembling, still in an agony, for hour after hour. At one time he had fully resolved to do that by which he would have proved to himself his conviction that honesty is the best policy, and then he sat doubting again—declaring to himself that honesty itself did not require him to do this meditated deed. “Let them find it,” he said to himself at last, aloud. “Let them find it. It is their business: not mine.” But still he sat looking up at the row of books opposite to him.

===Chinese===
可对整桩事情，她究竟必须怎么看？眼下她觉得自己不得不认定，那份失踪的文书终会被找出来。在她看来，唯有这个解答想起来还不至于叫人胆寒。另一个解答——遗嘱毁于她伯父亲的之手——她是全然拒斥的。倘若找不出来，那么——！再往下呢？那时岂不明显是有谁在干着欺诈的勾当？果真如此，又是谁干的？这些念头逼上心头，她便不能不想起那张灰白的脸，那双发抖的手，还有不时从那人额头上沁出的大颗汗珠。他受苦，是自然的。他自觉到周围人人怀着敌意，因而心神不宁，也是自然的。可是，她坐在客厅里听阿普约翰先生讲明两份遗嘱的种种情形时，那些叫她想不留意都不成的惧怕之状，只怕也未必样样都有来由。一个清白的人，会因为身周处境艰难，就抖成那副模样吗？除了负疚，还有什么能在这样的心绪里自己败露出来？再说，倘若那份遗嘱当真遭了人手，除了他这双手又能是谁的手？旁的还有谁有利害牵连？兰菲尔还有哪一个人，会不巴望那份要把产业留给她的遗嘱完好无缺？那份产业她并不眼红。乡绅之所以指名他做继承人，那些道理何等有力，她自认是承认过的；可她对自己立言：倘若那后一份文书找不出来，那便是他干下了一桩骇人听闻的黑暗勾当。怀着这些搅得心口不宁的念头，她睁着眼睛熬过了长夜漫漫的好几个钟头。

阿普约翰先生去后，仆人们也都就寝，男管家照平日的规矩把门上了闩、又加了双闩，亨利表哥（Cousin Henry）却还独自坐在书房里。自答过阿普约翰先生的那些问话之后，他没再同任何人交谈，只管一个人坐着，手肘边的桌上点着一支蜡烛。男管家进去看过他两回，问他可要什么东西，又劝他不如上床安歇。可这位继承人——倘若他真是继承人的话——只嫌人家打搅，一心要人撇下他。于是人家真撇下了他，他便坐在那里。

这一刻，他的心里受着狠命的煎熬。有一样事他做得，另一样事他做不得——只要他拿得定主意就好。“诚实是上策！”“诚实是上策！”他把这句人人皆知的话在心里念了一千遍，嘴唇却一动不动，也不曾吐出一点声音。他坐在那儿，想把这一切想个透，竭力想个透。他坐在那儿，仍旧发着抖，仍旧在苦痛之中，一个钟头接着一个钟头。有一阵子他打定了十足的主意，要做那件足以向自己证明“诚实是上策”的事；转眼他又坐在那儿疑虑起来——对自己申辩说，单讲诚实本身，并不要求他去做这桩盘算中的事。“让他们找去，”末了他高声对自己说，“让他们找去。那是他们的事，不是我的。”可他仍旧坐在那儿，抬眼望着对面那一排书。

===Original===
When it was considerably after midnight, he got up from his chair and began to walk the room. As he did so, he wiped his brow continually as though he were hot with the exertion, but keeping his eye still fixed upon the books. He was urging himself, pressing upon himself the expression of that honesty. Then at last he rushed at one of the shelves, and, picking out a volume of Jeremy Taylor’s works, threw it upon the table. It was the volume on which the old Squire had been engaged when he read the last sermon which was to prepare him for a flight to a better world. He opened the book, and there between the leaves was the last will and testament which his uncle had executed.

At that moment he heard a step in the hall and a hand on the door, and as he did so with quick eager motion he hid the document under the book.

“It is near two o’clock, Mr. Henry,” said the butler. “What are you doing up so late?”

“I am only reading,” said the heir.

“It is very late to be reading. You had better go to bed. He never liked people to be a-reading at these contrairy hours. He liked folk to be all abed.”

===Chinese===
过了半夜好大一截，他从椅子上立起身来，开始在屋里踱步。踱着，他不停地揩额头，仿佛因为这般用力而浑身发热，眼睛却始终钉在那几排书上。他在催促自己，逼着自己把那份诚实表出来。末了他终于扑向一格书架，抽出一卷杰里米·泰勒（Jeremy Taylor）的布道文集，摔在桌上。正是这一卷：老乡绅临终诵读那篇为他飞升更美世界作预备的最后布道文时，手上翻弄的就是它。他翻开书——书页之间，夹着他伯父签署的最后一份遗嘱。

就在这一刻，他听见门厅里响起脚步声，一只手搭上了门。说时迟那时快，他赶忙以急疾的动作把那份文书压到了书底下。

“快两点了，亨利先生，”男管家说，“这么晚不睡，您在干什么呀？”

“我不过看看书，”继承人说。

“看书看到这个时辰，太晚了。您还是上床去吧。他老人家从不喜欢人家在这种颠来倒去不是时辰的钟点看书。他就喜欢人人都好好地在床上睡着。”

===Original===
The use of a dead man’s authority, employed against him by one who was, so to say, his own servant, struck even him as absurd and improper. He felt that he must assert himself unless he meant to sink lower and lower in the estimation of all those around him. “I shall stay just as late as I please,” he said. “Go away, and do not disturb me any more.”

“His will ought to be obeyed, and he not twenty-four hours under the ground,” said the butler.

“I should have stayed up just as long as I had pleased even had he been here,” said Cousin Henry. Then the man with a murmur took his departure and closed the door after him.

For some minutes Cousin Henry sat perfectly motionless, and then he got up very softly, very silently, and tried the door. It was closed, and it was the only door leading into the room. And the windows were barred with shutters. He looked round and satisfied himself that certainly no other eye was there but his own. Then he took the document up from its hiding-place, placed it again exactly between the leaves which had before enclosed it, and carefully restored the book to its place on the shelf.

===Chinese===
死人的权威，竟由一个按说算是他自家仆人的人拿来压他，这事连他也觉得荒唐失体。他觉出，他非立起自己的身份不可，不然他在周围所有人心目中还要一沉再沉。“我爱待到多晚就多晚，”他说，“去吧，别再来搅扰我。”

“他的遗愿就该遵着办，他人这才下土不到二十四个钟头呢，”男管家说。

“就算他老人家还在，我爱熬到多晚也照熬不误。”亨利表哥说。那人咕哝了一声，退了出去，随手带上了门。

好几分钟的工夫，亨利表哥坐着一动不动；随后他极轻极静地立起身来，去试那扇门。门关着，而通进这间屋的也只有这一扇门。窗户也都上了窗板。他四下里看了一圈，确信屋里除自己这双眼睛外再没有旁人的眼睛。于是他把文书从藏处取出，重新端端正正夹回原先夹着它的那两页之间，又小心翼翼地把那本书放回架上原位。

===Original===
He had not hidden the will. He had not thus kept it away from the eyes of all those concerned. He had opened no drawer. He had extracted nothing, had concealed nothing. He had merely carried the book from his uncle’s table where he had found it, and, in restoring it to its place on the shelves, had found the paper which it contained. So he told himself now, and so he had told himself a thousand times. Was it his duty to produce the evidence of a gross injustice against himself? Who could doubt the injustice who knew that he had been summoned thither from London to take his place at Llanfeare as heir to the property? Would not the ill done against him be much greater than any he would do were he to leave the paper there where he had chanced to find it?

In no moment had it seemed to him that he himself had sinned in the matter, till Mr. Apjohn had asked him whether his uncle had told him of this new will. Then he had lied. His uncle had told him of his intention before the will was executed, and had told him again, when the Cantors had gone, that the thing was done. The old man had expressed a thousand regrets, but the young one had remained impassive, sullen, crushed with a feeling of the injury done to him, but still silent. He had not dared to remonstrate, and had found himself unable to complain of the injustice.

There it was in his power. He was quite awake to the strength of his own position—but also to its weakness. Should he resolve to leave the document enclosed within the cover of the book, no one could accuse him of dishonesty. He had not placed it there. He had not hidden it. He had done nothing. The confusion occasioned by the absence of the will would have been due to the carelessness of a worn-out old man who had reached the time of life in which he was unfit to execute such a deed. It seemed to him that all justice, all honesty, all sense of right and wrong, would be best served by the everlasting concealment of such a document. Why should he tell of its hiding-place? Let them who wanted it search for it, and find it if they could. Was he not doing much in the cause of honesty in that he did not destroy it, as would be so easy for him?

===Chinese===
他没有藏遗嘱。他没有把遗嘱从一切有关之人的眼皮底下挪开。他没有开过一只抽屉。他没抽走一页纸，没藏起一样东西。他不过是把那本书从他发现它的伯父桌上拿起来，在把它放回架上原位的时候，发现了夹在里头的那张纸。此刻他这样对自己说，从前也已这样对自己说过一千遍。把一桩苛待自己的大不义的证据亲手呈递出来，难道是他的本分？他从伦敦被召到这里来，在兰菲尔接下产业继承人的名分——知道这情形的人，谁还能怀疑那桩不义？他若把那张纸留在偶然翻见的地方不管，那么加在他身上的冤屈，岂不远远大过他这样行将造下的任何冤屈？

在这件事上，他从不曾有一刻觉得自己有罪——直到阿普约翰先生问他，伯父可曾把这份新遗嘱告诉过他。那一下他说了谎。伯父在遗嘱签署之前，把自己的意向告诉过他；坎托两位先生走了以后，又告诉他事情已经办妥。老人表过千般的歉憾，年轻人却始终木然阴沉，被自己身受的委屈压垮了，却仍旧一声不响。他不敢抗争，也开不了口去埋怨那桩不公。

那东西就握在他的手心里。他对自己这步棋的份量看得一清二楚——对它的虚弱同样看得一清二楚。他若决意让那份文书照旧封在书壳里，谁也不能指责他不诚实。不是他放进去的。不是他藏的。他什么也没做。遗嘱下落不明引起的纷乱，只能归咎于一个心力交瘁的老人——他已活到了那样的岁数，本就不宜办这样的事了。在他看来，一切公道，一切诚实，一切是非之感，最能成全它们的莫过于让这样一份文书永远不见天日。它的藏处，他凭什么非说出去不可？谁想要，谁来找，找得着算他们本事。他本可以那么容易地把它毁掉，而他并没有毁——这难道不是在诚实的功德里出了一大份力么？

===Original===
But, if left there, would it not certainly be found? Though it should remain week after week, month after month—even should it remain year after year, would it not certainly be found at last, and brought out to prove that Llanfeare was not his own? Of what use to him would be the property—of what service;—how would it contribute to his happiness or his welfare, knowing, as he would know, that a casual accident, almost sure to happen sooner or later, might rob him of it forever? His imagination was strong enough to depict the misery to him which such a state of things would produce. How he would quiver when any stray visitor might enter the room! How terrified he would be at the chance assiduity of a housemaid! How should he act if the religious instincts of some future wife should teach her to follow out that reading which his uncle had cultivated?

He had more than once resolved that he would be mad were he to leave the document where he found it. He must make it known to those who were searching for it—or he must destroy it. His common sense told him that one alternative or the other must be chosen. He could certainly destroy it, and no one would be the wiser. He could reduce it, in the solitude of his chamber, into almost impalpable ashes, and then swallow them. He felt that, let suspicion come as it might into the minds of men, let Apjohn, and Powell, and the farmers—let Isabel herself—think what they might, no one would dare to accuse him of such a deed. Let them accuse him as they might, there would be no tittle of evidence against him.

But he could not do it. The more he thought of it, the more he had to acknowledge that he was incapable of executing such a deed. To burn the morsel of paper;—oh, how easy! But yet he knew that his hands would refuse to employ themselves on such a work. He had already given it up in despair; and, having told himself that it was impossible, had resolved to extricate the document and, calling Isabel up from her bed in the middle of the night, to hand it over to her at once. It would have been easy to say he had opened one book after another, and it would, he thought, be a deed grand to do. Then he had been interrupted, and insulted by the butler, and in his anger he had determined that the paper should rest there yet another day.

===Chinese===
可它若一直搁在那里，难道终归不会被人找到？哪怕一周一周、一月一月地搁下去——哪怕一年一年地搁下去，到头来难道不会终于被人翻出来，证明兰菲尔并不是他的？明知——他必定明知——一桩偶然的变故，十有八九迟早要来，便可能把这一切从他手里永远夺走，那这份产业于他还有什么用——还有什么益处；——又能给他的幸福安乐添上几分？他的想象足够有力，早已把这般处境将要带给他的苦况描画分明。随便哪个不速之客走进这间屋来，他该怎样发颤！哪个女仆偶然勤快一回，他该怎样魂飞魄散！将来娶下的妻子若是敬神成性，循着伯父生前培植的那番诵读接着读下去，他又当如何自处？

他不止一次下定决心：那份文书若还留在发现它的地方，他便是疯了。他必须把它晓谕那些搜寻的人——不然就必须毁掉它。常识告诉他，两条路必得择其一。他确乎能毁掉它，而且神不知鬼不觉。他可以在自己房里独自一人，把它烧成几乎捻不起来的灰烬，再把灰吞下肚去。他觉出，任凭人们心里起什么样的疑窦，任凭阿普约翰、鲍威尔和那些农户——任凭伊莎贝尔本人——爱怎么想怎么想，也没有谁敢指着他说干过这样的事。随他们怎么指控，压到他头上的罪证不会有分毫。

可他做不来。这事他越想，越不得不承认自己干不出这样的勾当。烧掉那一小片纸；——啊，多么容易！可他知道，他的两只手绝不肯去干这营生。他已在绝望中死了这条心；对自己说过了“办不到”，便决意把文书抽出来，半夜三更把伊莎贝尔从床上唤起来，立刻交到她手上。到那时只消说他是一本接一本地翻书翻出来的，这话出口并不难，而且在他想来，也算得一件堂堂皇皇的义举。偏偏这时候他叫人搅了，还受了男管家的一顿冲撞，一怒之下，他便断定那张纸且在那里再躺上一天。

## VI Mr. Apjohn’s Explanation / 第六章 阿普约翰的解释

===Original===
The reader need not be detained with any elaborate account of the funeral. Every tenant and every labourer about the place was there; as also were many of the people from Carmarthen. Llanfeare Church, which stands on a point of a little river just as it runs into a creek of the sea, is not more than four miles distant from the town; but such was the respect in which the old squire was held that a large crowd was present as the body was lowered into the vault. Then the lunch followed, just as Isabel had said. There was Cousin Henry, and there were the doctor and the lawyer, and there were the tenants who had been specially honoured by invitation, and there was Joseph Cantor the younger. The viands were eaten freely, though the occasion was not a happy one. Appetites are good even amidst grief, and the farmers of Llanfeare took their victuals and their wine in funereal silence, but not without enjoyment. Mr. Apjohn and Dr. Powell also were hungry, and being accustomed, perhaps, to such entertainments, did not allow the good things prepared to go waste. But Cousin Henry, though he made an attempt, could not swallow a morsel. He took a glass of wine, and then a second, helping himself from the bottle as it stood near at hand; but he ate nothing, and spoke hardly a word. At first he made some attempt, but his voice seemed to fail him. Not one of the farmers addressed a syllable to him. He had before the funeral taken each of them by the hand, but even then they had not spoken to him. They were rough of manner, little able to conceal their feelings; and he understood well from their bearing that he was odious to them. Now as he sat at table with them, he determined that as soon as this matter should be settled he would take himself away from Llanfeare, even though Llanfeare should belong to him. While they were at the table both the lawyer and the doctor said a word to him, making a struggle to be courteous, but after the first struggle the attempt ceased also with them. The silence of the man, and even the pallor of his face might be supposed to be excused by the nature of the occasion.

===Chinese===
葬礼的情形，不必再拿冗长的记述来耽搁读者。田庄上下每一个佃户、每一个雇工都到了场，卡马森（Carmarthen）城里来的也有好些人。兰菲尔（Llanfeare）教堂坐落在一道小河的尖端，河水恰在那里注入通海的一湾汊水，离城不足四英里；但老乡绅生前极受敬重，灵柩放入家族地穴时，到场的人群极为可观。随后便开午宴，一切如伊莎贝尔（Isabel）先前所说。亨利表哥（Cousin Henry）在座，医生和律师在座，蒙特邀而倍感荣宠的佃户们在座，年轻的约瑟夫·坎托（Joseph Cantor the younger）也在座。虽说场合并不欢愉，菜肴却吃得畅快。悲戚之中胃口照旧不误，兰菲尔的农场主们在丧礼一般的沉默里受用着酒饭，却并非没有滋味。阿普约翰先生（Mr. Apjohn）和鲍威尔医生（Dr. Powell）也都饿了，或许因为惯于这类应酬，不肯让备下的美馔白白糟蹋。唯独亨利表哥，虽也勉强一试，却一口也咽不下去。他饮了一杯酒，接着又饮一杯，自己伸手从近旁的瓶子里斟；但他什么也没吃，话也几乎一句不说。起初他还试着开口，嗓音却像是支不住了。农场主们没有一个向他吐过一个字。葬礼之前他曾一一握过他们的手，可即便那时他们也不曾与他搭话。这些人举止粗率，不大会掩饰自己的情感；他从他们的神色里看得明白，他们对他厌恶之极。此刻他与他们同席而坐，便打定主意：这件事一经了结，他就要离开兰菲尔，哪怕兰菲尔归他所有也在所不惜。席间，律师和医生都各自同他说过一句话，勉强撑着礼数；但这番挣扎一过，他们也就不再尝试了。这个人沉默不语，乃至他面色的苍白，都可以由这个场合作出开脱。

===Original===
“Now,” said Mr. Apjohn, rising from the table when the eating and drinking had ceased, “I think we might as well go into the next room. Miss Brodrick, who has consented to be present, will probably be waiting for us.”

They passed through the hall into the parlour in a long string, Mr. Apjohn leading the way, followed by Cousin Henry. There they found Isabel sitting with the housekeeper beside her. She shook hands in silence with the attorney, the doctor, and all the tenants, and then, as she took her seat, she spoke a word to Mr. Apjohn. “As I have felt it hard to be alone, I have asked Mrs. Griffith to remain with me. I hope it is not improper?”

“There can be no reason on earth,” said Mr. Apjohn, “why Mrs. Griffith should not hear the will of her master, who respected her so thoroughly.” Mrs. Griffith bobbed a curtsey in return for this civility, and then sat down, intently interested in the coming ceremony.

===Chinese===
“好了，”吃喝一停，阿普约翰先生便起身说道，“我看咱们不妨到隔壁屋里去。布罗德里克小姐（Miss Brodrick）已经答应到场，这会儿多半正在等我们。”

他们鱼贯穿过门厅，走进客厅，阿普约翰先生打头，亨利表哥跟在后面。只见伊莎贝尔坐在那里，格里菲斯太太（Mrs. Griffith）在她身旁。她默默与律师、医生以及众佃户一一握手，随后落座时向阿普约翰先生说了句话。“我一个人实在难捱，就请格里菲斯太太留在我身边。但愿这不失体统？”

“普天之下没有半点理由，”阿普约翰先生说，“可以不让格里菲斯太太听听她主人的遗嘱——她主人是那样敬重她的。”格里菲斯太太屈膝微微福了一福，答谢这份客套，随即坐下，全副心思都系在即将开始的仪式上。

===Original===
Mr. Apjohn took from his pocket the envelope containing the key, and, opening the little packet very slowly, very slowly opened the drawer, and took out from it a bundle of papers tied with red tape. This he undid, and then, sitting with the bundle loosened before him, he examined the document lying at the top. Then, slowly spreading them out, as though pausing over every operation with premeditated delay, he held in his hand that which he had at first taken; but he was in truth thinking of the words which he would have to use at the present moment. He had expected, but had expected with some doubt, that another document would have been found there. Close at his right hand sat Dr. Powell. Round the room, in distant chairs, were ranged the six farmers, each with his hat in hand between his knees. On a sofa opposite were Isabel and the housekeeper. Cousin Henry sat alone, not very far from the end of the sofa, almost in the middle of the room. As the operation went on, one of his hands quivered so much that he endeavoured to hold it with the other to keep it from shaking. It was impossible that anyone there should not observe his trepidation and too evident discomfort.

The document lying at the top of the bundle was opened out very slowly by the attorney, who smoothed it down with his hand preparatory to reading it. Then he looked at the date to assure himself that it was the last will which he himself had drawn. He knew it well, and was cognizant with its every legal quiddity. He could judiciously have explained every clause of it without reading a word, and might probably have to do so before the occasion was over; but he delayed, looking down upon it and still smoothing it, evidently taking another minute or two to collect his thoughts. This will now under his hand was very objectionable to him, having been made altogether in opposition to his own advice, and having thus created that “scolding” of which the Squire had complained to Isabel. This will bequeathed the whole of the property to Cousin Henry. It did also affect to leave a certain sum of money to Isabel, but the sum of money had been left simply as a sum of money, and not as a charge on the property. Now, within the last few days, Mr. Apjohn had learnt that there were no funds remaining for the payment of such a legacy. The will, therefore, was to him thoroughly distasteful. Should that will in truth be found to be the last will and testament of the old Squire, then it would be his duty to declare that the estate and everything upon it belonged to Cousin Henry, and that there would be, as he feared, no source from which any considerable part of the money nominally left to Miss Brodrick could be defrayed. To his thinking nothing could be more cruel, nothing more unjust, than this.

He had heard tidings which would make it his duty to question the authenticity of this will which was now under his hand; and now had come the moment in which he must explain all this.

===Chinese===
阿普约翰先生从口袋里取出那只装着钥匙的信封，极慢地拆开小纸包，又极慢地拉开抽屉，从中取出一束用红色细带捆扎的文件。他把带子解开，把那束文件摊松了摆在面前，端详最上面的那一份。随后他把文件缓缓展开，仿佛每一道手续都存心拖延，手里握着的仍是最先取出的那份；其实他满心想的是此刻非说不可的那番话。他原以为会在那里发现另一份文书，只是估摸得并没有多少把握。他的右手边紧挨着坐着鲍威尔医生。六位农场主围着屋子，远远地各坐一把椅子，人人手里捏着帽子，搁在两膝之间。对面的沙发上坐着伊莎贝尔和女管家。亨利表哥独自一人坐着，离沙发一端不远，差不多在屋子正当中。随着一道道手续进行下去，他的一只手抖得厉害，只得用另一只手把它攥住，不让它颤。在座的人不可能不看见他这副惶恐万状、局促不安的模样。

摊在文件束最上面的那份文书，被律师极其缓慢地展开，他用手把它抚平，以便宣读。随后他看了看日期，确认这正是他亲手起草的最后那份遗嘱。这份遗嘱他熟极了，其中每一条法律上的细微曲折他都了然于心。纵然一个字不读，他也能头头是道地讲解每一条款；而且不等这场合终了，多半还真得讲上一番。可他迟迟不动，低头望着它，手上把它抚了又抚，分明又花了一两分钟整理心思。眼下摊在他手下的这份遗嘱，叫他极为不快：它立得全然违背他自己的劝告，也因此惹出了乡绅向伊莎贝尔抱怨的那场“数落”。这份遗嘱把全部产业留给亨利表哥。其中倒也装模作样地给伊莎贝尔留了一笔款子，可那笔钱只是作为一笔款子留下的，并没有在产业上设定负担。就在前几天，阿普约翰先生已经得悉，庄上再没有余款可以支付这样一笔遗赠。因此，这份遗嘱在他眼里全无是处。倘若它果真被认定为老乡绅最后的有效遗嘱，那么他的职责便是宣告：庄园连同庄上的一切都归亨利表哥所有；而且正如他所担心的，将没有任何来源，可以支付名义上留给布罗德里克小姐的那笔钱中稍微像样的一部分。依他看，天下再没有比这更残忍、更不公的事了。

他业已听到消息，凭着这消息，质疑手头这份遗嘱的真实性便成了他的职责；而此刻，把这一切说明白的时刻到了。

===Original===
“The document which I hold here,” he said, “purports to be the last will of our old friend. Every will does that as a matter of course. But then there may always be another and a later will.” Here he paused, and looked round the room at the faces of the farmers.

“So there be,” said Joseph Cantor the younger.

“Hold your tongue, Joe, till you be asked,” said the father.

At this little interruption all the other farmers turned their hats in their hands. Cousin Henry gazed round at them, but said never a word. The lawyer looked into the heir’s face, and saw the great beads of sweat standing on his brow.

===Chinese===
“我手中拿的这份文书，”他说，“自称是我们老朋友最后立下的遗嘱。凡是遗嘱，自然都这么自称。可要知道，随时都可能另有别份、立得更晚的遗嘱。”说到这里他停了停，环视屋内，看着农场主们一张张脸。

“那可不，是有的。”年轻坎托说。

“乔，没人问你，就闭上嘴。”他父亲说。

经这小小的插嘴，其余几位农场主都在手里转动起帽子来。亨利表哥环视众人，却一声不吭。律师盯着继承人的脸，只见他额头上挂满了豆大的汗珠。

===Original===
“You hear what young Mr. Cantor has said,” continued the lawyer. “I am glad that he interrupted me, because it will make my task easier.”

“There now, feyther!” said the young man triumphantly.

“You hold your tongue, Joe, till you be asked, or I’ll lend ye a cuff.”

===Chinese===
“各位都听见年轻坎托先生方才的话了，”律师接着说，“他打断了我，我倒高兴，因为这样我的差事就好办多了。”

“瞧见没有，爹！”年轻人得意扬扬地说。

“没人问你，你就闭上嘴，乔，不然我就赏你一巴掌。”

===Original===
“Now I must explain,” continued Mr. Apjohn, “what passed between me and my dear old friend when I received instructions from him in this room as to this document which is now before me. You will excuse me, Mr. Jones,”—this he said addressing himself especially to Cousin Henry—“if I say that I did not like this new purpose on the Squire’s part. He was proposing an altogether new arrangement as to the disposition of his property; and though there could be no doubt, not a shadow of doubt, as to the sufficiency of his mental powers for the object in view, still I did not think it well that an old man in feeble health should change a purpose to which he had come in his maturer years, after very long deliberation, and on a matter of such vital moment. I expressed my opinion strongly, and he explained his reasons. He told me that he thought it right to keep the property in the direct line of his family. I endeavoured to explain to him that this might be sufficiently done though the property were left to a lady, if the lady were required to take the name, and to confer the name on her husband, should she afterwards marry. You will probably all understand the circumstances.”

===Chinese===
“现在，我必须说明一下，”阿普约翰先生接着说道，“我在这间屋里从我那位亲爱的老朋友手里接受指示、办理眼前这份文书的时候，我们两人之间有过怎样的往来。琼斯先生，请恕我直言，”——这话他是特特对着亨利表哥说的——“乡绅的这个新主意，我当初并不赞成。他打算对产业的处置作一番全然翻新的安排；虽然就他达成此项目的的心智能力而论，无可怀疑，连一丝一毫的怀疑也没有，可我仍旧认为，一位体弱多病的老人，改变他在盛年经过长久斟酌、就一桩如此性命交关的大事所下的决心，恐怕不妥。我直言不讳地讲了意见，他也说明了他的理由。他告诉我，他认为把产业留在他家直系一脉，才是正理。我竭力向他解释：这个目的照样可以达成，哪怕把产业留给一位女士——只要要求那位女士改用这个姓氏，日后出嫁，再把这姓氏授给她的丈夫。个中情由，诸位多半都明白。”

===Original===
“We understand them all,” said John Griffith, of Coed, who was supposed to be the tenant of most importance on the property.

“Well, then, I urged my ideas perhaps too strongly. I am bound to say that I felt them very strongly. Mr. Indefer Jones remarked that it was not my business to lecture him on a matter in which his conscience was concerned. In this he was undoubtedly right; but still I thought I had done no more than my duty, and could only be sorry that he was angry with me. I can assure you that I never for a moment entertained a feeling of anger against him. He was altogether in his right, and was actuated simply by a sense of duty.”

“We be quite sure of that,” said Samuel Jones, from The Grange, an old farmer, who was supposed to be a faraway cousin of the family.

===Chinese===
“这里头的情由我们全都明白。”科伊德（Coed）的约翰·格里菲斯（John Griffith）说；庄园上数他的佃户身份最是要紧。

“好，那么，我申说己见，或许过于用力了。可我不得不说，我当时的感受也实在强烈。因德弗·琼斯先生（Mr. Indefer Jones）说，凡关乎他良心的事，轮不到我来教训他。这一点他断然没错；可我仍旧觉得，我不过尽了本分，只是他不高兴我，徒然叫人惋惜。我可以向诸位担保，我对他没有一时一刻动过怒。他完全理直，驱使他的只是一份责任心。”

“这个我们拿得稳稳的。”住在格兰治（The Grange）的老农塞缪尔·琼斯（Samuel Jones）说；据说他同这一家是隔了好多房的远亲。

===Original===
“I have said all this,” continued the lawyer, “to explain why it might be probable that Mr. Jones should not have sent for me, if, in his last days, he felt himself called on by duty to alter yet once again the decision to which he had come. You can understand that if he determined in his illness to make yet another will—”

“Which he did,” said the younger Cantor, interrupting him.

“Exactly; we will come to that directly.”

“Joe, ye shall be made to sit out in the kitchen; ye shall,” said Cantor the father.

===Chinese===
“我把这些话统统说在前头，”律师接着道，“是为了说明：倘若琼斯先生在最后那些日子里，觉得责任在身，要再一次改动他已经定下的决定，那他多半就不会来请我了。诸位可以明白，倘若他在病中决意再立一份新遗嘱——”

“他正是这么办的。”年轻坎托插嘴道。

“正是；这个我们马上就要说到。”

“乔，看我不叫你坐到外头厨房里去；准叫你去。”老坎托说。

===Original===
“You can understand, I say, that he might not like to see me again upon the subject. In such case he would have come back to the opinion which I had advocated; and, though no man in his strong health would have been more ready to acknowledge an error than Indefer Jones, of Llanfeare, we all know that with failing strength comes failing courage. I think that it must have been so with him, and that for this reason he did not avail himself of my services. If there be such another will—”

===Chinese===
“所以我说，诸位可以明白，为着这件事，他也许不愿意再见我。若是那样，他就是回转到我从前主张的意见上来了；兰菲尔的因德弗·琼斯，论身强力壮时的爽快认错，世上没有人比他更痛快，可我们也都知道，力气衰了，胆气也就跟着衰了。我想他必定就是这般光景，也正为这个缘故，他才没有再借重我的效劳。倘若真有这么另一份遗嘱——”

===Original===
“There be!” said the irrepressible Joe Cantor the younger. Upon this his father only looked at him. “Our names is to it,” continued Joe.

“We cannot say that for certain, Mr. Cantor,” said the lawyer. “The old Squire may have made another will, as you say, and may have destroyed it. We must have the will before we can use it. If he left such a will, it will be found among his papers. I have turned over nothing as yet; but as it was here in this drawer and tied in this bundle that Mr. Jones was accustomed to keep his will—as the last will which I made is here, as I expected to find it, together with those which he had made before and which he seems never to have wished to destroy, I have had to explain all this to you. It is, I suppose, true, Mr. Cantor, that you and your son were called upon by the Squire to witness his signature to a document which he purported to be a will on Monday the 15th of July?”

===Chinese===
“就是有！”年轻坎托乔又按捺不住了。听了这话，他父亲只朝他望了一眼。“上头写着我们俩的名字哩。”乔接着说。

“这一点我们可不能说死，坎托先生，”律师说，“老乡绅或许照你说的另立过一份遗嘱，或许又把它毁掉了。遗嘱必须先拿到手，才谈得上使用。他若真留下这样一份遗嘱，总会从他存放的文书里找出来。我至今什么都没有翻检过；不过，琼斯先生素来是把遗嘱放在这只抽屉里、捆在这束文件中的——我起草的最后那份遗嘱就在这里，正如我预想的那样，连同他先前立下的、看来他从不曾想毁掉的各份都在——我才有必要把这些向诸位交代明白。坎托先生，我想确有其事吧：七月十五日，星期一那天，乡绅曾请你和你的儿子到场，见证他在一份自称是遗嘱的文书上签名？”

===Original===
Then Joseph Cantor the father told all the circumstances as they had occurred. When Mr. Henry Jones had been about a fortnight at Llanfeare, and when Miss Isabel had been gone a week, he, Cantor, had happened to come up to see the Squire, as it was his custom to do at least once a week. Then the Squire had told him that his services and those also of his son were needed for the witnessing of a deed. Mr. Jones had gone on to explain that this deed was to be his last will. The old farmer, it seemed, had suggested to his landlord that Mr. Apjohn should be employed. The Squire then declared that this would be unnecessary; that he himself had copied a former will exactly, and compared it word for word, and reproduced it with no other alteration than that of the date. All that was wanted would be his signature, efficiently witnessed by two persons who should both be present together with the testator. Then the document had been signed by the Squire, and after that by the farmer and his son. It had been written, said Joseph Cantor, not on long, broad paper such as that which had been used for the will now lying on the table before the lawyer, but on a sheet of square paper such as was now found in the Squire’s desk. He, Cantor, had not read a word of what had there been set down, but he had been enabled to see that it was written in that peculiarly accurate and laborious handwriting which the Squire was known to use, but not more frequently than he could help.

===Chinese===
于是老坎托约瑟夫把事情的来龙去脉照实讲了一遍。亨利·琼斯先生（Mr. Henry Jones）到兰菲尔约摸半个月、伊莎贝尔小姐走了有一个礼拜的时候，他坎托恰巧上庄里来探望乡绅——他素来的规矩，至少一礼拜来一回。乡绅当时告诉他，有一份文书需要见证，要借重他和他儿子的力量。琼斯先生接着说明，这份文书就是他的最后遗嘱。老佃户看来是向东家提议过，这事该请阿普约翰先生承办。乡绅随即宣称大可不必：他自己已把从前的一份遗嘱一字不差地誊录了一遍，逐字逐句核对过，誊本与原本再无二致，唯独改换了日期。所缺的只是他的签名，还须有两个人与立遗嘱人同在场，切实见证。于是乡绅在文书上签了字，随后是老佃户和他的儿子。约瑟夫·坎托说，那份东西写的不是如今摊在律师面前桌上这份遗嘱所用的那种长幅宽纸，而是一张方形纸，正是眼下在乡绅书桌里找见的那种。坎托自己不曾读过上面写下的一个字，但他看得分明，全篇是用乡绅素来使用的那手特别端正、一笔一画费死力的字迹写的——那手字人人知道，乡绅非到万不得已不肯多用。

===Original===
Thus the story was told—at least, all that there was to tell as yet. The drawer was opened and ransacked, as were also the other drawers belonging to the table. Then a regular search was made by the attorney, accompanied by the doctor, the butler, and the housemaid, and continued through the whole afternoon—in vain. The farmers were dismissed as soon as the explanation had been given as above described. During the remainder of the day Cousin Henry occupied a chair in the parlour, looking on as the search was continued. He offered no help, which was natural enough; nor did he make any remark as to the work in hand, which was, perhaps, also natural. The matter was to him one of such preponderating moment that he could hardly be expected to speak of it. Was he to have Llanfeare and all that belonged to it, or was he to have nothing? And then, though no accusation was made against him, though no one had insinuated that he had been to blame in the matter, still there was apparent among them all a strong feeling against him. Who had made away with this will, as to the existence of which at one time there was no doubt? Of course the idea was present to his mind that they must think that he had done so. In such circumstances it was not singular that he should say nothing and do nothing.

===Chinese===
事情的原委便是这样交代了——至少，眼下能说的统统说了。抽屉拉开来搜了一遍，桌子其余的抽屉也都翻过。随后由律师主持，医生、男管家和女仆随同，来了一场正经八百的大搜寻，整整延续了一个下午——一无所获。上面所说的那番解释一经讲完，农场主们便被遣散了。这一天余下的时光，亨利表哥占着客厅里的一把椅子，坐视搜寻继续进行。他不帮一点忙，这本在情理之中；对手头这件事他也不置一词，这多半同样在情理之中。这件事于他干系如此重大，旁人原不能指望他开口谈论。兰菲尔连同属它的一切，究竟归他，还是让他落个两手空空？况且，虽然没有人指控他，虽然没有人暗示他在这件事里有什么过错，众人之间对他的敌意却分明看得出来。这份遗嘱是谁毁掉的？它的存在，一度可是不容怀疑的。他心里自然明白，他们必定认定是他下的手。处在这般境地，他一言不发、一事不为，也就不足为奇了。

===Original===
Late in the evening Mr. Apjohn, just before he left the house, asked Cousin Henry a question, and received an answer.

“Mrs. Griffith tells me, Mr. Jones, that you were closeted with your uncle for about an hour immediately after the Cantors had left him on that Tuesday—just after the signatures had been written. Was it so?”

Again the drops of sweat came out and stood thick upon his forehead. But this Mr. Apjohn could understand without making an accusation against the man, even in his heart. The unexpressed suspicion was so heavy that a man might well sweat under the burden of it! He paused a moment, and tried to look as though he were thinking. “Yes,” said he; “I think I was with my uncle on that morning.”

===Chinese===
夜深了，阿普约翰先生临出宅门之前，问了亨利表哥一句话，也得到了一句回答。

“琼斯先生，格里菲斯太太告诉我，那个星期二，坎托父子刚离开你伯父，签名刚一写完，你就同他关起门来密谈了约莫一个钟头。有没有这回事？”

汗珠又一次沁出来，密密地挂在他的额头上。可这一点，阿普约翰先生即便在心里，也不必把它算作对这人的指控：这份没有说出口的怀疑实在太沉，一个人担着这样的重负，冒汗也是难怪！他顿了一顿，竭力摆出一副正在思量的模样。“有的，”他说，“我想那天早晨我是同伯父在一块儿来着。”

===Original===
“And you knew that the Cantors had been with him?”

“Not that I remember. I think I did know that somebody had been there. Yes, I did know it. I had seen their hats in the hall.”

“Did he say anything about them?”

“Not that I remember.”

===Chinese===
“那你可知道坎托父子刚在他那里待过？”

“记不得了。我想我倒是知道有谁来过。是的，我的确知道。我看见他们的帽子放在门厅里。”

“他提没提起过他们？”

“记不得了。”

===Original===
“Of what was he talking? Can you tell me? I rather fancy that he did not talk much to you.”

“I think it was then that he told me the names of all the tenants. He used to scold me because I did not understand the nature of their leases.”

“Did he scold you then?”

“I think so. He always scolded me. He did not like me. I used to think that I would go away and leave him. I wish that I had never come to Llanfeare. I do;—I do.”

There seemed to be a touch of truth about this which almost softened Mr. Apjohn’s heart to the poor wretch. “Would you mind answering one more question, Mr. Jones?” he said. “Did he tell you that he had made another will?”

===Chinese===
“他当时谈些什么？你能告诉我吗？我倒觉得，他同你谈得不多。”

“我想就是在那个时候，他把所有佃户的名字说给我听。他老数落我，因为我弄不懂他们租约的门道。”

“那么他当时数落你了？”

“好像是。他一向数落我。他不喜欢我。我从前常想，干脆一走了之，撇下他。但愿我从没来过兰菲尔。真的；——真的。”

这话里似乎有一点真情，几乎叫阿普约翰先生对这可怜虫心软了下来。“再回答一个问题，琼斯先生，你不介意吧？”他说，“他可曾告诉你，他又立了一份遗嘱？”

===Original===
“No.”

“Nor that he intended to do so?”

“No.”

“He never spoke to you about another will—a further will, that should again bestow the estate on your cousin?”

“No,” said Cousin Henry, with the perspiration still on his brow.

Now it seemed to Mr. Apjohn certain that, had the old man made such a change in his purpose, he would have informed his nephew of the fact.

===Chinese===
“没有。”

“也没有说他打算立？”

“没有。”

“关于另一份遗嘱——关于把产业重新授予你表妹的那份新遗嘱，他从来没有对你提过一个字？”

“没有。”亨利表哥说，额头上还挂着汗。

此刻阿普约翰先生几乎断定：老人倘若当真改了这个主意，是不会不把这事告诉侄子的。

## XXI Mr. Apjohn’s Success / 第二十一章 阿普约翰的成功

===Original===
Early on the Wednesday morning Mr. Apjohn and Mr. Brodrick were on foot, and preparing for the performance of their very disagreeable day’s work. Mr. Brodrick did not believe at all in the day’s work, and in discussing the matter with Mr. Apjohn, after they had determined upon their line of action, made his mind known very clearly. To him it was simply apparent that if the will had fallen into the power of a dishonest person, and if the dishonest man could achieve his purpose by destroying it, the will would be destroyed. Of Cousin Henry he knew nothing. Cousin Henry might or might not be ordinarily honest, as are other ordinary people. There might be no such will as that spoken of, or there might be a will accidentally hidden—or the will might have been found and destroyed. But that they should be able to find a will, the hiding-place of which should be known to Cousin Henry, was to his thinking out of the question. The subtler intellect of the other lawyer appreciating the intricacies of a weak man’s mind saw more than his companion. When he found that Mr. Brodrick did not agree with him, and perceived that the other attorney’s mind was not speculative in such a matter as this, he ceased to try to persuade, and simply said that it was the duty of both of them to leave no stone unturned. And so they started.

“I’ll take you about half a mile out of our way to show you Mr. Evans’s gate,” Mr. Apjohn said, after they had started. “His house is not above twenty minutes from Llanfeare, and should it be necessary to ask his assistance, he will know all about it. You will find a policeman there ready to come back with you. But my impression is that Cousin Henry will not attempt to prevent any search which we may endeavour to make.”

It was about ten when they reached the house, and, on being shown into the book-room, they found Cousin Henry at his breakfast. The front door was opened for them by Mrs. Griffith, the housekeeper; and when Mr. Apjohn expressed his desire to see Mr. Jones, she made no difficulty in admitting him at once. It was a part of the misery of Cousin Henry’s position that everybody around him and near to him was against him. Mrs. Griffith was aware that it was the purpose of Mr. Apjohn to turn her present master out of Llanfeare if possible, and she was quite willing to aid him by any means in her power. Therefore, she gave her master no notice of the arrival of the two strangers, but ushered them into the room at once.

Cousin Henry’s breakfast was frugal. All his meals had been frugal since he had become owner of Llanfeare. It was not that he did not like nice eating as well as another, but that he was too much afraid of his own servants to make known his own tastes. And then the general discomforts of his position had been too great to admit of relief from delicate dishes. There was the teapot on the table, and the solitary cup, and the bread and butter, and the nearly naked bone of a cold joint of mutton. And the things were not set after the fashion of a well-to-do gentleman’s table, but were put on as they might be in a third-rate London lodging, with a tumbled tablecloth, and dishes, plates, and cups all unlike each other.

“Mr. Jones,” said the attorney from Carmarthen, “this is your uncle, Mr. Brodrick, from Hereford.” Then the two men who were so nearly connected, but had never known each other, shook hands. “Of course, this matter,” continued Mr. Apjohn, “is of great moment, and Mr. Brodrick has come over to look after his daughter’s interests.”

===Chinese===
星期三一大早，阿普约翰先生和布罗德里克先生便已起身，准备去办这一天里极不受用的事务。布罗德里克对这天的差事根本不信，与阿普约翰商定行动方针之后，把自己的心思表露得再明白不过。在他看来，事情明摆着：倘若遗嘱落到了一个不诚实的人手里，而这个人毁掉遗嘱便能遂愿，那遗嘱必然已被毁掉。对亨利表哥其人他一无所知。亨利表哥也许像旁的普通人一样照常诚实，也许并不。兴许根本就没有人们说的那份遗嘱，兴许有一份遗嘱是偶然藏匿起来的——又兴许遗嘱已经被找到、销毁了。可要叫他们找到一份遗嘱、而藏匿之处又恰为亨利表哥所知，在他想来全无可能。另一位律师头脑更精细，能体察一个懦弱之人心思里的曲折，看到的比他的同伴多。他发现布罗德里克不与自己同心，又看出这位同行律师的心思在这样的问题上并不好作推究，便不再力劝，只说这是他们二人共同的职责，务必千方百计，绝不放过任何一处。于是两人便动身了。

“我带你绕半英里路，指给你看埃文斯先生家的大门。”动身之后，阿普约翰先生说道。“他家离兰菲尔不过二十分钟的路；万一需要请他帮忙，这里头的情形他全都清楚。那儿会有一名警察候着，随时可以跟你一道回来。不过依我看，亨利表哥大概不至于阻拦我们想做的任何搜查。”

他们到达宅子时已近十点，被引进书房，只见亨利表哥正在用早饭。前门是女管家格里菲斯太太替他们开的；阿普约翰先生表示要见琼斯先生，她毫不为难，随即领他们进去。亨利表哥处境的可悲之处之一，便是他身边周遭没有一个人不与他作对。格里菲斯太太心里明白，阿普约翰先生的用意就是要尽可能把她眼下的主人撵出兰菲尔，而她甘愿尽自己一切力量助他一臂之力。因此，来了两位生人，她并不通报主人，径直把二人引进了屋。

亨利表哥的早饭十分俭省。自从当上兰菲尔的主人，他的每顿饭都这样俭省。倒不是他不爱享用佳肴、不如旁人有口福，而是他太怕自己的仆人，不敢把自己的口味说出来。再说，他处境中种种说不出的别扭积在一起，也决非精美菜肴所能纾解。桌上摆着一把茶壶，一只孤零零的茶杯，黄油面包，还有一块冷羊肉，骨头已快剔得见了白。这些东西也不按殷实体面人家的餐桌样式陈设，倒像三等伦敦寄宿公寓里的摆法：桌布皱皱巴巴，碟子、盘子、杯子彼此无一件相配。

“琼斯先生，”卡马森来的律师说道，“这位是您伯父，赫里福德来的布罗德里克先生。”于是这两位血肉相连、却素未谋面的人握了握手。“自然，这件事，”阿普约翰先生接着说，“非同小可，布罗德里克先生正是为他女儿的利益才特地赶来的。”

===Original===
“I am very glad to see my uncle,” said Cousin Henry, turning his eye involuntarily towards the shelf on which the volume of sermons was resting. “I am afraid I can’t offer you much in the way of breakfast.”

“We breakfasted before we left Carmarthen,” said Mr. Apjohn. “If you do not mind going on, we will talk to you whilst you are eating.” Cousin Henry said that he did not mind going on, but found it impossible to eat a morsel. That which he did, and that which he endured during that interview, he had to do and had to endure fasting. “I had better tell you at once,” continued Mr. Apjohn, “what we want to do now.”

“What is it you want to do now? I suppose I have got to go into the assizes all the same on Friday?”

“That depends. It is just possible that it should turn out to be unnecessary.”

As he said this, he looked into Cousin Henry’s face, and thought that he discerned something of satisfaction. When he made the suggestion, he understood well how great was the temptation offered in the prospect of not having to encounter Mr. Cheekey.

===Chinese===
“伯父光临，我高兴得很。”亨利表哥说着，眼睛不由自主地瞟向那格书架，那卷布道文集就搁在上面。“只怕早饭没什么好招待二位的。”

“我们离开卡马森之前就吃过早饭了。”阿普约翰先生说。“你若不介意继续吃，我们就在你用饭的时候跟你谈。”亨利表哥说不介意，却发现自己一口也咽不下去。这场会面里他所做的事、所受的罪，全都得空着肚子来完成、来忍受。“我看最好马上告诉你，”阿普约翰先生接着道，“我们这回来想做什么。”

“你们这回想做什么？我想，星期五我还是照样得上巡回法庭出庭吧？”

“那要看情形。也说不定到头来根本用不着呢。”

说这话时，他注视着亨利表哥的脸，觉得瞧出了几分快意的神色。他抛出这句话时心里十分清楚：一想到可以不必同奇基先生照面，这诱惑是何等之大。

===Original===
