“Both Mr. Brodrick and I think it probable that your uncle’s last will may yet be concealed somewhere in the house.” Cousin Henry’s eye, as this was said, again glanced up at the fatal shelf.

“When Mr. Apjohn says that in my name,” said Mr. Brodrick, opening his mouth for the first time, “you must understand that I personally know nothing of the circumstances. I am guided in my opinion only by what he tells me.”

“Exactly,” said Mr. Apjohn. “As the father of the young lady who would be the heiress of Llanfeare if you were not the heir, I have of course told him everything—even down to the most secret surmises of my mind.”

“All right,” said Cousin Henry.

“My position,” continued Mr. Apjohn, “is painful and very peculiar; but I find myself specially bound to act as the lawyer of the deceased, and to carry out whatever was in truth his last will and testament.”

===Chinese===
“布罗德里克先生和我都认为，你伯父最后立下的遗嘱，很可能还藏在这座宅子的什么地方。”这话说出口时，亨利表哥的眼睛又一次朝那格要命的书架瞟了上去。

“阿普约翰先生这番话虽是借我的名义说的，”布罗德里克先生头一回开口道，“但你要明白，个中情形我个人一概不知。我的看法，全凭他告诉我的话。”

“正是。”阿普约翰先生说。“这位小姐若不是因为你做了继承人，本该是兰菲尔的女继承人；作为她的父亲，我自然把一切都告诉了他——连我心里最隐秘的揣测也没有瞒。”

“好吧。”亨利表哥说。

“我的处境，”阿普约翰先生接着说，“既痛苦又十分特殊；可我发觉自己格外有责任替逝者行使律师的职分，把他真正最后的遗嘱忠实执行到底。”

===Original===
“I thought that was proved at Carmarthen,” said Cousin Henry.

“No doubt. A will was proved—a will that was very genuine if no subsequent will be found. But, as you have been told repeatedly, the proving of that will amounts to nothing if a subsequent one be forthcoming. The great question is this; Does a subsequent will exist?”

“How am I to know anything about it?”

“Nobody says you do.”

“I suppose you wouldn’t come here and bring my uncle Brodrick down on me—giving me no notice, but coming into my house just when I am at breakfast, without saying a word to anyone—unless you thought so. I don’t see what right you have to be here at all!”

===Chinese===
“我以为那份遗嘱已经在卡马森认证过了。”亨利表哥说。

“不错，是认证过一份遗嘱——倘若找不到日后另立的遗嘱，那便是千真万确的遗嘱。可是，正如已经跟你反复说过的，一旦有一份更晚的遗嘱现身，认证那份遗嘱便一文不值。要紧的问题只有一条：究竟存不存在一份更晚的遗嘱？”

“这叫我怎么会知道？”

“谁也没有说你知情。”

“我看你们若不是这么想，也不会跑到这里来，还把我伯父布罗德里克搬来压我——招呼也不打一个，偏挑我用早饭的时候闯进我家，跟谁都不吐一个字。我实在看不出你们凭什么待在这里！”

===Original===
He was trying to pluck up his spirit in order that he might get rid of them. Why, oh! why had he not destroyed that document when, on the previous night, it had been brought out from its hiding-place, purposely in order that it might be burned?

“It is common, Mr. Jones, for one gentleman to call upon another when there is business to be done,” said Mr. Apjohn.

“But not common to come to a gentleman’s house and accuse him of making away with a will.”

“Nobody has done that,” said Mr. Brodrick.

“It is very like it.”

“Will you allow us to search again? Two of my clerks will be here just now, and will go through the house with us, if you will permit it.”

===Chinese===
他正在竭力鼓起胆气，好把这两个人打发走。为什么不呢？唉！前一天夜里，那份文书明明已经从藏处取了出来，专意要拿去烧掉，他为什么偏偏没有毁掉它？

“琼斯先生，有事相商，一位绅士登门拜访另一位绅士，本是常事。”阿普约翰先生说。

“可跑到人家宅子上，指责人毁弃遗嘱，这可不算常事。”

“没有人这样指责过。”布罗德里克先生说。

“那也差不离了。”

“你能不能容我们再搜一遍？我手下的两个办事员即刻就到，只要你点头，他们就同我们把宅子细细查一遍。”

===Original===
Cousin Henry sat staring at them. Not long ago he had himself asked one of Mr. Apjohn’s clerks why they did not search again. But then the framing of his thoughts had been different. At that moment he had been desirous of surrendering Llanfeare altogether, so that he might also get rid of Mr. Cheekey. Now he had reached a bolder purpose. Now he was resolved to destroy the will, enjoy the property, and face the barrister. An idea came across his mind that they would hardly insist upon searching instantly if he refused. A petition to that effect had already been made, and a petition implies the power of refusal on the part of him petitioned.

“Where do you want to look?” he asked.

Upon this Mr. Brodrick allowed his eyes to wander round the room. And Cousin Henry’s eyes followed those of his uncle, which seemed to him to settle themselves exactly upon the one shelf.

“To search the house generally; your uncle’s bedroom, for instance,” said Mr. Apjohn.

“Oh, yes; you can go there.” This he said with an ill-formed, crude idea which sprang to his mind at the moment. If they would ascend to the bedroom, then he could seize the will when left alone and destroy it instantly—eat it bit by bit if it were necessary—go with it out of the house and reduce it utterly to nothing before he returned. He was still a free agent, and could go and come as he pleased. “Oh, yes; you can go there.”

===Chinese===
亨利表哥坐在那里直勾勾地盯着他们。不久之前，他还亲口问过阿普约翰先生手下的一名办事员，为什么不重新搜一遍。可那时他心里的盘转与如今全然不同。当时他一心想把兰菲尔整个让出去，好连奇基先生也一并摆脱。如今他却打定了更大胆的主意：如今他决意毁掉遗嘱，安享产业，直面那位出庭律师。他心头掠过一个念头：只要他一口回绝，他们未必就坚持立刻搜查。此前确曾有过一次这样的恳请，而既是恳请，被恳请的一方自然有权拒绝。

“你们想到哪里找？”他问。

听了这话，布罗德里克先生的目光在屋里四下游移。亨利表哥的眼睛紧随着他伯父的目光，在他看来，那目光仿佛恰恰落定了在那一格书架上。

“把宅子各处都搜一搜；比如说，你伯父的卧室。”阿普约翰先生说。

“哦，行；那屋你们尽管去。”这话背后，是此刻蓦地涌上他心头的一个不成形、欠斟酌的念头。他们若是上楼去搜卧室，他就趁屋里只剩自己一个人的时候把遗嘱抓到手里，立刻毁掉——必要时就一口一口嚼碎咽下——揣着它出门，等回来之前叫它化得干干净净。他如今还是个自由身，来去随他高兴。“哦，行；那屋你们尽管去。”

===Original===
But this was not at all the scheme which had really formed itself in Mr. Apjohn’s brain. “Or perhaps we might begin here,” he said. “There are my two clerks just arrived in the fly.”

Cousin Henry became first red and then pale, and he endeavoured to see in what direction Mr. Brodrick had fixed his eye. Mr. Apjohn himself had not as yet looked anywhere round the books. He had sat close at the table, with his gaze fixed on Cousin Henry’s face, as Cousin Henry had been well aware. If they began to search in the room, they would certainly find the document. Of that he was quite sure. Not a book would be left without having been made to disclose all that it might contain between its leaves. If there was any chance left to him, it must be seized now—now at this very moment. Suddenly the possession of Llanfeare was endeared to him by a thousand charms. Suddenly all fear of eternal punishment passed away from his thoughts. Suddenly he was permeated by a feeling of contrition for his own weakness in having left the document unharmed. Suddenly he was brave against Mr. Cheekey, as would be a tiger against a lion. Suddenly there arose in his breast a great desire to save the will even yet from the hands of these Philistines.

“This is my private room,” he said. “When I am eating my breakfast I cannot let you disturb me like that.”

“In a matter such as this you wouldn’t think of your own comfort!” said Mr. Apjohn severely. “Comfort, indeed! What comfort can you have while the idea is present to you that this house in which you live may possibly be the property of your cousin?”

===Chinese===
然而这远不是阿普约翰先生脑中真正盘算好的章程。“要不咱们就从这屋里搜起。”他说。“我的两个办事员坐轻便马车刚到。”

亨利表哥的脸先是一红，随即转白，一面竭力想看清布罗德里克先生的眼睛盯住了哪个方向。阿普约翰自己还没有朝书堆里的哪一处看过一眼。他一直紧靠桌子坐着，目光钉在亨利表哥的脸上——这一点亨利表哥心里明镜一般。他们一旦在这屋里搜起来，那份文书必被发现，这一点他有十足的把握。没有一本书会放过，每一本都得把书页间夹的东西统统吐出来。倘若还有一线生机，那便必须立刻抓住——就是此刻，一秒钟也不能等。忽然间，占有兰菲尔平添了千般的滋味，叫他万难割舍。忽然间，对永恒惩罚的恐惧从他的思虑中退得无影无踪。忽然间，一种悔恨浸透了他的全身——恨自己懦弱，竟容那份文书安然无损地留到今天。忽然间，他面对奇基先生勇气陡增，恰如猛虎之对雄狮。忽然间，他胸中涌起一个强烈的渴望：哪怕到了这步田地，也要把遗嘱从这帮非利士人的手掌心里救出来。

“这是我的私室。”他说。“我在用早饭，不能容你们这样搅扰。”

“这样的大事当头，你倒想自己的安逸！”阿普约翰先生声色俱厉。“安逸，好个安逸！你住在这座宅子里，心里时刻悬着它说不定是你表妹的财产，你还有半分安逸可言？”

===Original===
“It’s very little comfort you’ve left me among you.”

“Face it out, then, like a man; and when you have allowed us to do all that we can on her behalf, then enjoy your own, and talk of comfort. Shall I have the men in and go on with the search as I propose?”

If they were to find it—as certainly they would—then surely they would not accuse him of having hidden it! He would be enabled to act some show of surprise, and they would not dare to contradict him, even should they feel sure in their hearts that he had been aware of the concealment! There would be great relief! There would be an end of so many troubles! But then how weak he would have been—to have had the prize altogether within his grasp and to have lost it! A burst of foul courage swelled in his heart, changing the very colour of his character for a time as he resolved that it should not be so. The men could not search there—so he told himself—without further authority than that which Mr. Apjohn could give them. “I won’t be treated in this way!” he said.

“In what way do you mean, Mr. Jones?”

===Chinese===
“你们齐心合力，留给我的安逸可真够少的。”

“那就拿出个男子汉的样子来，硬扛到底；等你容我们替她把能做的都做完，你再去享你的安逸，再来谈安逸。我是不是就叫人进来，照我的提议把搜查进行下去？”

倘若让他们搜出来——那是十拿九稳的——他们总不见得反咬一口，说他藏了遗嘱！他尽可以装出一副惊讶的模样，他们纵然心里笃定他早知藏匿之处，也断不敢当面驳他！那该是多大的解脱！多少烦恼从此一笔勾销！可转念一想，那样他又显得多么孱弱——奖赏本来已经整个攥在手心，竟又拱手丢了出去！一股卑劣的胆气陡然在他心里鼓胀起来，把他的脾性都一时改换了颜色；他拿定主意，决计不让这事发生。他对自己说：单凭阿普约翰先生能给的那点权柄，这些人搜不了这间屋子。“我不容你们这样对待我！”他说。

“你这话是什么意思，琼斯先生？”

===Original===
“I won’t have my house searched as though I were a swindler and a thief. Can you go into any man’s house and search it just as you please, merely because you are an attorney?”

“You told my man the other day,” said Mr. Apjohn, “that we might renew the search if we pleased.”

“So you may; but you must get an order first from somebody. You are nobody.”

“You are quite right,” said Mr. Apjohn, who was not at all disposed to be angry in regard to any observation offered personally to himself. “But surely it would be better for you that this should be done privately. Of course we can have a search-warrant if it be necessary; but then there must be a policeman to carry it out.”

“What do I care for policemen?” said Cousin Henry. “It is you who have treated me badly from first to last. I will do nothing further at your bidding.”

===Chinese===
“我不容我的宅子被搜来搜去，好像我是个骗子、是个贼。只因为你是律师，就可以随便闯进什么人的家里想搜就搜吗？”

“前两天你对我说的人讲过，”阿普约翰先生说，“只要我们愿意，尽可以重新再搜。”

“那是可以；可你们得先从什么人手里讨一道命令来。你们什么都不是。”

“你这话一点儿不错。”阿普约翰先生说；人家话里若只是冲着他本人，他向来一点火气也没有。“可这样的事私下办了，对你总归好些。搜查令当然随时领得到，只要必要；不过那样一来，就得有警察拿着令状来执行了。”

“警察来了我又怕什么？”亨利表哥说。“从头到尾亏待我的正是你们。往后你们吩咐什么，我一件也不办。”

===Original===
Mr. Apjohn looked at Mr. Brodrick, and Mr. Brodrick looked at Mr. Apjohn. The strange attorney would do nothing without directions from the other, and the attorney who was more at home was for a few moments a little in doubt. He got up from his chair, and walked about the room, while Cousin Henry, standing also, watched every movement which he made. Cousin Henry took his place at the further end of the table from the fire, about six feet from the spot on which all his thoughts were intent. There he stood, ready for action while the attorney walked up and down the room meditating what it would be best that he should do next. As he walked he seemed to carry his nose in the air, with a gait different from what was usual to him. Cousin Henry had already learned something of the man’s ways, and was aware that his manner was at present strange. Mr. Apjohn was in truth looking along the rows of the books. In old days he had often been in that room, and had read many of the titles as given on the backs. He knew the nature of many of the books collected there, and was aware that but very few of them had ever been moved from their places in the old Squire’s time for any purpose of use. He did not wish to stand and inspect them—not as yet. He walked on as though collecting his thoughts, and as he walked he endeavoured to fix on some long set of sermons. He had in his mind some glimmering of remembrance that there was such a set of books in the room. “You might as well let us do as we propose,” he said.

“Certainly not. To tell you the truth, I wish you would go away, and leave me.”

“Mr. Cheekey will hear all about it, and how will you be able to answer Mr. Cheekey?”

“I don’t care about Mr. Cheekey. Who is to tell Mr. Cheekey? Will you tell him?”

“I cannot take your part, you know, if you behave like this.”

===Chinese===
阿普约翰先生望着布罗德里克先生，布罗德里克先生望着阿普约翰先生。这位外乡来的律师没有同伴的指示便什么也不肯做，而更熟悉此地的这位律师一时也有几分踌躇。他从椅子上立起身，在屋里踱来踱去；亨利表哥也站起来，他的一举一动都看在眼里。亨利表哥拣了桌子离壁炉远的那一头站定，距他一腔心思所系的那一处约莫六英尺。他站在那里，蓄势待发；那位律师则在屋里踱上踱下，思量下一步最好怎么办。他踱步时鼻子仿佛朝天翘着，步态也与平日不同。亨利表哥对这人的做派已有几分了解，知道他此刻的神气有些古怪。阿普约翰其实正沿着那一排排书挨排看去。从前往事，他常到这间屋里来，书脊上印的许多书名他都读过。那里收藏的许多书是什么性质，他都心中有数，也知道老乡绅在世时，这些书极少数曾为什么用处挪动过原位。他并不想站着把书细细检视——至少眼下还不想。他只顾走下去，仿佛在收拢心思，一面走一面竭力要在心里认定某一套卷帙浩繁的布道文放在何处。他依稀记得屋里当真有这么一套书。“你还是依我们的提议办的好。”他说。

“断然不行。实话对你说，我巴不得你们走开，别来搅扰我。”

“奇基先生会把这一切听个明白；到时候你拿什么去应对奇基先生？”

“奇基先生我才不在乎。谁去告诉奇基先生？难道你去告诉他？”

“你也知道，你这般行事，我可没法再护着你。”

===Original===
As he spoke, Mr. Apjohn had stopped his walk, and was standing with his back close to the bookshelves, with the back of his head almost touching the set of Jeremy Taylor’s works. There were ten volumes of them, and he was standing exactly in front of them. Cousin Henry was just in front of him, doubting whether his enemy’s position had not been chosen altogether by accident, but still trembling at the near approach. He was prepared for a spring if it was necessary. Anything should be hazarded now, so that discovery might be avoided. Mr. Brodrick was still seated in the chair which he had at first occupied, waiting till that order should be given to him to go for the magistrate’s warrant.

Mr. Apjohn’s eye had caught the author’s name on the back of the book, and he remembered at once that he had seen the volume—a volume with Jeremy Taylor’s name on the back of it—lying on the old man’s table. “Jeremy Taylor’s Works. Sermons.” He remembered the volume. That had been a long time ago—six months ago; but the old man might probably take a long time over so heavy a book. “You will let me look at some of these,” he said, pointing with his thumb over his back.

“You shall not touch a book without a regular order,” said Cousin Henry.

Mr. Apjohn fixed the man’s eye for a moment. He was the smaller man of the two, and much the elder; but he was wiry, well set, and strong. The other was soft, and unused to much bodily exercise. There could be no doubt as to which would have the best of it in a personal struggle. Very quickly he turned round and got his hand on one of the set, but not on the right one. Cousin Henry dashed at him, and in the struggle the book fell to the ground. Then the attorney seized him by the throat, and dragged him forcibly back to the table. “Take them all out one by one, and shake them,” he said to the other attorney—“that set like the one on the floor. I’ll hold him while you do it.”

===Chinese===
说着这话，阿普约翰先生已停下脚步，背脊紧贴书架立定，后脑勺几乎碰着那套杰里米·泰勒文集。文集共十卷，他站的正是这十卷的正前方。亨利表哥恰在他对面，一面疑心敌人的站位是否纯属偶然，一面又因逼得这样近而浑身发颤。必要时他准备扑将上去。事到如今，只消能躲过搜出，什么都豁得出去。布罗德里克先生仍坐在他起初坐的那把椅子上，等着一道吩咐，好去讨治安法官的令状。

阿普约翰的眼睛早瞥见了书脊上作者的名字，随即想起曾见过那一卷——书脊上印着杰里米·泰勒名字的那一卷——就摊在老人的桌上。“杰里米·泰勒文集。布道文。”那卷书他记得。那已是很久以前的事——半年前了；不过这样一部沉甸甸的大书，老人多半要翻上许久。“这几本，总得容我看看。”他说着，用拇指朝自己背后一指。

“没有正式的命令，一本书你也休想碰。”亨利表哥说。

阿普约翰把此人的目光逼定了一瞬。两个人里他身量小些，年岁也大得多，却精瘦结实、体格匀整、筋骨有力；另一个则绵软无力，素来不惯多耗体力。真要动起手来谁占上风，毫无疑义。他霍地转过身，一把抓住那套书中的一卷，却抓的卷数不对。亨利表哥朝他扑了上去，扭打之间，那卷书跌落在地。律师随即使一把扼住他的喉咙，硬把他拖回桌边。“把它们一本一本全抽出来抖一抖，”他对另一位律师说——“照地上那一卷那样的一套。我按住他，你来抖。”

===Original===
Mr. Brodrick did as he was told, and, one by one, beginning from the last volume, he shook them all till he came to volume 4. Out of that fell the document.

“Is it the will?” shouted Mr. Apjohn, with hardly breath enough to utter the words.

Mr. Brodrick, with a lawyer’s cautious hands, undid the folds, and examined the document. “It certainly is a will,” he said—“and is signed by my brother-in-law.”

===Chinese===
布罗德里克先生依言行事，从最末一卷起，一卷一卷地抖过去，直到第四卷。那份文书便从这一卷里滑落出来。

“是不是遗嘱？”阿普约翰先生高声喊道，几乎连把话说完的气力都没有了。

布罗德里克先生用律师特有的谨慎双手解开折痕，验看那文书。“千真万确是一份遗嘱。”他说——“而且是我内兄签署的。”

## XIX Mr. Apjohn Sends for Assistance / 第十九章 阿普约翰派人求援

===Original===
The last words in the last chapter were spoken by Mr. Apjohn to his confidential clerk in a tone of triumph. He had picked up something further, and, conscious that he had done so by his own ingenuity, was for a moment triumphant. But when he came to think over it all alone—and he spent many hours just at present in thinking of this matter—he was less inclined to be self-satisfied. He felt that a great responsibility rested with him, and that this weighed upon him peculiarly at the present moment. He was quite sure not only that a later will had been made, but that it was in existence. It was concealed somewhere, and Cousin Henry knew the secret of its hiding-place. It had existed, at any rate, that morning; but now came the terrible question whether the man, driven to his last gasp in his misery, would not destroy it. Not only had Mr. Apjohn discovered the secret, but he was well aware that Cousin Henry was conscious that he had done so, and yet not a word had been spoken between them which, should the will now be destroyed, could be taken as evidence that it had ever existed. Let the paper be once burnt, and Cousin Henry would be safe in possession of the property. Mr. Cheekey might torment his victim, but certainly would not extract from him a confession such as that. The hiding of the will, the very place in which it was hidden, might possibly be extracted. It was conceivable that ingenuity on one side and abject terror on the other might lead a poor wretch to betray the secret; but a man who has committed a felony will hardly confess the deed in a court of law. Something of all this would, thought Mr. Apjohn, occur to Cousin Henry himself, and by this very addition to his fears he might be driven to destroy the will. The great object now should be to preserve a document which had lived as it were a charmed life through so many dangers. If anything were to be done with this objective—anything new—it must be done at once. Even now, while he was thinking of it, Cousin Henry was being taken slowly home in Mr. Powell's fly, and might do the deed as soon as he found himself alone in the book-room. Mr. Apjohn was almost sure that the will was concealed somewhere in the book-room. That long-continued sojourn in the chamber, of which the whole country had heard so much, told him that it was so. He was there always, watching the hiding-place. Would it be well that searchers should again be sent out, and that they should be instructed never to leave that room till after Cousin Henry's examination should be over? If so, it would be right that a man should be sent off instantly on horseback, so as to prevent immediate destruction. But then he had no power to take such a step in reference to another man's house. It was a question whether any magistrate would give him such a warrant, seeing that search had already been made, and that, on the failure of such search, that Squire's will had already been proved. A man's house is his castle, let the suspicion against him be what it may, unless there be evidence to support it. Were he to apply to a magistrate, he could only say that the man's own manner and mode of speech had been evidence of his guilt. And yet how much was there hanging, perhaps, on the decision of the moment! Whether the property should go to the hands of her who was entitled to enjoy it, or remain in the possession of a thief such as this, might so probably depend on the action which should be taken, now, at this very instant!

===Chinese===
上一章末了的几句话，是阿普约翰先生（Mr. Apjohn）带着得胜的口气对他的心腹办事员说的。他又探到了一点新的东西，并且明白这是全凭自己的机敏得来的，一时颇为得意。可是等到独自把事情从头细想——眼前他正把一个又一个钟头花在这件事的思虑上——那份自得便消减了。他觉得一副重大的担子压在自己身上，而在此一刻，这担子压得分外沉重。他不仅确信后来另立过一份遗嘱，而且确信它至今尚在。它藏在某个地方，而亨利表哥（Cousin Henry）知道藏处的秘密。至少到那天早晨它还在；可是跟着便来了那个可怕的问题：这个人被苦楚逼到只剩最后一口气的时候，会不会索性把它毁掉。阿普约翰先生不仅自己发现了这个秘密，而且十分清楚亨利表哥心里明白他已发现；然而两人之间不曾说过一句话——一旦遗嘱此刻被毁，那里面找不出一句可以当作它存在过的凭据。那张纸一经烧掉，亨利表哥便可以安安稳稳地把产业握在手里。奇基先生（Mr. Cheekey）尽可以把他的猎物折磨个够，却断然榨不出这样一纸供状。遗嘱被人藏起、藏在哪个所在，这些或者还掏得出来：可以想见，一边是盘问者的机巧，一边是吓破了胆的惊惶，两下夹攻，或许会把一个可怜虫逼得吐露秘密；可是犯了重罪的人，却很少会在法庭上招认自己的罪行。阿普约翰先生想，这层道理亨利表哥自己多少也会想到，而恰恰是恐惧的这份加重，可能逼得他下手毁掉遗嘱。如今的要图，便是保住这份文书——它闯过了这许多凶险，仿佛一直有神明护持，才活到今天。若要为这个目的做点什么——做什么新的举措——就必须立刻动手。就在此刻，就在他思虑之间，亨利表哥正坐着鲍威尔先生（Mr. Powell）的轻便马车，被慢慢地送回庄上；他一发觉自己独自待在书房里，随时可能下手。阿普约翰先生几乎断定，遗嘱就藏在书房里的某处。他在那间屋里经久不散的枯守——此事全郡上下早已谈论得很多——正向他表明情形如此。他总是在那儿，守着那个藏处。再派一拨搜查的人前去，吩咐他们在对亨利表哥的盘问终结之前绝不可离开那间屋子——这样做好不好？果要这样办，那就应当立刻派人骑马飞驰而去，以防遗嘱立时遭毁。可是话说回来，对别人的宅子，他没有权限走这一步。何况先前的搜查已经行过，搜而不获之后，乡绅的遗嘱也已经认证在案；治安法官（magistrate）还肯不肯发给他这样一张令状，实在难说。只要拿不出证据作凭依，一个人的家宅便是他的城堡，任凭旁人对他怀什么样的疑心。他若去申请，所能陈说的也只有：这人自己的举止言谈，便是他犯罪的明证。然而这转瞬之间的决断，上头也许悬着多大的干系！这份产业究竟要交到有权享用它的那人手里，还是继续留在这么一个窃贼手中，多半就系于此刻、就在这一瞬该采取的行动！

===Original===
Mr. Ricketts, his confidential clerk, was the only person with whom he had fully discussed all the details of the case—the only person to whom he had expressed his own thoughts as they had occurred to him. He had said a word to the clerk in triumph as Cousin Henry left him, but a few minutes afterwards recalled him with an altered tone. “Ricketts,” he said, “the man has got that will with him in the book-room at Llanfeare.”

“Or in his pocket, sir,” suggested Ricketts.

“I don’t think it. Wherever it be at this moment, he has not placed it there himself. The Squire put it somewhere, and he has found it.”

“The Squire was very weak when he made that will, sir,” said the clerk. “Just at that time he was only coming down to the dining-room, when the sun shone in just for an hour or two in the day. If he put the will anywhere, it would probably be in his bedroom.”

===Chinese===
里基茨先生（Mr. Ricketts），他的心腹办事员，是唯一同他把这桩案子的枝枝节节细细商讨过的人——唯一一个他念头一起便向之和盘托出的人。亨利表哥告辞的时候，他得意洋洋地对办事员说了一句；可几分钟之后，他又把人叫了回来，口气全变了。“里基茨，”他说，“那份遗嘱就在那人手里，藏在他兰菲尔（Llanfeare）的书房里。”

“或者就揣在他自己的口袋里，先生。”里基茨提示道。

“我看不见得。此刻它不论在哪儿，反正不是他亲手放的。是乡绅把它搁在了什么地方，后来叫他找着了。”

“先生，乡绅立那份遗嘱的时候，身子已经很弱了，”办事员说，“那阵子一天当中，只有日头照进来的一两个钟头，他才下得楼到饭厅来。他若把遗嘱搁在了哪儿，多半是搁在自己卧室里。”

===Original===
“The man occupies another chamber?” asked the attorney.

“Yes, sir; the same room he had before his uncle died.”

“It’s in the book-room,” repeated Mr. Apjohn.

“Then he must have put it there.”

“But he didn’t. From his manner, and from a word or two that he spoke, I feel sure that the paper has been placed where it is by other hands.”

===Chinese===
“那人住的是另一间屋子？”律师问。

“是的，先生；就是他伯父去世以前他自己住的那间。”

“遗嘱在书房里。”阿普约翰先生重说了一遍。

“那必定是他放进去的。”

“可他没放过。从他的神气，从他偶尔吐出的一两句话，我敢断定：那张纸是别人的手放到如今这个地方的。”

===Original===
“The old man never went into the book-room. I heard every detail of his latter life from Mrs. Griffith when the search was going on. He hadn’t been there for more than a month. If he wanted anything out of the book-room, after the young lady went away, he sent Mrs. Griffith for it.”

“What did he send for?” asked Mr. Apjohn.

“He used to read a little sometimes,” said the clerk.

“Sermons?” suggested Mr. Apjohn. “For many years past he has read sermons to himself whenever he has failed in going to church. I have seen the volumes there on the table in the parlour when I have been with him. Did they search the books?”

“Had every volume off the shelves, sir.”

===Chinese===
“老人家从来不进书房。搜查进行的那阵子，他晚年生活的种种细情，我全是从格里菲斯太太（Mrs. Griffith）嘴里听来的。他已经有一个多月没进过那间屋了。小姐走了以后，书房里他要什么，都是打发格里菲斯太太去取。”

“都打发取过些什么？”阿普约翰先生问。

“他有时候也拿点书读读。”办事员说。

“布道文集吧？”阿普约翰先生点破道。“这么多年来，凡没能上教堂的日子，他都在家里自己读布道文集。我陪着他的时候，就见过那几卷书摆在客厅桌上。他们搜过那些书没有？”

“书架上的书每本都取下来了，先生。”

===Original===
“And opened every one of them?”

“That I can’t tell. I wasn’t there.”

“Every volume should have been shaken,” said Mr. Apjohn.

“It’s not too late yet, sir,” said the clerk.

“But how are we to get in and do it? I have no right to go into his house, or any man’s, to search it.”

“He wouldn’t dare to hinder you, sir.”

===Chinese===
“每一本都翻开来看过？”

“这个我说不上。我当时不在场。”

“每一本都该抖它一抖的。”阿普约翰先生说。

“现在补做还不晚，先生。”办事员说。

“可我们怎么进得去动手呢？进他的宅子——进任何人的宅子——去搜查，我都没有这个权利。”

“他不敢拦您，先生。”

===Original===
Then there was a pause before anything further was said.

“The step is such a strong one to take,” said the lawyer, “when one is guided only by one’s own inner conviction. I have no tittle of evidence in my favour to prove anything beyond the fact that the old Squire in the latter days of his life did make a will which has not been found. For that we have searched, and, not finding it, have been forced to admit to probate the last will which we ourselves made. Since that nothing has come to my knowledge. Guided partly by the man’s ways while he has been at Llanfeare, and partly by his own manner and hesitation, I have come to a conclusion in my own mind; but it is one which I would hardly dare to propose to a magistrate as a ground for action.”

“But if he consented, sir?”

“Still, I should be hardly able to justify myself for such intrusion if nothing were found. We have no right to crush the poor creature because he is so easily crushable. I feel already pricks of conscience because I am bringing down Jack Cheekey upon him. If it all be as I have suggested—that the will is hidden, let us say in some volume of sermons there—what probability is there that he will destroy it now?”

===Chinese===
接着是片刻的沉默，两个人都没有再往下说。

“只凭自己心里的一点信念行事，”律师说，“这一步就迈得太重了。除了老乡绅晚年确实立过一份至今未曾寻获的遗嘱这个事实之外，我没有一丝一毫于己有利的证据，可以证明任何更进一步的情形。为着那份遗嘱，我们搜过了；没有搜到，只得把自己经手立下的最后那份遗嘱送去认证。从那以后，再没有什么传到我耳中。一部分凭这人在兰菲尔的行止，一部分凭他本人的神气与支吾，我心里得出了一个结论；可这个结论，我几乎不敢向治安法官提出，作为采取行动的依据。”

“可要是他本人准了呢，先生？”

“即便准了，倘若到头来一无所得，这样的擅闯我也难以自圆其说。这可怜虫是这般经不起碾轧，我们可没有权利因为他容易碾碎就去碾碎他。单是想到要把杰克·奇基派到他头上，我的良心已经在受针刺了。倘若一切果真如我所料——遗嘱就藏着，比方说，就藏在那儿某一卷布道文集里——那么他如今就把它毁掉的可能又有多大呢？”

===Original===
“He would before the trial, I think.”

“But not at once? I think not. He will not allow himself to be driven to the great crime till the last moment. It is quite on the cards that his conscience will even at last be too strong for it.”

“We owe him something, sir, for not destroying it when he first found it.”

“Not a doubt! If we are right in all this, we do owe him something—at any rate, charity enough to suppose that the doing of such a deed must be very distasteful to him. When I think of it I doubt whether he’ll do it at all.”

“He asked me why they didn’t come and search again.”

“Did he? I shouldn’t wonder if the poor devil would be glad enough to be relieved from it all. I’ll tell you what I’ll do, Ricketts. I’ll write to Miss Brodrick’s father, and ask him to come over here before the trial. He is much more concerned in the matter than I am, and should know as well what ought to be done.”

===Chinese===
“我想，开庭之前他是会毁的。”

“可不至于立刻就下手吧？我看不会。不到最后的关头，他不会容许自己被逼到那桩大罪跟前。而且他的良心到头来或许终究强过邪念，叫他下不了手——这也大有可能。”

“先生，他头一回发现遗嘱的时候没有毁掉它，单凭这一点，我们就欠了他一份情。”

“一点疑问也没有！倘若我们全然料对了，我们确实欠他一点什么——至少，也该存一分宽厚，姑且认定干这等勾当必定是他极不愿意的事。我一想到这里，就疑心他究竟会不会动手。”

“他还问过我，他们怎么不再来搜一遍。”

“是吗？这可怜的魔鬼若巴不得有人来替他把这一切解脱掉，我也毫不奇怪。里基茨，我跟你说说我打算怎么办。我写信给布罗德里克小姐（Miss Brodrick）的父亲，请他在开庭之前过这边来。这件事同他的干系比我大得多，该怎么办，他心里也应当一样有数。”

===Original===
The letter was written urging Mr. Brodrick to come at once. “I have no right to tell you,” Mr. Apjohn said in his letter, “that there is ground for believing that such a document as that I have described is still existing. I might too probably be raising false hope were I to do so. I can only tell you of my own suspicion, explaining to you at the same time on what ground it is founded. I think it would be well that you should come over and consult with me whether further steps should be taken. If so, come at once. The trial is fixed for Friday the 30th.” This was written on Thursday the 22nd. There was, therefore, not much more than a week’s interval.

===Chinese===
信随即写就，敦请布罗德里克先生（Mr. Brodrick）速来。“我无权告诉你，”阿普约翰先生在信中写道，“有根据可以相信，我先前向你说起过的那份文书现今确实还在。果真那样说，我多半是在挑起一场虚妄的指望。我只能把我的疑心告知于你，同时向你说明这疑心凭着什么立脚。我看你最好过这边来，同我商议一下是否还须再走下一步。若然，请即刻动身。开庭定在三十日，星期五。”信是二十二日星期四写的，因此前后相隔只有一个星期多一点。

===Original===
“You will come with me,” said Mr. Brodrick to the Rev. William Owen, after showing to him the letter from the attorney at Hereford.

“Why should I go with you?”

“I would wish you to do so—on Isabel’s behalf.”

“Isabel and I are nothing to each other.”

“I am sorry to hear you say that. It was but the other day that you declared that she should be your wife in spite of herself.”

“So she shall, if Mr. Henry Jones be firmly established at Llanfeare. It was explained to me before why your daughter, as owner of Llanfeare, ought not to marry me, and, as I altogether agreed with the reason given, it would not become me to take any step in this matter. As owner of Llanfeare she will be nothing to me. It cannot therefore be right that I should look after her interests in that direction. On any other subject I would do anything for her.”

===Chinese===
布罗德里克先生在赫里福德（Hereford）把那位律师的来信给威廉·欧文牧师（Rev. William Owen）看过之后，对他说：“你同我一道去。”

“我为什么要同你一道去？”

“我要你这么做——为了伊莎贝尔（Isabel）。”

“伊莎贝尔同我，如今彼此什么都不是。”

“听你说出这样的话来，我难过。不几天之前你还声言，纵然她本人不愿意，她也得做你的妻子。”

“只要亨利·琼斯先生（Mr. Henry Jones）在兰菲尔坐稳了位子，她终究还是要做我的妻子。从前已经有人向我说明过缘由：你的女儿做了兰菲尔的主人，为什么就不应当嫁我；我对所给的理由完全信服，自然不便在这件事上再迈一步。她做了兰菲尔的主人，同我便再没有瓜葛。既然如此，我还要往那个方向去照应她的利益，就说不过去了。旁的事情，为她我什么都肯干。”

===Original===
The father no doubt felt that the two young people were self-willed, obstinate, and contradictory. His daughter wouldn’t marry the clergyman because she had been deprived of her property. The clergyman now refused to marry his daughter because it was presumed that her property might be restored to her. As, however, he could not induce Mr. Owen to go with him to Carmarthen, he determined to go alone. He did not give much weight to this new story. It seemed to him certain that the man would destroy the will—or would already have destroyed it—if in the first instance he was wicked enough to conceal it. Still the matter was so great and the question so important to his daughter’s interest that he felt himself compelled to do as Mr. Apjohn had proposed. But he did not do it altogether as Mr. Apjohn had proposed. He allowed other matters to interfere, and postponed his journey till Tuesday the 27th of the month. Late on that evening he reached Carmarthen, and at once went to Mr. Apjohn’s house.

Cousin Henry’s journey into Carmarthen had been made on the previous Thursday, and since that day no new steps had been taken to unravel the mystery—none at least which had reference to Llanfeare. No further search had been made among the books. All that was known in Carmarthen of Cousin Henry during these days was that he remained altogether within the house. Were he so minded, ample time was allowed to him for the destruction of any document. In the town, preparation went on in the usual way for the assizes, at which the one case of interest was to be the indictment of Mr. Evans for defamation of character. It was now supposed by the world at large that Cousin Henry would come into court; and because this was believed of him there was something of a slight turn of public opinion in his favour. It would hardly be the case that the man, if really guilty, would encounter Mr. Cheekey.

During the days that had elapsed, even Mr. Apjohn himself had lost something of his confidence. If any further step was to be taken, why did not the young lady’s father himself come and take it? Why had he been so dilatory in a matter which was of so much greater importance to himself than to anyone else? But now the two attorneys were together, and it was necessary that they should decide upon doing something—or nothing.

===Chinese===
做父亲的无疑觉得这两个年轻人任性、执拗，而且专好彼此顶着来。他的女儿不肯嫁那位牧师，因为她的产业被人夺了去；那位牧师如今又拒不肯娶他的女儿，因为人们揣想她的产业或许物归原主。然而他既劝不动欧文先生同去卡马森（Carmarthen），便打定主意独自走一遭。这番新的说法，他并没有怎么放在心上。在他看来，这人若头一遭就坏得肯把遗嘱藏起来，那他多半会把它毁掉——要不就是早已毁了。只是事情如此重大，这个问题对女儿的利益又如此攸关，他觉得自己是非照阿普约翰先生的提议办不可了。不过他并没有全然照阿普约翰先生所提议的那样做。他容旁的事情插了进来，把行期推到本月二十七日，星期二。那天夜里很晚他才抵达卡马森，随即去了阿普约翰先生家中。

亨利表哥上卡马森（Carmarthen）来是先前那个星期四的事；从那天起，为解开这个谜再没有采取过新的步骤——至少，同兰菲尔沾边的步骤一步也没有。那些书不曾再搜过。这些日子里，卡马森城中关于亨利表哥的传闻只有一条：他成天闭门不出。他果真存了那个心，要毁掉任何一份文书，光阴尽够他用的。城里照旧进行着巡回法庭（assizes）的各项准备；这场审判中唯一引人瞩目的案子，将是对埃文斯先生（Mr. Evans）以损害他人名誉为罪名的起诉。如今外界一般都料想亨利表哥会到庭；世人既这样看待他，舆论便微微起了一点转向，偏向着他。这人倘若当真有罪，多半断不会来同奇基先生照面。

这许多日子过去，连阿普约翰先生自己也失了几分把握。倘若还要走下一步，那位小姐的父亲自己怎么不亲自来走？一件对他本人比谁都干系更重的事，他怎么这样拖延？可如今两位律师聚到了一处，总得决断：或是有所行动，或是不动。

===Original===
“I hoped you would have been here last week,” said Mr. Apjohn.

“I couldn’t get away. There were things I couldn’t possibly leave.”

“It is so important,” said Mr. Apjohn.

“Of course it is important—of most vital importance—if there be any hope.”

===Chinese===
“我原指望你上礼拜就到了。”阿普约翰先生说。

“我脱不开身。有些事我实在丢不下。”

“这事太要紧了。”阿普约翰先生说。

“当然要紧——只要还有一丝指望，那就是性命交关的要紧。”

===Original===
“I have told you exactly what I think and feel.”

“Yes, yes. I know how much more than kind, how honourable you have been in all this matter. You still think that the will is hidden?”

“I did think so.”

“Something has changed your opinion?”

===Chinese===
“我的想法和心情，我已经一五一十告诉过你了。”

“是，是。这件事从头至尾，你的好意远超出本分，你的为人又这样光明磊落，我心里全都有数。你还认定那份遗嘱是藏着的？”

“我原是这么认定的。”

“有什么叫你改变了看法？”

===Original===
“I can hardly say that either,” said Mr. Apjohn. “There was ground on which to form my opinion, and I do not know that there is any ground for changing it. But in such a matter the mind will vacillate. I did think that he had found the will shut up in a volume of sermons, in a volume which his uncle had been reading during his illness, and that he had left the book in its place upon the shelf. That, you will say, is a conclusion too exact for man to reach without anything in the shape of absolute evidence.”

“I do not say so; but then as yet I hardly know the process by which that belief has been reached.”

“But I say so;—I say that is too exact. There is more of imagination in it than of true deduction. I certainly should not recommend another person to proceed far on such reasoning. You see it has been in this way.” Then he explained to his brother attorney the process of little circumstances by which he had arrived at his own opinion;—the dislike of the man to leave the house, his clinging to one room, his manifest possession of a secret as evinced by his conversations with Farmer Griffith, his continual dread of something, his very clinging to Llanfeare as a residence which would not have been the case had he destroyed the will, his exaggerated fear of the coming cross-examination, his ready assertion that he had destroyed nothing and hidden nothing—but his failure to reply when he was asked whether he was aware of any such concealment. Then the fact that the books had not been searched themselves, that the old Squire had never personally used the room, but had used a book or one or two books which had been taken from it; that these books had been volumes which had certainly been close to him in those days when the lost will was being written. All these and other little details known to the reader made the process by which Mr. Apjohn had arrived at the conclusion which he now endeavoured to explain to Mr. Brodrick.

“I grant that the chain is slight,” said Mr. Apjohn, “so slight that a feather may break it. The strongest point in it all was the look on the man’s face when I asked him the last question. Now I have told you everything, and you must decide what we ought to do.”

===Chinese===
“这个我也说不上，”阿普约翰先生说。“我的看法原是有些根据的，我不知道如今有什么根据足以推翻它。不过这一类事情，人心总归要摇摆。我原先认定：遗嘱是他发现的，就收在一卷布道文集里——他伯父病中还在读着的那一卷——而他把那本书原样放回了书架上原来的位置。你会说，这样的结论未免太精确了，没有铁证，人断然到不了这一步。”

“我倒没说它太精确；只是这个信念循着怎样的路子达到的，我至今还没听明白。”

“可我偏要这么说；——我说它是太精确了。这里头想象的成分多，真切推演的成分少。换了旁人，我断不肯劝他顺着这样的推理走远。你听，事情是这样来的。”于是他向同行律师讲明了他形成己见的那一连串小情节：——这人不肯离家，死守着一间屋子；分明怀揣一件秘密，他同格里菲斯先生的谈话说得再明白不过；他无时无刻不在惧怕着什么；他偏偏死守着兰菲尔住下不走——倘若遗嘱毁在他手里，他断不会这样；他对即将临头的交叉盘问怕得离了谱；人家一问，他满口应承自己什么也没毁、什么也没藏——可问到他可知道有这么一桩藏匿时，他却答不上来。再加上那些书本身并不曾搜检过；老乡绅本人从来不进那间屋，只是叫人从里面取一本书、或者一两本书来用；而那几卷书，在遗失的遗嘱写就的那些日子里，准是他手边不离的东西。凡此种种，连同读者已经知道的旁的小情节，便凑成了阿普约翰先生达到结论的那条路径——此刻他正竭力把这个结论向布罗德里克先生解释明白。

“我承认这条链子不结实，”阿普约翰先生说，“轻得一根羽毛就能压断。其中顶硬的一点，是我问到最后那个问题时那人脸上的神气。如今我全都告诉你了，我们该怎么办，得由你来断。”

===Original===
But Mr. Brodrick was a man endowed with lesser gifts than those of the other attorney. In such a matter Mr. Apjohn was sure to lead. “What do you think yourself?”

“I would propose that we, you and I, should go together over to Llanfeare tomorrow and ask him to allow us to make what further search we may please about the house. If he permitted this—”

“But would he?”

“I think he would. I am not at all sure but what he would wish to have the will found. If he did, we could begin and go through every book in the library. We would begin with the sermons, and soon know whether it be as I have suggested.”

“But if he refused?”

===Chinese===
只是布罗德里克先生的天分不及他那同行律师。这类事情，领头的自然总是阿普约翰先生。“那你自己怎么看？”

“我的提议是：明天你我两人一道过兰菲尔去，请他容许我们随自己的意思，在宅子里再作进一步的搜查。他若容了这个——”

“可他肯吗？”

“我想他肯。他心里未尝不巴望遗嘱被找出来，这也很难说。他若肯，我们就动手，把书房里每一本书都过一遍。从布道文集搜起，我的猜想对不对，很快便见分晓。”

“可他若不肯呢？”

===Original===
“Then I think I would make bold to insist on remaining there while you went to a magistrate. I have indeed already prepared Mr. Evans of Llancolly, who is the nearest magistrate. I would refuse to leave the room, and you would then return with a search warrant and a policeman. But as for opening the special book or books, I could do that with or without his permission. While you talk to him I will look round the room and see where they are. I don’t think much of it all, Mr. Brodrick; but when the stake is so high, it is worth playing for. If we fail in this, we can then only wait and see what the redoubtable Mr. Cheekey may be able to do for us.”

Thus it was settled that Mr. Brodrick and Mr. Apjohn should go out to Llanfeare on the following morning.

===Chinese===
“到那时，我想我就斗胆赖在那儿不走，打发你去见治安法官。兰科利（Llancolly）的埃文斯先生是离得最近的治安法官，我其实已经预先同他打过招呼了。我拒不离屋，你便拿着搜查令、带着一名警察回来。至于翻开那几卷要紧的书，他准许也好，不准许也好，我横竖都做得到。你同他交涉的时候，我就在屋里四下察看，看它们摆在哪儿。这一切，布罗德里克先生，我看也没有多大的把握；可是赌注下得这样高，就值得赌这一场。这一步若再落空，我们就只好等着瞧，看那令人生畏的奇基先生能替我们做点什么了。”

于是便这样议定：第二天早晨，布罗德里克先生和阿普约翰先生一齐动身到兰菲尔去。

## XVII Mr. Cheekey / 第十七章 奇基先生

===Original===
A month had been left for Cousin Henry to consider what he would do—a month from the day in which he had been forced to accede to Mr. Apjohn’s proposal up to that on which he would have to stand before the barrister at Carmarthen, should he be brave enough at last to undergo the ordeal. He had in truth resolved that he would not undergo the ordeal. He was quite sure of himself that nothing short of cart-ropes or of the police would drag him into the witness-box. But still there was the month. There were various thoughts filling his mind. A great expense was being incurred—most uselessly, if he intended to retreat before the day came—and who would pay the money? There was hardly a hope left in his bosom that the property would remain in his hands. His hopes indeed now ran in altogether another direction. In what way might he best get rid of the property? How most readily might he take himself off from Llanfeare and have nothing more to do with the tenants and their rents? But still it was he who would be responsible for this terrible expense. It had been explained to him by the lawyer, that he might either indict the proprietor of the newspaper on a criminal charge or bring a civil action against him for damages. Mr. Apjohn had very strongly recommended the former proceeding. It would be cheaper, he had said, and would show that the man who brought it had simply wished to vindicate his own character. It would be cheaper in the long-run—because, as the lawyer explained, it would not be so much his object to get a verdict as to show by his presence in the court that he was afraid of no one. Were he to sue for damages, and, as was probable, not to get them, he must then bear the double expense of the prosecution and defence. Such had been the arguments Mr. Apjohn had used; but he had considered also that if he could bind the man to prosecute the newspaper people on a criminal charge, then the poor victim would be less able to retreat. In such case as that, should the victim’s courage fail him at the last moment, a policeman could be made to fetch him and force him into the witness-box. But in the conduct of a civil action no such constraint could be put upon him. Knowing all this, Mr. Apjohn had eagerly explained the superior attractions of a criminal prosecution, and Cousin Henry had fallen into the trap. He understood it all now, but had not been ready enough to do so when the choice had been within his power. He had now bound himself to prosecute, and certainly would be dragged into Carmarthen, unless he first made known the truth as to the will. If he did that, then he thought that they would surely spare him the trial. Were he to say to them, “There; I have at last myself found the will. Here, behold it! Take the will and take Llanfeare, and let me escape from my misery,” then surely they would not force him to appear in reference to a matter which would have been already decided in their own favour. He had lost that opportunity of giving up the will through Mr. Griffith, but he was still resolved that some other mode must be discovered before the month should have run by. Every day was of moment, and yet the days passed on and nothing was done. His last idea was to send the will to Mr. Apjohn with a letter, in which he would simply declare that he had just found it amongst the sermons, and that he was prepared to go away. But as the days flew by the letter was left unwritten, and the will was still among the sermons.

It will be understood that all this was much talked of in Carmarthen. Mr. Henry Jones, of Llanfeare, was known to have indicted Mr. Gregory Evans, of the *Carmarthen Herald*, for the publication of various wicked and malicious libels against himself; and it was known also that Mr. Apjohn was Mr. Jones’s attorney in carrying on the prosecution. But not the less was it understood that Mr. Apjohn and Mr. Evans were not hostile to each other in the matter. Mr. Apjohn would be quite honest in what he did. He would do his best to prove the libel—on condition that his client were the honest owner of the property in question. In truth, however, the great object of them all was to get Henry Jones into a witness-box, so that, if possible, the very truth might be extracted from him.

===Chinese===
留给亨利表哥（Cousin Henry）考虑何去何从的，还有一个月的工夫——从他被逼着应承阿普约翰先生（Mr. Apjohn）提议的那一天算起，直到他须站到卡马森（Carmarthen）的出庭律师面前去的那一天为止，倘若他终究鼓得起勇气去经受那场严酷考验的话。实则他早已打定主意：那场考验，他是不去受的。他对自己有十足的把握：除非动用拖大车的粗绳，或是出动警察，否则谁也休想把他拽上证人席。可这一个月终归还摆在那里。种种念头在他心里翻腾。一大笔开销正在花出去——倘若他打算在限期之前退缩，这钱便花得最无用处——那么钱由谁来付？产业还能留在他手里，这指望在他胸中已所剩无几。他眼下的指望，其实已整个转向了另一个方向。怎样处置这份产业对他最为合算？怎样才能尽快地从兰菲尔（Llanfeare）脱身，同佃户们和他们那些租金从此两不相干？可这笔吓人的开销，到头来还是要由他担着。那位律师已经向他解释过，他既可以按刑事罪名控告报纸业主，也可以提起民事诉讼向对方索赔。阿普约翰先生曾极力举荐前一种办法。他说，这样花费更省，而且表明兴讼的人不过是想洗雪自己的名声。长远算来也更省钱——因为，据律师解释，他的目标与其说是赢得裁决，不如说是借亲自到庭昭示天下：他谁也不怕。倘若他起诉索赔，而多半又索赔不成，那他就得把起诉、应诉两笔费用一齐扛起来。阿普约翰先生讲的就是这样一番道理；可他心里还另有一层盘算：倘若能把这个人拴住，非按刑事罪名去告报馆那班人不可，那么那位可怜的受害者临阵脱逃的余地就小了。到了那步田地，受害者倘若在最后关头胆气一泄，唤个警察去把他提来、押上证人席便是。而经办一桩民事诉讼，这样的强制手段却使不上。把这些门道看得分明之后，阿普约翰先生便极力渲染刑事控告的种种优越之处，亨利表哥也就一头栽进了圈套。如今他把这一切看透了，可当初抉择还在他手里的时候，他却没能看得这么快。事到如今，他已具结在案、非控不可，除非他先把关于遗嘱的真相和盘托出，否则必定要被拖到卡马森去。倘若他这么做了，他想他们定然会饶过他这场官司。他只消对他们说：“瞧，遗嘱到底是我自己找着的。喏，都在这儿！遗嘱拿去，兰菲尔也拿去，放我脱离苦海吧。”——那么他们必定不会再逼他为一桩早已按他们自己的心意了结的事出庭作证。借格里菲斯先生（Mr. Griffith）之手把遗嘱交出去的那个机会，他已然失掉；可他仍旧拿定主意，赶在这一个月过完之前，总得再寻出一条别的路子来。每一天都关紧要，可日子一天天过去，什么也没做成。他最后的一个主意，是把遗嘱寄给阿普约翰先生，随附一封信，信里只消声明他刚在布道文中把它找了出来，并且他准备一走了之。可日子一天天飞过去，信始终没有写，遗嘱依旧夹在布道文中间。

可以想见，这一切在卡马森成了人人热议的话题。兰菲尔的亨利·琼斯先生（Mr. Henry Jones）控告《卡马森先驱报》（Carmarthen Herald）的格雷戈里·埃文斯先生（Mr. Gregory Evans）刊登多篇恶意中伤他的下流诽谤文字，此事人尽皆知；大家也都知道，经办这桩控案的律师正是琼斯先生的阿普约翰先生。可人们同样心照不宣：在这桩事上，阿普约翰先生同埃文斯先生彼此并非敌手。阿普约翰先生行事自会光明磊落。他会竭力证明诽谤成立——前提是他的当事人确系争议产业名正言顺的主人。然而说到底，他们众人的头号目标，是把亨利·琼斯弄上证人席，好从那里头——若有可能——把实实在在的真相榨出来。

===Original===
Day by day and week by week since the funeral the idea had grown and become strong in Carmarthen that some wicked deed had been done. It irked the hearts of them all that such a one as Henry Jones should do such a deed and not be discovered. Old Indefer Jones had been respected by his neighbours. Miss Brodrick, though not personally well known in the county, had been spoken well of by all men. The idea that Llanfeare should belong to her had been received with favour. Then had come that altered intention in the old squire’s mind, and the neighbours had disapproved. Mr. Apjohn had disapproved very strongly, and though he was not without that reticence so essentially necessary to the character of an attorney, his opinion had become known. Then the squire’s return to his old purpose was whispered abroad. The Cantors had spoken very freely. Everything done and everything not done at Llanfeare was known in Carmarthen. Mr. Griffith had at length spoken, being the last to abandon all hope as to Cousin Henry’s honesty.

Everyone was convinced that Cousin Henry had simply stolen the property; and was it to be endured that such a deed as that should have been done by such a man and that Carmarthen should not find it out? Mr. Apjohn was very much praised for his energy in having forced the man to take his action against Mr. Evans, and no one was more inclined to praise him than Mr. Evans himself. Those who had seen the man did believe that the truth would be worked out of him; and those who had only heard of him were sure that the trial would be a time of intense interest in the borough. The sale of the newspaper had risen immensely, and Mr. Evans was quite the leading man of the hour.

“So you are going to have Mr. Balsam against me?” said Mr. Evans to Mr. Apjohn one day. Now Mr. Balsam was a very respectable barrister, who for many years had gone the Welsh circuit, and was chiefly known for the mildness of his behaviour and an accurate knowledge of law—two gifts hardly of much value to an advocate in an assize town.

===Chinese===
葬礼过后，一天又一天、一星期又一星期，卡马森城里渐渐传开并且坐实了一种看法：一桩恶事做下了。像亨利·琼斯这样一个人干了这样的事却不受揭发，叫人人心里都咽不下这口气。老因德弗·琼斯（Indefer Jones）素来受邻里敬重。布罗德里克小姐（Miss Brodrick）虽在本郡没有多少私交，人人提起她却都说好。兰菲尔将来归她所有，这个说法当初人人心悦诚服。随后老乡绅的心思变了卦，邻里们便不以为然。阿普约翰先生不以为然得尤其厉害；他虽不缺律师这一行当万万不可少的那种守口如瓶，他的看法还是传了出来。再后来，老乡绅回心转意、重申旧念的消息，也悄悄传扬开了。坎托两位先生（the Cantors）说话向来直白。兰菲尔做下的和没做下的一切，卡马森没有不知道的。格里菲斯先生最后也开了口——在亨利表哥的诚实问题上，他是最后一个放弃希望的人。

人人深信，亨利表哥分明是把产业偷到了手；这样的人干下这样的事，而卡马森竟查它不出，这能忍得下去吗？阿普约翰先生逼着这个人向埃文斯先生提起控诉，这份魄力大受称道，而称赞他称赞得最起劲的，莫过于埃文斯先生本人。亲眼见过这个人的人都相信，真话终归能从他身上掏出来；只是耳闻过他的人也都笃定，这场庭审将是全城轰动的一段日子。报纸的销路大涨，埃文斯先生俨然成了红极一时的头面人物。

“这么说，你要请巴尔萨姆（Balsam）先生来对付我了？”一天，埃文斯先生对阿普约翰先生说。这位巴尔萨姆先生是位极可敬的出庭律师，在威尔士巡回区执业多年，素以举止温文、律法精熟见称——这两样长处，在一个巡回法庭开庭的市镇上，对一个出庭律师来说，几乎算不得什么大用的本钱。

===Original===
“Yes, Mr. Evans. Mr. Balsam, I have no doubt, will do all that we want.”

“I suppose you want to get me into prison?”

“Certainly, if it shall be proved that you have deserved it. The libels are so manifest that it will be only necessary to read them to a jury. Unless you can justify them, I think you will have to go to prison.”

“I suppose so. You will come and see me, I am quite sure, Mr. Apjohn.”

“I suppose Mr. Cheekey will have something to say on your behalf before it comes to that.”

Now Mr. John Cheekey was a gentleman about fifty years of age, who had lately risen to considerable eminence in our criminal courts of law. He was generally called in the profession—and perhaps sometimes outside it—“Supercilious Jack,” from the manner he had of moving his eyebrows when he was desirous of intimidating a witness. He was a strong, young-looking, and generally good-humoured Irishman, who had a thousand good points. Under no circumstances would he bully a woman—nor would he bully a man, unless, according to his own mode of looking at such cases, the man wanted bullying. But when that time did come—and a reference to the Old Bailey and assize reports in general would show that it came very often—Supercilious Jack would make his teeth felt worse than any terrier. He could pause in his cross-examination, look at a man, projecting his face forward by degrees as he did so, in a manner which would crush any false witness who was not armed with triple courage at his breast—and, alas! not unfrequently a witness who was not false. For unfortunately, though Mr. Cheekey intended to confine the process to those who, as he said, wanted bullying, sometimes he made mistakes. He was possessed also of another precious gift—which, if he had not invented, he had brought to perfection—that of bullying the judge also. He had found that by doing so he could lower a judge in the estimation of the jury, and thus diminish the force of a damnatory charge. Mr. Cheekey’s services had been especially secured for this trial, and all the circumstances had been accurately explained to him. It was felt that a great day would have arrived in Carmarthen when Mr. Cheekey should stand up in the court to cross-examine Cousin Henry.

===Chinese===
“不错，埃文斯先生。巴尔萨姆先生想必定能满足我们的一切指望。”

“我猜，你是想把我送进大牢吧？”

“若是证明你罪有应得，那是自然。那些诽谤文字昭然若揭，只消向陪审团念一遍就够了。你若不能证明它们属实，我想你就得进大牢。”

“想必是吧。你一定会来看我的，这我很有把握，阿普约翰先生。”

“真到那一步之前，只怕奇基先生（Mr. Cheekey）还有话要替你说哩。”

这位约翰·奇基先生年纪五十上下，近来在我国刑事法庭上声名鹊起。同行里——兴许有时行外也一样——都管他叫“目中无人杰克”（Supercilious Jack），因为他存心要震住证人的时候，总有那么一挑眉毛的做派。他是个身强体健、瞧着比实际年轻、脾性通常和善的爱尔兰人，浑身上下有千百样好处。无论什么情形，他决不欺负女人——男人他也不欺负，除非照他自己看这类事的眼光看来，那个人欠欺负。可一旦那个时候真到了——只要翻一翻老贝利（the Old Bailey）和各巡回法庭的案报，就知道那个时候来得何等频繁——“目中无人杰克”咬起人来，比哪一条梗犬都叫人吃不消。他会在交叉盘问的半途停下来，盯着一个人看，脸随着那目光一点点朝前凑，那副架势足以压垮任何一个胸中没有备下三倍胆气的伪证人——唉，而且也屡屡压垮了本不是伪证的人。因为遗憾的是，奇基先生虽有意把这番手段只用在照他说来欠欺负的人身上，有时却也看走了眼。他还有一样宝货——这本事即便不是他发明的，也是他磨到了炉火纯青的——便是连法官也一并镇唬。他发现这样一来，他能叫法官在陪审团眼里跌了身价，从而挫掉那纸谴责性总结指示的几分锋芒。这一场官司，是特意重金聘定奇基先生的手艺的，全部案情也已原原本本向他交代清楚。人人都觉得，等奇基先生站起身来、当庭交叉盘问亨利表哥的那一天，卡马森的大日子就到了。

===Original===
“Yes,” said Mr. Evans, chuckling, “I think that Mr. Cheekey will have something to say to it. What will be the result, Mr. Apjohn?” he asked abruptly.

“How am I to say? If he can only hold his own like a man, there will, of course, be a verdict of guilty.”

“But can he?” asked he of the newspaper.

“I hope he may with all my heart—if he have done nothing that he ought not to have done. In this matter, Mr. Evans, I have altogether a divided sympathy. I dislike the man utterly. I don’t care who knows it. No one knows it better than he himself. The idea of his coming here over that young lady’s head was from the first abhorrent to me. When I saw him, and heard him, and found out what he was—such a poor, cringing, cowardly wretch—my feeling was of course exacerbated. It was terrible to me that the old squire, whom I had always respected, should have brought such a man among us. But that was the old squire’s doing. He certainly did bring him, and as certainly intended to make him his heir. If he did make him his heir, if that will which I read was in truth the last will, then I hope most sincerely that all that Mr. Cheekey may do may be of no avail against him. If that be the case, I shall be glad to have an opportunity of calling upon you in your new lodgings.”

===Chinese===
“是啊，”埃文斯先生低声笑着说，“我想奇基先生在这上头总有话要说。结果会怎么样呢，阿普约翰先生？”他冷不丁问道。

“叫我怎么说？他只要能像个男子汉一样撑得住自己，那当然就是一纸有罪的裁决。”

“可他撑得住吗？”办报的这位问道。

“我打心底里指望他撑得住——倘若他没做过什么不该做的事。在这桩事上，埃文斯先生，我的同情是彻头彻尾分作两半的。我打心眼里厌恶这个人。谁爱知道谁知道。没有人比他自己知道得更清楚。让他越过那位小姐的头顶跑到这儿来，这念头打一开始就叫我深恶痛绝。待我一见他的为人、一听他的谈吐、摸清了他是个什么东西——这样一个可怜巴巴、卑躬屈节、胆小如鼠的贱骨头——我的恶感自然更添了一层。我一向敬重的老乡绅，竟把这样一个人弄到我们中间来，这在我真是痛心的事。可那是老乡绅自己的作为。他确乎把他召了来，也确乎有意立他做继承人。倘若他当真立了他做继承人，倘若我宣读过的那份遗嘱确乎是最后遗嘱，那么我诚恳地指望，奇基先生纵有千般手段，在他身上也一概无济于事。果真如此，等你住进新的寓所，我倒乐意寻个机会去拜访你。”

===Original===
“But if there was another will, Mr. Apjohn—a later will?”

“Then of course, there is the doubt whether this man be aware of it.”

“But if he be aware of it?”

“Then I hope that Mr. Cheekey may tear him limb from limb.”

===Chinese===
“可倘使还有另一份遗嘱呢，阿普约翰先生——一份更晚的遗嘱？”

“那么问题当然就在于，这个人知不知道它。”

“可他要是知道呢？”

“那我就指望奇基先生把他大卸八块。”

===Original===
“But you feel sure that it is so?”

“Ah; I do not know about that. It is very hard to be sure of anything. When I see him I do feel almost sure that he is guilty; but when I think of it afterwards, I again have my doubts. It is not by men of such calibre that great crimes are committed. I can hardly fancy that he should have destroyed a will.”

“Or hidden it?”

“If it were hidden, he would live in agony lest it were discovered. I used to think so when I knew that he passed the whole day sitting in one room. Now he goes out for hours together. Two or three times he has been down with old Griffith at Coed, and twice young Cantor found him lying on the sea cliff. I doubt whether he would have gone so far afield if the will were hidden in the house.”

===Chinese===
“可你确信事情就是这样？”

“唉；这个我可说不上来。凡事都难得有十足的把握。我见他的时候，确实几乎断定他有罪；可过后再一想，疑心又来了。干下弥天大罪的，从来不是他这种材料的人。他毁掉一份遗嘱——这我简直想象不出来。”

“或者把它藏了起来呢？”

“倘若遗嘱是藏着的，他准会日夜提心吊胆，怕它被人搜出来。早先听说他整天价枯坐一屋的时候，我也是这么想的。如今他一出门就是几个钟头。有两三回他到科伊德（Coed）格里菲斯老头那里去，还有两回，年轻坎托撞见他躺在海崖上。倘若遗嘱就藏在宅子里，我疑心他未必会跑得这么远。”

===Original===
“Can he have it on his own person?”

“He is not brave enough for that. The presence of it there would reveal itself by the motion of his hands. His fingers would always be on the pocket that contained it. I do not know what to think. And it is because I am in doubt that I have brought him under Mr. Cheekey’s thumbscrew. It is a case in which I would, if possible, force a man to confess the truth even against himself. And for this reason I have urged him to prosecute you. But as an honest man myself, I am bound to hope that he may succeed if he be the rightful owner of Llanfeare.”

“No one believes it, Mr. Apjohn. Not one in all Carmarthen believes it.”

“I will not say what I believe myself. Indeed I do not know. But I do hope that by Mr. Cheekey’s aid or otherwise we may get at the truth.”

===Chinese===
“会不会就带在他自己身上？”

“他没有那个胆量。东西若在身上，他两只手的一举一动早把它招出来了。他的手指头会一刻不离那只装着它的口袋。我不知道该怎么想。正因为拿不定，我才把他交到奇基先生的拇指夹底下。这桩案子，倘若可能，我巴望能逼一个人招出真话来，哪怕这真话于他自己不利。也是为了这个缘故，我才撺掇他来控告你。可我自己既是个正直的人，只要他果真是兰菲尔名正言顺的主人，我就必定指望他成功。”

“没有人信这个，阿普约翰先生。全卡马森没有一个信的。”

“我自己信什么，我不说。的确，我也不知道。但我确实指望，凭着奇基先生的助力，或是凭别的什么，我们终能把真相摸到底。”

===Original===
In his own peculiar circle, with Mr. Geary the attorney, with Mr. Jones the auctioneer, and Mr. Powell, the landlord of the Bush Hotel, Mr. Evans was much more triumphant. Among them, and indeed, with the gentlemen of Carmarthen generally, he was something of a hero. They did believe it probable that the interloper would be extruded from the property which did not belong to him, and that the doing of this would be due to Mr. Evans. “Apjohn pretends to think that it is very doubtful,” said he to his three friends.

“Apjohn isn’t doubtful at all,” said Mr. Geary, “but he is a little cautious as to expressing himself.”

“Apjohn has behaved very well,” remarked the innkeeper. “If it wasn’t for him we should never have got the rascal to come forward at all. He went out in one of my flies, but I won’t let them charge for it on a job like that.”

“I suppose you’ll charge for bringing Cousin Henry into the court,” said the auctioneer. They had all got to call him Cousin Henry since the idea had got abroad that he had robbed his Cousin Isabel.

===Chinese===
在自己那个特有的圈子里——同律师吉尔里（Geary）先生、拍卖商琼斯先生，还有布什客栈（the Bush Hotel）的老板鲍威尔先生（Mr. Powell）聚在一处——埃文斯先生就要意气昂扬得多。在他们中间，说实在的，在卡马森的绅士们中间，他俨然是位英雄人物。他们当真相信，那个鸠占鹊巢的家伙多半会被从他那份不属于他的产业里撵出去，而这件事的功绩要归于埃文斯先生。“阿普约翰嘴上说这事还没个准儿呢，”他对三位朋友说。

“阿普约翰哪里是拿不定，”吉尔里先生说，“他只是说话上头谨慎一点罢了。”

“阿普约翰办事很是漂亮，”客栈老板说。“要不是他，那个无赖压根儿不会露面。他来时坐的就是我店里的轻便马车，可这样的事，我不许他们收这笔车钱。”

“我看，把亨利表哥送进法庭，你总归要收钱的吧，”拍卖商说。自打他抢了表妹伊莎贝尔（Isabel）的产业这个说法传扬开去，大家便都管他叫亨利表哥了。

===Original===
“I’d bring him too for nothing, and stand him his lunch into the bargain, rather than that he shouldn’t have the pleasure of meeting Mr. Cheekey.”

“Cheekey will get it out of him, if there is anything to get,” said Mr. Evans.

“My belief is that Mr. Cheekey will about strike him dumb. If he has got anything in his bosom to conceal, he will be so awestruck that he won’t be able to open his mouth. He won’t be got to say he did it, but he won’t be able to say he didn’t.” This was Mr. Geary’s opinion.

“What would that amount to?” asked Mr. Powell. “I’m afraid they couldn’t give the place back to the young lady because of that.”

===Chinese===
“把他送进法庭，我也白送，还倒贴他一顿午饭，只求他莫要错过了拜会奇基先生的那份乐子。”

“只要他肚里有货，奇基总有法子掏出来，”埃文斯先生说。

“依我看，奇基先生差不多能把他吓哑了。他胸口里若真藏着什么，给那一镇唬，连嘴都张不开。要他说他干了，那是办不到；可他要说自己没干，他也说不出口。”这便是吉尔里先生的高见。

“那又能顶什么用？”鲍威尔先生问。“单凭这个，只怕他们也不能把产业断还给那位小姐。”

===Original===
“The jury would acquit Mr. Evans. That’s about what it would amount to,” said the attorney.

“And Cousin Henry would go back to Llanfeare, and have all his troubles over,” remarked Mr. Jones. This they deemed to be a disastrous termination to all the trouble which they were taking, but one which seemed by no means improbable.

They all agreed that even Mr. Cheekey would hardly be able to extract from the man an acknowledgment that he had with his own hands destroyed the will. Such a termination as that to a cross-examination had never been known under the hands of the most expert of advocates. That Cousin Henry might be stricken dumb, that he might faint, that he might be committed for contempt of court—all these events were possible, or perhaps, not impossible; but that he should say, “Yes, I did it, I burnt the will. Yes, I, with my own hands,”—that they all declared to be impossible. And, if so, Cousin Henry would go back again to Llanfeare confirmed in his possession of the property.

“He will only laugh at us in his sleeve when it is over,” said the auctioneer.

===Chinese===
“陪审团会开脱埃文斯先生。大致也就是这么个结果，”律师说。

“亨利表哥呢，就回兰菲尔去，一切的烦难也就到头了，”琼斯先生说。他们认定，他们这般费心劳力，到头来若落得这么个收场，便是天大的晦气；然而这收场看上去绝非全无可能。

大家都同意，即便是奇基先生，也休想从这个人嘴里榨出一句招认，说他亲手毁掉了遗嘱。交叉盘问收得这样一桩战果，最老练的讼师手下也从来不曾有过。亨利表哥也许会被吓得作声不得，也许会晕厥过去，也许会以藐视法庭罪被收监——这些结局都有可能，或者说，未必不可能；可要他说出“是，是我干的，我把遗嘱烧了。是，是我亲手烧的”，——那他们都一口咬定是断断不能的。果真如此，亨利表哥便又要回兰菲尔去，稳稳当当地把产业攥在手里。

“等事情一了，他不过袖着手偷笑我们一场罢了，”拍卖商说。

===Original===
They little knew the torments which the man was enduring, or how unlikely it was that he should laugh in his sleeve at anyone. We are too apt to forget when we think of the sins and faults of men how keen may be their conscience in spite of their sins. While they were thus talking of Cousin Henry, he was vainly endeavouring to console himself with the reflection that he had not committed any great crime, that there was still a road open to him for repentance, that if only he might be allowed to escape and repent in London, he would be too glad to resign Llanfeare and all its glories. The reader will hardly suppose that Cousin Henry will return after the trial to laugh in his sleeve in his own library in his own house.

A few days afterwards Mr. Apjohn was up in town and had an interview with Mr. Balsam, the barrister. “This client of mine does not seem to be a nice sort of country gentleman,” said Mr. Balsam.

“Anything but that. You will understand, Mr. Balsam, that my only object in persuading him to indict the paper has been to put him into a witness-box. I told him so, of course. I explained to him that unless he would appear there, he could never hold up his head.”

===Chinese===
他们哪里知道这个人此刻正受着怎样的煎熬，更不知道他要袖着手偷笑谁，又是何等地笑不出来。我们想到人们的罪愆与过失时，往往容易忘记：纵然犯了罪，他们的良心又可以是何等敏锐。他们这样议论亨利表哥的时候，他正徒然地拿这些念头宽慰自己：他没有犯下什么弥天大罪，悔罪之路在他面前还敞着，只要容他脱身、到伦敦去悔过，他便巴不得把兰菲尔和它的一切荣华一并交出去。读者想必不会以为，庭审过后，亨利表哥还会回到自家宅中、自己那间书房里去袖手窃笑吧。

几天之后，阿普约翰先生上了伦敦，同出庭律师巴尔萨姆先生见了一面。“我这位当事人，看着不大像一位正派的乡绅，”巴尔萨姆先生说。

“正派，断断乎谈不上。你要明白，巴尔萨姆先生，我劝他控告报馆，唯一的用意就是把他弄上证人席。这我自然是对他明说了的。我向他讲清楚，他若不肯到那儿露面，就一辈子抬不起头来。”

===Original===
“Very unwillingly. He would have given his right hand to escape. But I gave him no alternative. I so put it before him that he could not refuse to do as I bade him without owning himself to be a rascal. Shall I tell you what I think will come of it?”

“What will come of it?”

“He will not appear. I feel certain that he will not have the courage to show himself in the court. When the day comes, or, perhaps, a day or two before, he will run away.”

“What will you do then?”

===Chinese===
“是万分不情愿地听的。为求脱身，他就是把右手给出去也甘心。可我不给他留第二条路。我把话摆到他面前，摆得他若不照我吩咐的去办，便等于自认是个无赖。要不要我告诉你，我料定这事会有什么收场？”

“会有什么收场？”

“他不会出庭。我有十足的把握，他没有那份胆量站到法庭上去。到了那天，兴许提前一两天，他就会溜之大吉。”

“那你们怎么办呢？”

===Original===
“What will you do then?”

“Ah, that’s the question. What shall we do then? He is bound to prosecute, and will have to pay the penalty. In such a case as this I think we could have him found and brought into court for the next assizes. But what could we do then? Though we were ever so rough to him in the way of contempt of court and the rest of it, we cannot take the property away. If he has got hold of the will and destroyed it, or hidden it, we can do nothing as to the property as long as he is strong enough to hold his tongue. If he can be made to speak, then I think we shall get at it.”

Mr. Balsam shook his head. He was quite willing to believe that his client was as base as Mr. Apjohn represented him to be; but he was not willing to believe that Mr. Cheekey was as powerful as had been assumed.

===Chinese===
“那你们怎么办呢？”

“唉，难就难在这儿。到那时我们怎么办？控案他是推脱不掉的，不出庭便得领受惩罚。到了这般田地，我想我们总能把他找出来，押到下一季巡回法庭上去。可到了那儿又能怎样？纵使我们在藐视法庭之类的事情上对他再不客气三分，产业总归夺不走。他若把遗嘱弄到了手，毁了它，或是藏了它，那么只消他闭得住嘴、顶得住阵，关于产业我们就一筹莫展。倘若有法子叫他开口，我想我们就能摸到底了。”

巴尔萨姆先生摇了摇头。他十分乐意相信自己的当事人卑劣如阿普约翰先生所描绘的那样；可他并不乐意相信，奇基先生的本事当真如人们认定的那般通天。

## XII Mr. Owen / 第十二章 欧文先生

===Original===
In this way Isabel spent four very uncomfortable weeks in her new home before Mr. Owen returned to Hereford. Nor was her discomfort much relieved by the prospect of his return. She knew all the details of his circumstances, and told herself that the man would be wrong to marry without any other means than those he at present possessed. Nor did she think of herself that she was well qualified to be the wife of a poor gentleman. She believed that she could starve if it were required of her, and support her sufferings with fortitude. She believed that she could work—work from morning till night, from week to week, from month to month, without complaining; but she did not think that she could make herself sweet as a wife should be sweet to a husband with a threadbare coat, or that she could be tender as a mother should be tender while dividing limited bread among her children. To go and die and have done with it, if that might be possible, was the panacea of her present troubles most commonly present to her mind. Therefore, there was no comfort to her in that promised coming of her lover of which the girls chattered to her continually. She had refused her lover when she held the proud position of the heiress of Llanfeare—refused him, no doubt, in obedience to her uncle's word, and not in accordance with her own feelings; but still she had refused him. Afterwards, when she had believed that there would be a sum of money coming to her from her uncle's will, there had been room for possible doubt. Should the money have proved sufficient to cause her to be a relief rather than a burden to the husband, it might have been her duty to marry him, seeing that she loved him with all her heart—seeing that she was sure of his love. There would have been much against it even then, because she had refused him when she had been a grand lady; but, had the money been forthcoming, there might have been a doubt. Now there could be no doubt. Should she who had denied him her hand because she was her uncle's heiress—on that avowed ground alone—should she, now that she was a pauper, burden him with her presence? He, no doubt, would be generous enough to renew his offer. She was well aware of his nobility. But she, too, could be generous, and, as she thought, noble. Thus it was that her spirit spoke within her, bidding her subject all the sweet affections of her heart to a stubborn pride.

The promised return, therefore, of Mr. Owen did not make her very happy.

===Chinese===
就这样，在欧文先生（Mr. Owen）回到赫里福德（Hereford）之前，伊莎贝尔（Isabel）在新家度过了极不自在的四个星期。而一想到他行将归来，她的不自在也并未因此稍减。她对他的境况知根知底，心里明白：一个人若除手头现有的一点进项之外别无恒产，便不该结婚。她也不认为自己有本领做好一位穷绅士的妻子。她相信自己若是非挨饿不可，便挨得下去，还能以坚忍撑住苦难；她相信自己能够做工——从早做到晚，从这周做到下周，从这个月做到下个月，毫无怨言；可她不自认为能对一个衣衫寒酸的丈夫，做得像妻子对该丈夫该有的那样温存，也不自认为能在给孩子们分有限面包的时候，做得像母亲对孩子该有的那样慈爱。若有可能，索性一走、一死、一了百了——这是她心头最常浮现的、医治眼下诸般苦恼的万灵药。因此，妹妹们喋喋不休向她念叨她那位情人许诺的归来，在她听来毫无慰藉可言。她拒绝过她的情人，那还是她身居兰菲尔（Llanfeare）继承人这一令人骄傲的地位之时——毫无疑问，她的拒绝是遵从伯父之言，并非出于本心；但她终究拒绝过他。后来，她一度以为伯父的遗嘱会给她留下一笔钱，那时事情尚有可疑的余地。倘若那笔钱足以使她成为丈夫的助力而非拖累，那么嫁给他或许倒是她的本分——须知她全心爱着他，也确知他爱她。即便在那时，反对的理由也还很多，因为她曾以阔小姐的身份拒绝过他；但倘若那笔钱当真到手，事情或许还可以两说。如今却再无可疑。她当初拒绝把手许给他，唯一公然说出口的理由，就是她是伯父的继承人；如今她成了穷光蛋，难道反倒要以自己的存在去拖累他？他无疑会慷慨到再度求婚。他的高尚她一清二楚。可她也能慷慨，而且——她自认为——也能高尚。她心中就是这个声音在说话，吩咐她把满心甜蜜的爱恋统统屈从于一种固执的骄傲。

所以，欧文先生许诺的归来并没有使她怎么快活。

===Original===
“He will be here tomorrow,” said her stepmother to her. “Mrs. Richards expects him by the late train tonight. I looked in there yesterday and she told me.” Mrs. Richards was the respectable lady with whom Mr. Owen lodged.

“I dare say he will,” said Isabel wearily—sorry, too, that Mr. Owen's goings and comings should have been investigated.

“Now, Isabel, let me advise you. You cannot be so unjust to Mr. Owen as to make him fancy for a moment that you will refuse your uncle's money. Think of his position—about two hundred and fifty a year in all! With your two hundred added it would be positive comfort; without it you would be frightfully poor.”

“Do you think I have not thought of it?”

“I suppose you must. But then you are so odd and so hard, so unlike any other girl I ever saw. I don't see how you could have the face to refuse the money, and then to eat his bread.”

===Chinese===
“他明天就到，”继母对她说，“理查兹太太（Mrs. Richards）等他今夜乘末班车到她家。我昨天顺路过去坐了坐，是她告诉我的。”理查兹太太是欧文先生寄宿那家的可敬女主人。

“那他多半会到，”伊莎贝尔倦倦地说；欧文先生的来踪去迹竟也遭人打听，她心里也很不是滋味。

“好了，伊莎贝尔，听我一句劝。你不该对欧文先生这样不公道，叫他哪怕有一会儿工夫疑心你会拒绝你伯父的钱。替他的处境想想——统共一年两百五十镑！添上你的两百镑，日子就能过得实实在在舒坦；没有这两百镑，他就穷得可怕了。”

“你以为我没有想过吗？”

“我想你总归想过。可你就是这么古怪，这么硬心肠，跟我见过的哪个姑娘都不一样。我不明白，你怎么有脸先拒绝那笔钱，回头倒去吃他的面包。”

===Original===
This was an unfortunate speech as coming from Mrs. Brodrick, because it fortified Isabel in the reply she was bound to make. Hitherto the stepmother had thought it certain that the marriage would take place in spite of such maiden denials as the girl had made; but now the denial had to be repeated with more than maiden vigour.

“I have thought of it,” said Isabel—“thought of it very often, till I have told myself that conduct such as that would be inexpressibly base. What! to eat his bread after refusing him mine when it was believed to be so plentiful! I certainly have not face enough to do that—neither face nor courage for that. There are ignoble things which require audacity altogether beyond my reach.”

“Then you must accept the money from your cousin.”

“Certainly not,” said Isabel; “neither that nor yet the position which Mr. Owen will perhaps offer me again.”

“Of course he will offer it to you.”

===Chinese===
这话出自布罗德里克太太（Mrs. Brodrick）之口，实在失策，因为它反倒叫伊莎贝尔把非说不可的回答说得愈发斩钉截铁。到此时为止，继母一直认定，尽管姑娘说过那些少女式的回绝之辞，婚事终究是十拿九稳的；可如今，这回绝不得不用远超少女口气的力度重说一遍了。

“我想过，”伊莎贝尔说——“常常想，想到后来我只能对自己说：那样的行径卑鄙得没法形容。什么！人人都以为我的面包多得很的时候，我拒绝了他，回头倒去吃他的面包！我可没有那样的脸皮——既没有那样的脸皮，也没有那样的胆量。有些卑贱事，所要的无耻气魄，我压根儿够不着。”

“那你就得收下你表弟的钱。”

“当然不收，”伊莎贝尔说，“钱不收，欧文先生或许会再次向我提出的那名分，我也不要。”

“他当然是会向你提的。”

===Original===
“Then he must be told that on no consideration can his offer be accepted.”

“This is nonsense. You are both dying for each other.”

“Then we must die. But as for that, I think that neither men nor young women die for love nowadays. If we love each other, we must do without each other, as people have to learn to do without most of the things that they desire.”

“I never heard of such nonsense, such wickedness! There is the money. Why should you not take it?”

===Chinese===
“那就得明明白白告诉他：无论怎么讲，他的提议都不能接受。”

“这是胡话。你们两个都在为对方要死要活。”

“那就死好了。不过说到这个，我看如今无论男人还是姑娘，都不再为爱情去死了。我们若彼此相爱，就得彼此将就着没有对方过活，就像人对自己想要的大多数东西，都不得不学着过没有它们的日子。”

“我从没听过这样的混账话，这样造孽的话！钱就在那儿，你为什么不拿？”

===Original===
“I can explain to you, mother,” she said sternly, it being her wont to give the appellation but very seldom to her stepmother, “why I should not take Mr. Owen, but I cannot tell you why I cannot take my cousin's money. I can only simply assure you that I will not do so, and that I most certainly shall never marry any man who would accept it.”

“I consider that to be actual wickedness—wickedness against your own father.”

“I have told papa. He knows I will not have the money.”

“Do you mean to say that you will come here into this house as an additional burden, as a weight upon your poor father's shoulder, when you have it in your power to relieve him altogether? Do you not know how pressed he is, and that there are your brothers to be educated?” Isabel, as she listened to this, sat silent, looking upon the ground, and her stepmother went on, understanding nothing of the nature of the mind of her whom she was addressing. “He had reason to expect, ample reason, that you would never cost him a shilling. He had been told a hundred times that you would be provided for by your uncle. Do you not know that it was so?”

“I do. I told him so myself when I was last here before Uncle Indefer's death.”

===Chinese===
“妈妈，我为什么不该接受欧文先生，这我可以对你讲明白，”她正色说——这个称呼她平日是很轻易不肯用在继母身上的——“可我为什么不能拿表弟的钱，这个我说不上来。我只能简单地向你保证：这事我决计不做；而且谁若接受了那笔钱，我肯定永远不会嫁给他。”

“我认为这就是十足的造孽——造你亲生父亲的孽。”

“爸爸我已经说过了。他知道我不要那笔钱。”

“你的意思是，明明有本领把他彻底解脱出来，却偏要进这个家门，做一份额外的负担，做压在你可怜父亲肩头的一块重石？你不知道他手头多么紧，你的弟弟们还等着受教育吗？”伊莎贝尔听着这些，默然坐着，两眼望着地面；继母却继续往下说，对听话人怎样的心性一无所知。“他满有理由指望——十足的理由——你绝不会花他一个先令。人家跟他讲过一百遍，你伯父会为你作安排。你不知道是这样的吗？”

“我知道。因德弗伯父（Uncle Indefer）去世前我上一回住在这里的时候，这话还是我亲口对他讲的。”

===Original===
“And yet you will do nothing to relieve him? You will refuse this money, though it is your own, when you could be married to Mr. Owen tomorrow?” Then she paused, waiting to find what might be the effect of her eloquence.

“I do not acknowledge papa's right or yours to press me to marry any man.”

“But I suppose you acknowledge your right to be as good as your word? Here is the money; you have only got to take it.”

“What you mean is that I ought to acknowledge my obligation to be as good as my word. I do. I told my father that I would not be a burden to him, and I am bound to keep to that. He will have understood that at the present moment I am breaking my promise through a mistake of Uncle Indefer's which I could not have anticipated.”

===Chinese===
“可你竟不肯为他解一点围？明明明天就能嫁给欧文先生，却偏要拒绝这笔钱，尽管它是你自己的？”说到这里她停了停，想看看这番雄辩起了什么效力。

“爸爸也好，你也好，都无权逼我嫁给什么人，这一点我不认。”

“可我想，你总认你自己说话算话的本分吧？钱就在这儿，你只消伸手拿就是。”

“你的意思是，我应当认下自己有说话算话的义务。这个我认。我跟父亲说过不给他添负担，我就非守住这句话不可。他总会明白，眼下我之所以要失信，是因德弗伯父一时的失误所致，那是我无论如何预料不到的。”

===Original===
“You are breaking your promise because you will not accept money that is your own.”

“I am breaking my promise, and that is sufficient. I will go out of the house and will cease to be a burden. If I only knew where I could go, I would begin tomorrow.”

“That is all nonsense,” said Mrs. Brodrick, getting up and bursting out of the room in anger. “There is a man ready to marry you, and there is the money. Anybody can see with half an eye what is your duty.”

===Chinese===
“你失信，只因为你不肯收下本来属于你自己的钱。”

“我是在失信，这够了。我会离开这个家，不再做负担。只要我知道能去哪里，明天就动身。”

“一派胡言，”布罗德里克太太说着站起身，怒气冲冲夺门而出。“有人等着娶你，钱也摆在那儿。你的本分是什么，随便哪个闭着一只眼都看得出来。”

===Original===
Isabel, with all the eyes that she had, could not see what was her duty. That it could not be her duty to take a present of money from the man whom she believed to be robbing her of the estate she felt quite sure. It could not be her duty to bring poverty on a man whom she loved—especially not as she had refused to confer wealth upon him. It was, she thought, clearly her duty not to be a burden upon her father, as she had told him that no such burden should fall upon him. It was her duty, she thought, to earn her own bread, or else to eat none at all. In her present frame of mind she would have gone out of the house on the moment if anyone would have accepted her even as a kitchenmaid. But there was no one to accept her. She had questioned her father on the matter, and he had ridiculed her idea of earning her bread. When she had spoken of service, he had become angry with her. It was not thus that he could be relieved. He did not want to see his girl a maidservant or even a governess. It was not thus that she could relieve him. He simply wanted to drive her into his views, so that she might accept the comfortable income which was at her disposal, and become the wife of a gentleman whom everyone esteemed. But she, in her present frame of mind, cared little for any disgrace she might bring on others by menial service. She was told that she was a burden, and she desired to cease to be burdensome.

===Chinese===
伊莎贝尔把两只眼睛都用上，也看不出自己的本分何在。有一点她确信无疑：从一个她认定正在掠夺她那份产业的人手里接受钱财馈赠，绝不可能是她的本分。让自己所爱的男人受穷，绝不可能是她的本分——尤其在她先前不肯把财富带给他之后。她认为，不给父亲添负担显然是她的本分，因为她亲口说过，不让这样的负担落到他身上。她认为，自己挣面包吃，否则干脆不吃，才是她的本分。拿她眼下这种心绪，只要有人肯收她，哪怕当个厨房女佣，她立刻就会离开这个家。可是没有人肯收她。这事她问过父亲，他却笑话她想自己挣面包的念头。她一提出去帮佣，他就对她发起火来。他不是靠这个法子解脱的。他不愿看见自己的女儿去做婢下女仆，连家庭女教师也不愿。她靠这个是解脱不了他的。他无非是想把她逼进他那套打算里去，好让她接受那份唾手可得的舒坦进项，嫁给一位人人敬重的绅士。可在她如今的心绪里，哪怕做伺候人的粗活会给旁人招来什么耻辱，她也全不放在心上。人家说她是个负担，她就想不再成为负担。

===Original===
Thinking it over all that night, she resolved that she would consult Mr. Owen himself. It would, she thought, be easy—or if not easy at any rate feasible—to make him understand that there could be no marriage. With him she would be on her own ground. He, at least, had no authority over her, and she knew herself well enough to be confident of her own strength. Her father had a certain right to insist. Even her stepmother had a deputed right. But her lover had none. He should be made to understand that she would not marry him—and then he could advise her as to that project of being governess, housemaid, schoolmistress, or whatnot.

===Chinese===
那一夜她想来想去，拿定主意要同欧文先生本人商量。她想着，要叫他明白婚事断无可能，总归容易——纵使不容易，至少办得到。同他打交道，她是在自己的地盘上。他至少对她没有任何支配之权，而她深知自己的性子，相信自己有这份力量。父亲固然有几分坚持的权利，继母也有一份转托的权利，唯独情人没有。得叫他明白她不会嫁他——然后，做家庭女教师也好，做女仆也好，做学堂女教师也好，还是别的什么，他尽可以就这些打算给她出主意。

===Original===
On the following morning he came, and was soon closeted with her. When he arrived, Isabel was sitting with Mrs. Brodrick and her sisters, but they soon packed up their hemmings and sewings, and took themselves off, showing that it was an understood thing that Isabel and Mr. Owen were to be left together. The door was no sooner closed than he came up to her, as though to embrace her, as though to put an arm round her waist before she had a moment to retreat, preparing to kiss her as though she were already his own. She saw it all in a moment. It was as though, since her last remembered interview, there had been some other meeting which she had forgotten—some meeting at which she had consented to be his wife. She could not be angry with him. How can a girl be angry with a man whose love is so good, so true? He would not have dreamed of kissing her had she stood there before him the declared heiress of Llanfeare. She felt more than this. She was sure by his manner that he knew that she had determined not to take her cousin's money. She was altogether unaware that there had already been some talking that morning between him and her father; but she was sure that he knew. How could she be angry with him?

But she escaped. “No, not that,” she said. “It must not be so, Mr. Owen;—it must not. It cannot be so.”

===Chinese===
次日早晨他来了，不多时便同她闭门独处。他到的时候，伊莎贝尔正陪布罗德里克太太和妹妹们坐着，可她们不多一会儿就各自收起锁边缝纫的针线活，自顾自走开了，可见留下伊莎贝尔同欧文先生单独在一起，已是彼此心照的事。门刚一关上，他就朝她走近，像要拥抱她，像要趁她来不及退避就一条胳膊揽住她的腰，摆出要吻她的架势，仿佛她早已是他的人。她刹那间把一切都看在眼里。仿佛自她记忆所及的上次会面之后，还另有过一次她忘掉了的相会——一次她答应了做他妻子的相会。她没法对他生气。一个姑娘家，面对一个爱情这样好、这样真的男人，怎么气得起来？倘若站在他面前的还是那位名正言顺的兰菲尔继承人，他是做梦也不会想到吻她的。她感觉到的还不止于此。从他的神态看，她确信他知道她已拿定主意不拿表弟的钱。她全然不知那天早晨他已同她父亲谈过一番；但她确信他知道。她怎么气得起来呢？

可她还是躲开了。“不，别这样，”她说。“不行的，欧文先生；——不行的。断然不行。”

===Original===
“Tell me one thing, Isabel, before we go any further, and tell me truly. Do you love me?”

She was standing about six feet from him, and she looked hard into his face, determined not to blush before his eyes for a moment. But she could hardly make up her mind as to what would be the fitting answer to his demand.

“I know,” said he, “that you are too proud to tell me a falsehood.”

“I will not tell you a falsehood.”

“Do you love me?” There was still a pause. “Do you love me as a woman should love the man she means to marry?”

“I do love you!”

===Chinese===
“伊莎贝尔，往下说之前，先答我一句话，老实答我。你爱我吗？”

她站在离他约六英尺的地方，紧紧盯着他的脸，打定主意不在他眼前有一刻红脸。可他这一问该怎么答才得体，她心里几乎拿不定主意。

“我知道，”他说，“你这么傲气，绝不会对我说假话。”

“我不会对你说假话。”

“你爱我吗？”仍旧沉默了片刻。“你爱我，是不是像一个女人爱她打算嫁的那个男人那样爱我？”

“我是爱你的！”

===Original===
“Then, in God's name, why should we not kiss? You are my love and I am yours. Your father and mother are satisfied that it should be so. Seeing that we are so, is it a disgrace to kiss? Having won your heart, may I not have the delight of thinking that you would wish me to be near you?”

“You must know it all,” she said, “though it may be unwomanly to tell so much.”

“Know what?”

“There has never been a man whose touch has been pleasant to me;—but I could revel in yours. Kiss you? I could kiss your feet at this moment, and embrace your knees. Everything belonging to you is dear to me. The things you have touched have been made sacred to me. The Prayerbook tells the young wife that she should love her husband till death shall part them. I think my love will go further than that.”

“Isabel! Isabel!”

“Keep away from me! I will not even give you my hand to shake till you have promised to be of one mind with me. I will not become your wife.”

===Chinese===
“那么，看在上帝分上，我们为什么不能亲吻？你是我的心上人，我也是你的。你父亲母亲都乐意如此。既然如此，亲吻难道是丢脸的事？我既已赢得你的心，难道不能满心欢喜地想你愿意我守在你身边吗？”

“你总得知道全部，”她说，“虽说把这些说出来，也许有失女子的体统。”

“知道什么？”

“从来没有任何男人的触碰叫我受用；——可你的触碰，我能为之陶醉。吻你？这会儿我恨不得吻你的脚，抱你的双膝。凡属于你的，我都珍爱。你碰过的东西，在我眼里都成了圣物。《祈祷书》教年轻妻子爱丈夫，直到死亡将两人分开。我想我的爱会比那更长远。”

“伊莎贝尔！伊莎贝尔！”

“离我远一点！不等你答应同我一条心，我连手也不给你握。我不会做你的妻子。”

===Original===
“You shall become my wife!”

“Never! Never! I have thought it out, and I know that I am right. Things have been hard with me.”

“Not to me! They will not have been hard to me when I shall have carried my point with you.”

“I was forced to appear before your eyes as the heiress of my uncle.”

“Has that made any difference with me?”

“And I was forced to refuse you in obedience to him who had adopted me.”

===Chinese===
“你一定会做我的妻子！”

“决不！决不！这事我想透了，我知道自己没有错。我的日子一直很难。”

“对我可不难！等我把你争过来了，就更算不得难了。”

“我在你眼前，一直是被迫以伯父继承人的身份出现的。”

“那在我这儿起过什么作用吗？”

“而我拒绝你，也是迫不得已，只为顺从那位收养我的人。”

===Original===
“I understand all that very completely.”

“Then he made a new will, and left me some money.”

“Of all that I know, I think, every particular.”

“But the money is not there.” At this he nodded his head as though smiling at her absurdity in going back over circumstances which were so well understood by both of them. “The money is offered to me by my cousin, but I will not take it.”

“As to that I have nothing to say. It is the one point on which, when we are married, I shall decline to give you any advice.”

“Mr. Owen,” and now she came close to him, but still ready to spring back should it be necessary, “Mr. Owen, I will tell you what I have told no one else.”

===Chinese===
“这些我全都明白得很。”

“后来他另立了一份新遗嘱，给我留了些钱。”

“这些我想我全都知道，连细枝末节也一清二楚。”

“可是那笔钱不在了。”听到这里，他点了点头，像是笑她傻气，把两人早已了然于胸的原委又从头数说一遍。“表弟把钱送到了我面前，可我不拿。”

“这件事我无可置喙。等你我成了婚，这是唯一一点我不肯给你出主意的。”

“欧文先生，”这回她凑近了他，却仍随时准备在必要时就弹开去，“欧文先生，有件事我谁也没有告诉过，我要告诉你。”

===Original===
“Why me?”

“Because I trust you as I trust no one else.”

“Then tell me.”

“There is another will. There was another will rather, and he has destroyed it.”

“Why do you say that? You should not say that. You cannot know it.”

“And, therefore, I say it only to you, as I would to my own heart. The old man told me so—in his last moments. And then there is the look of the man. If you could have seen how his craven spirit cowered beneath my eyes!”

===Chinese===
“为什么告诉我？”

“因为我信你，胜过信任何人。”

“那就说吧。”

“还有一份遗嘱。不如说，曾有过另一份遗嘱，他把它毁了。”

“你怎么能这样讲？这话不该讲。你不可能知道。”

“所以这话我只对你说，就像只对自己的心说。老人家亲口对我讲的——在他弥留的时刻。再说还有他那个神情。你若亲眼见过他那怯懦的灵魂在我目光底下如何瑟缩就好了！”

===Original===
“One should not judge by such indications. One cannot but see them and notice them; but one should not judge.”

“You would have judged had you seen. You could not have helped judging. Nothing, however, can come of it, except this—that not for all the world would I take his money.”

“It may be right, Isabel, that all that should be discussed between you and me—right if you wish it. It will be my delight to think that there shall be no secret between us. But, believe me, dearest, it can have no reference to the question between us.”

“Not that I should be absolutely penniless?”

“Not in the least.”

“But it will, Mr. Owen. In that even my father agrees with me.” In this she was no doubt wrong. Her father had simply impressed upon her the necessity of taking the money because of her lover's needs. “I will not be a burden at any rate to you; and as I cannot go to you without being a burden, I will not go at all. What does it matter whether there be a little more suffering or a little less? What does it matter?”

===Chinese===
“人不该凭这种迹象下论断。迹象摆在那里，叫人不能不看见、不能不留意；可人不该据此论断。”

“你见了也会论断的。你没法不论断。不过这事不会有什么结果，只有一样——他的钱，拿整个世界来换我也不拿。”

“这些事你我之间尽可以谈，伊莎贝尔——只要你愿意，谈是对的。想到你我之间从此没有秘密，我会满心欢喜。可是相信我，最亲爱的，这同我们之间的那个问题毫不相干。”

“同我分文全无这件事不相干吗？”

“丝毫不相干。”

“可是相干，欧文先生。在这一点上连我父亲也同我想的一样。”这一点她无疑弄错了。她父亲不过是念着情人的窘迫，才再三跟她强调那笔钱非拿不可。“至少我不做你的负担；既然去了就必然是负担，那我索性不去。多受一点苦或少受一点苦，又有什么要紧？有什么要紧呢？”

===Original===
“It matters a great deal to me.”

“A man gets over that quickly, I think.”

“So does a woman—if she be the proper sort of woman for getting over her difficulties of that kind. I don't think you are.”

“I will try.”
