“I won't.” This he said, looking full into her face. “My philosophy teaches me to despise the grapes which hang too high, but to make the most of those which come within my reach. Now, I look upon you as being within my reach.”

“I am not within your reach.”

===Chinese===
“对我可要紧得很。”

“我想，男人这种痛苦好得快。”

“女人也一样——如果她是那种善于从这类难处中自拔的女人。我看你不是。”

“我会试着去习惯。”

“我不试。”他说这话时直直地望着她的脸。“我的人生哲学教我蔑视挂得太高的葡萄，却要好好受用够得着的那些。眼下，我就把你看作我够得着的。”

“我可不是你够得着的。”

===Original===
“Yes; pardon me for my confidence, but you are. You have confessed that you love me.”

“I do.”

“Then you will not be so wicked as to deny to me that which I have a right to demand? If you love me as a woman should love the man who is to become her husband, you have no right to refuse me. I have made good my claim, unless there be other reasons.”

“There is a reason.”

“None but such as I have to judge of. Had your father objected, that would have been a reason; or when your uncle disapproved because of the property, that was a reason. As to the money, I will never ask you to take it, unless you can plead that you yourself are afraid of the poverty—.” Then he paused, looking at her as though he defied her to say so much on her own behalf. She could not say that, but sat there panting, frightened by his energy.

“Nor am I,” he continued very gently, “the least in the world. Think of it, and you will find that I am right; and then, when next I come, then, perhaps, you will not refuse to kiss me.” And so he went.

===Chinese===
“是的；恕我自信，你是的。你已经承认你爱我。”

“我是爱你。”

“那么，你总不至于狠心到这样不义，把我有权要求的东西也拒绝给我吧？你若像一个女人爱她将嫁的丈夫那样爱我，你就没有权利拒绝我。我的名分已经立定了，除非另有别的缘故。”

“有一个缘故。”

“除了由我来判断的缘故，别的都不算。倘若你父亲反对，那算一个缘故；从前你伯父为了那份产业不赞成，那也算一个缘故。至于钱，我绝不会叫你拿，除非你能辩白说，你自己害怕贫穷——”说到这里他停住了，望着她，仿佛存心激她，看她敢不敢为自己说出这样的话来。这话她说不出口，只是坐在那里气喘吁吁，叫他这股气势吓住了。

“至于我自己，”他接着说，语气十分温和，“也半点儿不怕。你想想吧，就会明白我是对的；等下回我来，那时候，你或许就不肯拒绝吻我了。”说完他便走了。

===Original===
Oh, how she loved him! How sweet would it be to submit her pride, her independence, her maiden reticences to such a man as that! How worthy was he of all worship, of all confidence, of all service! How definitely better was he than any other being that had ever crossed her path! But yet she was quite sure that she would not marry him.

===Chinese===
啊，她多么爱他！把她的骄傲、她的独立、她少女的矜持缄默，统统交付给这样一个人，该是多么甜美！他配得上一切的敬慕、一切的信赖、一切的侍奉！比起她生平所遇的任何一个人，他分明都要好出一大截！然而她仍旧十分确定：她不会嫁他。

## V Preparing for the Funeral / 第五章 准备葬礼

===Original===
Isabel, when she was left alone, felt that a terrible weight of duty was imposed on her. She seemed to be immediately encompassed by a double world of circumstances. There was that world of grief which was so natural, but which would yet be easy, could she only be allowed to sit down and weep. But it was explained to her that until after the funeral, and till the will should have been read, everything about Llanfeare must be done by her and in obedience to her orders. This necessity of action—of action which in her present condition of mind did not seem clear to her—was not at all easy.

The doctor was good to her, and gave her some instruction before he left her. “Shall I give the keys to my cousin?” she said to him. But even as she said this there was the doubt on her mind what those last words of her uncle had been intended to mean. Though her grief was very bitter, though her sorrow was quite sincere, she could not keep herself from thinking of those words. It was not that she was anxious to get the estate for herself. It was hardly in that way that the matter in these moments presented itself to her. Did the meaning of those words impose on her any duty? Would it be right that she should speak of them, or be silent? Ought she to suppose that they had any meaning, and if so, that they referred to the will?

“I think that you should keep the keys till after the will has been read,” said the doctor.

“Even though he should ask for them?”

===Chinese===
众人散尽、只余她一个人时，伊莎贝尔（Isabel）觉得有一副可怕的担子压上了自己的肩头。她仿佛立时被两重境遇之网围裹起来。一重是哀伤之境——这本是再自然不过的事，而且原不难应付，只要容她坐下来痛哭一场便是。可人们向她说明白了：在葬礼过后、遗嘱宣读之前，兰菲尔（Llanfeare）的一切事务都必须由她来操办，都得听凭她的吩咐。这种行动的必要——以她此刻的心境而论，这种必要于她似乎全然莫名其妙——实在一点也不轻松。

医生待她很好，临走之前给了她一番指点。“钥匙，我要不要交给我表哥？”她对医生说。可话一出口，她心里便又浮起那层疑窦：伯父那几句临终的话，究竟是要表示什么意思。她的悲恸尽管深创，哀伤尽管纯真，那几句话却在她心头萦绕，排遣不开。倒不是她急着想把产业弄到自己手里。此刻在她心里，这件事几乎全不是从这个路数上呈现的。那几句话的意思，可曾把什么本分加在她身上？把它们说出来是对的，还是缄默是对的？她可该认定那些话另有含意——倘若有的话——又可是指着那份遗嘱说的？

“我看，钥匙你应当留着，等遗嘱宣读过了再说，”医生说。

“哪怕他来要呢？”

===Original===
“Even though he should ask for them,” said the doctor. “He will not press such a request if you tell him that I say it ought to be so. If there be any difficulty, send for Mr. Apjohn.”

Mr. Apjohn was the lawyer; but there had been quite lately some disagreement between her uncle and Mr. Apjohn, and this advice was not palatable to her.

“But,” continued Dr. Powell, “you will not find any difficulty of that kind. The funeral had better be on Monday. And the will, I suppose, can be read afterwards. Mr. Apjohn will come out and read it. There can be no difficulty about that. I know that Mr. Apjohn’s feelings are of the kindest towards your uncle and yourself.”

Mr. Apjohn had taken upon himself to “scold” her uncle because of the altered will—the will that had been altered in favour of Cousin Henry. So much the old man had said to Isabel himself. “If I think it proper, he has no right to scold me,” the old man had said. The “scolding” had probably been in the guise of that advice which a lawyer so often feels himself justified in giving.

===Chinese===
“哪怕他来要，也照样不交，”医生说，“只要你告诉他，说这是我的意见、理当如此，他就不会苦苦相求了。倘若遇上什么难处，就打发人去请阿普约翰先生（Mr. Apjohn）。”

阿普约翰先生是家里的律师；可就在不久之前，伯父同他还闹过一场龃龉，这番忠告她听着实在不受用。

“不过，”鲍威尔医生（Dr. Powell）接着说，“那样的难处你不会碰上。葬礼顶好定在星期一。至于遗嘱，我想随后就可以宣读，到时由阿普约翰先生来府上宣读。这件事不会有难处。我知道，阿普约翰先生对你伯父、对你，情分都是顶厚的。”

为了那份改动过的遗嘱——那份改得有利于亨利表哥（Cousin Henry）的遗嘱——阿普约翰先生曾自作主张地把伯父“训”了一顿。这情形是老人亲口对伊莎贝尔说过的。“只要我自己觉得妥当，他就没权利训我，”老人当时这样说。那番“训斥”，多半不过是律师时常自觉有理由进的那种忠告，换了个名目罢了。

===Original===
Isabel thought that she had better keep those words to herself, at any rate for the present. She almost resolved that she would keep those words altogether to herself, unless other facts should come out which would explain their meaning and testify to their truths. She would say nothing of them in a way that would seem to imply that she had been led by them to conceive that she expected the property. She did certainly think that they alluded to the property. “It is all right. It is done.” When her uncle had uttered these words, using the last effort of his mortal strength for the purpose, he no doubt was thinking of the property. He had meant to imply that he had done something to make his last decision “right” in her favour. She was, she thought, sure of so much. But then she bore in mind the condition of the old man’s failing mind—those wandering thoughts which would so naturally endeavour to fix themselves upon her and upon the property in combination with each other. How probable was it that he would dream of something that he would fain do, and then dream that he had done it! And she knew, too, as well as the lawyer would know himself, that the words would go for nothing, though they had been spoken before a dozen witnesses. If a later will was there, the later will would speak for itself. If no later will was there, the words were empty breath.

But above all was she anxious that no one should think that she was desirous of the property—that no one should suppose that she would be hurt by not having it. She was not desirous, and was not hurt. The matter was so important, and had so seriously burdened her uncle’s mind, that she could not but feel the weight herself; but as to her own desires, they were limited to a wish that her uncle’s will, whatever it might be, should be carried out. Not to have Llanfeare, not to have even a shilling from her uncle’s estate, would hurt her but little—would hurt her heart not at all. But to know that it was thought by others that she was disappointed—that would be a grievous burden to her! Therefore she spoke to Dr. Powell, and even to her cousin, as though the estate were doubtless now the property of the latter.

Henry Jones at this time—during the days immediately following his uncle’s death—seemed to be so much awestruck by his position, as to be incapable of action. To his Cousin Isabel he was almost servile in his obedience. With bated breath he did suggest that the keys should be surrendered to him, making his proposition simply on the ground that she would thus be saved from trouble; but when she told him that it was her duty to keep them till after the funeral, and that it would be her duty to act as mistress in the house till after that ceremony, he was cringing in his compliance.

===Chinese===
伊莎贝尔想，那几句话她顶好先搁在自己心里，至少眼下是这样。她几乎拿定了主意：除非日后有别的事实显露出来，既能说明那些话的含意，又能证实那些话不假，否则她要把那些话全然守在心底。她说起那些话的时候，决不肯带一点口风，叫人以为她听了那几句话，便自以为要得这份产业了。那些话指的确实是产业，这一点她心中笃定。“全都妥了。事情办成了。”伯父说这几句话的时候，拿出了他弥留之际气力的最后一搏，他心里想的无疑是那份产业。他是要表示：他已经做了一点事，把他最后的决定改“妥”了，改成于她有利。她想，她所笃定的也就到此为止。可转念她又记起老人那日渐衰竭的神智——那些恍惚游移的念头，偏偏最自然不过地总要往她身上缠，又同那份产业缠作一团。他一心想做一桩事，遂在恍惚之间自以为已经做成了——这有多么可能！而且她同律师本人一样明白：那几句话纵然当着十二个见证人说出口，也是算不得数的。倘若那里有一份后立的遗嘱，后立的遗嘱自己会说话。倘若没有后立的遗嘱，那些话便不过是一口空空的气。

可顶要紧的是，她切切盼望谁也不要以为她贪图那份产业——谁也不要以为她得不到它便会委屈难受。她并不贪图，也不委屈。这件事干系重大，又曾那样沉重地压过伯父的心，她自己也不可能不觉出那份份量；至于她自己的心愿，不过限于一点：伯父的遗嘱，无论写的是什么，都应当照办。得不到兰菲尔，伯父的产业里连一个先令也拿不着，这对她伤害甚小——伤到她心坎上的，分毫也没有。可要是她知道旁人认定她失望了——那才是压在她身上的一副重担！所以她对鲍威尔医生说话，甚至对她表哥说话，都只当那份产业眼下无疑已经归了后者。

亨利·琼斯（Henry Jones）在这几天里——就在伯父故去后的那几日——仿佛叫自己的处境吓破了胆，竟至一事做不来。对自己的表妹伊莎贝尔，他服服帖帖，几乎近于奴颜婢膝。他倒是屏着气息、怯生生地提过一回，说钥匙理应交到他手上，提出的理由也只有一条：这样她可以省些麻烦；可等她告诉他，把钥匙留到葬礼过后是她的本分，而且在那个仪式之前，宅子里当家主妇的职分也落在她身上，他便卑屈地百依百顺了。

===Original===
“Whatever you think best, Isabel, shall be done. I would not interfere for a moment.”

Then some time afterwards, on the following day, he assured her that whatever might be the nature of the will, she was to regard Llanfeare as her home as long as it would suit her to remain there.

“I shall go back to papa very soon,” she had said, “as soon, indeed, as I can have my things packed up after the funeral. I have already written to papa to say so.”

“Everything shall be just as you please,” he replied; “only, pray, believe that if I can do anything for your accommodation it shall be done.”

To this she made some formal answer of courtesy, not, it may be feared, very graciously. She did not believe in his civility; she did not think he was kind to her in heart, and she could not bring herself to make her manner false to her feelings. After that, during the days that remained before the funeral, very little was said between them. Her dislike to him grew in bitterness, though she failed to explain even to herself the cause of her dislike. She did know that her uncle had been in truth as little disposed to love him as herself, and that knowledge seemed to justify her. Those last words had assured her at any rate of that, and though she was quite sure of her own conscience in regard to Llanfeare, though she was certain that she did not covet the possession of the domain, still she was unhappy to think that it should become his. If only for the tenants’ sake and the servants, and the old house itself, there were a thousand pities in that. And then the belief would intrude itself upon her that her uncle in the last expression of his wishes had not intended his nephew to be his heir.

===Chinese===
“伊莎贝尔，凡是你认为妥当的，一概照办。我断断不肯插一句嘴。”

过了一阵，到了第二天，他又向她担保：无论遗嘱写的是什么，只要她住得乐意，兰菲尔尽可以一直当作她的家。

“我不久就要回爸爸那里去，”她说，“真的，只要葬礼一过、我把东西收拾停当就走。这话我已经写信告诉爸爸了。”

“一切都随你高兴，”他答道；“只求你信得过，凡能为你的安逸方便尽一分力的，无不照办。”

对这话她客客气气地应了一声，只怕不见得怎么和婉。他那番殷勤她原不信；她认定他心里待她并不好，也决不肯叫自己的举止违了自己的心思，硬装出另一副样子来。自此以后，直到葬礼的那几日里，两人之间几乎无话。她对他的嫌恶一日苦似一日，虽则连她自己都说不清这嫌恶的缘故。她确乎知道，伯父在世时实在同她自己一样不喜欢他，这么一想，她的嫌恶仿佛便有了凭据。那几句遗言至少向她担保了这一点；而她尽管对兰菲尔一事问心无愧，尽管笃定自己并不贪图那片家业的归属，可一想到它终要落到他手里，终究快活不起来。单为那些佃户、那些仆人、还有那座老宅本身着想，这也是千般的可惜。而且还有一个念头硬往她心上挤：伯父最后吐露心愿的时候，原没打算叫自己的侄儿做继承人。

===Original===
Then, in these days reports reached her which seemed to confirm her own belief. It had not been the habit of her life to talk intimately with the servants, even though at Llanfeare there had been no other woman with whom she could talk intimately. There had been about her a sense of personal dignity which had made such freedom distasteful to herself, and had repressed it in them. But now the housekeeper had come to her with a story to which Isabel had found it impossible not to listen. It was reported about the place that the Squire had certainly executed another will a few days after Isabel had left Llanfeare.

“If so,” said Isabel sternly, “it will be found when Mr. Apjohn comes to open the papers.”

But the housekeeper did not seem satisfied with this. Though she believed that some document had been written, Mr. Apjohn had not been sent for, as had always been done on former similar occasions. The making of the Squire’s will had been a thing always known and well understood at Llanfeare. Mr. Apjohn had been sent for on such occasions, and had returned after a day or two, accompanied by two clerks. It was quite understood that the clerks were there to witness the will. The old butler, who would bring in the sherry and biscuits after the operation, was well acquainted with all the testamentary circumstances of the occasions. Nothing of that kind had occurred now; but old Joseph Cantor, who had been a tenant on the property for the last thirty years, and his son, Joseph Cantor the younger, had been called in, and it was supposed that they had performed the duty of witnessing the document. The housekeeper seemed to think that they, when interrogated, had declined to give any information on the subject. She herself had not seen them, but she had seen others of the tenants, and she was certain, she said, that Llanfeare generally believed that the old Squire had executed a will during the absence of his niece.

In answer to all this Isabel simply said that if a new will, which should turn out to be the real will, had actually been made, it would be found among her uncle’s papers. She knew well the manner in which those other wills had been tied and deposited in one of the drawers of her uncle’s tables. She had been invited to read them all, and had understood from a thousand assurances that he had wished that nothing should be kept secret from her. The key of the very drawer was at this moment in her possession. There was nothing to hinder her from searching, should she wish to search. But she never touched the drawer. The key which locked it she placed in an envelope, and put it apart under another lock and key. Though she listened, though she could not but listen, to the old woman’s narrative, yet she rebuked the narrator. “There should be no talking about such things,” she said. “It had been,” she said, “her uncle’s intention to make his nephew the owner of Llanfeare, and she believed that he had done so. It was better that there should be no conversation on the matter until the will had been read.”

===Chinese===
就在这几天里，有些传闻传到她耳朵里，似乎印证了她自己的看法。她素来的习惯，是不与仆人们亲密闲谈的，虽说在兰菲尔，她也再没有一个可以贴身说话的女人。她身上自有一种身份的尊严，使她自己厌于这般亲昵，也把他们的亲昵压了下去。可如今女管家来到她跟前，讲起一桩事，伊莎贝尔想不听也不能不听。宅子内外纷纷传说：伊莎贝尔离开兰菲尔才几天，乡绅便确乎又立下了一纸遗嘱。

“果真如此，”伊莎贝尔正色道，“等阿普约翰先生来开启文书的时候，自然找得出来。”

可女管家听了这话，似乎并不满足。她相信确实写过一份文书，可这一回却不曾打发人去请阿普约翰先生，而往昔遇到同类的事情，从来都是要请的。乡绅立遗嘱这件事，在兰菲尔向来是人所共知、人人心里有数的。遇到那种场合，总要把阿普约翰先生请来，住上一两天，随身带着两个书记员。人人都明白，那两个书记员便是来给遗嘱做见证的。仪式过后端上雪利酒和饼干的老男管家，对历次立遗嘱的种种情形也都熟稔得很。这一回却没有丝毫这样的动静；只是把老约瑟夫·坎托——他在这份产业上做了三十年佃户——和他儿子小约瑟夫·坎托唤了进去，想来给文书做见证这份差事，便是他们二位担当的了。女管家似乎觉得，这二人被问起来的时候，只推不知，不肯吐半个字的口风。她本人没有见过他们，却见过佃户里的旁人；她说她断定，兰菲尔上下都相信，老乡绅是在侄女不在家的那几天里立下遗嘱的。

对这一席话，伊莎贝尔只淡淡答道：倘若真立了一份新遗嘱，而它又终归算得真遗嘱的话，那它自会在伯父的文书堆里找出来。那些旧遗嘱是怎样扎好、收在她伯父桌子的一只抽屉里的，她知道得清清楚楚。伯父曾请她把它们一一读过，又再三再四地向她担保，说他对她决无一点秘密。那只抽屉的钥匙，此刻正在她手里。她要搜，谁也拦不住。可她从不去碰那只抽屉。开抽屉的那把钥匙，她装进一只信封，另锁在别处，又加上一道锁。老妇人的那番话，她虽听着——虽不能不听——却还是把讲这话的人申斥了一顿。“这种事情，不该拿到嘴上议论，”她说。“伯父的意思，”她说，“是要叫他的侄儿做兰菲尔的主人，她相信他已经这样办了。在遗嘱宣读之前，这桩事情顶好谁也不要提。”

===Original===
During these days she did not go beyond the precincts of the garden, and was careful not to encounter any of the tenants, even when they called at the house. Mr. Apjohn she did not see, nor Dr. Powell again, till the day of the funeral. The lawyer had written to her more than once, and had explained to her exactly the manner in which he intended to proceed. He, with Dr. Powell, would be at the house at eleven o’clock; the funeral would be over at half-past twelve; they would lunch at one, and immediately afterwards the will should be “looked for” and read. The words “looked for” were underscored in his letter, but no special explanation of the underscoring was given. He went on to say that the tenants would, as a matter of course, attend the funeral, and that he had taken upon himself to invite some few of those who had known the Squire most intimately, to be present at the reading of the will. These he named, and among them were Joseph Cantor the elder, and Joseph Cantor the younger. It immediately occurred to Isabel that the son was not himself a tenant, and that no one else who was not a tenant was included in the list. From this she was sure that Mr. Apjohn had heard the story which the housekeeper had told her. During these days there was little or no intercourse between Isabel and her cousin. At dinner they met, but only at dinner, and even then almost nothing was said between them. What he did with himself during the day she did not even know. At Llanfeare there was a so-called book-room, a small apartment, placed between the drawing-room and the parlour, in which were kept the few hundred volumes which constituted the library of Llanfeare. It had not been much used by the late Squire except that from time to time he would enter it for the sake of taking down with his own hands some volume of sermons from the shelves. He himself had for years been accustomed to sit in the parlour, in which he ate his meals, and had hated the ceremony of moving even into the drawing-room. Isabel herself had a sitting-room of her own upstairs, and she, too, had never used the book-room. But here Cousin Henry had now placed himself, and here he remained through the whole day, though it was not believed of him that he was given to much reading. For his breakfast and his supper he went to the parlour alone. At dinner time Isabel came down. But through all the long hours of the day he remained among the books, never once leaving the house till the moment came for receiving Mr. Apjohn and Dr. Powell before the funeral. The housekeeper would say little words about him, wondering what he was doing in the book-room. To this Isabel would apparently pay no attention, simply remarking that it was natural that at such a time he should remain in seclusion.

“But he does get so very pale, Miss Isabel,” said the housekeeper. “He wasn’t white, not like that when he come first to Llanfeare.” To this Isabel made no reply; but she, too, had remarked how wan, how pallid, and how spiritless he had become.

===Chinese===
这些天里，她没有迈出花园的范围半步，佃户上门来，她也小心回避，不肯照面。葬礼那天之前，她没见过阿普约翰先生，也没再见过鲍威尔医生。律师不止一次给她写信，把他打算如何进行的步骤讲解得明明白白：他同鲍威尔医生十一点钟到宅子里；葬礼十二点半完毕；一点钟众人用午饭；随后立刻“寻找”遗嘱，加以宣读。“寻找”两个字，信里底下画了线，画线却并未附带什么特别的解释。他接着写道，佃户们照例要来送葬，而他自己做主，请了几位同乡绅交情最深的人到场听读遗嘱。这几位他都具了名，其中便有老坎托和小坎托——约瑟夫·坎托父子。伊莎贝尔立刻想到：做儿子的本人并不是佃户，而这名单里除他之外，再没有第二个不是佃户的人。由此她断定，女管家对她讲的那番话，阿普约翰先生已经听说了。这些日子里，伊莎贝尔同她表哥之间简直没有来往。吃正餐的时候碰面，也只在正餐桌上，而且即便在饭桌上，两人之间也几乎一言不发。他白天做些什么，她连知道也不知道。兰菲尔有一间所谓书房，是正厅与客厅之间的一间小屋，兰菲尔的藏书——几百册书——都收在那里。已故的乡绅素来不大用它，只是间或走进去，亲手从书架上取下一卷布道文来。他自己多年来惯常坐在客厅里——他的饭也在那里吃——连挪一步到正厅去的那种客套他也嫌厌。伊莎贝尔自己在楼上另有一间起坐的屋子，她也从来不用那书房。可如今亨利表哥偏偏占下了这间屋子，整天守在里面，虽说谁也不信他是个爱读书的人。早饭和晚饭，他独自到客厅里去用。到正餐的时候，伊莎贝尔才下楼来。可那漫长的白日里，他始终守在那堆书中间，一次也没有出过门，直到葬礼之前迎候阿普约翰先生和鲍威尔医生的那个时辰才罢。女管家会有一搭没一搭地讲起他，纳闷他关在书房里到底干些什么。对这些话，伊莎贝尔表面上不加理会，只淡淡说一句：在这样的时辰，他闭门独处也是人之常情。

“可他真的白得吓人哪，伊莎贝尔小姐，”女管家说，“他刚来兰菲尔那会儿，可没有这么白，没有这个样儿。”对这话伊莎贝尔没有答理；可他变得多么憔悴、多么灰败、多么萎靡无神，她也早就看在眼里了。

===Original===
On the Monday morning, when the men upstairs were at work on their ghastly duty, before the coming of the doctor and the lawyer, she went down to him, to tell him something of the programme for the day. Hitherto he had simply been informed that on that morning the body would be buried under the walls of the old parish church, and that after the funeral the will would be read. Entering the room somewhat suddenly she found him seated, vacant, in a chair, with an open book indeed on the table near him, but so placed that she was sure that he had not been occupied with it. There he was, looking apparently at the bookshelves, and when she entered the room he jumped up to greet her with an air of evident surprise.

“Mr. Apjohn and Dr. Powell will be here at eleven,” she said.

“Oh, ah; yes,” he replied.

“I thought I would tell you, that you might be ready.”

===Chinese===
星期一早晨，楼上的人们正做着自己那桩阴森的活计，医生和律师还没到，她下楼到他那里去，把当天的安排对他说了说。在这以前，人家只简单告诉过他：这天上午，遗体要安葬在老教区教堂的墙根底下，葬礼过后宣读遗嘱。她进门进得略略有些突然，只见他怔怔地坐在一把椅子上，手边桌上倒也摊着一本书，只是那书摆放的样子，她敢断定他并没有看它。他就那么坐着，眼睛望着书架；她进屋时，他霍地立起身来迎她，一脸掩不住的惊讶。

“阿普约翰先生和鲍威尔医生十一点钟到，”她说。

“噢，啊；是的，”他应道。

“我想着还是告诉你一声，你好有个准备。”

===Original===
“Yes; that is very kind. But I am ready. The men came in just now, and put the band on my hat, and laid my gloves there. You will not go, of course?”

“Yes; I shall follow the body. I do not see why I should not go as well as you. A woman may be strong enough at any rate for that. Then they will come back to lunch.”

“Oh, indeed; I did not know that there would be a lunch.”

“Yes; Dr. Powell says that it will be proper. I shall not be there, but you, of course, will be present to take the head of the table.”

===Chinese===
“是；多谢费心。不过我有准备了。方才他们进来，在我帽子上加了黑纱带，手套也搁在那儿了。你自然是不去的了？”

“不，我要随在灵柩后面去送葬。我看不出为什么你去了我就去不得。一个女人家，单为这个，气力总还够使。随后他们回来用午饭。”

“噢，原来如此；我倒不知道还有一顿午饭。”

“是的；鲍威尔医生说这样才是正理。我不在席上，可你自然要在场坐主位的。”

===Original===
“If you wish it.”

“Of course; it would be proper. There must be someone to seem at any rate to entertain them. When that is over Mr. Apjohn will find the will, and will read it. Richard will lay the lunch here, so that you may go at once into the parlour, where the will will be read. They tell me that I am to be there. I shall do as they bid me, though it will be a sore trouble to me. Dr. Powell will be there, and some of the tenants. Mr. Apjohn has thought it right to ask them, and therefore I tell you. Those who will be present are as follows:—John Griffith, of Coed; William Griffith, who has the home farm; Mr. Mortimer Green, of Kidwelly; Samuel Jones, of Llanfeare Grange; and the two Cantors, Joseph Cantor the father, and Joseph the son. I don’t know whether you know them by appearance as yet.”

“Yes,” said he, “I know them.” His face was almost sepulchral as he answered her, and as she looked at him she perceived that a slight quiver came upon his lips as she pronounced with peculiar clearness the two last names on the list.

“I thought it best to tell you all this,” she added. “If I find it possible, I shall go to Hereford on Wednesday. Most of my things are already packed. It may be that something may occur to stop me, but if it is possible I shall go on Wednesday.”

===Chinese===
“既然你觉得应当如此。”

“自然是了；这才合乎正理。总得有个人应酬客人，哪怕做做样子也是要的。那桩事一了，阿普约翰先生便会找出遗嘱来，当场宣读。午饭就摆在这里，由理查德（Richard）张罗，这样你用完便可以径直到客厅里去，遗嘱就在那里宣读。他们说要我也在场。他们叫我怎样我便怎样，虽然这在我是一桩苦楚。鲍威尔医生会在场，还有几位佃户。阿普约翰先生认为应当请他们来，所以我把名单告诉你。到场的有：科伊德（Coed）的约翰·格里菲斯（John Griffith）；承种家用田庄的威廉·格里菲斯（William Griffith）；基德韦利（Kidwelly）的莫蒂默·格林先生（Mr. Mortimer Green）；兰菲尔格兰治（Llanfeare Grange）的塞缪尔·琼斯（Samuel Jones）；还有坎托父子——当父亲的约瑟夫·坎托和当儿子的小约瑟夫。不知道你到现在认不认得他们的相貌。”

“认得，”他说，“我认得他们。”他回答她的时候，脸色几乎像坟墓里的人一般死灰；她望着他，发觉她把名单上最后两个名字咬得格外清楚的时候，他嘴唇上掠过一丝轻微的颤动。

“我想，这些情形还是都告诉你的好，”她又说。“若是走得开，我星期三就动身去赫里福德（Hereford）。我的东西大多已经收拾好了。也许会有什么事把我绊住，可只要走得成，我星期三就走。”

## XIII The *Carmarthen Herald* / 第十三章 《卡马森先驱报》

===Original===
There was a great deal said at Carmarthen about the old Squire’s will. Such scenes as that which had taken place in the house, first when the will was produced, then when the search was made, and afterwards when the will was read, do not pass without comment. There had been many present, and some of them had been much moved by the circumstances. The feeling that the Squire had executed a will subsequent to that which had now been proved was very strong, and the idea suggested by Mr. Apjohn that the Squire himself had, in the weakness of his latter moments, destroyed this document, was not generally accepted. Had he done so, something of it would have been known. The ashes of the paper or the tattered fragments would have been seen. Whether Mr. Apjohn himself did or did not believe that it had been so, others would not think it. Among the tenants and the servants at Llanfeare there was a general feeling that something wrong had been done. They who were most inclined to be charitable in their judgment, such as John Griffith of Coed, thought that the document was still hidden, and that it might not improbably be brought to light at last. Others were convinced that it had fallen into the hands of the present possessor of the property, and that it had been feloniously but successfully destroyed. No guess at the real truth was made by anyone. How should a man have guessed that the false heir should have sat there with the will, as it were, before his eyes, close at his hand, and neither have destroyed it nor revealed its existence?

Among those who believed the worst as to Cousin Henry were the two Cantors. When a man has seen a thing done himself he is prone to believe in it—and the more so when he has had a hand in the doing. They had been selected for the important operation of witnessing the will, and did not in the least doubt that the will had been in existence when the old Squire died. It might have been destroyed since. They believed that it had been destroyed. But they could not be brought to understand that so great an injustice should be allowed to remain on the face of the earth without a remedy or without punishment. Would it not be enough for a judge to know that they, two respectable men, had witnessed a new will, and that this new will had certainly been in opposition to the one which had been so fraudulently proved? The younger Cantor especially was loud upon the subject, and got many ears in Carmarthen to listen to him.

The *Carmarthen Herald*, a newspaper bearing a high character through South Wales, took the matter up very strongly, so that it became a question whether the new Squire would not be driven to defend himself by an action for libel. It was not that the writer declared that Cousin Henry had destroyed the will, but that he published minute accounts of all that had been done at Llanfeare, putting forward in every paper as it came out the reason which existed for supposing that a wrong had been done. That theory that old Indefer Jones had himself destroyed his last will without saying a word of his purpose to anyone was torn to tatters. The doctor had been with him from day to day, and must almost certainly have known it had such an intention been in his mind. The housekeeper would have known it. The nephew and professed heir had said not a word to anyone of what had passed between himself and his uncle. Could they who had known old Indefer Jones for so many years, and were aware that he had been governed by the highest sense of honour through his entire life, could they bring themselves to believe that he should have altered the will made in his nephew’s favour, and then realtered it, going back to his intentions in that nephew’s favour, without saying a word to his nephew on the subject? But Henry Jones had been silent as to all that occurred during those last weeks. Henry Jones had not only been silent when the will was being read, when the search was being made, but had sat there still in continued silence. “We do not say,” continued the writer in the paper, “that Henry Jones since he became owner of Llanfeare has been afraid to mingle with his brother men. We have no right to say so. But we consider it to be our duty to declare that such has been the fact. Circumstances will from time to time occur in which it becomes necessary on public grounds to inquire into the privacy of individuals, and we think that the circumstances now as to this property are of this nature.” As will be the case in such matters, these expressions became gradually stronger, till it was conceived to be the object of those concerned in making them to drive Henry Jones to seek for legal redress—so that he might be subjected to cross-examination as to the transactions and words of that last fortnight before his uncle’s death. It was the opinion of many that if he could be forced into a witness-box, he would be made to confess if there were anything to confess. The cowardice of the man became known—or was rather exaggerated in the minds of those around him. It was told of him how he lived in the one room, how rarely he left the house, how totally he was without occupation. More than the truth was repeated as to his habits, till all Carmarthenshire believed that he was so trammelled by some mysterious consciousness of crime as to be unable to perform any of the duties of life. When men spoke to him he trembled; when men looked at him he turned away.

===Chinese===
关于老乡绅的遗嘱，卡马森（Carmarthen）城里议论纷纷。宅子里先后出现的那些场面——先是遗嘱拿了出来，接着是四处搜寻，随后又是宣读遗嘱——是不会不招来议论的。当时在场的人不少，其中有些人深受触动。人们心里有个强烈的预感：老乡绅立过一份遗嘱，比如今验明的这一份更晚；而阿普约翰先生（Mr. Apjohn）提出的那个说法——老乡绅本人在弥留时的衰弱之中毁掉了那份文件——却没有得到普遍的认可。他若真做了这事，总不免有些痕迹走漏。纸灰也罢，撕碎的残片也罢，总该有人见过。阿普约翰先生自己信不信这一层且不论，旁人是不会信的。兰菲尔（Llanfeare）的佃户和仆人中间，普遍有一种感觉：出了什么亏心事了。判事最肯往宽厚处想的那些人，比如科伊德（Coed）的约翰·格里菲斯（John Griffith），认为那份文件仍然藏着，到最后多半还有见天日的一天。另一些人则深信，它落进了产业现任主人的手里，叫这个人像做贼一样毁掉了，而且毁得干干净净。实情究竟如何，谁也没有猜着。又有谁能猜得到，那个冒牌的继承人竟一直坐在那儿，那份遗嘱仿佛就在他眼前、一伸手就够得着，他既没有毁掉它，也没有吐露它的存在呢？

把亨利表哥（Cousin Henry）往最坏处信的，就有坎托两位先生（the two Cantors）。一件事，人若亲眼见过它做成，便容易信以为真——倘若自己还插过手，就更是如此。立遗嘱时那桩要紧的差事——到场做见证——选中的正是他们两位，因此他们丝毫也不怀疑，老乡绅咽气的时候，那份遗嘱确实还在人间。往后它或许遭了毁弃。他们相信它就是遭了毁弃。可他们说什么也弄不明白：这样大的不义，怎么竟容它留在这世面上，既无人补救，也无人惩处？只要让法官知道，他们两位体面人曾给一份新遗嘱做过见证，而这份新遗嘱分明同那份靠欺诈验明的遗嘱针锋相对，难道这还不够吗？年轻坎托在这桩事上尤其嚷得响，卡马森城里多少人都竖起耳朵听他说。

《卡马森先驱报》（Carmarthen Herald）这份报纸在全南威尔士素负盛名，这回把这事抓得极紧，抓得人们都起了疑心：这位新乡绅，怕不是要被逼得提起一桩诽谤诉讼来为自己辩白了。倒不是说执笔的人明言亨利表哥毁掉了遗嘱，而是他把兰菲尔宅中做下的事一五一十登了出来，每期报纸一出，都要摆一摆现有的理由，说明人们为何应当怀疑一桩冤屈已经做下。至于那个说法——老因德弗·琼斯（Indefer Jones）亲手毁掉了自己的最后遗嘱，却对谁也没提过半句自己的打算——早已被驳得体无完肤。大夫天天守在他身边，他心里若真有过这个念头，大夫几乎必定知情。女管家也该知道。那位侄子、名义上的继承人，对他同伯父之间发生过的事，对谁也没有吐过半个字。那些认识老因德弗·琼斯多年、深知他一辈子行事都受最崇高的荣誉感支配的人，难道能让自己相信：他把那份立给侄子的遗嘱改掉了，随后又改了回来，重新回到偏向那侄子的打算上，却对侄子本人只字不提？可亨利·琼斯（Henry Jones）对最后那几个星期里发生的一切，始终一声不响。宣读遗嘱的时候他不开口，搜寻遗嘱的时候他不开口，往后他还坐在那儿，一直沉默到底。“我们并不是说，”报上那支笔继续写道，“亨利·琼斯自从做了兰菲尔的主人，就不敢同他的同胞乡亲来往了。我们没有权利这样讲。但我们认为，我们有责任声明：事实确乎如此。世间时时会有这样的情形：出于公众的缘由，不得不去追究个人的私事；我们认为，眼下围绕这份产业的种种情形，正属于此类。”这类事情向来如此，这些措辞渐渐越写越重，直到人们认定，说这些话的那些人的用意，就是要逼得亨利·琼斯去寻求法律的救济——好让他就伯父死前最后那两个星期里的事情和言语，受一番交叉盘问。许多人的看法是：只要能把他逼上证人席，倘若真有什么可招认的，就能叫他招认出来。这个人的怯懦渐渐人人皆知——或者说，在周围人们的心目中被添油加醋了。人们传说他如何整天守着一间屋子，如何难得出门，如何全然无事可做。关于他的习性，传的比实情还多，直到全卡马森郡（Carmarthenshire）都相信：他被某种神秘的罪疚感捆住了手脚，连生活里分内的任何事都做不成。人同他讲话，他发抖；人看他一眼，他扭过头去。

===Original===
All his habits were inquired into. It was said of him that the *Carmarthen Herald* was the only paper that he saw, and declared of him that he spent hour after hour in spelling the terrible accusations which, if not absolutely made against him, were insinuated. It became clear to lawyers, to Mr. Apjohn himself, that the man, if honest, should, on behalf of the old family and long-respected name, vindicate himself by prosecuting the owner of the paper for libel. If he were honest in the matter, altogether honest, there could be no reason why he should fear to encounter a hostile lawyer. There were at last two letters from young Joseph Cantor printed in the paper which were undoubtedly libellous—letters which young Cantor himself certainly could not have written—letters which all Carmarthen knew to have been written by someone connected with the newspaper, though signed by the young farmer—in which it was positively declared that the old Squire had left a later will behind him. When it was discussed whether or no he could get a verdict, it was clearly shown that the getting of a verdict should not be the main object of the prosecution. “He has to show,” said Mr. Apjohn, “that he is not afraid to face a court of justice.”

But he was afraid. When we last parted with him after his visit to Coed he had not seen the beginning of these attacks. On the next day the first paper reached him, and they who were concerned in it did not spare to send him the copies as they were issued. Having read the first, he was not able to refuse to read what followed. In each issue they were carried on, and, as was told of him in Carmarthen, he lingered over every agonizing detail of the venom which was entering into his soul. It was in vain that he tried to hide the paper, or to pretend to be indifferent to its coming. Mrs. Griffith knew very well where the paper was, and knew also that every word had been perused. The month’s notice which had been accepted from her and the butler in lieu of the three months first offered had now expired. The man had gone, but she remained, as did the two other women. Nothing was said as to the cause of their remaining; but they remained. As for Cousin Henry himself, he was too weak, too frightened, too completely absorbed by the horrors of his situation to ask them why they stayed, or to have asked them why they went.

He understood every word that was written of him with sharp, minute intelligence. Though his spirit was cowed, his mind was still alive to all the dangers of his position. Things were being said of him, charges were insinuated, which he declared to himself to be false. He had not destroyed the will. He had not even hidden it. He had only put a book into its own place, carrying out as he did so his innocent intention when he had first lifted the book. When these searchers had come, doing their work so idly, with such incurious futility, he had not concealed the book. He had left it there on its shelf beneath their hands. Who could say that he had been guilty? If the will were found now, who could reasonably suggest that there had been guilt on his part? If all were known—except that chance glance of his eye which never could be known—no one could say that he was other than innocent! And yet he knew of himself that he would lack strength to stand up in court and endure the sharp questions and angry glances of a keen lawyer. His very knees would fail to carry him through the court. The words would stick in his jaws. He would shake and shiver and faint before the assembled eyes. It would be easier for him to throw himself from the rocks on which he had lain dreaming into the sea than to go into a court of law and there tell his own story as to the will. They could not force him to go. He thought he could perceive as much as that. The action, if action there were to be, must originate with him. There was no evidence on which they could bring a charge of felony or even of fraud against him. They could not drag him into the court. But he knew that all the world would say that if he were an honest man, he himself would appear there, denounce his defamers, and vindicate his own name. As day by day he failed to do so, he would be declaring his own guilt. Yet he knew that he could not do it.

===Chinese===
他的一切习性都被人打听了个遍。据说他看的唯一一份报纸就是《卡马森先驱报》，又说他一个钟头接着一个钟头，一个字一个字地拼读那些可怕的指控——那些指控即便没有直截了当地加在他头上，也是拐弯抹角地影射他。律师们看得分明，阿普约翰先生本人也看得分明：这个人倘若清白，就该为那个古老的家族和那长久受人敬重的姓氏着想，控告报馆老板诽谤，为自己洗雪名声。他在这桩事上若是清白的，彻头彻尾的清白，就没有道理害怕同一个敌对的律师交手。最后，报上登出了两封以年轻约瑟夫·坎托（Joseph Cantor）之名刊出的信，那些信无疑够得上诽谤——这样的信，年轻的坎托本人断然写不出来——全卡马森都知道，信是报馆里什么人捉刀的，署的却是那个年轻农人的名字——信里斩钉截铁地宣称，老乡绅身后留有一份更晚的遗嘱。人们议论他究竟能不能赢得裁决的时候，有一条已经说得明明白白：打赢官司不该是这场控诉的主要目的。“他必须证明，”阿普约翰说，“他不怕面对法庭。”

可他害怕。上一回我们同他分手，是在他去了科伊德之后，那时这一场攻击还没有开头。第二天，第一份报纸就到了他手上；与这桩事相干的人们毫不迟疑，报纸一期期印出来，便一期期给他寄去。头一份既已读过，后面的他就再也推脱不开了。每一期上都接着写下去；而据卡马森的人们传说，那股毒汁一寸一寸渗进他的灵魂，每一个煎熬人的细节，他都流连不去地细看。他想把报纸藏起来，或是装出一副满不在乎的样子，都属枉然。格里菲斯太太（Mrs. Griffith）心里雪亮，报纸搁在哪儿她知道，上头字字句句都被人读过，她也知道。当初最初提出的是三个月的期限，改从她同男管家那里收了一个月的通知，如今这一个月也已到期。男管家走了，她却留了下来，另外两个女仆也一样。她们留下的缘由，谁也没有提起；可她们就是留下了。至于亨利表哥本人，他太软弱、太惊惶、整个儿被自身处境的种种恐怖吞没了，既无心去问她们为什么留下，就连当初男管家为什么走，他也不曾问过。

写他的每一个字，他都看得明白，领会得又尖细又透彻。他的人虽说吓破了胆，他的头脑对自己处境的种种凶险，仍旧一概了悟。人们在议论他，罪名在影射他，他对自己申辩说，那些都是假的。他没有毁掉遗嘱。他连藏都没有藏它。他不过是把一本书放回了原位，而他当初捧起那本书的时候，本怀着一颗清白的心。那些搜查的人上门来，做得那样马虎，那样心不在焉、一无所获，他没有把书藏起来。他就让书留在架上，摊在他们手底下。谁能说他有罪？倘若遗嘱如今被找出来，谁能有根有据地说他有什么罪责？倘若一切都为人所知——唯独他眼睛里那偶然的一瞥除外，而那一瞥是永远无法为人所知的——谁也不能说他不是清白的！然而他心里明白，他没有那份力气站到法庭上去，去忍受一个尖刻的律师锋利的盘问和恼怒的目光。单是他的两条膝盖，就撑着他走不完那条进法庭的路。话会粘在他的牙关里。在满堂目光之下，他会发抖、打战、晕倒。同走进法庭、当众讲出自己关于遗嘱的那段经历相比，从他躺过做梦的那些礁石上纵身跳进大海，对他反倒容易些。他们没法逼他去。他以为自己能看出这一层。诉讼，倘若真要打官司，必须由他本人提起。没有任何证据能让他们告他重罪，连欺诈也告不上。他们拖他不到法庭上去。可他知道，全世界都会说：他若是个诚实的人，就该自己到那儿去，痛斥诽谤他的人，洗雪自己的名声。他一天一天地不去，便是一天一天地招认自己的罪。可他知道，他做不来。

===Original===
Was there no escape? He was quite sure now that the price at which he held the property was infinitely above its value. Its value! It had no value in his eyes. It was simply a curse of which he would rid himself with the utmost alacrity if only he could rid himself of all that had befallen him in achieving it. But how should he escape? Were he now himself to disclose the document and carry it into Carmarthen, prepared to deliver up the property to his cousin, was there one who would not think that it had been in his possession from before his uncle’s death, and that he had now been driven by his fears to surrender it? Was there one who would not believe that he had hidden it with his own hands? How now could he personate that magnanimity which would have been so easy had he brought forth the book and handed it with its enclosure to Mr. Apjohn when the lawyer came to read the will?

He looked back with dismay at his folly at having missed an opportunity so glorious. But now there seemed to be no escape. Though he left the room daily, no one found the will. They were welcome to find it if they would, but they did not. That base newspaper lied of him—as he told himself bitterly as he read it—in saying that he did not leave his room. Daily did he roam about the place for an hour or two—speaking, indeed, to no one, looking at no one. There the newspaper had been true enough. But that charge against him of self-imprisonment had been false as far as it referred to days subsequent to the rebuke which his housekeeper had given him. But no one laid a hand upon the book. He almost believed that, were the paper left open on the table, no eye would examine its contents. There it lay still hidden within the folds of the sermon, that weight upon his heart, that incubus on his bosom, that nightmare which robbed him of all his slumbers, and he could not rid himself of its presence. Property, indeed! Oh! if he were only back in London, and his cousin reigning at Llanfeare!

John Griffith, from Coed, had promised to call upon him; but when three weeks had passed by, he had not as yet made his appearance. Now, on one morning he came and found his landlord alone in the book-room. “This is kind of you, Mr. Griffith,” said Cousin Henry, struggling hard to assume the manner of a man with a light heart.

===Chinese===
难道就没有一条脱身之路了吗？如今他已十分确定：他为握有这份产业所付出的代价，远远高过产业本身的价值。它的价值！在他眼里它毫无价值。它不过是一样祸祟；只要他能把得它到手所遭的那一切一并摆脱，他巴不得连它也立刻甩脱。可他怎么脱身呢？倘若这会儿由他自己把那份文件亮出来，带去卡马森，准备把产业交还给表妹，难道会有谁不认为：这文件自他伯父死前就揣在他手里，而他如今是叫恐惧逼得才交出来的？难道会有谁不相信：是他亲手把它藏起来的？当初那位律师来宣读遗嘱的时候，他若把那本书拿出来，连同里面的夹藏一并递到阿普约翰先生手上，那份慷慨原是何等容易；事到如今，他还能到哪里去装扮这份慷慨呢？

他回想起自己竟错失了那样一个光辉的机会，真是愚不可及，不由得黯然。可如今看来已无路可走。他虽然天天走出房间，却没有一个人找到遗嘱。人们若肯找到它，他求之不得；可他们没有找。那份下作的报纸造他的谣——他读着报，对自己辛酸地说——说他不离自己的房间。他倒是天天在庄子上游荡一两个钟头——确实不同任何人讲话，也不看任何人一眼。这一层，报纸倒也不算说错。至于说他自我禁闭的罪名，就女管家训过他以后那些日子而言，却是冤枉了他。可就是没有一个人伸手去碰那本书。他几乎相信，哪怕把那张报纸摊开在桌上，也不会有一双眼睛去翻看它的内容。它依旧藏在布道文的折页里——压在他心头的一块重石，盘踞在他胸口的梦魇，夺尽他睡眠的噩梦——而他没法摆脱它的纠缠。产业，好个产业！唉！他若能回到伦敦去，让表妹坐镇兰菲尔，该有多好！

科伊德的约翰·格里菲斯早答应过要来拜望他；可三个星期过去，他还一直没有露面。这天早上，他来了，见他的东家独自一人在书房里。“您来真好，格里菲斯先生，”亨利表哥说，费了好大的劲，想装出一副心境轻松的人的样子。

===Original===
“I have come, Mr. Jones,” said the farmer very seriously, “to say a few words which I think ought to be said.”

“What are they, Mr. Griffith?”

“Now, Mr. Jones, I am not a man as is given to interfering—especially not with my betters.”

“I am sure you are not.”

“And, above all, not with my own landlord.” Then he paused; but as Cousin Henry could not find an appropriate word either for rebuke or encouragement, he was driven to go on with his story. “I have been obliged to look at all those things in the *Carmarthen Herald*.” Then Cousin Henry turned deadly pale. “We have all been driven to look at them. I have taken the paper these twenty years, but it is sent now to every tenant on the estate, whether they pay or whether they don’t. Mrs. Griffith, there, in the kitchen has it. I suppose they sent it to you, sir?”

“Yes; it does come,” said Cousin Henry, with the faintest attempt at a smile.

===Chinese===
“琼斯先生，我今天是来，”那农人十分郑重地说，“说几句话的。依我看，这几句话不能不说。”

“是什么话呢，格里菲斯先生？”

“琼斯先生，我这人向来不是爱管闲事的——尤其不管比我体面的人的闲事。”

“我知道您决不是那样的人。”

“尤其是，更不管我自己的东家。”说到这里，他停了停；可亨利表哥找不出一句合适的话来，责备不是，鼓励也不是，他只得把话头继续下去。“《卡马森先驱报》上登的那些东西，我不得不看了。”亨利表哥登时脸色惨白。“我们人人都不得不看。这份报纸我订了二十年了，如今庄子上每一个佃户都收到它，交租的也好，不交租的也好。我家格里菲斯太太也有一份，就搁在厨房里。想来他们也给先生您寄了吧？”

“是；是寄的，”亨利表哥说，勉强挤出一丝淡淡的、比没有还勉强的笑。

===Original===
“And you have read what they say?”

“Yes, the most of it.”

“It has been very hard, sir.” At this Cousin Henry could only affect a ghastly smile. “Very hard,” continued the farmer. “It has made my flesh creep as I read it. Do you know what it all means, Mr. Jones?”

“I suppose I know.”

“It means—that you have stolen—the estates—from your cousin—Miss Brodrick!” This the man said very solemnly, bringing out each single word by itself. “I am not saying so, Mr. Jones.”

“No, no, no,” gasped the miserable wretch.

===Chinese===
“那上头说的话，您都读了？”

“读了，大半都读了。”

“这些话对您太狠了，先生。”听到这里，亨利表哥只能强作一个惨然的笑。“太狠了，”那农人接着说。“我读的时候，肉皮都发麻。这一切是什么意思，琼斯先生，您知道吗？”

“我想我知道。”

“它的意思是——您把产业——从您表妹——布罗德里克小姐（Miss Brodrick）手里——偷了来！”这个人说这话的时候极其郑重，一个字一个字单独往外吐。“这话可不是我说的，琼斯先生。”

“不，不，不，”那可怜虫上气不接下气地说。

===Original===
“No, indeed. If I thought so, I should not be here to tell you what I thought. It is because I believe that you are injured that I am here.”

“I am injured; I am injured!”

“I think so. I believe so. I cannot tell what the mystery is, if mystery there be; but I do not believe that you have robbed that young lady, your own cousin, by destroying such a deed as your uncle’s will.”

“No, no, no.”

“Is there any secret that you can tell?”

===Chinese===
“可不是我说的，实在不是。我若真那么想，也就不会上这儿来把我的想法告诉您了。我正是相信您受了冤屈，才到这儿来的。”

“我是受了冤屈的；我是受了冤屈的！”

“我也是这么想。我也这么信。这里头有什么蹊跷，倘若真有什么蹊跷，我说不上来；可我不相信，您毁掉了您伯父的那份遗嘱那样一纸文书，夺了那位小姐的产业——她是您嫡亲的表妹啊。”

“没有，没有，没有。”

“您有什么秘密，能说给人听的么？”

===Original===
Awed, appalled, stricken with utter dismay, Cousin Henry sat silent before his questioner.

“If there be, sir, had you not better confide it to someone? Your uncle knew me well for more than forty years, and trusted me thoroughly, and I would fain, if I could, do something for his nephew. If there be anything to tell, tell it like a man.”

Still Cousin Henry sat silent. He was unable to summon courage at the instant sufficient to deny the existence of the secret, nor could he resolve to take down the book and show the document. He doubted, when the appearance of a doubt was in itself evidence of guilt in the eyes of the man who was watching him. “Oh, Mr. Griffith,” he exclaimed after a while, “will you be my friend?”

“I will indeed, Mr. Jones, if I can—honestly.”

===Chinese===
亨利表哥坐在盘问他的人面前，又惧又骇，万念俱灰，一言不发。

“倘若真有，先生，您托付给一个人岂不更好？您伯父认识我四十多年，对我信得很，若能为他这位侄子尽一点力，我是求之不得的。有什么可说的，就拿出个男子汉的样子说出来吧。”

亨利表哥还是坐着不言语。这一刻他鼓不起足够的勇气，否认有什么秘密；却也下不了决心，去取下那本书、把文件亮出来。他迟疑着，而在这个盯住他的人眼里，迟疑的模样本身便是罪证。“哦，格里菲斯先生，”过了一会儿他嚷道，“您肯做我的朋友吗？”

“只要我做得到，琼斯先生，我当然肯——但要做得光明正大。”

===Original===
“I have been cruelly used.”

“It has been hard to bear,” said Mr. Griffith.

“Terrible, terrible! Cruel, cruel!” Then again he paused, trying to make up his mind, endeavouring to see by what means he could escape from this hell upon earth. If there were any means, he might perhaps achieve it by aid of this man. The man sat silent, watching him, but the way of escape did not appear to him.

“There is no mystery,” he gasped at last.

“None?” said the farmer severely.

===Chinese===
“我受了狠心的亏待。”

“那确是难熬的，”格里菲斯先生说。

“可怕，可怕！狠心，狠心！”接着他又停住了，竭力想拿定主意，想看出一条什么路子，好让他逃出这人间地狱。倘若真有路子，兴许借这个人的帮助，他就能走通。那人坐着不出声，只管望着他；可那条脱身之路，在他眼里偏偏就是不肯出现。

“没有什么蹊跷，”他到最后喘着气说。

“没有什么？”那农人声色俱厉。

===Original===
“No mystery. What mystery should there be? There was the will. I have destroyed nothing. I have hidden nothing. I have done nothing. Because the old man changed his mind so often, am I to be blamed?”

“Then, Mr. Jones, why do you not say all that in a court of law—on your oath?”

“How can I do that?”

“Go to Mr. Apjohn, and speak to him like a man. Bid him bring an action in your name for libel against the newspaper. Then there will be an inquiry. Then you will be put into a witness-box, and be able to tell your own story on your oath.”

Cousin Henry, groaning, pale and affrighted, murmured out something signifying that he would think of it. Then Mr. Griffith left him. The farmer, when he entered the room, had believed his landlord to be innocent, but that belief had vanished when he took his leave.

===Chinese===
“没有什么蹊跷。能有什么蹊跷？遗嘱就在那儿。我什么也没有毁掉。我什么也没有藏。我什么也没有做。就因为老头子再三再四地改主意，就该怪罪到我头上来吗？”

“那么，琼斯先生，您为什么不到法庭上去，对着誓言把这些话都说出来呢？”

“我怎么能做得到呢？”

“去见阿普约翰先生，拿出个男子汉的样子同他讲。请他出面，用您的名义提起一桩控告报馆的诽谤诉讼。到那时自会有一番查究。到那时您就会被请上证人席，就能对着誓言，把您自己的经历亲口讲出来。”

亨利表哥呻吟着，脸色惨白，惊惶万状，含含糊糊地咕哝了几句，意思是这事容他想一想。格里菲斯先生随即离开了他。那农人进屋的时候，本还相信他的东家是清白的；待到他告辞出来，这份相信已经烟消云散。

## VIII The Reading of the Will / 第八章 宣读遗嘱

===Original===
On the whole of the next day the search was continued. In spite of his late watches, Cousin Henry rose up early, not looking at anything that was being done while the search was continued in other rooms, but still sitting, as he had heretofore sat, among the books. The two men whom Mr. Apjohn had sent from his office, together with the butler and Mrs. Griffith, began their work in the old man’s bedroom, and then carried it on in the parlour. When they came to the book-room, as being the next in turn, Cousin Henry took his hat and went out into the garden. There, as he made short turns upon the gravel path, he endeavoured to force himself away from the close vicinity of the window; but he could not do it. He could not go where he would have been unable to see what was being done. He feared—he trembled in his fear—lest they should come upon the guilty volume. And yet he assured himself again and again that he wished that they might find it. Would it not in every way be better for him that they should find it? He could not bring himself to destroy it, and surely, sooner or later, it would be found.

Every book was taken from its shelf, apparently with the object of looking into the vacant spaces behind them. Through the window he could see all that was done. As it happened, the compartment in which was the fatal shelf—on which was the fatal volume—was the last that they reached. No attempt was made to open the books one by one; but then this volume, with so thick an enclosure to betray it, would certainly open of itself. He himself had gone to the place so often that certainly the enclosure would betray itself. Well, let it betray itself! No one could say that he had had guilty cognizance of its whereabouts! But yet he knew that he would have been unable to speak, would have gasped, and would surely have declared himself to be guilty by his awestruck silence.

Three by three the books came down, and then were replaced. And now they were at the shelf! Why could he not go away? Why must he stand there fixed at the window? He had done nothing—nothing, nothing; and yet he stood there trembling, immovable, with the perspiration running off his face, unable to keep his eyes for a moment from what they were doing! At last the very three came down, in the centre of which was the volume containing the will. There was a tree against which he leaned, unable to support himself, as he looked into the room. The vacant place was searched, and then the three books were replaced! No attempt was made to examine the volumes. The men who did the work clearly did not know that these very volumes had been in constant use with the old Squire. They were replaced, and then the search, as far as the room was concerned, was over. When they were gone, Cousin Henry returned again to the room, and there he remained during the rest of the day. The search as it was carried on elsewhere had no interest for him.

===Chinese===
第二天一整天，搜寻仍在继续。亨利表哥尽管近来守夜熬得更深，还是一大早就起了身；别处屋子里搜寻进行的时候，他什么也不去看，只是照旧坐在那些书中间。阿普约翰先生（Mr. Apjohn）从事务所派来的两个人，会同男管家和格里菲斯太太（Mrs. Griffith），先在老人家卧室里动手，随后又转到客厅。轮到书房的时候，亨利表哥抓起帽子，走到花园里去了。他在砾石小径上踱着短短的来回，竭力逼自己离开窗子跟前；可是办不到。他没法走到一个望不见里面动静的地方去。他害怕——他怕得浑身发颤——生怕他们一下子翻出那本藏着罪证的卷册。可他又一遍一遍对自己说，他巴不得他们把它找出来。他们若把它找出来，于他方方面面岂不都更好？他终究下不了手去毁掉它，而这东西迟早横竖是要被发现的。

架上的书一本一本全取了下来，看样子是打算查看书后空出的地方。隔着窗子，里面的动静他一览无余。也是事有凑巧，那格致命的书架——致命的那卷书就搁在上面——恰是他们最后才够到的一格。倒也没有人要把书一本本翻开来看；只是这一卷里夹着那么厚的一包东西，包不住的，早晚自己会翻开。那地方他自己去过那么多次，那一包东西断乎瞒不住人。也罢，就让它自己去招吧！谁能说他心里明白它藏在哪儿，说他因此有罪！可他也知道，真到那时他一句话也说不出，只会喘不上气，光是那副惊惶的沉默，也就把自己是个罪人招认出来了。

书三本一茬地取下来，又放回去。这会儿他们到那格书架了！他怎么就走不开呢？为什么偏偏钉在窗前不动？他什么也没干——什么也没干，什么也没干；可他还是站在那儿发抖，挪不动步，汗一道道从脸上淌下来，眼睛一刻也离不得他们在做的事！末了，正是那三本取了下来，当中一本，就夹着遗嘱。他倚着一棵树，朝屋里望的时候，连站都站不稳了。空出来的位置搜了一遍，跟着那三本书又放回了原处！竟没有一个人想到把书翻开验看。干这活计的人显然不知道，这几卷书正是老乡绅日常手不释卷的。书放回去了，就这间屋子而论，搜寻也算了了。他们走后，亨利表哥又回到屋里，这一天余下的时辰便一直待在那儿。别处搜寻的进展，于他毫无趣味。

===Original===
Whatever harm might be done to others, whoever else might be injured, certainly no one was ill-treated as he had been ill-treated. It was thus he thought of it. Even should the will never be found, how cruel would be the injustice done to him! He had not asked to be made heir to the property! It was not his doing. He had been invited to come in order that he might be received as the heir, and since he had come, everyone about the place had misused him. The tenants had treated him with disdain; the very servants had been insolent; his Cousin Isabel, when he had offered to share everything with her, had declared that he was hateful to her; and his uncle himself had heaped insult upon injury, and had aggravated injustice with scorn.

“Yes; I had intended that you should be my heir, and have called you hither for that purpose. Now I find you to be so poor a creature that I have changed my mind.” That in truth was what his uncle had said to him and had done for him. Who, after that, would expect him to go out of his way in search of special magnanimity? Let them find the will if they wanted it! Even though he should resolve himself to have nothing to do with the property, even though he should repudiate any will in his own favour, still he would not tell them where this will might be found. Why should he help them in their difficulty?

Every carpet was taken up, every piece of furniture was moved, every trunk and box in the house was examined, but it occurred to no one that every book should be opened. It was still July, and the day was very long. From six in the morning till nine at night they were at work, and when the night came they declared that every spot about the place had been searched.

===Chinese===
别人纵然受害，旁人纵然吃亏，横竖没有谁受的委屈比他更重。他心里就是这么看的。哪怕那遗嘱永远找不出来，落在他头上的不义又是何其残忍！这份产业的继承人，又不是他自己讨来的！那不是他干的。是他被请了来，好让他以继承人的身份受接纳；可他既来了，这地方上下人等就没有一个不亏待他。佃户们拿白眼看他；连仆人们也傲慢无礼；他表妹伊莎贝尔（Isabel），当他提出愿把一切与她分享的时候，竟说她恨他恨得咬牙；他伯父本人，更是在伤害之上再堆羞辱，在不义之外又加轻蔑。

「不错，我原是要你做我的继承人，也是为这个才把你唤了来。如今我看你竟是这样一个不成器的东西，我便改了主意。」——他伯父对他说的话、为他做的事，说到底便是这一句。事既如此，谁还能指望他特地委屈自己，去修那种格外的宽宏大量？他们要遗嘱，让他们自己找去！纵使他打定主意同这份产业再不相干，纵使他肯把一切于他有利的遗嘱一概不认，他也决不告诉他们这份遗嘱上哪儿去找。他们有难处，凭什么要他帮衬？

地毯一块块掀了起来，家具一件件挪了窝，宅子里大大小小的箱匣都翻检过了，可就没有一个人想到把每本书都打开来看看。那时还是七月，白昼正长。从清早六点直到夜里九点，他们不停地搜，到了晚上便宣称，庄上每一处角落都搜遍了。

===Original===
“I think, Miss, that the old Squire did destroy it. He was a little wandering at last.” It was thus that Mrs. Griffith had expressed her opinion to Isabel.

Isabel was sure that it was not so, but said nothing in reply.

If she could only get away from Llanfeare and have done with it, she would be satisfied. Llanfeare had become odious to her and terrible! She would get away, and wash her hands of it. And yet she was aware how sad would be her condition. Mr. Apjohn had already explained to her that the Squire had so managed his affairs as to have left no funds from which could be paid the legacy which had nominally been left to her. She had told her father when at Hereford that her uncle had taken such care of her that she would not become a burden upon him. Now it seemed that she would have to return home without a shilling of her own. For one so utterly penniless to think of marrying a man who had little but his moderate professional income would, she felt, be mean as well as wrong. There must be an end to everything between her and Mr. Owen. If her father could not support her, she must become a governess or, failing that, a housemaid. But even the poorhouse would be better than Llanfeare, if Llanfeare were to be the property of Cousin Henry.

===Chinese===
「小姐，我看老乡绅当真把它毁了。他临了那阵子，脑筋已有些糊涂了。」格里菲斯太太就是这样向伊莎贝尔说出她的看法的。

伊莎贝尔确信决不是这样，却没有答话。

只要能离开兰菲尔（Llanfeare），从此一了百了，她也就称心了。兰菲尔已经变得令她憎恶，令她心惊！她要走，从此撒手不管。可她也明白，自己的处境将何等凄凉。阿普约翰先生早已向她说明：老乡绅措置家业，竟没有留下半点现款，足敷支付名义上遗给她的那笔赠款。她在赫里福德（Hereford）时曾对父亲说，伯父把她安排得妥妥帖帖，她决不会成为他的拖累。如今看来，她只好身无一个先令地回娘家去了。一个身无分文的人，却想去嫁一个除了一份不多的执业进项而外别无长物的男人——她觉得，这既卑屈，又对不起人。她同欧文先生（Mr. Owen）之间，一切都该了断了。父亲若养不活她，她只能去当家庭女教师；再不然，就去当女仆。可即便济贫院，也比兰菲尔强——倘若兰菲尔当真要归了亨利表哥的话。

===Original===
Mr. Apjohn had told her that she could not now leave the place on the Wednesday as she had intended. On the Wednesday he again came to Llanfeare, and then she saw him before he proceeded to his business. It was his intention now to read the last will which had been found, and to explain to those who heard it that he proposed, as joint executor with Dr. Powell, to act upon that as the last will;—but still with a proviso that another will might possibly be forthcoming. Though he had in a measure quarrelled with the Squire over the making of that will, nevertheless, he had been appointed in it as the executor, such having been the case in the wills previously made. All this he explained to her up in her room, assenting to her objection to be again present when the will should be read.

“I could not do it,” she said; “and of what use could it be, as I know everything that is in it? It would be too painful.”

He, remembering the futile legacy which it contained for herself, and the necessity which would be incumbent upon him to explain that there were no funds for paying it, did not again ask her to be present.

“I shall go tomorrow,” she said.

Then he asked her whether she could not remain until the beginning of next week, urging objections to this final surrender of Llanfeare; but she was not to be turned from her purpose. “Llanfeare will have been surrendered,” she said; “the house will be his to turn me out of if he pleases.”

===Chinese===
阿普约翰先生告诉过她，她不能照原定的打算在星期三就动身离开。到了星期三，他又一次来到兰菲尔，她在他着手办事之前先见了他。他如今的意思，是把已经找到的那份最后遗嘱宣读一遍，并向听读的人说明：他准备同鲍威尔医生（Dr. Powell）一道，作为共同的遗嘱执行人，即以这一份为最后遗嘱而行事；——但仍附一项但书：另一份遗嘱也兴许还会出现。他为立这份遗嘱的事，同老乡绅多少有过一番争执；然而遗嘱里指名的执行人依旧是他，先前历次所立遗嘱也都是如此。这些他都在她楼上的房间里一一向她说明，并且依了她，不再要她在宣读遗嘱的时候到场。

「我做不到，」她说，「再说听了又有什么用处？里面的每一个字我都知道。那也太叫人受罪了。」

他想到里面遗给她本人的那笔赠款不过有名无实，又想到届时他势必还得说明并无现款可以支付，便没有再请她到场。

「我明天就走。」她说。

于是他劝她能不能待到下星期开头，就这样把兰菲尔终局交割出去一事力陈种种异议；可她主意已定，劝不回头。「到那时兰菲尔已经交出去了，」她说，「宅子就归了他，他高兴撵我，随时可以撵。」

===Original===
“He would not do that.”

“He shall not have the chance. I could not hide it from you if I would. He and I do not love each other. Since he has been here I have kept away from him with disgust. He cannot but hate me, and I will not be a guest in his house. Besides, what can I do?”

“The will will not have been proved, you know.”

“What difference will there be in that? It will be proved at once. Of course he will have the keys, and will be master of everything. There are the keys.” As she said this she handed over to him various bunches. “You had better give them to him yourself when you have read the will, so that I need have nothing to say to him. There are some books of mine which my uncle gave me. Mrs. Griffith will pack them, and send them to me at Hereford—unless he objects. Everything else belonging to me I can take with me. Perhaps you will tell them to send a fly out for me in time for the early train.”

And so it was settled.

===Chinese===
「他不会那么做的。」

「可我不能给他这个机会。我即便想瞒，也瞒不过您。他和我，彼此谁也不喜欢谁。他来了以后，我一直怀着厌恶躲着他。他不会不恨我，我也不愿在他家里做客人。何况，我又能怎样呢？」

「要知道，遗嘱到那时候还没经过查验呢。」

「那又有什么两样？查验马上就会办的。钥匙自然归他，一切自然归他做主。喏，钥匙都在这儿。」说着她把几串钥匙都递到他手里。「您宣读完遗嘱，最好亲自把钥匙交给他，那样我用不着同他说一句话。我有一些书，是伯父给我的。格里菲斯太太会替我装好，寄到赫里福德去——只要他不阻拦。其余属于我的东西，我随身带走就行。劳驾您叫他们及时打发一辆出租马车来接我，好赶上早班火车。」

事情就这样定了下来。

===Original===
Then that will was read—that will which we know not to have been the last will—in the presence of Cousin Henry, of Dr. Powell, who had again come out with Mr. Apjohn, and of the farmers, who were collected as before.

It was a long, tedious document, in which the testator set forth at length his reasons for the disposition which he made of the property. Having much considered the matter, he had thought the estate should descend to the male heir, even in default of a regular deed of entail. Therefore, although his love for his dearest niece, Isabel Brodrick, was undiminished, and his confidence in her as perfect as ever, still he had thought it right to leave the old family property to his nephew, Henry Jones. Then, with all due circumstances of description, the legacy was made in favour of his nephew. There were other legacies; a small sum of money to Mr. Apjohn himself, for the trouble imposed upon him as executor, a year’s wages to each of his servants and other matters of the kind. There was also left to Isabel that sum of four thousand pounds of which mention has been made. When the lawyer had completed the reading of the document, he declared that to the best of his knowledge no such money was in existence. The testator had no doubt thought that legacies so made would be paid out of the property, whereas the property could be made subject to no such demand unless it had, by proper instrument to that effect, been charged with the amount.

“But,” he said, “Mr. Henry Jones, when he comes into possession of the estate, will probably feel himself called upon to set that matter right, and to carry out his uncle’s wishes.”

Upon this Cousin Henry, who had not as yet spoken a word throughout the ceremony, was profuse in his promises. Should the estate become his, he would certainly see that his uncle’s wishes were carried out in regard to his dear cousin. To this Mr. Apjohn listened, and then went on to explain what remained to be said. Though this will, which he had now read, would be acted upon as though it were the last will and testament of the deceased—though, in default of that for which futile search had been made, it certainly was what it purported to be—still there existed in full force all those reasons which he had stated on the Monday for supposing that the late Squire had executed another. Here Joseph Cantor, junior, gave very strong symptoms of his inclination to reopen that controversy, but was stopped by the joint efforts of his father and the lawyer. If such a document should ever be found, then that would be the actual will and not the one which he had now read. After that, when all due formalities had been performed, he took his leave, and went back to Carmarthen.

===Chinese===
于是那份遗嘱便宣读了——那份我们明知并非最后遗嘱的遗嘱——亨利表哥在场，又随阿普约翰先生一道前来的鲍威尔医生在场，还照前次的样子，把佃户们也召集了来。

那是一份冗长沉闷的文书，立遗嘱人在里面把处置产业的种种理由原原本本说了一遍。他对此事思虑再三，认为产业应当传给男嗣，即便并无正式的限定继承契据，也当如此。因此，尽管他对他最亲爱的侄女伊莎贝尔·布罗德里克（Isabel Brodrick）的疼爱分毫未减，对她的信任一如既往地完满，他仍然认为，把这份传了世代的祖业留给侄儿亨利·琼斯（Henry Jones）才是正理。接着，便以一切应有的名目文辞，把这份遗赠郑重落在侄儿名下。此外还有别项遗赠：给阿普约翰先生本人一小笔款子，酬他身为执行人所费的一场辛劳；给每个仆人一年的工钱；还有诸如此类的款项。遗给伊莎贝尔的，便是前文提过的那四千英镑。律师读毕文书，随即声明：据他所知，这样一笔钱并不存在。立遗嘱人想必以为，这样立下的遗赠自会从产业里开销支付；殊不知，除非以一份正式文书为此在产业上设定负担，产业是不能受人这样追索的。

「不过，」他说，「亨利·琼斯先生接了产业之后，想必会觉得自己有责任把这件事料理妥当，成全他伯父的遗愿。」

听到这里，全场仪式上还没开过口的亨利表哥满口应承下来。产业一旦归他，他必定办到：关于他亲爱的表妹，伯父的心愿一定照办不误。阿普约翰先生听罢，便接着把余下该说的话说完。他此刻宣读的这份遗嘱，固然要当作死者的最后遗嘱与遗命来遵行——既然枉费搜寻的那一份并无下落，这一份自然便是它自称的那份——然而，他星期一陈述过的那些理由，即认为已故老乡绅另立过一份遗嘱的理由，依然完完全全成立。说到这里，小约瑟夫·坎托（Joseph Cantor, junior）分明又露出要重翻那桩公案的架势，却让他父亲和律师合力按了下去。那样的文书倘若有一日真的找了出来，那才是真正的遗嘱，而不是他此刻宣读的这一份。这之后，一切应有的手续履行完毕，他便告辞回卡马森（Carmarthen）去了。

===Original===
The keys were given up to Cousin Henry, and he found himself to be, in fact, the lord and master of the house, and the owner of everything within it. The butler, Mrs. Griffith, and the gardener gave him notice to quit. They would stay, if he wished it, for three months, but they did not think that they could be happy in the house now that the old Squire was dead, and that Miss Isabel was going away. There certainly did not come to him at the present moment any of the pleasures of ownership. He would have been willing—he thought that he would have been willing—to abandon Llanfeare altogether, if only it could have been abandoned without any of the occurrences of the last month. He would have been pleased that there should have been no Llanfeare.

But as it was, he must make up his mind to something. He must hide the paper in some deeper hiding place, or he must destroy it, or he must reveal it. He thought that he could have dropped the book containing the will into the sea, though he could not bring himself to burn the will itself. The book was now his own, and he might do what he liked with it. But it would be madness to leave the paper there!

Then again there came to him the idea that it would be best for him, and for Isabel too, to divide the property. In one way it was his—having become his without any fraudulent doing on his part. So he declared to himself. In another way it was hers—though it could not become hers without some more than magnanimous interference on his part. To divide it would certainly be best. But there was no other way of dividing it but by a marriage. For any other division, such as separating the land or the rents, no excuse could be made, nor would any such separation touch the fatal paper which lay between the leaves of the book. Were she to consent to marry him, then he thought he might find courage to destroy the paper.

It was necessary that he should see her on that afternoon, if only that he might bid her adieu, and tell her that she should certainly have the money that had been left her. If it were possible he would say a word also about that other matter.

===Chinese===
钥匙交到了亨利表哥手上，他发觉自己如今实实在在成了这宅子的主子和东家，宅子里的一切都归他所有。男管家、格里菲斯太太和园丁都向他递了辞工的知会。他若要他们留，他们可以再留三个月；只是老乡绅一死，伊莎贝尔小姐又要离去，他们觉得再在这宅子里待着，心里不会快活。此刻到他身上的，确乎没有半分做业主的乐趣。他倒宁愿——他想他宁愿——把兰菲尔整个儿撇下，只要撇下它时不必经过上个月那些事。他倒乐意世上压根儿没有兰菲尔这地方。

可事已至此，他总得拿定一个主意。要么把那张纸藏进更深的地方，要么毁掉它，要么把它公之于众。他想，把夹着遗嘱的那本书抛进海里，他做得到；可单叫他亲手烧那张遗嘱，他下不了这个手。书如今是他自己的了，他爱怎么处置都行。可把那张纸照旧留在那儿，那简直是发疯！

这时候，又一个念头回到他心上：把产业分开，于他于伊莎贝尔都是上策。从一个角度看，产业是他的——而它到他手里，并无他半点欺诈的作为；他心里就是这样替自己声辩的。从另一个角度看，产业又是她的——只是不经他一番超乎宽宏的干预，产业到不了她手里。分开自然最好。可分开的办法，除了结亲，再没有第二条路。旁的分法，比如把地分开，或把租金分开，都说不出口，而且那样分开，也碰不着夹在书页当中的那张致命的纸。她若肯嫁他，他想，他或许就找得出勇气，把那张纸毁掉。

这天下午他必须见她一面，哪怕只为道一声别，告诉她那笔遗给她的钱她必定到手。若有可能，另一桩事他也想顺带提上一句。

===Original===
“You did not hear the will read,” he said to her.

“No,” she answered abruptly.

“But you have been told its contents?”

“I believe so.”

“About the four thousand pounds?”

“There need be no question about the four thousand pounds. There is not a word to be said about it—at any rate between you and me.”

===Chinese===
「遗嘱宣读的时候，你没在场。」他对她说。

「没有。」她冲口答道。

「不过内容有人告诉你了吧？」

「我想是吧。」

「四千英镑的事呢？」

「四千英镑用不着再提。那是一句也不必说了——至少在你我之间。」

===Original===
“I have come to tell you,” said he—not understanding her feeling in the least, and evidently showing by the altered tone of his voice that he thought that his communication would be received with favour—“I have come to tell you that the legacy shall be paid in full. I will see to that myself as soon as I am able to raise a penny on the property.”

“Pray do not trouble yourself, Cousin Henry.”

“Oh, certainly I shall.”

“Do not trouble yourself. You may be sure of this, that on no earthly consideration would I take a penny from your hands.”

“Why not?”

“We take presents from those whom we love and esteem, not from those we despise.”

===Chinese===
「我是来告诉你的，」他说——她这点心思他半点也没懂得，话音一变，明明白白露出他自以为这番知会必受欢迎——「我是来告诉你，那笔遗赠一定如数照付。只要我能从这份产业上筹出一个子儿，这事我就亲自去办。」

「千万不必劳您费心，亨利表哥。」

「哦，我是一定要费的。」

「不必劳您的费心。您把这一点记牢：天底下无论什么好处，也换不动我从您手里拿一个子儿。」

「为什么不？」

「礼物，我们只受我们所爱、所敬重的人的，不受我们所鄙夷的人的。」

===Original===
“Why should you despise me?” he asked.

“I will leave that to yourself to judge of; but be sure of this, that though I were starving I would take nothing from your hands.”

Then she got up, and, retiring into the inner room, left him alone. It was clear to him then that he could not divide the property with her in the manner that he had suggested to himself.

===Chinese===
「您凭什么鄙夷我？」他问。

「这留给您自己判断去；只有一句话您记住：我就是饿死，也不会从您手里拿一样东西。」

说罢她站起身来，退进里间去了，把他一个人撂在那儿。到这时他才看明白：照他心里盘算的那个法子，这份产业是没法同她分的。

## IV The Squire’s Death / 第四章 乡绅之死

===Original===
With a sore heart Isabel went her way to Hereford—troubled because she saw nothing but sorrow and vexation in store for her uncle.

“I know that I am getting weaker every day,” he said. And yet it was not long since he had spoken of living for two years.

“Shall I stay?” asked Isabel.

“No; that would be wrong. You ought to go to your father. I suppose that I shall live till you come back.”

“Oh, Uncle Indefer!”

“What if I did die? It is not that that troubles me.” Then she kissed him and left him. She knew how vain it was to ask any further questions, understanding thoroughly the nature of his sorrow. The idea that this nephew must be the master of Llanfeare was so bitter to him that he could hardly endure it; and then, added to this, was the vexation of the nephew’s presence. That three weeks should be passed alone with the man—three weeks of the little that was left to him of life, seemed to be a cruel addition to the greater sorrow! But Isabel went, and the uncle and nephew were left to do the best they could with each other’s company.

===Chinese===
伊莎贝尔（Isabel）怀着满心痛楚，动身往赫里福德（Hereford）去了——她心里烦乱难安，因为在她看来，等着她伯父的只有忧愁和烦恼。

“我知道自己一天衰似一天，”他说。可就在不久之前，他还说自己能再活上两年呢。

“我留下来不好么？”伊莎贝尔问。

“不；那可就不对了。你该去你父亲那里。我想，你回来之前我总还活着。”

“哎，因德弗伯父（Uncle Indefer）！”

“我若死了又怎么样？叫我心烦的并不是这个。”于是她吻了他，离开了他。再问下去也是枉然，这一点她明白，他那悲哀的根由她也看得透透的。一想到这个侄儿注定要做兰菲尔（Llanfeare）的主人，他就苦得几乎受不住；这之上，又添上那侄儿人在眼前的一桩烦恼。同这个人单独厮守三个星期——他残生所剩的那一点点里，偏要有三个星期这样度过——似乎是在那更大的悲哀之上，又狠心添了一层苦楚！可伊莎贝尔还是走了，只剩下伯父和侄儿两个人，彼此将就着做伴过日子。

===Original===
Isabel had not seen Mr. Owen or heard from him since the writing of that letter in which she had told him of her uncle’s decision. Now it would be necessary that she should meet him, and she looked forward to doing so almost with fear and trembling. On one point she had made up her mind, or thought that she had made up her mind. As she had refused him when supposed to be heiress of Llanfeare, she certainly would not accept him, should he feel himself constrained by a sense of honour to renew his offer to her now that her position was so different. She had not accused him in her own heart of having come to her because of her supposed wealth. Thinking well of him in other matters, she thought well of him also in that. But still there was the fact that she had refused him when supposed to be an heiress; and not even to secure her happiness would she allow him to think that she accepted him because of her altered circumstances. And yet she was in love with him, and had now acknowledged to herself that it was so. Her position in this as in all things seemed to be so cruel! Had she been the heiress of Llanfeare she could not have married him, because it would then have been her duty to comply with the wishes of her uncle. No such duty would now be imposed upon her, at any rate after her uncle’s death. As simple Isabel Brodrick she might marry whom she would without bringing discredit upon the Indefer Joneses. But that which she had been constrained to do before her uncle had changed his purpose now tied her hands.

It did seem to her cruel; but she told herself that it was peculiarly her duty to bear such cruelty without complaint. Of her uncle’s intense love to her she was fully aware, and, loving him as warmly, was prepared to bear everything on his account. His vacillation had been unfortunate for her, but in everything he had done the best according to his lights. Perhaps there was present to her mind something of the pride of a martyr. Perhaps she gloried a little in the hardship of her position. But she was determined to have her glory and her martyrdom all to herself. No human being should ever hear from her lips a word of complaint against her Uncle Indefer.

The day after her arrival her father asked her a few questions as to her uncle’s intentions in reference to the property.

===Chinese===
自从写了那封把伯父的决定告知他的信以后，伊莎贝尔再没有见过欧文先生（Mr. Owen），也没有得到他的音信。如今她少不得要同他见面了，而想到这里，她几乎怀着惴惴畏惧的心情。有一件事她拿定了主意——或者自以为拿定了主意。她既在人家认她是兰菲尔的继承人时回绝了他，那么如今她的境况已全然不同，纵使他觉得碍于道义非向她重申前请不可，她也断然不能应允。在自己心里，她从不曾指摘他是冲着她那想当然的家财来的。他别的事情她都往好处想，这一件她也往好处想。可她毕竟是在人家当她做女继承人的时候回绝了他的；纵然是为了自己的幸福，她也决不容他以为，她之所以应允，是因为她的境遇变了。然而她是爱他的，这一点如今她已经向自己承认了。她这番处境，同她事事遭际一样，似乎都残酷得很！她若做了兰菲尔的女继承人，便不能嫁他，因为那时依从伯父的心愿便是她的本分。如今这本分不会再压到她身上，至少伯父过世之后是不会了。做一个普普通通的伊莎贝尔·布罗德里克（Isabel Brodrick），她尽可嫁自己愿意嫁的人，而不至于给因德弗·琼斯家丢脸。可她在伯父没有改变主意之前不得不做下的那桩事，如今捆住了她的双手。

这在她看来固然残酷；可她对自己说，不加怨言地承受这样的残酷，正是她分内的本分。伯父对她的挚爱，她体察得再透彻不过；她也同样炽热地爱着他，甘愿为他担当一切。他的摇摆不定于她是不幸；但无论什么事，他都尽着自己目力所及做到了最好。或许她心头未尝没有几分殉道者的骄傲。或许她对自己的处境之苦，还暗暗觉得有几分得意。但她决意要把这份荣耀、这份殉道，全然归自己一人独享。天底下不该有任何人，从她嘴里听到一句埋怨因德弗伯父的话。

她到家的第二天，父亲就产业的事，向她问起伯父的意向，问了几句。

===Original===
“I think it is all settled,” she said. “I think it has been left to my Cousin Henry.”

“Then he has changed his mind,” said her father angrily. “He did mean to make you his heiress?”

“Henry is at Llanfeare now, and Henry will be his heir.”

“Why has he changed? Nothing can be more unjust than to make a promise in such a matter and then to break it.”

“Who says that he made a promise? You have never heard anything of the kind from me. Papa, I would so much rather not talk about Llanfeare. Ever since I have known him, Uncle Indefer has been all love to me. I would not allow a thought of mine to be polluted by ingratitude towards him. Whatever he has done, he has done because he has thought it to be the best. Perhaps I ought to tell you that he has made some charge on the property on my behalf, which will prevent my being a burden upon you.”

===Chinese===
“我想事情全都定了，”她说，“我想是留给我亨利表哥（Cousin Henry）了。”

“这么说他是变了卦了，”父亲怒冲冲地说，“他本来是要立你做继承人的，是不是？”

“亨利如今在兰菲尔，亨利会做他的继承人。”

“他为什么变卦？这种事，许下了诺言又亲手推翻，再没有比这更不公道的了。”

“谁说他许过诺言？这样的话，你从我嘴里可从来没听见过。爸爸，我顶不愿意谈兰菲尔的事。自从我认识他，因德弗伯父待我全是疼爱。我决不容自己心里起一丝对他忘恩负义的念头。他做的事，无论哪一件，都是因为他觉得那样最好。或许我该告诉你，他已在产业上以我为受益人设定了一笔款项，往后我不至于成为你们的累赘。”

===Original===
A week or ten days after this, when she had been nearly a fortnight at Hereford, she was told that William Owen was coming in to drink tea. This communication was made to her by her stepmother, in that serious tone which is always intended to convey a matter of importance. Had any other minor canon or any other gentleman been coming to tea, the fact would have been announced in a different manner.

“I shall be delighted to see him,” said Isabel, suppressing with her usual fortitude any slightest symptom of emotion.

“I hope you will, my dear. I am sure he is very anxious to see you.”

===Chinese===
这以后七八天，她在赫里福德住了快两个星期的时候，家里人告诉她，威廉·欧文（William Owen）要来吃茶。传这话的是她的继母，用的是那种郑重其事的腔调——人们向来用这种腔调表示事情要紧。倘若是随便哪位小法政牧师（minor canon）、随便哪位别的先生来吃茶，通报的口气断不会是这样。

“我见他高兴得很，”伊莎贝尔说，照她素来的坚忍，把一丝一毫动情的迹象都压了下去。

“但愿你肯见他，亲爱的。我确信他急着要见你呢。”

===Original===
Then Mr. Owen came and drank his tea in the midst of the family. Isabel could perceive that he was somewhat confused—not quite able to talk in his usual tone, and that he was especially anxious as to his manner towards her. She took her part in the conversation as though there were nothing peculiar in the meeting. She spoke of Llanfeare, of her uncle’s failing health, and of her cousin’s visit, taking care to indicate by some apparently chance word, that Henry was received there as the heir. She played her part well, evincing no sign of special feeling but her ear was awake to the slightest tone in his voice after he had received the information she had given him. She knew that his voice was altered, but she did not read the alteration altogether aright.

“I shall call in the morning,” he said, as he gave her his hand at parting. There was no pressure of the hand, but still he had addressed himself especially to her.

Why should he come in the morning? She had made up her mind, at the spur of the moment, that the news which he had heard had settled that matter forever. But if so, why should he come in the morning? Then she felt, as she sat alone in her room, that she had done him a foul injustice in that spur of the moment. It must be that she had done him an injustice, or he would not have said that he would come. But if he could be generous, so could she. She had refused him when she believed herself to be the heiress of Llanfeare, and she certainly would not accept him now.

===Chinese===
随后欧文先生来了，在一家人中间坐下来吃茶。伊莎贝尔看得出他有些心神不定——说话的调子不像平日，而且他对怎样待她分外小心在意。她照样在谈话里应着她的那份酬答，仿佛这次会面毫无特别之处。她谈起兰菲尔，谈起伯父日渐衰弱的身体，谈起表哥来庄上做客的事，说话之间又留意用几句看似随口的话点明：亨利在那边是当继承人接待的。她的这出戏演得很好，没有露出一点特别的情愫；可是自他把消息听进去之后，他嗓音里最细微的变化，她的耳朵都警醒地捕捉着。她知道他的声音变了，只是那变化究竟为何，她没有全然看准。

“明儿上午我再来拜访。”他告辞时伸手与她相握，这样说。握手时并没有用力，可这句话到底是专对着她一个人说的。

他干吗要上午来？当时她一冲动便拿定了主意，认定他听来的那个消息早把那桩事永远断送了。可果真如此，他干吗还要上午来？于是她独坐房中的时候觉出，方才那一阵冲动，她大大地冤屈了他。她准是冤屈了他，不然他不会说要来。可他既能宽宏大量，她也能。她自认为是兰菲尔的女继承人时回绝了他，如今更断然不会应允他。

===Original===
On the next morning about eleven o’clock he came. She had become aware that it was the intention of all the family that she should see him alone, and she made no struggle against that intention. As such intention existed, the interview must of course take place, and as well now as later. There was no confidence on the matter between herself and her stepmother—no special confidence between even herself and her half-sisters. But she was aware that they all supposed that Mr. Owen was to come there on that morning for the sake of renewing his offer to her. It was soon done when he had come.

“Isabel,” he said, “I have brought with me that letter which you wrote to me. Will you take it back again?” And he held it out in his hand.

“Nay; why should I take back my own letter?” she answered, smiling.

===Chinese===
第二天上午十一点钟上下，他来了。她看得出，一家人都存心让她同他单独见面，她也不去违拗这番用意。用意既已在，这场会面自然势在必行，早见晚见都一样。这件事上，她和继母之间并没有可以交底的情分——就连同她同父异母的妹妹们之间，也没有特别的知心。可她明白，他们都认定欧文先生这天上午登门，是要向她重申前请。他来了之后，事情不多时便见了分晓。

“伊莎贝尔，”他说，“你写给我的那封信，我带在身上。你可愿意收回去？”说着，他把信递了出来。

“嗳；我自己写的信，干吗要收回去？”她笑着答道。

===Original===
“Because I hope—I do not say I trust—but I hope that I may receive an altered answer.”

“Why should you hope so?” she asked, foolishly enough.

“Because I love you so dearly. Let me say something very plainly. If it be a long story, forgive me because of its importance to myself. I did think that you were—well, inclined to like me.”

“Like you! I always liked you. I do like you.”

===Chinese===
“因为我抱着希望——我不说满怀信托——只说我抱着希望，盼着换得一个不一样的回答。”

“你凭什么抱这样的希望？”她问道，问得傻气。

“因为我爱你爱得深。让我把话说得直截些。这话若嫌长，请你看在它对我何等要紧的份上担待。我确实当你是——嗯，有几分喜欢我的。”

“喜欢！我一向喜欢你。如今也喜欢你。”

===Original===
“I hoped more. Perhaps I thought more. Nay, Isabel, do not interrupt me. When they told me that you were to be your uncle’s heir, I knew that you ought not to marry me.”

“Why not?”

“Well, I knew that it should not be so. I knew that your uncle would think so.”

“Yes, he thought so.”

“I knew that he would, and I accepted your answer as conveying his decision. I had not intended to ask the heiress of Llanfeare to be my wife.”

“Why not? Why not?”

===Chinese===
“我指望的是更多。或许我心里想的也是更多。别打岔，伊莎贝尔。他们告诉我，你要做你伯父的继承人了，我便知道你不该嫁我。”

“为什么不该？”

“唉，我知道事情不该如此。我知道你伯父会这么想。”

“是的，他是这么想的。”

“我就知道他会这样，因此我把你的回答当作传达了他的决定。我本不曾打算求兰菲尔的女继承人做我的妻子。”

“为什么没打算？为什么？”

===Original===
“I had not intended to ask the heiress of Llanfeare to be my wife,” he said, repeating the words. “I learned last night that it was not to be so.”

“No; it is not to be so.”

“Then why should not Isabel Brodrick be the wife of William Owen, if she likes him—if only she can bring herself to like him well enough?”

She could not say that she did not like him well enough. She could not force herself to tell such a lie! And yet there was her settled purpose still strong in her mind. Having refused him when she believed herself to be rich, she could not bring herself to take him now that she was poor. She only shook her head mournfully.

“You cannot like me well enough for that?”

===Chinese===
“我本不曾打算求兰菲尔的女继承人做我的妻子，”他把那句话又说了一遍。“昨儿夜里我听说，事情不会再是那样了。”

“是啊；不会再是那样了。”

“那么，伊莎贝尔·布罗德里克既然喜欢威廉·欧文——只要她肯让自己把他喜欢得够深——为什么不能做威廉·欧文的妻子呢？”

她说不出自己喜欢他喜欢得不够的话。这样的谎，她逼自己也逼不出来！可她那个拿定了的主意，还硬硬地立在她心里。自以为富有的时候回绝了他，如今穷了，她再不能让自己去接受他。她只是哀伤地摇了摇头。

“你就不能把我喜欢到那个地步？”

===Original===
“It must not be so.”

“Must not? Why must not?”

“It cannot be so.”

“Then, Isabel, you must say that you do not love me.”

“I need say nothing, Mr. Owen.” Again she smiled as she spoke to him. “It is enough for me to say that it cannot be so. If I ask you not to press me further, I am sure that you will not do so.”

“I shall press you further,” he said, as he left her; “but I will leave you a week to think of it.”

===Chinese===
“这事不应当成。”

“不应当？为什么不应当？”

“这事成不了。”

“那么，伊莎贝尔，你就得说你并不爱我。”

“我什么也不必说，欧文先生。”她同他说话时又笑了笑。“我只消说这事成不了，也就尽够了。我若求你别再逼我，我确信你不会逼的。”

“我还是要来逼你的，”他离开她时说，“不过我留一个星期给你想。”

===Original===
She took the week to think of it, and from day to day her mind would change as she thought of it. Why should she not marry him, if thus they might both be happy? Why should she cling to a resolution made by her when she was in error as to the truth? She knew now, she was now quite certain, that when he had first come to her he had known nothing of her promised inheritance. He had come then simply because he loved her, and for that reason, and for that reason only, he had now come again. And yet—and yet, there was her resolution! And there was the ground on which she had founded it! Though he might not remember it now, would he not remember hereafter that she had refused him when she was rich and accepted him when she was poor? Where then would be her martyrdom, where her glory, where her pride? Were she to do so, she would only do as would any other girl. Though she would not have been mean, she would seem to have been mean, and would so seem to his eyes. When the week was over she had told herself that she must be true to her resolution.

There had been something said about him in the family, but very little. The stepmother was indeed afraid of Isabel, though she had endeavoured to conquer her own fear of using authority; and her half-sisters, though they loved her, held her in awe. There was so little that was weak about her, so little that was self-indulgent, so little that was like the other girls around them! It was known that Mr. Owen was to come again on a certain day at a certain hour, and it was known also for what purpose he was to come; but no one had dared to ask a direct question as to the result of his coming.

He came, and on this occasion her firmness almost deserted her. When he entered the room he seemed to her to be bigger than before, and more like her master. As the idea that he was so fell upon her, she became aware that she loved him better than ever. She began to know that with such a look as he now wore he would be sure to conquer. She did not tell herself that she would yield, but thoughts flitted across her as to what might be the best manner of yielding.

===Chinese===
这一个星期她当真用来想了，一天又一天，越想心思越变。既然两个人都能得福，她为什么不嫁他呢？一个在不明真相时拿定的主意，她凭什么要死守着不放？如今她知道，如今她千真万确地知道，他头一回来向她求婚时，对她那许诺中的继承权一无所知。他那时来，只因他爱她；这一回他再来，也只为这个缘故，单只为这个缘故。然而——然而，她有她的主意呀！她立这主意的那番根据也还立在原地呀！他眼下纵然不记得，日后岂不会记起：她富的时候回绝了他，穷的时候倒应允了他？那时她的殉道何在，荣耀何在，骄傲何在？她若那么做，不过是随便哪一个姑娘都会做的事。她本不俗，可看上去就俗了，在他眼里就俗了。一个星期过完，她对自己说：她必须忠于自己的主意。

这件事家里人也议论过几句，却极少。继母其实有些怕伊莎贝尔，尽管她竭力要压下自己那份不敢拿出长辈权威来的胆怯；同父异母的妹妹们虽爱她，却也敬她三分。她身上软弱的气息那么少，放纵自己的时候那么少，同她们身边那些姑娘相像的地方那么少！大家都知道欧文先生某日某时要再来，也都知道他来是为了什么；可是没有一个人敢直接问他这一来结果如何。

他来了，这一回，她的坚定几乎弃她而去。他进屋的时候，在她眼里似乎比先前高大，更像她的主人。这个念头一落到她心上，她便觉出自己比以往任何时候都更爱他。她开始明白，带着他此刻脸上这样的神情，他一定会赢。她没有对自己说她要屈服，可心头已有念头闪过：屈服，该用怎样的方式才最得体。

===Original===
“Isabel,” he said, taking her by the hand, “Isabel, I have come again, as I told you that I would.”
